Request Pipeline Handbook
Express · MongoDB · scale
Node · Express · MongoDB · production

Everything between the socket and the document

A backend handbook for engineers who already ship Express APIs and now have to make them correct, observable and survivable at a few million users. The event loop, the middleware chain, why ODMs exist at all, MongoDB modelling and indexes, then caching, queues, load balancing, sharding and the deployment mechanics — with three full project blueprints at the end.

Part I · Node runtime Part II · Express Part III · MongoDB & ODMs Part IV · Scaling Part V · Blueprints
00

The shape of a request

This is the diagram the whole book hangs from. Every chapter is one box in it, and every production incident you will ever debug happens at one of these boundaries.

client │ DNS → TCP → TLS ▼ CDN / edge cache hit? ──▶ response (0 origin cost) │ ▼ load balancer health checks, TLS termination, L7 routing │ ▼ reverse proxy nginx: compression, static, timeouts, buffering │ ▼ node process one thread of JS + libuv thread pool │ ├─ middleware request id → logging → cors → helmet → body parse │ ├─ rate limit token bucket in Redis │ ├─ auth verify token → load principal │ ├─ validate zod/joi schema, reject 400 early │ ├─ route handler thin: parse, call service, format │ ├─ service business rules, transactions, invariants │ └─ repository the ONLY layer that knows about Mongo │ ├──▶ cache Redis: read-through, locks, sessions, queues ├──▶ queue BullMQ / Kafka: email, webhooks, exports, outbox │ ▼ MongoDB replica set → primary for writes, secondaries for reads │ sharded when one primary is no longer enough ▼ disk the only place that actually remembers anything

Three habits separate senior backend work from working backend work, and they run through every chapter:

Numbers before opinions

“Add a cache” is not an answer. “p99 is 800 ms, 92% of it is one unindexed query returning 40 kB documents” is. Every scaling chapter starts from a measurement.

Failure is the default

Networks partition, primaries step down, queues back up, clients retry. Every design decision here is judged by what happens when the dependency is slow rather than down.

Boundaries you can enforce

If a route handler can reach the Mongo driver, it eventually will. Layering only helps when it is a rule the tooling checks, not a convention in a wiki.

Interview frame

The single most common senior interview question in this space is “your API is slow, walk me through what you do”. A complete answer names a tool at every layer of that diagram, in order, and starts by asking whether it is slow for everyone or for one query. Chapter 45 is that answer written out.

01

V8, libuv & the event loop

Node is one JavaScript thread plus a C library that does everything else. Almost every Node performance question — “why did one request slow down all the others?”, “why is my CPU at 100% with 20 requests?”, “what is the thread pool?” — is answered by that sentence and the six phases below.

What is actually in the process

V8 compiles and runs your JS. One isolate, one main thread, its own heap + GC. libuv event loop, thread pool (default 4), async file I/O, DNS, timers. c-ares async DNS (used by dns.resolve*, NOT by dns.lookup) OpenSSL TLS llhttp HTTP parsing node core the JS + C++ bindings you import as 'fs', 'http', 'crypto'… Network I/O ──▶ kernel (epoll/kqueue/IOCP) — no threads needed File I/O ──▶ libuv thread pool — 4 threads by default crypto/zlib ──▶ libuv thread pool — same 4 threads your JS ──▶ the one main thread — blocking here blocks everything
The consequence people miss

fs, crypto.pbkdf2, zlib and dns.lookup share a four-thread pool. Eight concurrent bcrypt hashes therefore queue two deep, and a single synchronous fs.readFileSync on a hot path stalls the entire process. Raise it with UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE (max 1024) only after measuring — more threads on fewer cores just adds contention.

The six phases

  1. timers — callbacks scheduled by setTimeout/setInterval whose threshold has elapsed.
  2. pending callbacks — some system errors deferred from the previous iteration (e.g. TCP ECONNREFUSED).
  3. idle / prepare — internal.
  4. poll — the important one: wait for I/O and run I/O callbacks. This is where the loop blocks when idle.
  5. check — setImmediate callbacks.
  6. close callbacks — socket.on('close') and friends.

Between every phase transition and after every individual callback, Node drains two microtask queues: process.nextTick first, then promise continuations. That ordering is the interview question.

javascript · ordering
console.log('1 sync');

setTimeout(() => console.log('6 timer'), 0);
setImmediate(() => console.log('7 check'));

process.nextTick(() => console.log('3 nextTick'));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('4 microtask'));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('5 microtask'));

console.log('2 sync');

// 1 sync → 2 sync → 3 nextTick → 4 microtask → 5 microtask → 6 timer → 7 check
//
// nextTick beats promises. Both beat any phase.
// An unbounded nextTick chain starves I/O completely — the loop never advances.

Blocking, measured

Node's throughput model assumes every callback returns in microseconds. A 200 ms synchronous callback does not slow one request by 200 ms; it adds 200 ms to the latency of every request queued behind it. That is why p99 latency collapses long before CPU saturates.

javascript · detect it
import { monitorEventLoopDelay } from 'node:perf_hooks';

const h = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 10 });
h.enable();
setInterval(() => {
  // Report this as a gauge. Anything over ~20ms p99 means the loop is congested.
  metrics.gauge('event_loop_delay_p99_ms', h.percentile(99) / 1e6);
  h.reset();
}, 10_000).unref();

// Cheaper alternative used by most APM agents:
import { performance } from 'node:perf_hooks';
let last = performance.now();
setInterval(() => {
  const now = performance.now();
  metrics.gauge('event_loop_lag_ms', now - last - 1000);
  last = now;
}, 1000).unref();
Loop delay p99
< 20 msabove this, queueing dominates latency
Single callback
< 10 msanything longer belongs in a worker or a queue
GC pause
< 50 msmajor GC; watch after raising heap limits
Thread pool
4shared by fs, crypto, zlib, dns.lookup

What to do with CPU work

WorkWhere it belongsWhy
JSON parse of a 5 MB bodyreject it, or stream-parseJSON.parse is synchronous and unbounded — it is a denial-of-service vector, not a performance issue
Password hashingasync argon2/bcrypt (thread pool), rate-limitedintentionally slow; never the sync variant
Image resize, PDF rendera queue with dedicated workersseconds of CPU; must not share the API's process
Report aggregationthe database, or a worker + queuelet Mongo aggregate; do not pull 200 k documents into Node
Per-request template rendering, small cryptoinline is finesub-millisecond
Tight numeric loops on large arraysworker_threadsreal parallelism, shared memory via SharedArrayBuffer
02

Modules, ESM & resolution

The CommonJS/ESM split is the most common source of “works locally, breaks in Docker” in Node backends. It is worth twenty minutes of understanding once.

CommonJSESM
Syntaxrequire / module.exportsimport / export
Loadingsynchronous, cached by resolved pathasynchronous, statically analysed, hoisted
Bindingsa copy of the value at require timelive bindings
Top-level awaitnoyes
Conditional importrequire() anywhereawait import() only
__dirnameavailableuse import.meta.dirname (or derive from import.meta.url)
Cyclespartial exports, silent bugshoisted declarations, clearer errors
Extensions in importsoptionalrequired for relative paths
json · package.json that actually works
{
  "name": "acme-api",
  "type": "module",
  "engines": { "node": ">=20.11" },
  "imports": {
    "#config": "./src/config/index.js",
    "#db/*": "./src/db/*.js"
  },
  "exports": {
    ".": { "import": "./src/index.js", "require": "./dist/index.cjs" },
    "./testing": "./src/testing/index.js"
  },
  "scripts": {
    "dev": "node --watch --env-file=.env src/server.js",
    "start": "node src/server.js",
    "test": "node --test --experimental-test-coverage",
    "lint": "eslint . && tsc --noEmit"
  }
}
Subpath imports beat ../../../

The imports field gives you internal aliases (#config, #db/mongo) with no bundler, no tsconfig path mapping, and no runtime resolver hack. It is the single cheapest structural improvement to a Node codebase.

javascript · ESM equivalents of CJS habits
// __dirname / __filename
import { dirname } from 'node:path';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
const __dirname = import.meta.dirname ?? dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));

// require a JSON file
import pkg from './package.json' with { type: 'json' };

// conditional / lazy load
const { default: sharp } = await import('sharp');

// "is this the entry point?" (the CJS require.main === module idiom)
import { argv } from 'node:process';
if (import.meta.url === new URL(argv[1], 'file:').href) main();

// createRequire when a dependency is CJS-only and needs resolution
import { createRequire } from 'node:module';
const require = createRequire(import.meta.url);
The failure you will hit

ERR_REQUIRE_ESM or Cannot use import statement outside a module means the file's module system disagrees with package.json's type. Pick ESM for new services, set "type": "module", use .cjs for the rare CommonJS file, and keep the extension on every relative import. Also: Node caches modules by resolved path, so a dependency present twice in node_modules is two separate singletons — the classic cause of “my config object is empty in one file”.

03

Streams, buffers & backpressure

Streams are how Node handles data larger than memory, and backpressure is the mechanism that stops a fast producer from killing your process. Getting this wrong is how a file-upload endpoint takes down an API server.

Readable
A source: a socket, a file, a Mongo cursor, an S3 object body.
Writable
A sink: the HTTP response, a file, an upload.
Duplex
Both, independently: a TCP socket.
Transform
A duplex whose output derives from its input: gzip, cipher, CSV parser.
highWaterMark
The buffer threshold. When the internal buffer exceeds it, write() returns false — that is backpressure.
javascript
import { pipeline } from 'node:stream/promises';
import { createReadStream, createWriteStream } from 'node:fs';
import { createGzip } from 'node:zlib';
import { Transform } from 'node:stream';

// WRONG: buffers the whole file in memory, ignores backpressure, leaks on error
const data = await readFile(path);          // 2 GB file → process dies
res.end(data);

// WRONG: manual piping without error handling — one leaked fd per failed request
createReadStream(path).pipe(res);           // no destroy on client disconnect

// RIGHT: pipeline() propagates errors and destroys every stream on failure
await pipeline(
  createReadStream(path),
  createGzip({ level: 6 }),
  res,
);

// A transform with correct backpressure: never push more than asked
const redact = new Transform({
  objectMode: true,
  transform(row, _enc, cb) {
    cb(null, { ...row, ssn: undefined });   // cb() signals "ready for more"
  },
});

// Streaming a large export straight from Mongo to the client — constant memory
app.get('/exports/orders.ndjson', async (req, res) => {
  res.setHeader('content-type', 'application/x-ndjson');
  const cursor = orders.find({ tenantId: req.tenant.id }).stream();
  await pipeline(
    cursor,
    new Transform({
      objectMode: true,
      transform: (doc, _e, cb) => cb(null, JSON.stringify(toDto(doc)) + '\n'),
    }),
    res,
  );
});
Always use pipeline()

.pipe() does not forward errors and does not destroy the source when the destination fails. A client that disconnects mid-download leaves the read stream open — file descriptors leak until the process hits EMFILE. pipeline() (or stream.pipeline with a callback) fixes both, and the promise form gives you try/catch.

Buffers, and the encoding bug everyone ships once

javascript
// Buffer.length is BYTES, not characters.
Buffer.from('héllo').length;      // 6
'héllo'.length;                   // 5
Buffer.byteLength('héllo');       // 6  ← use this for Content-Length

// Never allocate uninitialised memory that could reach a response:
Buffer.allocUnsafe(1024);         // contains old heap data — a real leak vector
Buffer.alloc(1024);               // zero-filled

// Constant-time comparison for secrets (tokens, HMACs, webhook signatures)
import { timingSafeEqual, createHmac } from 'node:crypto';
function verify(sig, body, secret) {
  const expected = createHmac('sha256', secret).update(body).digest();
  const given = Buffer.from(sig, 'hex');
  return given.length === expected.length && timingSafeEqual(given, expected);
}
// `sig === expected` leaks the answer through timing. This is exercised in the wild.
04

Async patterns & error handling

Node's failure modes are unusual because an unhandled rejection can take the process down and a swallowed one can hide a data-loss bug forever. The rules are short and non-negotiable.

javascript · the composition toolkit
// SEQUENTIAL when there is a real dependency
const user = await users.findById(id);
const orders = await ordersFor(user.id);

// PARALLEL when there is not — this is the most common easy win in a slow endpoint
const [user, entitlements, flags] = await Promise.all([
  users.findById(id),
  billing.entitlements(id),
  flags.forUser(id),
]);

// PARTIAL FAILURE tolerated: allSettled never rejects
const results = await Promise.allSettled(webhookTargets.map(post));
const failed = results.filter(r => r.status === 'rejected');

// FIRST SUCCESS (any) vs FIRST SETTLED (race)
const fastest = await Promise.any([primary(), replica()]);   // ignores failures
const first = await Promise.race([work(), timeout(5_000)]);  // classic timeout

// TIMEOUT with real cancellation (AbortSignal), not a dangling promise
const res = await fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5_000) });

// BOUNDED CONCURRENCY — unbounded Promise.all over 50k items exhausts sockets
async function mapLimit(items, limit, fn) {
  const out = new Array(items.length);
  let i = 0;
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: limit }, async () => {
    while (i < items.length) {
      const idx = i++;
      out[idx] = await fn(items[idx], idx);
    }
  }));
  return out;
}
Promise.all fails fast, but does not cancel

When one promise rejects, Promise.all rejects immediately — while the other operations keep running to completion. If they have side effects you now have a partial write with no error surfaced. Use allSettled plus explicit compensation, or pass one AbortController signal to all of them.

Process-level handlers

javascript · server.js
process.on('unhandledRejection', (reason) => {
  logger.fatal({ err: reason }, 'unhandled rejection');
  shutdown(1);                      // a rejected promise means unknown state
});

process.on('uncaughtException', (err, origin) => {
  logger.fatal({ err, origin }, 'uncaught exception');
  shutdown(1);                      // NEVER continue serving after this
});

// Graceful shutdown — the difference between a clean deploy and 502s
let shuttingDown = false;
async function shutdown(code = 0) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;

  server.close();                        // 1. stop accepting new connections
  await sleep(config.drainMs);           // 2. let the LB notice /health failing
  await Promise.race([                   // 3. finish in-flight requests
    once(server, 'close'),
    sleep(config.shutdownTimeoutMs),
  ]);
  await queue.close();                   // 4. stop taking new jobs
  await mongoose.connection.close(false);// 5. close the DB (false = don't force)
  await redis.quit();
  logger.flush?.();
  process.exit(code);
}
for (const sig of ['SIGTERM', 'SIGINT']) process.on(sig, () => shutdown(0));
Why you must exit on uncaughtException

An exception that escaped every handler means some code stopped executing at an arbitrary line — a lock unreleased, a transaction unclosed, a counter half-incremented. Continuing to serve requests from that process risks silent corruption. Log, drain, exit, and let the orchestrator restart you. Combine with a supervisor and you have a self-healing service; without an exit you have a haunted one.

AsyncLocalStorage: request context without threading it through

javascript
import { AsyncLocalStorage } from 'node:async_hooks';
export const ctx = new AsyncLocalStorage();

// middleware
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const store = {
    requestId: req.headers['x-request-id'] ?? crypto.randomUUID(),
    tenantId: null,          // set later by auth
    startedAt: performance.now(),
  };
  res.setHeader('x-request-id', store.requestId);
  ctx.run(store, next);      // everything downstream, sync or async, sees this
});

// anywhere, at any depth, with no parameter passing
export const log = pino().child({}); // base
export function logger() {
  const s = ctx.getStore();
  return log.child({ requestId: s?.requestId, tenantId: s?.tenantId });
}
// Cost: a small per-await overhead. Worth it — correlated logs across services
// are the difference between a five-minute and a five-hour investigation.
05

Clustering, workers & processes

One Node process uses one core for JavaScript. Using a 16-core machine therefore requires either 16 processes or an orchestrator running 16 containers — and the choice has real operational consequences.

clusterworker_threadsN containers
Memoryseparate heap per workershared process memory; SharedArrayBufferseparate
Use forusing all cores for I/O-bound HTTPCPU-bound work inside one serviceeverything, when you have an orchestrator
Failure isolationgood — one worker crash is survivablepoor — a worker can affect the processbest
Scaling unitcores on one boxthreads for computationpods across a fleet
Observabilityper-worker metrics need aggregationhardnative to the platform
Modern default

On Kubernetes or ECS, prefer one Node process per container and let the orchestrator run many. You get better isolation, real rolling deploys, per-instance metrics and horizontal autoscaling. Reach for cluster when you are on a single VM, and for worker_threads only for genuine CPU work.

javascript · cluster, done correctly
import cluster from 'node:cluster';
import { availableParallelism } from 'node:os';

if (cluster.isPrimary) {
  const n = Number(process.env.WEB_CONCURRENCY) || availableParallelism();
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) cluster.fork();

  let restarts = 0;
  cluster.on('exit', (worker, code, signal) => {
    logger.warn({ pid: worker.process.pid, code, signal }, 'worker died');
    // Backoff, or a crash loop burns CPU and hides the real error
    if (++restarts > n * 10) { logger.fatal('crash loop'); process.exit(1); }
    setTimeout(() => cluster.fork(), Math.min(1000 * restarts, 30_000));
  });

  // Zero-downtime reload: replace workers one at a time
  process.on('SIGUSR2', async () => {
    for (const w of Object.values(cluster.workers)) {
      w.send('shutdown');
      await once(w, 'exit');
      cluster.fork();
      await sleep(500);
    }
  });
} else {
  startServer();
}
javascript · worker_threads for CPU work
// pool.js — a minimal reusable worker pool
import { Worker } from 'node:worker_threads';
import { EventEmitter } from 'node:events';

export class WorkerPool extends EventEmitter {
  #idle = []; #queue = []; #all = [];
  constructor(file, size = availableParallelism()) {
    super();
    for (let i = 0; i < size; i++) this.#spawn(file);
  }
  #spawn(file) {
    const w = new Worker(file);
    w.on('message', (msg) => { w.__resolve?.(msg); this.#release(w); });
    w.on('error', (err) => { w.__reject?.(err); this.#spawn(file); });
    this.#all.push(w); this.#release(w);
  }
  #release(w) {
    const job = this.#queue.shift();
    if (job) return this.#run(w, job);
    this.#idle.push(w);
  }
  #run(w, { payload, resolve, reject }) {
    w.__resolve = resolve; w.__reject = reject;
    w.postMessage(payload);
  }
  run(payload) {
    return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
      const w = this.#idle.pop();
      w ? this.#run(w, { payload, resolve, reject })
        : this.#queue.push({ payload, resolve, reject });
    });
  }
  async destroy() { await Promise.all(this.#all.map(w => w.terminate())); }
}
Sizing

Use os.availableParallelism(), not os.cpus().length — in a container with a CPU limit the latter reports the host's cores and you will over-fork by an order of magnitude. In Kubernetes also set --max-old-space-size below the memory limit, or the OOM killer terminates the pod before V8 ever runs a full GC.

06

HTTP you have to know

Most API design arguments dissolve once everyone knows what the protocol already says. This chapter is the subset that comes up in reviews and interviews.

Status codes that carry real meaning

CodeMeansUse it when
200 / 201 / 204OK / Created / No content201 with a Location header on create; 204 for a successful delete with no body
202Acceptedwork was queued — return a status URL, not a fake result
304Not modifiedclient's If-None-Match matched your ETag — the cheapest response you can serve
400Malformedsyntactically invalid or failed schema validation
401 vs 403Unauthenticated vs unauthorised401: we do not know who you are (retry with credentials). 403: we know, and you may not. Getting this backwards breaks client retry logic.
404 vs 403Absent vs forbiddenreturn 404 for resources in another tenant — 403 confirms the id exists, which is an information leak
409Conflictuniqueness violation, or optimistic-concurrency version mismatch
412 / 428Precondition failed / requiredIf-Match did not match; or you require a precondition for a lost-update-sensitive write
422Unprocessablesyntactically fine, semantically invalid (business rule). Many APIs use 400 for both — pick one and document it.
429Too many requestsalways with Retry-After and rate-limit headers
499/client abort—not a real status; nginx logs it when the client disconnects. Watch it: a spike means your p99 exceeded client timeouts.
500 vs 502/503/504Our bug vs infrastructure503 with Retry-After when shedding load; 504 when an upstream timed out. Never return 500 for an expected failure.

Idempotency and safety

Safe
GET, HEAD, OPTIONS — no observable state change. Proxies and crawlers may call them freely, which is why a GET /orders/1/delete endpoint is a genuine outage waiting to happen.
Idempotent
GET, HEAD, PUT, DELETE — N identical calls have the same effect as one. Safe for clients and load balancers to retry.
Neither
POST, PATCH. A retried POST can create two orders. Fix with an Idempotency-Key header (Chapter 44).

Caching headers, in one table

HeaderEffect
Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutableFingerprinted static assets. Never revalidate.
Cache-Control: private, no-cachePer-user data: may be stored by the browser, but must revalidate every time. Not the same as no-store.
Cache-Control: no-storeNever write to disk or memory. Auth responses, anything with a token.
Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=300The workhorse for a read-heavy API behind a CDN: 60 s fresh at the edge, then serve stale for 5 minutes while refreshing in the background.
ETag + If-None-MatchStrong validator. Enables 304, and — with If-Match — optimistic concurrency on writes.
Vary: Accept-Encoding, AuthorizationTells caches which request headers change the response. Omitting Authorization on a per-user response behind a shared cache serves one user's data to another — a real and common breach.
The most expensive header mistake

A Cache-Control: public response containing per-user data, sitting behind a CDN. It will be served to other users. Default your API to private, no-store and opt individual endpoints into public caching deliberately.

Connection-level behaviour that affects your p99

  • Keep-alive. Node's default HTTP agent had keep-alive off historically; in modern versions it is on. Confirm it for every outbound client — TLS handshakes per request will dominate your latency budget.
  • HTTP/2 multiplexing removes head-of-line blocking at the application layer, so the classic “shard across six domains” advice is obsolete. Terminate it at the load balancer; Node behind it can speak HTTP/1.1.
  • Timeouts. Set server.headersTimeout, requestTimeout and keepAliveTimeout. A Node keepAliveTimeout shorter than the load balancer's idle timeout causes intermittent 502s — the LB reuses a connection Node has just closed.
  • Compression belongs at the proxy, not in Node — it is CPU on your single thread. If you must do it in Node, exclude already-compressed content types and set a size threshold.
javascript · timeouts that prevent 502s
const server = app.listen(port);

// Must be LONGER than the load balancer's idle timeout (ALB default 60s).
server.keepAliveTimeout = 65_000;
server.headersTimeout   = 66_000;   // must exceed keepAliveTimeout
server.requestTimeout   = 30_000;   // whole-request deadline (0 = disabled)
server.maxRequestsPerSocket = 0;    // unlimited; set for forced connection churn
07

Express architecture

Express is about 1 500 lines of code whose entire job is to hold an ordered array of functions and call them in turn. Once you see that, its behaviour stops being surprising.

app ── an array of layers ──▶ [ layer, layer, layer, … ] each layer = { path pattern, method?, handle(req,res,next) } app.use(fn) → layer matching any method, path prefix '/' app.use('/api', router) → layer whose handle is another router app.get('/x', fn) → layer matching GET and exactly '/x' app.use(err4args) → ERROR layer (arity 4) — skipped unless next(err) dispatch: walk the array in order ├─ pattern matches? no → next layer ├─ call handle(req, res, next) ├─ handler ends the response → dispatch STOPS ├─ handler calls next() → continue to next layer ├─ handler calls next(err) → jump to next ERROR layer └─ handler does neither → request hangs forever
javascript · a production app factory
import express from 'express';
import helmet from 'helmet';
import cors from 'cors';
import compression from 'compression';
import { pinoHttp } from 'pino-http';

export function createApp({ config, deps }) {
  const app = express();

  // 1. trust the proxy so req.ip and req.protocol are the CLIENT's, not the LB's.
  //    Use a specific hop count, never `true` — see the warning below.
  app.set('trust proxy', config.trustedProxyHops);
  app.disable('x-powered-by');
  app.set('etag', 'strong');
  app.set('query parser', 'simple');   // avoid prototype-pollution-prone parsing

  // 2. observability first, so even rejected requests are logged
  app.use(requestContext());           // AsyncLocalStorage + x-request-id
  app.use(pinoHttp({ logger, redact: ['req.headers.authorization', 'req.headers.cookie'] }));
  app.use(metricsMiddleware());

  // 3. security and protocol concerns
  app.use(helmet());
  app.use(cors(config.cors));
  app.use(compression({ threshold: 1024 }));   // prefer the proxy if you have one

  // 4. body parsing — always with an explicit limit
  app.use(express.json({ limit: '100kb', strict: true }));
  app.use(express.urlencoded({ extended: false, limit: '100kb' }));

  // 5. liveness/readiness BEFORE auth and rate limiting, or your LB gets 401s
  app.get('/livez', (_req, res) => res.status(200).send('ok'));
  app.get('/readyz', readiness(deps));

  // 6. the API
  app.use('/v1', rateLimit(config.rateLimit), routes(deps));

  // 7. terminal handlers, in this order and last
  app.use(notFoundHandler);
  app.use(errorHandler);               // arity 4 — must be registered last
  return app;
}
trust proxy: true is a vulnerability

With true, Express believes the leftmost value in X-Forwarded-For — which the client controls. Every IP-based rate limit and IP allowlist becomes trivially bypassable by sending a forged header. Set the number of proxies in front of you (app.set('trust proxy', 1)) or a specific subnet.

Express 5, and what changed

  • Async errors are caught. A rejected promise from a handler is passed to next(err) automatically. On Express 4 you need a wrapper (below) or express-async-errors.
  • Path matching is stricter — path-to-regexp v8 removed the loose regex-in-string forms; named wildcards are /*splat and optional segments use {/:id}.
  • Removed: res.send(status), res.json(status, body), app.del, req.param(name). Use res.status(n).json(body).
  • req.query is a getter, and req.body is undefined rather than {} when no parser matched — check for it.
javascript · the async wrapper (Express 4)
export const ah = (fn) => (req, res, next) =>
  Promise.resolve(fn(req, res, next)).catch(next);

// Without it, this throws into the void and the request hangs until timeout:
app.get('/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const user = await users.findById(req.params.id);   // throws → unhandled rejection
  res.json(user);
});

// With it, the error reaches your error middleware:
app.get('/users/:id', ah(async (req, res) => {
  const user = await users.findById(req.params.id);
  if (!user) throw new NotFound('user', req.params.id);
  res.json(toDto(user));
}));
Interview follow-up

“Why did my request hang?” The answer names the dispatcher: a middleware neither ended the response nor called next. The two everyday causes are a forgotten next() in a conditional branch, and an async handler that rejected on a framework version that does not forward rejections.

08

Middleware in depth

Order is the entire semantics. This chapter is the canonical order, the reasons for it, and the middleware every production API needs.

The order, and why
#MiddlewareWhy here
1request id + AsyncLocalStorageeverything downstream, including errors, must be correlatable
2request loggingso a request rejected at step 4 still appears in logs
3helmet / security headerscheap, and must apply to error responses too
4CORSmust precede anything that can reject, or the browser reports a CORS error instead of your real one
5health endpointsbefore auth and rate limiting — probes carry no credentials
6rate limitingbefore body parsing, so you do not spend CPU parsing traffic you will reject
7body parsing (with limits)after limits, before validation
8authenticationneeds headers/cookies, not the body
9tenant resolutionafter auth; everything below must be scoped
10authorisationper-route, closest to the handler
11validationimmediately before the handler, so the handler can trust its input absolutely
12route handler
13404 handlerafter all routes
14error handler (arity 4)must be last, or errors from later middleware bypass it
javascript · writing middleware correctly
// A factory, so it is configurable and testable in isolation
export function requireScopes(...required) {
  return (req, _res, next) => {
    const held = new Set(req.principal?.scopes ?? []);
    const missing = required.filter(s => !held.has(s));
    if (missing.length) return next(new Forbidden({ missingScopes: missing }));
    next();
  };
}

// Timing middleware: hook the response, do not wrap res.end by hand
export function metricsMiddleware() {
  return (req, res, next) => {
    const start = process.hrtime.bigint();
    res.on('finish', () => {
      const ms = Number(process.hrtime.bigint() - start) / 1e6;
      // Label with the ROUTE PATTERN, never the raw URL — /users/:id, not /users/8f3a…
      // Unbounded label cardinality is how you take down Prometheus.
      httpDuration.observe(
        { method: req.method, route: req.route?.path ?? 'unmatched', status: res.statusCode },
        ms / 1000,
      );
    });
    next();
  };
}

// Conditional application without branching inside the middleware
export const unless = (predicate, mw) => (req, res, next) =>
  predicate(req) ? next() : mw(req, res, next);

app.use(unless(r => r.path.startsWith('/webhooks/'), express.json({ limit: '100kb' })));
Webhooks need the raw body

Signature verification hashes the exact bytes the sender signed. express.json() consumes and re-serialises them, so any whitespace or key-order difference breaks the HMAC. Mount express.raw({ type: '*/*' }) on the webhook path before the JSON parser, verify, then parse yourself.

Error middleware

javascript · one error handler for the whole API
// Arity 4 is what marks this as an error handler. A default parameter or a
// rest parameter changes fn.length and Express will treat it as normal middleware.
export function errorHandler(err, req, res, _next) {
  const problem = toProblem(err);            // map any error to RFC 9457 shape

  // 5xx is our fault: log with the stack. 4xx is the caller's: log at info.
  const log = logger().child({ err, requestId: req.id });
  problem.status >= 500 ? log.error('request failed') : log.info('request rejected');

  if (res.headersSent) return req.destroy(); // cannot change the status now

  res
    .status(problem.status)
    .type('application/problem+json')
    .json({
      type: problem.type,                    // stable URI identifying the error class
      title: problem.title,                  // short, human, does not change per instance
      status: problem.status,
      detail: problem.detail,                // safe to show the caller
      instance: req.id,                      // correlate with your logs
      ...problem.extensions,                 // e.g. { errors: [{ path, message }] }
    });
}
Use a real error format

RFC 9457 problem details gives clients a machine-readable type they can branch on, a stable title for dashboards, and an instance that ties the user's screenshot to your logs. Inventing { success: false, message: '...' } means every client string-matches your prose, and you can never change a message again.

09

API & route design

API design is a compatibility contract you cannot take back. These are the decisions that are expensive to change later.

javascript · a resource router
const r = express.Router();

r.get   ('/orders',            validate(listOrdersSchema), ah(list));
r.post  ('/orders',            idempotent(), validate(createOrderSchema), ah(create));
r.get   ('/orders/:id',        validate(byIdSchema), ah(get));
r.patch ('/orders/:id',        requireMatch(), validate(patchOrderSchema), ah(patch));
r.delete('/orders/:id',        ah(remove));

// Sub-resources for one-to-many relationships
r.get   ('/orders/:id/items',  ah(listItems));

// Verbs are acceptable for actions that are NOT CRUD. Do not contort them
// into a PATCH just to stay "RESTful" — /orders/:id/cancel is clearer than
// PATCH { status: 'cancelled' } with hidden side effects and different auth.
r.post  ('/orders/:id/cancel', requireScopes('orders:write'), ah(cancel));
r.post  ('/orders/:id/refund', requireScopes('payments:write'), ah(refund));

// param middleware: load once, reuse across the route's handlers
r.param('id', ah(async (req, _res, next, id) => {
  const order = await orders.findScoped(id, req.tenant.id);
  if (!order) return next(new NotFound('order', id));   // 404, not 403 — see ch06
  req.order = order;
  next();
}));

Pagination: pick keyset, not offset

Offset (?page=42)Keyset / cursor
Cost at depthO(offset) — the database counts and discards every skipped rowO(log n) via an index seek
Correctness under writesduplicates and skips when rows are inserted or deletedstable
Jump to page Nyesno
Total countpossible but expensiveusually omitted or approximated
Verdictadmin tables onlyevery user-facing list
javascript · keyset pagination with a compound sort
// The cursor must encode EVERY field in the sort, or ties break the ordering.
const encode = (doc) => Buffer
  .from(JSON.stringify({ c: doc.createdAt.getTime(), i: doc._id.toString() }))
  .toString('base64url');
const decode = (s) => JSON.parse(Buffer.from(s, 'base64url').toString());

export async function list(req, res) {
  const limit = Math.min(Number(req.query.limit) || 25, 100);   // ALWAYS cap
  const filter = { tenantId: req.tenant.id, ...statusFilter(req.query.status) };

  if (req.query.cursor) {
    const { c, i } = decode(req.query.cursor);
    // Strict "less than" over the compound key, matching the index order exactly
    filter.$or = [
      { createdAt: { $lt: new Date(c) } },
      { createdAt: new Date(c), _id: { $lt: new ObjectId(i) } },
    ];
  }

  const docs = await orders
    .find(filter)
    .sort({ createdAt: -1, _id: -1 })      // index: { tenantId:1, createdAt:-1, _id:-1 }
    .limit(limit + 1)                      // one extra row tells us if there is more
    .toArray();

  const hasMore = docs.length > limit;
  const page = hasMore ? docs.slice(0, limit) : docs;

  res.json({
    data: page.map(toDto),
    page: { hasMore, nextCursor: hasMore ? encode(page.at(-1)) : null, limit },
  });
}

Filtering, sorting and sparse fieldsets — with an allowlist

javascript
// NEVER pass client input into a query or a sort. Both are injection vectors:
//   ?sort[$where]=...      → NoSQL injection
//   ?sort=unindexedField   → a full collection scan on demand (a DoS)
const SORTABLE = { createdAt: 'createdAt', total: 'totalCents', status: 'status' };
const FILTERABLE = new Set(['status', 'customerId', 'createdAfter', 'createdBefore']);
const FIELDS = new Set(['id', 'status', 'total', 'customer', 'createdAt']);

function buildSort(q) {
  const key = SORTABLE[String(q.sortBy ?? 'createdAt')];
  if (!key) throw new BadRequest({ sortBy: 'not sortable' });
  return { [key]: q.sortDir === 'asc' ? 1 : -1, _id: -1 };
}

function buildProjection(q) {
  if (!q.fields) return null;
  const requested = String(q.fields).split(',').filter(f => FIELDS.has(f));
  return Object.fromEntries(requested.map(f => [f, 1]));
}
Every sortable field needs an index

Exposing sort on an unindexed field hands any user a way to make the database scan the whole collection. Rate limiting does not save you — one request is enough. The allowlist and the index list should be the same list, checked in a test.

Compatibility rules

  • Additive changes are safe: new optional fields, new endpoints, new enum values if clients tolerate unknown ones (say so in the docs).
  • Breaking: removing or renaming a field, tightening validation, changing a type, changing a default, changing pagination shape, changing an error type.
  • Never change the meaning of an existing field. Add a new one and deprecate the old with a Deprecation and Sunset header plus usage metrics per client.
  • Version in the path (/v1) for a small number of major versions; it is visible in logs, routable at the proxy, and trivially cacheable. Header-based versioning is more elegant and harder to operate.
10

Validation at the boundary

The rule: nothing untyped reaches your service layer. Validate once, at the edge, and make the validated object the only thing the handler can see. This is also your first and cheapest security control.

javascript · zod schemas + a generic middleware
import { z } from 'zod';

export const createOrderSchema = {
  body: z.object({
    customerId: z.string().regex(/^[0-9a-f]{24}$/, 'must be an object id'),
    currency: z.enum(['GBP', 'EUR', 'USD']),
    items: z.array(z.object({
      sku: z.string().min(1).max(64),
      quantity: z.number().int().positive().max(999),
    })).min(1).max(100),                       // ALWAYS bound arrays
    note: z.string().max(500).optional(),
  }).strict(),                                 // reject unknown keys — mass-assignment guard
  query: z.object({}).strict(),
  params: z.object({}).strict(),
};

export const listOrdersSchema = {
  query: z.object({
    limit: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).default(25),
    cursor: z.string().max(200).optional(),
    status: z.enum(['draft', 'placed', 'shipped', 'cancelled']).optional(),
    sortBy: z.enum(['createdAt', 'total']).default('createdAt'),
    sortDir: z.enum(['asc', 'desc']).default('desc'),
  }).strict(),
};

export const validate = (schema) => (req, _res, next) => {
  const result = {};
  for (const part of ['body', 'query', 'params']) {
    if (!schema[part]) continue;
    const parsed = schema[part].safeParse(req[part]);
    if (!parsed.success) {
      return next(new ValidationError(parsed.error.issues.map(i => ({
        path: i.path.join('.'), message: i.message, code: i.code,
      }))));
    }
    result[part] = parsed.data;
  }
  // Replace the raw input so the handler CANNOT read unvalidated data.
  req.valid = result;
  next();
};
Why .strict() matters

Without it, { items: [...], role: 'admin', tenantId: 'other' } passes validation, and any code doing new Order(req.body) or Order.updateOne(filter, req.body) now writes attacker-chosen fields. That is mass assignment, and it is one of the most common real vulnerabilities in Node APIs. Either reject unknown keys or explicitly pick the fields you accept — never both-and-neither.

NoSQL injection

{ email: { $ne: null } } in a JSON body turns findOne({ email, password }) into “find any user”. Two defences, use both: schema validation that requires primitives (z.string() rejects an object), and Mongoose's sanitizeFilter / an explicit cast of every query value. Query strings are equally dangerous: ?email[$ne]= with the extended query parser produces the same object.

Where each kind of rule belongs

RuleLayerExample
Shape, type, format, boundsedge schema“quantity is a positive integer ≤ 999”
Business invariantservice / domain“an order cannot be cancelled after shipping”
Cross-entity consistencyservice, inside a transaction“stock must cover the order”
Uniquenessdatabase constraint + friendly mappingunique index on {tenantId, sku}; catch E11000 → 409
Referential integrityservice, or a schema validatorMongo has no foreign keys — you own this
Never validate uniqueness with a read

if (await findOne({email})) throw Conflict() is a race: two concurrent requests both read nothing and both insert. The only correct answer is a unique index, letting the write fail, and translating the duplicate-key error into a 409.

11

Authentication

The decision that shapes everything else is stateless tokens versus server-side sessions. There is a right answer for each situation, and “JWT because it scales” is not it.

Server-side sessionStateless JWT
StorageRedis / Mongo, cookie holds an opaque idnothing; the token is the state
Revocationimmediate — delete the recordimpossible until expiry, without a denylist (which reintroduces state)
Per-request costone cache lookup (~0.3 ms)a signature verification (~0.05 ms), no network
Payload freshnessalways currentstale until refresh — a demoted admin stays admin for the token's lifetime
Size~40 bytes0.5–2 kB on every request, including uploads
Good forfirst-party web apps, anything needing instant logoutservice-to-service, mobile, short-lived access tokens beside a refresh token
The senior answer

“For a first-party web app I use server-side sessions in Redis with a HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax cookie, because instant revocation and small requests matter more than avoiding a cache lookup. For mobile and third-party clients I use a short-lived access JWT — five to fifteen minutes — with a rotating, single-use refresh token stored server-side, which gives me revocation where it counts. What I avoid is a long-lived JWT with no server state, because there is then no way to log anyone out.”

javascript · password handling
import argon2 from 'argon2';
import { timingSafeEqual, randomBytes, createHash } from 'node:crypto';

// argon2id is the current recommendation; bcrypt (cost ≥ 12) remains acceptable.
// Never SHA-anything: it is fast, which is exactly wrong for passwords.
export const hash = (pw) => argon2.hash(pw, {
  type: argon2.argon2id, memoryCost: 19_456, timeCost: 2, parallelism: 1,
});

export async function login(email, password) {
  const user = await users.findOne({ email: String(email).toLowerCase() });

  // Always do the same work whether or not the user exists, or the response
  // time tells an attacker which emails are registered.
  const ok = user
    ? await argon2.verify(user.passwordHash, password)
    : await argon2.verify(DUMMY_HASH, password).catch(() => false);

  if (!ok || !user) throw new Unauthorized('invalid credentials');   // same message for both
  if (argon2.needsRehash(user.passwordHash)) {                       // transparent upgrade
    await users.updateOne({ _id: user._id }, { $set: { passwordHash: await hash(password) } });
  }
  return user;
}

// Password reset tokens: random, single-use, short-lived, and stored HASHED
export async function issueReset(userId) {
  const raw = randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
  await resets.insertOne({
    userId,
    tokenHash: createHash('sha256').update(raw).digest('hex'),  // a DB leak must not grant resets
    expiresAt: new Date(Date.now() + 15 * 60_000),
    usedAt: null,
  });
  return raw;                                                    // emailed once, never stored
}
javascript · refresh token rotation with reuse detection
// Each refresh issues a NEW refresh token and invalidates the old one.
// If an old token is presented again, it was stolen (or replayed) — kill the family.
export async function refresh(rawToken) {
  const tokenHash = sha256(rawToken);
  const rec = await refreshTokens.findOne({ tokenHash });

  if (!rec) throw new Unauthorized('invalid refresh token');

  if (rec.usedAt) {
    // Reuse detected: revoke every token descended from the same login.
    await refreshTokens.updateMany({ familyId: rec.familyId }, { $set: { revokedAt: new Date() } });
    logger().warn({ userId: rec.userId, familyId: rec.familyId }, 'refresh reuse — family revoked');
    throw new Unauthorized('session revoked');
  }
  if (rec.revokedAt || rec.expiresAt < new Date()) throw new Unauthorized('expired');

  const next = randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
  await refreshTokens.bulkWrite([
    { updateOne: { filter: { _id: rec._id }, update: { $set: { usedAt: new Date() } } } },
    { insertOne: { document: {
        userId: rec.userId, familyId: rec.familyId, tokenHash: sha256(next),
        expiresAt: new Date(Date.now() + 30 * 864e5), createdAt: new Date(), usedAt: null,
    } } },
  ]);

  return { accessToken: signAccess(rec.userId), refreshToken: next };
}
JWT verification mistakes that are exploited
  • Not pinning the algorithm. Always pass { algorithms: ['RS256'] }. Without it, a token with alg: none or an HMAC signed with the public key may be accepted.
  • Not validating iss, aud and exp. A valid token for a different audience is not a valid token for you.
  • Trusting kid blindly. Fetch keys from a cached JWKS endpoint and reject unknown key ids; never load a key from a URL in the token.
  • Storing the token in localStorage. Any XSS reads it. Use an HttpOnly cookie for browsers, or accept the risk knowingly for a native client.
  • Putting authorisation data in the token and never re-checking. Roles change; tokens do not.
javascript · cookie and session settings
app.use(session({
  name: 'sid',                            // not the default — do not advertise the stack
  store: new RedisStore({ client: redis, prefix: 'sess:', ttl: 86_400 }),
  secret: config.sessionSecrets,          // an ARRAY: [newest, ...older] enables rotation
  resave: false,
  saveUninitialized: false,               // no cookie until there is something to store
  rolling: true,                          // sliding expiry on activity
  cookie: {
    httpOnly: true,                       // JS cannot read it → XSS cannot steal it
    secure: config.isProd,                // HTTPS only
    sameSite: 'lax',                      // blocks the common CSRF vector
    maxAge: 86_400_000,
    path: '/',
    domain: config.cookieDomain,          // omit unless you genuinely need subdomains
  },
}));

// Regenerate the session id on privilege change, or you are open to session fixation
req.session.regenerate((err) => { req.session.userId = user.id; });
12

Authorisation & multi-tenancy

Authentication asks who you are; authorisation asks what you may do to this object. Most real breaches are authorisation bugs — specifically, a missing tenant filter.

javascript · the single most important pattern in this book
// WRONG — the classic broken-object-level-authorisation bug. Any authenticated
// user can read any order by guessing or enumerating an id.
app.get('/orders/:id', ah(async (req, res) => {
  res.json(await Order.findById(req.params.id));
}));

// RIGHT — the tenant is part of the QUERY, not a check after the fact.
app.get('/orders/:id', ah(async (req, res) => {
  const order = await Order.findOne({ _id: req.params.id, tenantId: req.tenant.id });
  if (!order) throw new NotFound('order', req.params.id);   // 404, never 403
  res.json(toDto(order));
}));

// BETTER — make it impossible to write the wrong version. A repository bound
// to the request's tenant, so no query can ever omit the scope.
export function scopedRepo(collection, tenantId) {
  const scope = (filter = {}) => ({ ...filter, tenantId });
  return {
    findOne: (f, o) => collection.findOne(scope(f), o),
    find:    (f, o) => collection.find(scope(f), o),
    updateOne: (f, u, o) => collection.updateOne(scope(f), u, o),
    deleteOne: (f, o) => collection.deleteOne(scope(f), o),
    insertOne: (doc) => collection.insertOne({ ...doc, tenantId }),
    // NOTE: no escape hatch. Cross-tenant work goes through an explicitly
    // named admin repository that is audited and separately authorised.
  };
}
app.use((req, _res, next) => { req.repos = buildRepos(db, req.tenant.id); next(); });
Test for it, do not hope

Write one test per endpoint that authenticates as tenant A and requests a resource belonging to tenant B, asserting 404. Generate them from the route table so a new endpoint without such a test fails CI. This is the highest-value test suite in a multi-tenant product, and almost nobody has it.

Choosing a model

ModelDecision inputFits
RBACthe subject's rolecoarse permissions, small fixed role set
Scopestoken capabilities (orders:write)APIs and OAuth clients; composes well with RBAC
ABACattributes of subject, resource and environment“a manager may approve expenses under £5 000 in their own department”
ReBACthe relationship graphsharing, folders, org hierarchies — the Google-Docs shape
javascript · a policy layer you can test in isolation
// Policies are pure functions of (principal, resource) — no db, no req, no res.
// That makes them exhaustively testable, which authorisation must be.
export const policies = {
  'order:read':   (p, o) => o.tenantId === p.tenantId &&
                            (p.roles.includes('admin') || o.customerId === p.userId),
  'order:cancel': (p, o) => policies['order:read'](p, o) &&
                            o.status === 'placed' &&
                            (p.roles.includes('support') || o.customerId === p.userId),
  'order:refund': (p, o) => o.tenantId === p.tenantId &&
                            p.scopes.includes('payments:write') &&
                            o.totalCents <= p.limits.refundCents,
};

export const can = (action) => (req, _res, next) => {
  const resource = req.order ?? req.resource;
  if (!policies[action]?.(req.principal, resource)) {
    // Log every denial with the action and ids — this is your audit trail.
    logger().warn({ action, resourceId: resource?._id }, 'authz denied');
    return next(new Forbidden(action));
  }
  next();
};

r.post('/orders/:id/refund', can('order:refund'), ah(refund));

Tenancy isolation strategies

StrategyIsolationCostWhen
Shared collections, tenantId on every documentlogical only — one missing filter leakscheapest; one connection pool, easy analyticsthe default for SaaS, with the scoped-repository discipline above
Database per tenantstrongconnection pools, migrations × N, noisy-neighbour still possiblehundreds of tenants, compliance or per-tenant restore requirements
Cluster per tenantstrongestvery high operational costa handful of enterprise customers with contractual isolation

With shared collections, put tenantId first in every compound index. It makes every query a bounded index range instead of a scan across all tenants, and it is what keeps one large tenant from degrading everyone else's queries.

13

Security hardening

A checklist, ordered by how often each item actually causes an incident in Node APIs.

  1. Broken object-level authorisation. Every query scoped by tenant and owner (Chapter 12). Cross-tenant tests in CI.
  2. Mass assignment. .strict() schemas, explicit field picking, never Model.updateOne(f, req.body).
  3. NoSQL injection. Primitive-typed schemas, mongoose.set('sanitizeFilter', true), query parser: 'simple'.
  4. Missing rate limits. Global, per-IP, per-user, and much tighter on login, password reset, signup and anything that sends email or costs money.
  5. Unbounded input. Body size limits, array length limits, string length limits, pagination caps, upload size and type limits.
  6. Secrets in the repo or the image. A secrets manager, injected at runtime, rotated, with a pre-commit scanner.
  7. Vulnerable dependencies. npm audit in CI, automated update PRs, and lockfile integrity — plus --ignore-scripts in CI installs.
  8. SSRF. Any URL a user supplies (webhooks, image imports, OAuth redirects) must be allowlisted by host and resolved IP, rejecting private ranges and metadata endpoints.
  9. Verbose errors. No stack traces or driver messages in responses. Problem details with a correlation id instead.
  10. Missing security headers. helmet() plus a real Content-Security-Policy if you serve any HTML.
javascript · the middleware stack, configured
import helmet from 'helmet';
import cors from 'cors';
import rateLimit from 'express-rate-limit';
import RedisStore from 'rate-limit-redis';
import mongoSanitize from 'express-mongo-sanitize';
import hpp from 'hpp';

app.use(helmet({
  contentSecurityPolicy: {
    directives: {
      defaultSrc: ["'self'"], scriptSrc: ["'self'"], objectSrc: ["'none'"],
      frameAncestors: ["'none'"], baseUri: ["'self'"], imgSrc: ["'self'", 'data:'],
      upgradeInsecureRequests: [],
    },
  },
  hsts: { maxAge: 31_536_000, includeSubDomains: true, preload: true },
  referrerPolicy: { policy: 'strict-origin-when-cross-origin' },
  crossOriginResourcePolicy: { policy: 'same-site' },
}));

// CORS: an explicit allowlist. Never `origin: true` with credentials — that
// reflects any origin and turns every authenticated endpoint into a CSRF sink.
app.use(cors({
  origin: (origin, cb) => cb(null, !origin || config.allowedOrigins.includes(origin)),
  credentials: true,
  methods: ['GET', 'POST', 'PATCH', 'DELETE'],
  allowedHeaders: ['content-type', 'authorization', 'idempotency-key', 'if-match'],
  exposedHeaders: ['etag', 'x-request-id', 'ratelimit-remaining'],
  maxAge: 86_400,
}));

app.use(hpp());                  // ?a=1&a=2 becomes an array — collapse it
app.use(mongoSanitize());        // strips $ and . from keys (belt AND braces with schemas)

// Distributed rate limiting — an in-memory limiter across 10 pods is a 10× limit
const limiter = (opts) => rateLimit({
  store: new RedisStore({ sendCommand: (...a) => redis.call(...a) }),
  standardHeaders: 'draft-7',
  legacyHeaders: false,
  keyGenerator: (req) => req.principal?.id ?? req.ip,   // per-user beats per-IP behind NAT
  ...opts,
});
app.use('/v1', limiter({ windowMs: 60_000, limit: 600 }));
app.use('/v1/auth/login', limiter({ windowMs: 900_000, limit: 10, skipSuccessfulRequests: true }));
app.use('/v1/auth/forgot', limiter({ windowMs: 3_600_000, limit: 5 }));
javascript · SSRF-safe outbound fetch
import { lookup } from 'node:dns/promises';
import ipaddr from 'ipaddr.js';

const BLOCKED = ['private', 'loopback', 'linkLocal', 'uniqueLocal', 'reserved'];

export async function safeFetch(rawUrl, init = {}) {
  const url = new URL(rawUrl);
  if (!['http:', 'https:'].includes(url.protocol)) throw new BadRequest('scheme');
  if (config.webhookHostAllowlist && !config.webhookHostAllowlist.includes(url.hostname)) {
    throw new BadRequest('host not allowed');
  }
  // Resolve and check EVERY address — a hostname can point at 169.254.169.254.
  const addrs = await lookup(url.hostname, { all: true });
  for (const { address } of addrs) {
    if (BLOCKED.includes(ipaddr.parse(address).range())) throw new BadRequest('private address');
  }
  return fetch(url, {
    ...init,
    redirect: 'manual',                        // a 302 to 127.0.0.1 defeats the check above
    signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5_000),
    headers: { ...init.headers, 'user-agent': 'acme-webhooks/1' },
  });
}
CSRF, precisely

CSRF only applies to credentials the browser attaches automatically — cookies and HTTP auth. If you authenticate with a bearer token in an Authorization header, you are not vulnerable (but you are then exposed to XSS instead). With cookies: set SameSite=Lax (which blocks the cross-site form POST), and add a double-submit or synchroniser token for anything state-changing. SameSite=None requires Secure and puts you fully back in CSRF territory.

14

Uploads, downloads & long responses

The default advice — multer to disk, then process — is how an API server runs out of disk and CPU simultaneously. At any real scale, files should not pass through your API process at all.

ApproachCost to your APIUse when
Buffer in memory (multer.memoryStorage)file size × concurrency in RAMtiny files only (an avatar under 1 MB), with a hard limit
Stream to disk then uploaddisk, IOPS, cleanup, and it does not work with more than one replicalegacy, or single-box deployments
Stream through to object storagebandwidth and sockets, but constant memorywhen you must inspect or transform the bytes
Pre-signed direct uploadnone — the client uploads straight to S3/GCSthe default for anything user-generated
javascript · pre-signed upload, the pattern to default to
// 1. Client asks for permission. We decide the key, the limits and the metadata.
r.post('/uploads', validate(uploadIntentSchema), ah(async (req, res) => {
  const { contentType, sizeBytes } = req.valid.body;
  if (!ALLOWED_TYPES.has(contentType)) throw new BadRequest({ contentType: 'unsupported' });
  if (sizeBytes > MAX_UPLOAD) throw new BadRequest({ sizeBytes: 'too large' });

  const key = `t/${req.tenant.id}/${crypto.randomUUID()}`;
  const upload = await s3.createPresignedPost({
    Bucket: config.uploadBucket,
    Key: key,
    Expires: 300,
    Conditions: [
      ['content-length-range', 1, MAX_UPLOAD],     // enforced by S3, not by us
      ['eq', '$Content-Type', contentType],
    ],
    Fields: { 'Content-Type': contentType },
  });

  // Record the INTENT. The object does not exist yet.
  await uploads.insertOne({
    _id: key, tenantId: req.tenant.id, userId: req.principal.id,
    status: 'pending', contentType, expectedBytes: sizeBytes, createdAt: new Date(),
  });

  res.status(201).json({ key, url: upload.url, fields: upload.fields });
}));

// 2. The client PUTs/POSTs directly to storage. Your API is not involved.
// 3. Storage fires an event (S3 → SQS/EventBridge → your worker), which
//    verifies the real size and type, scans it, generates derivatives, and
//    flips status to 'ready'. Never trust the client's claimed content type —
//    sniff the magic bytes in the worker.
// 4. A scheduled job deletes 'pending' intents older than an hour.
javascript · when the bytes must pass through you
import busboy from 'busboy';
import { pipeline } from 'node:stream/promises';

r.post('/documents', ah(async (req, res) => {
  const bb = busboy({
    headers: req.headers,
    limits: { files: 1, fileSize: 20 * 1024 * 1024, fields: 10, fieldSize: 4096 },
  });

  const done = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    let uploaded = null;
    bb.on('file', async (_name, stream, info) => {
      if (!ALLOWED_TYPES.has(info.mimeType)) { stream.resume(); return reject(new BadRequest('type')); }
      try {
        // Stream straight to storage — constant memory regardless of file size
        uploaded = await s3Upload({ Key: keyFor(req), Body: stream, ContentType: info.mimeType });
        // The limit fires AFTER the stream is consumed; check it or you store a truncated file
        if (stream.truncated) return reject(new BadRequest('file too large'));
      } catch (e) { reject(e); }
    });
    bb.on('filesLimit', () => reject(new BadRequest('too many files')));
    bb.on('error', reject);
    bb.on('close', () => resolve(uploaded));
  });

  await pipeline(req, bb);
  res.status(201).json(toDto(await done));
}));
Four upload rules
  1. Never trust the filename. Generate your own key. A filename of ../../etc/passwd or x.php is a real attack.
  2. Never trust the declared content type. Sniff the magic bytes server-side and re-encode images rather than serving originals.
  3. Serve user content from a different origin with Content-Disposition: attachment and X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, or a stored HTML file becomes stored XSS on your domain.
  4. Enforce size at the storage layer, because a client can lie about Content-Length.

Long-running work: return 202, not a held connection

javascript
// A 40-second export holds a socket, a worker slot and a load-balancer
// connection, and dies on the first proxy timeout. Queue it instead.
r.post('/reports', validate(reportSchema), ah(async (req, res) => {
  const job = await reportQueue.add('generate', {
    tenantId: req.tenant.id, userId: req.principal.id, params: req.valid.body,
  }, { jobId: `report:${req.idempotencyKey}`, attempts: 3, backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 2000 } });

  res
    .status(202)
    .location(`/v1/reports/${job.id}`)
    .json({ id: job.id, status: 'queued' });
}));

r.get('/reports/:id', ah(async (req, res) => {
  const report = await req.repos.reports.findOne({ _id: req.params.id });
  if (!report) throw new NotFound('report', req.params.id);
  res.json({
    id: report._id, status: report.status, progress: report.progress,
    downloadUrl: report.status === 'ready' ? await signedGet(report.key, 600) : null,
    error: report.error ?? null,
  });
}));
15

Errors as a first-class design

A typed error hierarchy, mapped to HTTP once, is worth more than any amount of defensive coding. It makes the API predictable for clients and the logs usable for you.

javascript · errors.js
export class AppError extends Error {
  constructor(message, { status = 500, type = 'about:blank', detail, extensions, cause, expose } = {}) {
    super(message, { cause });                 // `cause` preserves the original error
    this.name = new.target.name;
    this.status = status;
    this.type = type;
    this.detail = detail ?? message;
    this.extensions = extensions;
    // Whether the detail is safe to show the caller. 5xx defaults to false.
    this.expose = expose ?? status < 500;
    Error.captureStackTrace?.(this, new.target);
  }
}

export class BadRequest    extends AppError { constructor(e) { super('bad request', { status: 400, type: 'urn:acme:bad-request', extensions: { errors: e } }); } }
export class ValidationError extends AppError { constructor(issues) { super('validation failed', { status: 422, type: 'urn:acme:validation', extensions: { errors: issues } }); } }
export class Unauthorized  extends AppError { constructor(d) { super('unauthorized', { status: 401, type: 'urn:acme:unauthorized', detail: d }); } }
export class Forbidden     extends AppError { constructor(action) { super('forbidden', { status: 403, type: 'urn:acme:forbidden', extensions: { action } }); } }
export class NotFound      extends AppError { constructor(kind, id) { super(`${kind} not found`, { status: 404, type: 'urn:acme:not-found', extensions: { kind, id } }); } }
export class Conflict      extends AppError { constructor(d, ext) { super('conflict', { status: 409, type: 'urn:acme:conflict', detail: d, extensions: ext }); } }
export class PreconditionFailed extends AppError { constructor() { super('precondition failed', { status: 412, type: 'urn:acme:stale-write' }); } }
export class TooManyRequests extends AppError { constructor(retryAfter) { super('rate limited', { status: 429, type: 'urn:acme:rate-limited', extensions: { retryAfter } }); } }
export class Unavailable   extends AppError { constructor(dep) { super('dependency unavailable', { status: 503, type: 'urn:acme:unavailable', extensions: { dep } }); } }

// ONE place translates driver and library errors into the hierarchy.
export function toProblem(err) {
  if (err instanceof AppError) return err;

  // MongoDB duplicate key → 409 with the offending field
  if (err.code === 11000) {
    const field = Object.keys(err.keyPattern ?? {}).filter(k => k !== 'tenantId');
    return new Conflict('already exists', { fields: field });
  }
  if (err.name === 'CastError')       return new BadRequest([{ path: err.path, message: 'invalid id' }]);
  if (err.name === 'ValidationError') return new ValidationError(Object.values(err.errors).map(e => ({ path: e.path, message: e.message })));
  if (err.name === 'MongoServerSelectionError') return new Unavailable('mongodb');
  if (err.type === 'entity.too.large') return new AppError('payload too large', { status: 413 });
  if (err.type === 'entity.parse.failed') return new BadRequest([{ path: 'body', message: 'invalid JSON' }]);
  if (err.name === 'TimeoutError' || err.code === 'ABORT_ERR') return new AppError('upstream timeout', { status: 504 });

  // Unknown: 500, and the message is NEVER exposed
  return new AppError('internal error', { status: 500, cause: err, expose: false });
}
Retryability belongs in the contract

Add a boolean the client can act on — retryable: true on 429, 502, 503, 504; false on 4xx validation. Without it every client invents its own guess, and the ones that guess wrong hammer you during an incident.

Optimistic concurrency, so two writers cannot silently overwrite

javascript
// GET returns the version as an ETag
res.set('etag', `"${order.version}"`).json(toDto(order));

// PATCH requires the client to state which version it read
export async function patch(req, res) {
  const expected = Number(String(req.get('if-match') ?? '').replace(/"/g, ''));
  if (!Number.isInteger(expected)) throw new AppError('If-Match required', { status: 428 });

  const result = await req.repos.orders.updateOne(
    { _id: req.params.id, version: expected },     // the version IS the lock
    { $set: req.valid.body, $inc: { version: 1 } },
  );
  if (result.matchedCount === 0) {
    // Either it does not exist, or someone else wrote first. Distinguish them.
    const exists = await req.repos.orders.findOne({ _id: req.params.id }, { projection: { _id: 1 } });
    throw exists ? new PreconditionFailed() : new NotFound('order', req.params.id);
  }
  res.status(204).end();
}
16

Logging, metrics & tracing

Three different tools for three different questions. Confusing them produces expensive dashboards nobody uses during an incident.

Logs
What happened to this one request? High cardinality, high detail, expensive to store, queried after the fact.
Metrics
Is the system healthy right now? Low cardinality, aggregated, cheap, alertable.
Traces
Where did the 900 ms go? Per-request spans across services, usually sampled.
javascript · structured logging that is actually useful
import pino from 'pino';

export const logger = pino({
  level: config.logLevel,                       // info in prod, debug in dev
  base: { service: 'orders-api', version: config.gitSha, env: config.env },
  // Redaction is not optional: tokens, cookies and PII must never reach the log store
  redact: {
    paths: [
      'req.headers.authorization', 'req.headers.cookie', 'res.headers["set-cookie"]',
      '*.password', '*.passwordHash', '*.token', '*.refreshToken', '*.cardNumber', '*.ssn',
    ],
    censor: '[redacted]',
  },
  formatters: { level: (label) => ({ level: label }) },
  timestamp: pino.stdTimeFunctions.isoTime,
});

// One log line per request, at completion, with everything needed to debug it.
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const start = process.hrtime.bigint();
  res.on('finish', () => {
    logger.info({
      requestId: req.id,
      tenantId: req.tenant?.id,
      userId: req.principal?.id,
      method: req.method,
      route: req.route?.path ?? 'unmatched',    // pattern, not raw URL
      status: res.statusCode,
      durationMs: Number(process.hrtime.bigint() - start) / 1e6,
      bytes: Number(res.getHeader('content-length') ?? 0),
      ip: req.ip,
      ua: req.get('user-agent'),
      dbMs: req.timings?.db,                    // accumulated by the repo layer
      cacheHits: req.timings?.cacheHits,
    }, 'request');
  });
  next();
});
Two logging mistakes that cost real money

Logging the whole request and response body — your log bill becomes larger than your compute bill, and you have almost certainly stored PII. Log identifiers and sizes; add body logging behind a per-request debug flag. Using the raw URL as a metric label — /orders/6f1a… creates a new time series per id and takes down your metrics backend. Always the route pattern.

javascript · the four metrics you actually need
import { Counter, Histogram, Gauge, register } from 'prom-client';

// RED: Rate, Errors, Duration — per route. This is 80% of the value.
export const httpDuration = new Histogram({
  name: 'http_request_duration_seconds',
  help: 'request duration',
  labelNames: ['method', 'route', 'status'],
  buckets: [0.005, 0.01, 0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2.5, 5, 10],
});
export const dbDuration = new Histogram({
  name: 'db_operation_duration_seconds',
  labelNames: ['collection', 'op'],
  buckets: [0.001, 0.005, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.5, 1, 5],
});
export const queueDepth = new Gauge({ name: 'queue_depth', labelNames: ['queue', 'state'] });
export const bizEvents = new Counter({ name: 'business_events_total', labelNames: ['event', 'outcome'] });

app.get('/metrics', async (_req, res) => {
  res.type(register.contentType).end(await register.metrics());
});
// Never expose /metrics publicly — bind it to an internal port or require a token.
javascript · OpenTelemetry, wired before anything else
// tracing.js — MUST be imported first (node --import ./tracing.js src/server.js)
// so the auto-instrumentations can patch http, express and mongodb.
import { NodeSDK } from '@opentelemetry/sdk-node';
import { getNodeAutoInstrumentations } from '@opentelemetry/auto-instrumentations-node';
import { OTLPTraceExporter } from '@opentelemetry/exporter-trace-otlp-http';

new NodeSDK({
  serviceName: 'orders-api',
  traceExporter: new OTLPTraceExporter({ url: process.env.OTEL_ENDPOINT }),
  instrumentations: [getNodeAutoInstrumentations({
    '@opentelemetry/instrumentation-fs': { enabled: false },   // very noisy
  })],
}).start();

// Then add spans only where the automatic ones do not explain the time:
import { trace } from '@opentelemetry/api';
const tracer = trace.getTracer('orders');

export async function priceOrder(order) {
  return tracer.startActiveSpan('priceOrder', async (span) => {
    try {
      span.setAttribute('order.items', order.items.length);
      return await doPricing(order);
    } catch (err) {
      span.recordException(err);
      span.setStatus({ code: 2 });
      throw err;
    } finally { span.end(); }
  });
}
Interview follow-up

“How would you find out why p99 latency doubled?” The order matters: check the RED metrics to see whether it is all routes or one; check whether error rate moved with it; look at event-loop delay and GC to rule out the process; look at DB operation duration and the slow-query log; then open a trace from a slow request and read the span waterfall. Only then start changing code.

17

Configuration & secrets

Configuration should fail loudly at boot, not silently at 3am when an environment variable was mistyped in one environment.

javascript · config/index.js
import { z } from 'zod';

const schema = z.object({
  NODE_ENV: z.enum(['development', 'test', 'production']),
  PORT: z.coerce.number().int().default(3000),
  LOG_LEVEL: z.enum(['fatal','error','warn','info','debug','trace']).default('info'),

  MONGO_URI: z.string().url(),
  MONGO_MAX_POOL: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(500).default(50),
  REDIS_URL: z.string().url(),

  SESSION_SECRETS: z.string().transform(s => s.split(',').filter(Boolean))
                    .refine(a => a.length > 0 && a.every(s => s.length >= 32),
                            'need ≥32-char secrets'),
  JWT_PUBLIC_KEY: z.string().min(1),

  ALLOWED_ORIGINS: z.string().transform(s => s.split(',').map(o => o.trim())),
  TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS: z.coerce.number().int().min(0).max(5).default(1),
  SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS: z.coerce.number().int().default(15_000),
});

const parsed = schema.safeParse(process.env);
if (!parsed.success) {
  // Crash at boot with every problem listed. Do NOT default your way past a
  // missing secret — a service that starts misconfigured is worse than one
  // that refuses to start, because the orchestrator will not roll it back.
  console.error('invalid configuration:\n' +
    parsed.error.issues.map(i => `  ${i.path.join('.')}: ${i.message}`).join('\n'));
  process.exit(78);                        // EX_CONFIG
}

const env = parsed.data;
export const config = Object.freeze({
  env: env.NODE_ENV,
  isProd: env.NODE_ENV === 'production',
  port: env.PORT,
  logLevel: env.LOG_LEVEL,
  mongo: { uri: env.MONGO_URI, maxPoolSize: env.MONGO_MAX_POOL },
  redisUrl: env.REDIS_URL,
  sessionSecrets: env.SESSION_SECRETS,
  allowedOrigins: env.ALLOWED_ORIGINS,
  trustedProxyHops: env.TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS,
  shutdownTimeoutMs: env.SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS,
});
Config vs secrets
Config may live in the manifest and be visible in a dashboard. Secrets come from a manager (Secrets Manager, Vault, sealed secrets), are injected at runtime, are rotatable without a code change, and never appear in logs, error messages or an image layer.
Rotation
Accept an array of signing secrets: verify against all, sign with the first. That makes rotation a two-deploy operation with no logout event.
Feature flags
Not config. Config is per-environment and changes at deploy; flags are per-request and change at runtime. Keep them in a flag service with a cached local snapshot and safe defaults, so an outage of the flag service does not take you down.
Twelve-factor caveat
Environment variables are readable by anything in the process — including a compromised dependency — and appear in crash dumps. For high-value secrets prefer reading from a mounted file or fetching at boot and keeping only in memory.
18

Contracts, versioning & deprecation

An API contract that lives only in a wiki page is not a contract. Generate it from the same schemas you validate with, and the documentation cannot drift.

javascript · one schema, three uses
import { extendZodWithOpenApi, OpenApiGeneratorV31, OpenAPIRegistry } from '@asteasolutions/zod-to-openapi';
extendZodWithOpenApi(z);

export const OrderDto = z.object({
  id: z.string().openapi({ example: '65f1c2…' }),
  status: z.enum(['draft', 'placed', 'shipped', 'cancelled']),
  totalCents: z.number().int().openapi({ description: 'minor units of `currency`' }),
  currency: z.enum(['GBP', 'EUR', 'USD']),
  createdAt: z.string().datetime(),
}).openapi('Order');

const registry = new OpenAPIRegistry();
registry.registerPath({
  method: 'post', path: '/v1/orders', tags: ['orders'],
  summary: 'Create an order',
  request: { body: { content: { 'application/json': { schema: createOrderSchema.body } } },
             headers: z.object({ 'idempotency-key': z.string().uuid() }) },
  responses: {
    201: { description: 'created', content: { 'application/json': { schema: OrderDto } } },
    409: { description: 'duplicate idempotency key with a different body' },
    422: { description: 'validation failed', content: { 'application/problem+json': { schema: ProblemDto } } },
  },
});

// The same object validates requests (ch10), documents the API, and generates
// a typed client. Hand-written OpenAPI is always out of date by the second week.
Contract testing

Generate the spec in CI and diff it against the committed one. A breaking diff — a removed field, a narrowed type, a new required property — fails the build unless the version was bumped. That single check prevents most accidental client breakage, and it is far cheaper than a full consumer-driven contract test suite.

Versioning strategies

StrategyProsCons
Path /v1/ordersvisible in logs and traces, routable at the proxy, trivially cacheable, obvious to callersduplicated routes; encourages copying whole trees
Header Accept: application/vnd.acme.v2+jsonone URL per resource; fine-grainedinvisible in logs, easy to forget, needs Vary, hard to test by hand
Date-pinned Acme-Version: 2026-04-01Stripe's model: each client is pinned, transformations applied per versiona transformation layer to build and maintain forever
No versioning, additive onlysimplest, cheapestonly works with real discipline and clients that tolerate unknown fields
javascript · deprecating a field responsibly
// 1. Add the new field; keep writing both. Nothing breaks.
// 2. Announce, with machine-readable headers (RFC 8594 / 9745):
res.set('deprecation', 'Sun, 01 Mar 2026 00:00:00 GMT');
res.set('sunset', 'Wed, 01 Jul 2026 00:00:00 GMT');
res.set('link', '<https://docs.acme.com/changelog/total-cents>; rel="deprecation"');

// 3. MEASURE who still uses it, per client. Without this you can never remove it.
if ('total' in projectionRequested) {
  deprecatedFieldUse.inc({ field: 'order.total', client: req.principal?.clientId ?? 'unknown' });
}

// 4. Contact the top consumers. 5. Remove after the sunset date, in a new
//    major version. Removing a field with unknown usage is how you break a
//    customer's integration on a Friday.
19

Testing an Express API

The highest-value backend tests are integration tests against a real database in a container. Mocking the database mostly tests your mocks.

LevelScopeSpeedWhat it catches
Unitpure functions: pricing, policies, mappers, validators< 1 mslogic errors, edge cases, every policy branch
Integrationapp + real Mongo + real Redis via testcontainers10–100 msqueries, indexes, transactions, middleware order, auth, status codes — the bugs that reach production
Contractgenerated spec vs committed specfastaccidental breaking changes
End-to-enddeployed environment, real dependenciessecondsconfiguration, networking, IAM — things only real infrastructure has
Loadk6 / artillery against a production-like envminutesconnection-pool limits, N+1, GC pressure, autoscaling behaviour
javascript · integration setup with testcontainers
import { MongoDBContainer } from '@testcontainers/mongodb';
import { RedisContainer } from '@testcontainers/redis';
import request from 'supertest';

let mongo, redisC, app, db;

before(async () => {
  // A real replica set, so transactions and change streams work like production
  mongo = await new MongoDBContainer('mongo:7').start();
  redisC = await new RedisContainer('redis:7').start();
  ({ app, db } = await buildTestApp({
    mongoUri: mongo.getConnectionString(),
    redisUrl: redisC.getConnectionUrl(),
  }));
  await applyIndexes(db);          // the SAME index definitions as production
});

after(async () => { await mongo.stop(); await redisC.stop(); });

// Truncate rather than recreate: two orders of magnitude faster than a fresh container
beforeEach(async () => {
  const cols = await db.collections();
  await Promise.all(cols.map(c => c.deleteMany({})));
});

it('rejects a cross-tenant read with 404', async () => {
  const a = await seedTenant(db, 'a');
  const b = await seedTenant(db, 'b');
  const order = await seedOrder(db, { tenantId: b.id });

  await request(app)
    .get(`/v1/orders/${order._id}`)
    .set('authorization', `Bearer ${tokenFor(a.user)}`)
    .expect(404);                  // NOT 403 — see chapter 06
});

it('is idempotent on retry', async () => {
  const key = crypto.randomUUID();
