  const body = { customerId: cid, currency: 'GBP', items: [{ sku: 'x', quantity: 1 }] };

  const first = await request(app).post('/v1/orders')
    .set('idempotency-key', key).set('authorization', auth).send(body).expect(201);
  const second = await request(app).post('/v1/orders')
    .set('idempotency-key', key).set('authorization', auth).send(body).expect(201);

  assert.equal(first.body.id, second.body.id);
  assert.equal(await db.collection('orders').countDocuments(), 1);
});
Why an in-memory Mongo is not enough

mongodb-memory-server is convenient, but a single node has no transactions, no change streams, no read preferences and different index behaviour under concurrency — exactly the areas where bugs hide. Use a containerised replica set for the suite; keep the in-memory server only for the fastest local loop.

javascript · the tests that pay for themselves
// 1. Every route rejects unauthenticated access
for (const { method, path } of protectedRoutes) {
  it(`${method} ${path} requires auth`, () =>
    request(app)[method.toLowerCase()](path).expect(401));
}

// 2. Every route enforces tenant isolation (generated from the route table)
// 3. Every sortable field has an index — otherwise it is a DoS vector
it('every sortable field is indexed', async () => {
  const indexes = await db.collection('orders').indexes();
  const covered = new Set(indexes.flatMap(i => Object.keys(i.key)));
  for (const field of Object.values(SORTABLE)) assert.ok(covered.has(field), field);
});

// 4. No query in the suite performs a collection scan
it('no COLLSCAN in the hot paths', async () => {
  const plan = await db.collection('orders')
    .find({ tenantId, status: 'placed' }).sort({ createdAt: -1 })
    .explain('executionStats');
  assert.equal(plan.queryPlanner.winningPlan.inputStage?.stage !== 'COLLSCAN', true);
  assert.ok(plan.executionStats.totalDocsExamined <= plan.executionStats.nReturned * 2);
});
20

Structure that survives a team

The layering does not matter nearly as much as whether it is enforced. Pick a shape, then make the linter check it.

text · feature-first, layered inside
src/
  server.js                # process concerns only: listen, signals, shutdown
  app.js                   # createApp(): middleware order, mounts routers
  config/index.js          # validated, frozen config object
  platform/                # cross-cutting infrastructure
    mongo.js               #   connection, pool settings, index bootstrap
    redis.js
    queue.js
    logger.js  metrics.js  tracing.js
    errors.js  problem.js
    middleware/            #   requestContext, auth, validate, rateLimit, idempotency
  modules/
    orders/
      routes.js            # HTTP only: parse req.valid → call service → format
      service.js           # business rules, transactions, invariants, events
      repository.js        # the ONLY file importing the Mongo driver for orders
      schemas.js           # zod: request schemas + DTOs (feed OpenAPI)
      mappers.js           # document ⇄ domain ⇄ DTO
      events.js            # what this module publishes
      __tests__/
    payments/  customers/  webhooks/
  jobs/                    # queue consumers — separate process, same modules
  index.js

Dependency rules, enforced by eslint-plugin-boundaries:
  routes      → service, schemas          (never repository, never mongo)
  service     → repository, other services' PUBLIC api, platform
  repository  → platform/mongo            (never service, never routes)
  modules     → platform                  (never another module's internals)
javascript · eslint boundary rules
// eslint.config.js — the difference between a convention and a rule
export default [{
  plugins: { boundaries },
  settings: {
    'boundaries/elements': [
      { type: 'routes',     pattern: 'src/modules/*/routes.js' },
      { type: 'service',    pattern: 'src/modules/*/service.js' },
      { type: 'repository', pattern: 'src/modules/*/repository.js' },
      { type: 'platform',   pattern: 'src/platform/**' },
    ],
  },
  rules: {
    'boundaries/element-types': ['error', {
      default: 'disallow',
      rules: [
        { from: 'routes',     allow: ['service', 'platform'] },
        { from: 'service',    allow: ['repository', 'service', 'platform'] },
        { from: 'repository', allow: ['platform'] },
      ],
    }],
    'no-restricted-imports': ['error', { paths: [
      { name: 'mongodb', message: 'only repositories may import the driver' },
    ] }],
  },
}];
javascript · what each layer looks like
// routes.js — thin. No business logic, no database, no try/catch.
r.post('/orders', idempotent(), validate(createOrderSchema), ah(async (req, res) => {
  const order = await req.services.orders.place(req.valid.body, req.principal);
  res.status(201).location(`/v1/orders/${order.id}`).json(toDto(order));
}));

// service.js — owns invariants and transactions. Knows nothing about HTTP.
export function makeOrderService({ repos, payments, events, clock }) {
  return {
    async place(input, principal) {
      const customer = await repos.customers.findOne({ _id: input.customerId });
      if (!customer) throw new BadRequest([{ path: 'customerId', message: 'unknown' }]);
      if (customer.status === 'blocked') throw new Forbidden('customer:blocked');

      const priced = price(input, customer);              // pure, unit-tested
      if (priced.totalCents <= 0) throw new ValidationError([{ path: 'items', message: 'empty' }]);

      const order = await repos.withTransaction(async (session) => {
        const created = await repos.orders.insertOne({ ...priced, status: 'placed',
          createdAt: clock.now() }, { session });
        await repos.inventory.reserve(priced.items, { session });
        await repos.outbox.insertOne({ type: 'order.placed', payload: { id: created._id } }, { session });
        return created;
      });
      return order;                                       // the outbox publishes; see ch41
    },
  };
}

// repository.js — the only place that knows Mongo exists.
export function makeOrderRepo(db, tenantId) {
  const col = db.collection('orders');
  const scope = (f = {}) => ({ ...f, tenantId });
  return {
    findOne: (f, o) => col.findOne(scope(f), o),
    insertOne: async (doc, o) => {
      const _id = new ObjectId();
      await col.insertOne({ _id, tenantId, version: 1, ...doc }, o);
      return { _id, tenantId, version: 1, ...doc };
    },
  };
}
Interview follow-up

“Is this over-engineered for a small service?” The honest answer: the layer that always pays for itself is the repository, because it makes every query testable and every tenant filter enforceable in one place. A separate service layer pays for itself as soon as one operation touches two collections or needs a transaction. What is genuinely optional is a use-case class per operation — say so, rather than reciting the pattern.

21

Why ORMs & ODMs exist

“Why do we need Mongoose? The driver already works” is a genuinely good question, and answering it well requires naming the specific problems these libraries solve — and the specific costs they impose. Both halves matter.

The problem being solved

A database speaks in rows or documents. Your application speaks in objects with behaviour and invariants. The gap between those two is called the object-relational impedance mismatch, and it consists of five concrete problems:

  1. Representation. A database has its own type system — ObjectId, Decimal128, BSON dates, nested documents. Your code wants domain types. Somebody has to convert, in both directions, consistently, in one place.
  2. Structure. A schemaless store will happily accept { emial: 'x' }. Six months later half your documents have a typo'd field and no code path reads it. Something must define and enforce shape.
  3. Identity and change tracking. Two reads of the same row should not produce two objects that disagree. Updates should send only what changed.
  4. Associations. Relationships across collections or tables require joins, or N+1 round trips, or manual batching. Doing this by hand at every call site is where the performance goes.
  5. Repetition. Timestamps, soft deletes, audit fields, tenant scoping, optimistic version counters, validation, serialisation. Written once per collection by hand, these drift apart.

The vocabulary

Driver
The lowest layer. Speaks the wire protocol, manages the connection pool and topology, and returns raw documents. mongodb for Node.
ODM
Object–Document Mapper, for document stores: Mongoose, Typegoose, Prisma's Mongo connector. Adds schema, validation, hooks, virtuals, population, casting.
ORM
Object–Relational Mapper, for SQL: Prisma, TypeORM, Sequelize, Drizzle, Hibernate. Adds all of the above plus relational joins, migrations and often a unit of work.
Query builder
Composable, type-safe SQL generation without object mapping: Knex, Kysely, Drizzle's core. The middle ground.
Active Record
The object is the row and knows how to save itself: user.save(). Mongoose and Sequelize. Quick to write, and business logic tends to accumulate on the model.
Data Mapper
Objects are ignorant of persistence; a separate repository moves them. Prisma, TypeORM's repository mode. More ceremony, cleaner domain layer, easier testing.
Unit of Work
Accumulates changes and flushes them in one transaction, tracking identity so the same row maps to one object. Powerful, and the source of ORMs' surprising behaviour.

What you actually gain, with the code

javascript · the same operation, three ways
// ---------- raw driver ----------
// Everything is explicit, and everything is your responsibility.
const now = new Date();
const doc = {
  _id: new ObjectId(),
  tenantId: new ObjectId(tenantId),        // you must remember to cast
  email: String(body.email).toLowerCase(), // you must remember to normalise
  passwordHash: await hash(body.password),
  roles: Array.isArray(body.roles) ? body.roles : [],
  version: 1,                              // you must remember the counters
  createdAt: now, updatedAt: now,          // you must remember the timestamps
};
if (!/^[^@]+@[^@]+$/.test(doc.email)) throw new ValidationError(...);   // and validation
await db.collection('users').insertOne(doc);

// ---------- ODM (Mongoose) ----------
// Casting, normalisation, validation, timestamps and version come from the schema.
const user = await User.create({ email: body.email, password: body.password, roles: body.roles });

// ---------- repository over the driver ----------
// The middle path: one place owns the concerns, no library magic.
const user = await repos.users.create(body);   // repository does the above, once
What each layer buys you
ConcernDriverRepository over driverODM / ORM
Schema & validationyou write ita validator per module (zod)declarative, automatic
Type castingmanual, easy to forgetcentralised in mappersautomatic (and occasionally surprising)
Timestamps, versions, soft deletemanual per queryin the repositoryplugins/options
Relationsmanual $lookup or N+1explicit batch loaderspopulate/include — convenient, easy to make N+1
Query surfaceeverything the server supportseverythingthe subset the library models; escape hatch needed for the rest
Observabilityyou instrument itone place to instrumenthooks or plugins; the generated query may not be obvious
Costdiscipline at every call sitea little boilerplatea dependency, overhead, and hidden behaviour

The costs, stated honestly

Where ORMs and ODMs hurt
  • N+1 queries. populate and lazy relations make one query look like one query and issue a hundred. This is the single most common ORM-caused performance problem in production.
  • Opaque queries. You debug the library's output, not your own intent. Always log the generated query in development.
  • Overhead. Mongoose hydrates every document into a full model with getters, setters and change tracking. On a 10 000-document read that is measurable — which is exactly what .lean() exists for.
  • The leaky abstraction. Aggregation pipelines, $expr, bulk writes, change streams, hints — sooner or later you drop to the driver, and now you have two mental models in one codebase.
  • Validation illusions. Mongoose validators run in the application, not the server. updateMany, a script, or another service writes whatever it likes. Uniqueness and required-ness must also exist as database constraints.

How to decide

SituationReach forBecause
Small team, CRUD-heavy product, many collectionsODM (Mongoose)schema, hooks and casting remove hundreds of lines and enforce consistency by default
Analytics, reporting, heavy aggregationdriver behind repositoriesthe work is pipelines, which the ODM only wraps
High-throughput writes, tight latency budgetdriver + bulk operationshydration and middleware cost per document
Strong domain model, DDD, complex invariantsdriver + explicit mappers (data mapper)you want the domain object free of persistence concerns
Relational data with real joins and constraintsPostgres + Prisma/Drizzleif you keep reaching for $lookup everywhere, the data is relational — that is the honest answer
TypeScript-first, want generated typesPrisma or Typegoosetypes derived from the schema rather than maintained alongside it
The answer to give in an interview

“An ODM solves five things: type casting at the boundary, schema enforcement in a schemaless store, cross-cutting concerns like timestamps and versioning, relationship loading, and serialisation. I use Mongoose for CRUD-shaped modules because that boilerplate is genuinely repetitive and error-prone. I drop to the driver for aggregation, bulk writes and anything latency-critical, and I always put both behind a repository so the rest of the app cannot tell which one I used — that also means the choice is reversible. What I never rely on is ODM-level validation as a guarantee, because it only applies to writes that go through the application: uniqueness lives in a unique index, and required fields get a server-side JSON Schema validator.”

22

MongoDB fundamentals

The document model's guarantees and limits determine every modelling decision that follows.

FactConsequence
A document is limited to 16 MBnever embed an unbounded array — a comments array on a viral post eventually fails every write
Writes to a single document are atomic, including nested fields and arraysembedding is how you get transactional consistency without a transaction
Multi-document atomicity requires an explicit transactionpossible since 4.0 (replica set) and 4.2 (sharded), but with a cost — see Chapter 26
Storage is WiredTiger: document-level concurrency, snapshot isolation, compressionconcurrent writers to different documents do not block each other
No joins in the query language; $lookup exists only in aggregationmodel for your read patterns, or accept aggregation cost on every read
No foreign keys, no cascading deletesreferential integrity is your application's job, permanently
Field order is preserved; documents in a collection need not share a shapeflexibility you must constrain deliberately, or the collection becomes unreadable
_id is always indexed and immutablechoose it deliberately — it is your best free index

BSON types worth knowing

javascript
ObjectId()        // 12 bytes: 4-byte timestamp | 5-byte random | 3-byte counter
                  // → roughly monotonic, so it sorts by creation time and
                  //   gives good B-tree locality on insert. Do NOT treat the
                  //   timestamp as authoritative; it is second-resolution and
                  //   client-generated.
Decimal128        // exact decimal — the ONLY correct type for money if you
                  // are not storing integer minor units. `Double` is not.
Date              // millisecond-precision UTC. Store UTC, render local.
Long / Int32      // JS numbers are doubles: integers above 2^53 lose precision.
                  // Use Long, or a string, for external 64-bit ids.
Binary(UUID)      // store UUIDs as binary subtype 4, not as 36-char strings —
                  // a third of the size and faster to compare.
Timestamp         // internal replication type. Not for application data.
Money and floats

0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3 applies to your ledger. Store integer minor units (totalCents: 1099) or Decimal128, and never Double. If you have already shipped doubles, the migration is painful but is not optional — the discrepancies compound.

Choosing _id

ChoiceGoodBad
ObjectId (default)compact, roughly time-sorted, good insert locality, freeleaks approximate creation time; not portable across systems
Random UUID v4generated anywhere, no coordination, no information leakterrible index locality on high-volume inserts — random B-tree writes
UUID v7 / ULIDtime-ordered and client-generatable — the best of bothstore as binary, not a string
A natural key ("tenant:sku")free unique constraint, no extra index, no lookup to resolveimmutable forever; a business rename becomes a migration
Compound _id ({t, d})excellent for time-series and per-tenant localityawkward in URLs and clients
javascript · connection settings that matter
const client = new MongoClient(config.mongo.uri, {
  maxPoolSize: 50,               // per PROCESS. 10 pods × 50 = 500 server connections.
  minPoolSize: 5,                // keep some warm to avoid handshake latency spikes
  maxIdleTimeMS: 60_000,
  waitQueueTimeoutMS: 5_000,     // fail fast instead of queueing forever under load
  serverSelectionTimeoutMS: 5_000,
  connectTimeoutMS: 10_000,
  socketTimeoutMS: 45_000,       // must exceed your slowest legitimate query
  retryWrites: true,             // driver retries a failed write ONCE — safe because
                                 // retryable writes are idempotent server-side
  retryReads: true,
  readConcern: { level: 'majority' },
  writeConcern: { w: 'majority', wtimeoutMS: 5_000 },
  compressors: ['zstd', 'snappy'],
  appName: 'orders-api',         // shows up in server logs and profiler — always set it
});
Pool sizing arithmetic

Total connections = pods × maxPoolSize, and MongoDB Atlas tiers cap connections (a few hundred on small tiers). Autoscaling to 40 pods with a pool of 100 is 4 000 connections and an immediate outage. Size the pool from concurrency-per-pod, not optimism, and alert on connection count.

23

Data modelling: embed or reference

In a relational database you normalise and then optimise reads with indexes and views. In MongoDB you model for the queries you will actually run. Getting this wrong is not a tuning problem; it is a rewrite.

The decision

Embed whenReference when
The child is always read with the parentThe child is queried independently
The relationship is one-to-few and boundedThe relationship is one-to-many or unbounded
The child has no identity outside the parentThe child is a first-class entity with its own lifecycle
You need atomic updates across parent and childThe child changes far more often than the parent
The data is a point-in-time snapshot (an order's line prices)The data must be consistent everywhere when it changes (a product's price)
Total document size stays comfortably under 16 MBMany parents share the same child (many-to-many)
The one-line heuristic

Data that is read together should be stored together. Then check the two escape conditions: is the embedded array unbounded, and would updating one embedded field require rewriting a huge document? If either is true, reference instead.

The same domain, modelled well and badly

javascript · orders — the correct model
// EMBED line items: bounded (≤100 by validation), always read with the order,
// and deliberately a SNAPSHOT — the price at purchase time must not change when
// the product's price changes. This is denormalisation as a business requirement.
{
  _id: ObjectId('…'),
  tenantId: ObjectId('…'),
  status: 'placed',
  customer: {                      // EXTENDED REFERENCE: id plus the few fields
    id: ObjectId('…'),             // every order view needs, avoiding a lookup
    name: 'Ada Lovelace',          // stale if the customer renames — acceptable,
    email: 'ada@example.com',      // and arguably correct for an invoice
  },
  items: [
    { sku: 'KB-01', name: 'Keyboard', unitCents: 8900, quantity: 1, taxRateBp: 2000 },
  ],
  totals: { subtotalCents: 8900, taxCents: 1780, totalCents: 10680, currency: 'GBP' },
  payment: { provider: 'stripe', intentId: 'pi_…', status: 'succeeded' },
  version: 3,
  createdAt: ISODate('…'), updatedAt: ISODate('…'),
}

// REFERENCE events: unbounded, append-only, queried by time range.
// Embedding them would grow the order document forever.
{ _id: ObjectId('…'), orderId: ObjectId('…'), tenantId: ObjectId('…'),
  type: 'order.shipped', at: ISODate('…'), actor: { kind: 'system' }, data: { carrier: 'dpd' } }
javascript · the three modelling mistakes
// 1. UNBOUNDED ARRAY — works for a year, then every write to a popular post fails
{ _id: postId, title: '…', comments: [ /* 400,000 subdocuments */ ] }
//    Fix: a comments collection, or the SUBSET pattern (below).

// 2. RELATIONAL MODEL IN A DOCUMENT STORE — five $lookups to render one screen
{ _id, orderId } // order_items
{ _id, orderId } // order_addresses
{ _id, orderId } // order_totals
//    Fix: embed the parts that are always read together. If you genuinely need
//    five joins, the honest conclusion is that this data is relational.

// 3. UNBOUNDED GROWTH IN PLACE — MongoDB must relocate the document as the
//    array grows, and every index entry pointing at it must be updated
{ _id: userId, activityLog: [ /* one entry per page view, forever */ ] }
//    Fix: the BUCKET pattern (below).

The patterns worth knowing by name

Extended reference

Store the id plus the two or three fields every read needs. Removes a lookup from the hot path. Accept staleness, or update dependents asynchronously on change.

Subset

Embed the most recent or top-N children (last 10 comments) and keep the full set in another collection. The common read touches one document; “show all” pages the other collection.

Bucket

Group time-series points into one document per entity per hour or day, with an array of measurements and a count. Turns millions of tiny documents into thousands of dense ones — far fewer index entries and far less overhead.

Computed / precomputed

Store the aggregate (order count, average rating, unread total) and update it with $inc on write. Trades write cost and eventual drift for O(1) reads. Add a reconciliation job.

Schema versioning

A schemaVersion field on every document, so a lazy migration can read old and new shapes simultaneously. This is what makes zero-downtime schema change possible.

Outlier

Most users have ten followers; some have ten million. Flag the outliers (hasOverflow: true) and store their overflow separately, so the common case stays a single document read.

Polymorphic / single collection

Store related-but-different shapes in one collection with a discriminator, so one query returns a mixed feed in order. Index the discriminator.

Approximation

For view counts and similar, increment probabilistically or batch increments in Redis and flush periodically. A view counter does not need to be exact and should not cost a write per view.

javascript · bucket pattern, concretely
// One document per device per hour. 3,600 readings become 1 document with an
// array, instead of 3,600 documents with 3,600 index entries each.
await metrics.updateOne(
  { deviceId, hour: startOfHour(ts) },
  {
    $push: { readings: { $each: [{ t: ts, v: value }], $slice: -3600 } },
    $inc: { count: 1, sum: value },
    $min: { min: value },
    $max: { max: value },
    $setOnInsert: { deviceId, hour: startOfHour(ts), tenantId },
  },
  { upsert: true },
);
// The $inc/$min/$max fields make hourly rollups a projection, not an aggregation.
// For genuine time-series, also consider a native time-series collection, which
// applies columnar compression and this bucketing automatically.
javascript · many-to-many, without a join table
// Store the array on the side you query FROM, and index it (a multikey index).
// Users→roles is small and bounded, so embed on the user:
{ _id: userId, tenantId, email, roleIds: [ObjectId('…'), ObjectId('…')] }
// index: { tenantId: 1, roleIds: 1 }   → "who has this role" is still indexed

// If BOTH directions are queried at scale and the arrays are large, keep a
// separate membership collection with indexes on both keys — the relational
// answer, chosen deliberately:
{ _id, userId, roleId, tenantId, grantedAt }
// indexes: { userId: 1, roleId: 1 } and { roleId: 1, userId: 1 }
Interview follow-up

“How would you model a social feed?” The expected reasoning: fan-out-on-write (a feed document per user, updated when someone they follow posts) gives O(1) reads and expensive writes for celebrity accounts; fan-out-on-read (query posts from everyone you follow) gives cheap writes and expensive reads. Real systems do both — write fan-out for normal accounts, read fan-out for the few thousand with millions of followers, merged at read time. Naming that hybrid is the answer; picking one and defending it absolutely is not.

24

Indexes & query plans

Indexes are the difference between a 2 ms query and a 4 000 ms one, and almost every “Mongo is slow” claim is a missing or mis-ordered compound index. This chapter is the most directly useful in the book.

Index types

TypeDefinitionUse
Single field{ email: 1 }equality or range on one field
Compound{ tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 }almost everything — see ESR below
Multikey{ tags: 1 } on an array fieldone entry per array element; only one array field per index
Text{ title: 'text', body: 'text' }basic full-text; one per collection. Use Atlas Search or Elasticsearch for anything real
Geospatial{ location: '2dsphere' }$near, $geoWithin
Hashed{ userId: 'hashed' }even shard distribution; cannot serve range queries
Wildcard{ 'attrs.$**': 1 }genuinely unpredictable user-defined attributes. A last resort — large and slow
TTL{ expiresAt: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 0 }sessions, OTPs, idempotency keys, soft-deleted rows. Deletion is a background sweep every ~60 s, not instant
Partial{ partialFilterExpression: { status: 'active' } }index only the rows you query — smaller and cheaper than a full index
Sparse{ sparse: true }skip documents missing the field; superseded by partial for most uses
Unique{ unique: true }the only correct way to enforce uniqueness

ESR: the rule that decides compound index order

Equality fields tested with == → FIRST Sort fields in the sort order → SECOND Range fields tested with < > in → LAST query : { tenantId: X, status: 'placed', createdAt: { $gte: D } } sort : { createdAt: -1 } index : { tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 } └─ E ────────┘ └─ E ──┘ └─ S and R ─┘ Why: equality narrows to a contiguous range of the index. A range field placed before a sort field forces MongoDB to sort in memory — and an in-memory sort above 100 MB simply fails the query unless allowDiskUse is set.
javascript · index definitions as code
// Keep every index in source control and apply at deploy, never by hand in a shell.
export const INDEXES = {
  orders: [
    // the list endpoint: tenant + status filter, newest first
    { key: { tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 }, name: 'tenant_status_created' },
    // customer's order history
    { key: { tenantId: 1, 'customer.id': 1, createdAt: -1 }, name: 'tenant_customer_created' },
    // idempotency: unique per tenant, only where the key exists
    { key: { tenantId: 1, idempotencyKey: 1 }, name: 'tenant_idem', unique: true,
      partialFilterExpression: { idempotencyKey: { $exists: true } } },
    // the outbox: only unpublished rows are ever queried
    { key: { publishedAt: 1 }, name: 'outbox_pending',
      partialFilterExpression: { publishedAt: null } },
    // expire cancelled drafts automatically
    { key: { expiresAt: 1 }, name: 'ttl_drafts', expireAfterSeconds: 0 },
  ],
  sessions: [{ key: { expiresAt: 1 }, name: 'ttl', expireAfterSeconds: 0 }],
};

export async function applyIndexes(db) {
  for (const [collection, specs] of Object.entries(INDEXES)) {
    await db.collection(collection).createIndexes(specs);   // idempotent
  }
}
Never build an index on a large collection in the foreground

A foreground build on a busy collection blocks all operations on that database. Modern MongoDB builds are hybrid and far safer, but the discipline stands: build on a secondary, or use a rolling build across the replica set, during a low-traffic window, and monitor the progress. And never let application code call createIndex on every boot at scale — run it as a migration step.

Reading an explain plan

javascript
const plan = await orders
  .find({ tenantId, status: 'placed' })
  .sort({ createdAt: -1 })
  .limit(25)
  .explain('executionStats');

// The four numbers that matter:
plan.executionStats.nReturned;            // 25
plan.executionStats.totalKeysExamined;    // want ≈ nReturned
plan.executionStats.totalDocsExamined;    // want ≈ nReturned (0 if covered)
plan.executionStats.executionTimeMillis;

// The stages, best to worst:
//   IXSCAN                     index scan            ✅
//   IXSCAN → FETCH             index then documents  ✅ normal
//   PROJECTION_COVERED         answered by index alone, no FETCH  ✅✅ best
//   IXSCAN → FETCH → SORT      in-memory sort        ⚠️  wrong index order
//   COLLSCAN                   full collection scan  ❌
//   SORT_KEY_GENERATOR + SORT  blocking sort         ⚠️  100 MB limit
Keys examined
≈ nReturnedratio > 10 means the index is not selective
Docs examined
≈ nReturned0 means a covered query — the ideal
In-memory sort
0any SORT stage means a missing index
Index size
fits in RAMindexes larger than the cache thrash the disk

Covered queries

javascript
// If every field in the filter, sort AND projection is in the index, MongoDB
// answers from the index alone — no document fetch. Often 5–10× faster.
await orders
  .find(
    { tenantId, status: 'placed' },
    { projection: { _id: 0, createdAt: 1, status: 1 } },   // _id: 0 is REQUIRED
  )
  .sort({ createdAt: -1 })
  .toArray();
// index: { tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 }  → PROJECTION_COVERED

// This is the technique behind fast "counts by status" and dashboard tiles:
// put the few fields the tile needs into one compound index and never touch
// the documents at all.
Index costs, which people forget
  • Every index is written on every insert, update to an indexed field, and delete. Ten indexes make writes roughly ten times more expensive in index maintenance.
  • Indexes consume RAM. If the working set of indexes exceeds the WiredTiger cache, you start reading indexes from disk and performance falls off a cliff.
  • An index that duplicates a prefix of another is redundant: {a:1} is unnecessary if {a:1,b:1} exists, because a compound index serves any prefix of its keys.
  • Find unused indexes with $indexStats and drop them — but check every environment first, because a nightly report may be the only user.
javascript · find the indexes nobody uses
const stats = await db.collection('orders').aggregate([{ $indexStats: {} }]).toArray();
stats
  .map(s => ({ name: s.name, ops: s.accesses.ops, since: s.accesses.since }))
  .sort((a, b) => a.ops - b.ops)
  .forEach(s => console.log(s));
// Zero ops since a date well before your last deploy = a candidate for removal.
// Note: stats are per-node and reset on restart. Check every member.
25

The aggregation framework

A pipeline of stages, each transforming a stream of documents. It replaces pulling data into Node and looping — which is both slower and, at any real volume, impossible.

javascript · a real reporting pipeline, annotated
const report = await orders.aggregate([
  // 1. $match FIRST, always. It is the only stage that can use an index,
  //    and everything after it is proportional to what survives here.
  { $match: {
      tenantId,
      status: { $in: ['placed', 'shipped'] },
      createdAt: { $gte: from, $lt: to },
  } },

  // 2. $project/$unset early to shrink the documents flowing through the pipeline.
  { $project: { _id: 0, createdAt: 1, 'customer.id': 1, items: 1, totals: 1 } },

  // 3. Reshape. $unwind turns each array element into its own document.
  { $unwind: '$items' },

  // 4. Group. _id is the grouping key; it may be a document for a composite key.
  { $group: {
      _id: { day: { $dateTrunc: { date: '$createdAt', unit: 'day', timezone: 'Europe/London' } },
             sku: '$items.sku' },
      units: { $sum: '$items.quantity' },
      revenueCents: { $sum: { $multiply: ['$items.unitCents', '$items.quantity'] } },
      orders: { $addToSet: '$customer.id' },
  } },
  { $addFields: { customers: { $size: '$orders' } } },
  { $unset: 'orders' },

  // 5. Sort AFTER grouping — but note this sort cannot use an index, so keep
  //    the grouped result small.
  { $sort: { '_id.day': 1, revenueCents: -1 } },

  // 6. Bound the output. An unbounded aggregation is an unbounded response.
  { $limit: 5_000 },
], {
  allowDiskUse: true,              // required if any stage exceeds 100 MB
  maxTimeMS: 20_000,               // ALWAYS set — an unbounded pipeline is a DoS
  hint: 'tenant_status_created',   // pin the index when the planner picks wrong
  comment: 'daily-sku-revenue',    // shows up in the profiler and currentOp
  readPreference: 'secondaryPreferred',   // keep analytics off the primary
}).toArray();

Stages worth knowing

StageDoesNote
$matchfilterthe only index-using stage — put it first
$groupaggregate by keyblocking; 100 MB memory limit per stage
$lookupleft outer joinexpensive; use localField/foreignField form with an index on the foreign field, and never on an unbounded result
$unwindarray → documentsmultiplies document count; preserveNullAndEmptyArrays to keep empties
$facetseveral pipelines over one inputthe correct way to get data and a total count in one round trip
$setWindowFieldswindow functionsrunning totals, ranks, moving averages — replaces self-joins
$merge / $outwrite results to a collectionmaterialised views; $merge can upsert incrementally, $out replaces
$densify / $fillfill gaps in a seriesdays with no orders still appear in the chart
$bucket / $bucketAutohistogramsprice bands, latency distributions
$graphLookuprecursive traversalorg charts, category trees, referral chains
$unionWithconcatenate another collectionmerged feeds, archive plus live data
$samplerandom documentsuses a fast path only when it is the first stage and the sample is small
javascript · $facet: page and total in one query
const [result] = await orders.aggregate([
  { $match: { tenantId, status: 'placed' } },
  { $facet: {
      data: [
        { $sort: { createdAt: -1 } },
        { $skip: 0 }, { $limit: 25 },
        { $project: { _id: 1, status: 1, 'totals.totalCents': 1, createdAt: 1 } },
      ],
      total: [{ $count: 'value' }],
      byStatus: [{ $group: { _id: '$status', n: { $sum: 1 } } }],
  } },
]).toArray();

const page = result.data;
const total = result.total[0]?.value ?? 0;
// One round trip instead of three. But note: $facet cannot use an index for
// the stages inside it, so on a large match set prefer separate queries, or
// an approximate count from $collStats.
javascript · $setWindowFields: running totals and ranks
await orders.aggregate([
  { $match: { tenantId, createdAt: { $gte: from } } },
  { $setWindowFields: {
      partitionBy: '$customer.id',
      sortBy: { createdAt: 1 },
      output: {
        lifetimeCents: { $sum: '$totals.totalCents',
                         window: { documents: ['unbounded', 'current'] } },
        orderNumber:   { $documentNumber: {} },
        rolling7:      { $avg: '$totals.totalCents',
                         window: { range: [-7, 0], unit: 'day' } },
      },
  } },
]).toArray();
Aggregation is a denial-of-service vector

An aggregation over an unfiltered collection with allowDiskUse and no maxTimeMS will consume the primary's CPU and cache until it finishes. Rules: $match with an indexed, tenant-scoped filter first; maxTimeMS on every pipeline; run analytics against secondaries or an analytics node; and never let a user-supplied pipeline reach the database.

Materialised views with $merge

For dashboards that are read constantly and change slowly, run the pipeline on a schedule with $merge into a daily_sku_revenue collection, and have the API read that collection with a simple indexed query. Reads become sub-millisecond and the expensive work happens once, off the request path. Incremental $merge means you only recompute the affected days.

26

Transactions & consistency

MongoDB has real multi-document ACID transactions, and you should still avoid needing them. This chapter covers when they are correct, what they cost, and the patterns that replace them.

javascript · a transaction, with retry handling
// withTransaction retries on TransientTransactionError and
// UnknownTransactionCommitResult automatically — do NOT hand-roll this.
export async function transfer(fromId, toId, cents) {
  const session = client.startSession();
  try {
    return await session.withTransaction(async () => {
      // Every operation must pass the session, or it runs OUTSIDE the transaction.
      const from = await accounts.findOneAndUpdate(
        { _id: fromId, balanceCents: { $gte: cents } },   // the guard IS the check
        { $inc: { balanceCents: -cents } },
        { session, returnDocument: 'after' },
      );
      if (!from) throw new Conflict('insufficient funds');

      await accounts.updateOne({ _id: toId }, { $inc: { balanceCents: cents } }, { session });
      await ledger.insertOne({ fromId, toId, cents, at: new Date() }, { session });
      return from;
    }, {
      readConcern: { level: 'snapshot' },
      writeConcern: { w: 'majority' },
      readPreference: 'primary',        // transactions always read from the primary
      maxCommitTimeMS: 5_000,
    });
  } finally {
    await session.endSession();         // ALWAYS — a leaked session leaks server resources
  }
}
What transactions cost
  • 60-second default limit (transactionLifetimeLimitSeconds). A long transaction is aborted, and its work is wasted.
  • Write conflicts. Snapshot isolation means concurrent writes to the same document abort one of them. Under contention your retry rate — and latency — climbs sharply.
  • All reads go to the primary, so you lose secondary read scaling for that operation.
  • Cache pressure. The snapshot must be retained for the transaction's duration.
  • Sharded transactions add two-phase commit across shards — noticeably slower and harder to reason about.
Design them away first

Before writing a transaction, ask whether the data that must change atomically can live in one document. Single-document updates are atomic for free, at full speed, with no retries. Order plus line items plus totals in one document needs no transaction; the same data across three collections does. That is the modelling argument from Chapter 23 restated as a consistency argument.

Read and write concerns

SettingMeaningUse
w: 1acknowledged by the primary onlymetrics, logs — data you can afford to lose on a failover
w: 'majority'replicated to a majority; survives primary failurethe default for anything that matters
j: truewritten to the journal on diskimplied by majority in modern versions
readConcern: 'local'whatever this node has, may be rolled backdefault; fine for most reads
readConcern: 'majority'only data that cannot be rolled backwhen you must not show data that might vanish
readConcern: 'snapshot'a consistent point-in-time viewtransactions, multi-query consistency
readConcern: 'linearizable'reflects all writes acknowledged before it startedrare; expensive; single-document reads only
javascript · read-your-own-writes without going to the primary
// The classic bug: write with w:'majority', then immediately read from a
// secondary that has not replicated yet — the user does not see their own edit.
const session = client.startSession({ causalConsistency: true });
await orders.updateOne({ _id }, { $set: { status: 'placed' } }, { session });
// This read is guaranteed to observe the write above, even on a secondary.
const fresh = await orders.findOne({ _id }, { session, readPreference: 'secondaryPreferred' });

// Simpler alternative for a web app: after a write, read from the primary for
// that user's next request (a short "sticky primary" window), or return the
// updated document from findOneAndUpdate and skip the read entirely.

The patterns that replace transactions

Single-document atomicity

Model so the invariant lives inside one document. findOneAndUpdate with the invariant in the filter is a compare-and-swap: no transaction, no retries.

Transactional outbox

Write the state change and an outbox row in one atomic operation, then publish asynchronously. Gives you exactly the “update the database and tell the world” guarantee that a distributed transaction would — see Chapter 41.

Saga / compensation

For a workflow across services, model it as a sequence of local transactions with explicit compensating actions. You give up atomicity and gain the ability to reason about partial failure.

Idempotent retries

Make each step safe to repeat (Chapter 44), then a crash mid-workflow is recoverable by replay rather than rollback.

27

Mongoose in depth

Used deliberately, Mongoose removes real boilerplate. Used by default, it introduces performance problems that are hard to see. Here is the line between the two.

javascript · a schema worth copying
import mongoose, { Schema } from 'mongoose';

// Global safety settings — set these once, at startup.
mongoose.set('strictQuery', true);      // unknown fields in a filter throw, not silently match all
mongoose.set('sanitizeFilter', true);   // strips $-prefixed operators from user input
mongoose.set('autoIndex', false);       // NEVER build indexes from app boot in production

const OrderSchema = new Schema({
  tenantId: { type: Schema.Types.ObjectId, required: true, index: true },
  status: { type: String, required: true, enum: ['draft','placed','shipped','cancelled'], default: 'draft' },
  customer: {
    id:    { type: Schema.Types.ObjectId, required: true, ref: 'Customer' },
    name:  { type: String, required: true, trim: true, maxlength: 200 },
    email: { type: String, required: true, lowercase: true, trim: true },
  },
  items: {
    type: [new Schema({
      sku: { type: String, required: true, maxlength: 64 },
      unitCents: { type: Number, required: true, min: 0 },
      quantity: { type: Number, required: true, min: 1, max: 999 },
    }, { _id: false })],
    validate: { validator: (a) => a.length > 0 && a.length <= 100, message: '1–100 items' },
  },
  totalCents: { type: Number, required: true, min: 0 },
  idempotencyKey: { type: String, select: false },   // never returned unless asked for
}, {
  timestamps: true,                    // createdAt / updatedAt maintained for you
  optimisticConcurrency: true,         // __v is checked on save → no lost updates
  versionKey: '__v',
  minimize: false,                     // keep empty objects rather than dropping them
  toJSON: {
    virtuals: true,
    transform(_doc, ret) {
      ret.id = ret._id.toString();
      delete ret._id; delete ret.__v; delete ret.tenantId;   // never leak internals
      return ret;
    },
  },
});

// Compound indexes belong on the schema, applied by a migration
OrderSchema.index({ tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 });
OrderSchema.index({ tenantId: 1, idempotencyKey: 1 }, { unique: true,
  partialFilterExpression: { idempotencyKey: { $type: 'string' } } });

// Virtuals: derived, not stored
OrderSchema.virtual('total').get(function () { return this.totalCents / 100; });

// Instance and static methods keep query logic out of controllers
OrderSchema.methods.canCancel = function () { return this.status === 'placed'; };
OrderSchema.statics.findScoped = function (id, tenantId) {
  return this.findOne({ _id: id, tenantId });
};

// Query helper — chainable, and impossible to forget
OrderSchema.query.forTenant = function (tenantId) { return this.where({ tenantId }); };

export const Order = mongoose.model('Order', OrderSchema);
// usage: await Order.find().forTenant(t).where({ status: 'placed' }).lean();

Hooks, and where they betray you

javascript
// DOCUMENT middleware — `this` is the document. Runs on .save() only.
OrderSchema.pre('save', function (next) {
  if (this.isModified('items')) this.totalCents = recompute(this.items);
  next();
});

// QUERY middleware — `this` is the QUERY, not a document. Runs on find/update.
OrderSchema.pre(/^find/, function () {
  this.where({ deletedAt: null });          // soft-delete by default
});

// This is the trap: document hooks do NOT run for query updates.
await order.save();                     // pre('save') runs
await Order.updateOne({ _id }, { $set: { items } });   // pre('save') does NOT run
                                        // → totalCents is now wrong, silently.
// Options: put the invariant in pre('findOneAndUpdate')/pre('updateOne') as
// well, or better — move it to the service layer where it cannot be bypassed.
Validation and hooks are application-level only

They do not run for updateMany by default, for bulk writes, for the raw driver, for a migration script, or for another service touching the same database. If a rule must always hold, back it with a database constraint: a unique index, and a server-side $jsonSchema validator on the collection.

Performance: lean, populate, and the N+1

javascript
// HYDRATED: full model instances with getters, setters and change tracking.
// Necessary if you will call .save() or use virtuals/methods.
const orders = await Order.find({ tenantId }).limit(1000);

// LEAN: plain objects straight from BSON. Typically 3–10× faster and a
// fraction of the memory. Use it for every read-only path — which is most of them.
const orders = await Order.find({ tenantId }).limit(1000).lean();

// POPULATE: a SECOND query with an $in on the collected ids — not a join.
// Fine for one level and a bounded set.
const orders = await Order.find({ tenantId })
  .populate({ path: 'customer.id', select: 'name email', options: { lean: true } })
  .lean();

// THE N+1: populate inside a loop, or a virtual populate per document.
for (const o of orders) {
  o.customer = await Customer.findById(o.customer.id);   // 1 + N queries ❌
}
// Fix: one query, then a map.
const ids = [...new Set(orders.map(o => o.customer.id.toString()))];
const customers = await Customer.find({ _id: { $in: ids } }).lean();
const byId = new Map(customers.map(c => [c._id.toString(), c]));
for (const o of orders) o.customer = byId.get(o.customer.id.toString());

// Or model it away entirely with the extended-reference pattern (ch23), which
// is why that pattern exists.
javascript · instrument every query so slowness is visible
mongoose.set('debug', (collection, method, ...args) => {
  logger().debug({ collection, method, args: args.slice(0, 2) }, 'mongoose');
});

// Better: driver-level command monitoring, which sees the real query and duration
client.on('commandSucceeded', (e) => {
  dbDuration.observe({ collection: e.reply?.cursor?.ns ?? e.commandName, op: e.commandName },
                     e.duration / 1000);
  if (e.duration > 200) logger().warn({ command: e.commandName, ms: e.duration }, 'slow query');
});
Interview follow-up

“When would you not use Mongoose?” Name the specific cases: aggregation pipelines (it only wraps them), bulk writes at high throughput (hydration and middleware cost per document), change streams, and any path where you have measured the hydration overhead. Then say what you do instead — the driver behind the same repository interface, so callers cannot tell.

28

Migrations & schema evolution

A schemaless store does not remove the schema, it moves it into your code — and into every document already on disk. Changing it means changing hundreds of millions of documents while they are being read and written. Every safe change has the same shape, and every outage is somebody skipping one of its steps.

The invariant that generates everything else

There is exactly one rule, and the rest of this chapter is its consequences: at every instant, the code running in production must be able to read every document shape present in the collection. Not the shape you intended, not the shape after the backfill finishes — every shape that actually exists, including the one your rollback target expects. Hold that invariant and no schema change can take the API down. Break it for thirty seconds and you get a stream of TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined from a code path nobody tested.

The invariant forbids the two changes engineers reach for first: renaming a field in place, and adding a field the new code requires. Both create a window in which running code meets a document it cannot read. What replaces them is expand/contract, also called parallel change — six phases, each individually reversible, spread over days or weeks rather than one deploy.

  1. Expand the schema. Add the new field, index, collection or validator. Nothing reads it, nothing writes it. Old code is unaffected because old code does not know it exists.
  2. Dual-write. Deploy code that writes both shapes on every create and update, and still reads the old one. From this moment the new field is correct for all new and touched documents; only history is stale.
  3. Backfill. A separate, resumable, throttled job walks the historic documents. It runs for hours or days. It is not part of a deploy and must never block one.
  4. Switch reads. Behind a flag, start reading the new field with a fallback to the old. Verify, then remove the fallback. This is the only phase with a real rollback question, and a flag answers it in seconds.
  5. Stop writing the old shape. Delete the dual-write. The old field is now frozen — still present, still readable by any image you might roll back to.
  6. Contract. After a full rollback window has passed, $unset the old field, drop the old index, tighten the validator. This is the one irreversible step.
EXPAND ──────────────────────────────▶ CONTRACT ─────────────────▶ phase │ 1 add │ 2 dual-write │ 3 backfill │ 4 read new │ 5 stop old │ 6 drop ──────────┼──────────┼──────────────┼────────────┼────────────┼────────────┼────────── writes │ old │ old + NEW │ old + NEW │ old + NEW │ NEW │ NEW reads │ old │ old │ old │ NEW (+old) │ NEW │ NEW rollback │ redeploy │ redeploy │ redeploy │ flip flag │ redeploy │ RESTORE risk │ none │ none │ none │ low │ low │ TERMINAL
javascript · phases 2 and 4, in the repository where they belong
// PHASE 2 — dual-write. Both fields are written on every mutation, so the
// new field is authoritative for anything touched from this deploy onwards.
async function saveOrder(order) {
  await orders.updateOne({ _id: order._id }, { $set: {
    total:      order.totalCents / 100,   // OLD shape: float pounds (kept alive)
    totalCents: order.totalCents,         // NEW shape: integer minor units
    schemaVersion: 2,
  } }, { upsert: true });
}

// PHASE 4 — tolerant read. The fallback is what lets the backfill run at its
// own pace: a document the job has not reached yet still renders correctly.
function readTotalCents(doc) {
  if (doc.totalCents !== undefined) return doc.totalCents;
  // The trap this whole migration exists to fix: 19.99 * 100 === 1998.9999...
  return Math.round(doc.total * 100);
}

// The flag is not decoration. It is the rollback for phase 4, and it must be
// evaluated per request, not read once at boot — otherwise turning it off
// requires a restart, which is exactly what you do not want mid-incident.
const total = flags.enabled('orders.read-cents') ? readTotalCents(doc) : doc.total * 100;

Why updateMany({}, …) on 200 M documents is an incident

The command looks atomic. It is not: it is one long-running operation that yields the lock constantly, applies each document change individually, and offers you no way to see how far it has got or to undo what it has already done. On a large collection four separate things go wrong, usually in this order.

The oplog floods. Every modified document produces an oplog entry. Two hundred million $set entries at roughly 180 bytes each is about 36 GB of oplog written in a burst, into a capped collection whose default size is 5% of the disk and whose ceiling is 50 GB. Your oplog window — normally measured in days — collapses to minutes.

Secondaries fall behind and then fall out. Each secondary must apply that same 36 GB. If one lags past the oplog window, it cannot find its next entry, enters RECOVERING, and requires a full initial sync: hours of copying while it serves nothing. Every secondaryPreferred read in your application lands on the primary instead, and the primary is already saturated.

The cache goes dirty. WiredTiger recruits application threads to help evict once dirty content passes eviction_dirty_trigger (20% of cache). At that point your request-path writes stop being milliseconds and start being seconds, with no error to point at.

There is no undo. db.killOp() stops the operation; it does not reverse it. You now have a collection in which an unknown, unrecorded subset of documents has the new shape and the rest does not, and no way to tell which is which except a full scan.

The failure mode with a name: oplog rollover

Symptoms in order: rs.printSecondaryReplicationInfo() shows lag climbing linearly, then replSetGetStatus reports a member in RECOVERING, then the logs carry we are too stale to use <host> as a sync source. Recovery is an initial sync — hours on a multi-terabyte set — during which you are running with reduced redundancy and a single healthy secondary. A stepdown at that moment loses majority. This is not a slow query; it is an availability incident caused by one line pasted into a shell.

Batch size
500–1 000one bulkWrite; small enough to abort cleanly, big enough to amortise round trips
Lag ceiling
10 spause the backfill above this; page above 60 s
Oplog window
> 24 hbelow this a lagging secondary needs a full initial sync
Dirty cache
< 5%WiredTiger stalls application writes at 20%

A backfill you can stop, resume and audit

A backfill is a piece of production software, not a snippet. It needs four properties, and the code below exists to give it each of them: resumable (a checkpoint outside the process), idempotent (a predicate that matches only unfinished work, so re-running is a no-op), throttled (it watches replication lag and yields), and bounded (it pages by _id range, never by skip).

javascript · backfill/0031-order-total-cents.js — run as a Job, never in the API process
import { MongoClient } from 'mongodb';

const JOB = 'orders.totalCents.v1';
const BATCH = 500;
const LAG_CEILING_MS = 10_000;
const sleep = (ms) => new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));

const client = await MongoClient.connect(process.env.MONGO_URL, {
  maxPoolSize: 4,        // a migration must not compete with request traffic for the pool
  appName: JOB,          // shows up in currentOp and the profiler — you will need it
});
const db = client.db();
const orders = db.collection('orders');
const control = db.collection('_backfills');

// THE PREDICATE IS THE CONTRACT. It matches only documents that still need
// work, which is what makes the entire job idempotent: crash it, run it twice,
// run two copies by accident — already-migrated documents are untouched.
const PENDING = { totalCents: { $exists: false } };

async function replicationLagMs() {
  const s = await db.admin().command({ replSetGetStatus: 1 });
  const primary = s.members.find((m) => m.stateStr === 'PRIMARY');
  const secondaries = s.members.filter((m) => m.stateStr === 'SECONDARY');
  if (!primary || secondaries.length === 0) return 0;
  return Math.max(...secondaries.map((m) => primary.optimeDate - m.optimeDate));
}

let stopping = false;
process.on('SIGTERM', () => { stopping = true; });   // finish the batch, checkpoint, exit 0

const state = await control.findOne({ _id: JOB });
let lastId = state?.lastId ?? null;                  // resume exactly where we stopped
let migrated = state?.migrated ?? 0;

while (!stopping) {
  // A range scan on _id is the only cursor that is both index-driven and stable
  // while the collection is being written to. skip(n) is O(n) AND drifts: a
  // document inserted or deleted behind the cursor shifts every later page.
  const batch = await orders.find(
    lastId ? { _id: { $gt: lastId } } : {},
    { projection: { _id: 1, total: 1, totalCents: 1 },
      sort: { _id: 1 }, limit: BATCH, maxTimeMS: 30_000, readPreference: 'primary' },
  ).toArray();

  if (batch.length === 0) break;                     // end of collection
  lastId = batch[batch.length - 1]._id;              // advance even if nothing matched

  const ops = batch
    .filter((d) => d.totalCents === undefined)
    .map((d) => ({ updateOne: {
      // Repeat PENDING in the FILTER, not just in JS. Between the read and the
      // write a request may have set the field; this makes the write a no-op
      // instead of clobbering a newer, correct value with a computed one.
      filter: { _id: d._id, ...PENDING },
      update: { $set: { totalCents: Math.round(d.total * 100), schemaVersion: 2 } },
    } }));

  if (ops.length > 0) {
    const res = await orders.bulkWrite(ops, {
      ordered: false,   // one rejected document must not abandon the other 499
      // w:'majority' is correctness AND throttle: each batch waits for the
      // secondaries, so the job can never outrun replication by more than a batch.
      writeConcern: { w: 'majority', wtimeout: 10_000 },
    });
    migrated += res.modifiedCount;
  }

  // Checkpoint AFTER the write. Die between the two and the next run redoes one
  // batch — harmless, because the write is idempotent. The reverse order would
  // silently skip up to 500 documents and you would never find out.
  await control.updateOne({ _id: JOB },
    { $set: { lastId, migrated, updatedAt: new Date() } }, { upsert: true });

  const lag = await replicationLagMs();
  if (lag > LAG_CEILING_MS) {
    console.warn({ lagMs: lag }, 'replication lag above ceiling — backing off');
    await sleep(Math.min(60_000, lag));
  } else {
    await sleep(50);          // baseline: ~10k docs/min, ~14M/day. Tune, do not remove.
  }
}
Answering “are we done?” without a full scan

The obvious index does not exist: partialFilterExpression rejects $exists: false with Expression not supported in partial index — it accepts only equality, $exists: true, $type, the range operators, $in and $and/$or over those. Two things that do work. Index the migrated state — createIndex({ schemaVersion: 1 }, { partialFilterExpression: { schemaVersion: { $gte: 2 } } }) — and track countDocuments({ schemaVersion: { $gte: 2 } }), an index scan, against estimatedDocumentCount(). And read progress straight off the checkpoint document: when lastId has passed the maximum _id in the collection, the pass is complete. Reserve the honest countDocuments(PENDING) full scan for the single final gate before phase 6, and run it on a secondary.

Lazy migration: let the read path do the work

The alternative to a sweep is to migrate each document the moment somebody reads it. Every document carries a schemaVersion, the repository owns a chain of one-step upgrade functions, and nothing above the repository ever sees an old shape. It costs no write capacity, it never lags replication, and it is the right answer for collections with a long cold tail — audit records, archived orders, dormant accounts — where a full sweep would rewrite terabytes to serve nobody.

javascript · on-read migration with a version chain
// v1: { name: 'Ada Lovelace' }
// v2: { firstName: 'Ada', lastName: 'Lovelace' }
// v3: { firstName, lastName, locale: 'en-GB' }
const CURRENT = 3;

// Each step moves exactly ONE version forward and is a pure function. Never
// write a 1-to-3 shortcut: you will need the intermediate step the day you add v4.
const STEPS = {
  1: (d) => { const [first, ...rest] = (d.name ?? '').split(' ');
              const { name, ...keep } = d;
              return { ...keep, firstName: first, lastName: rest.join(' '), schemaVersion: 2 }; },
  2: (d) => ({ ...d, locale: d.country === 'US' ? 'en-US' : 'en-GB', schemaVersion: 3 }),
};

export function normalise(doc) {
  let d = doc;
  // Absent schemaVersion means version 1 — documents written before the field
  // existed. Defaulting to CURRENT here is the classic way to silently skip them.
  let v = doc.schemaVersion ?? 1;
  while (v < CURRENT) { d = STEPS[v](d); v = d.schemaVersion; }
  return d;
}

export async function getUser(id) {
  const doc = await users.findOne({ _id: id });
  if (!doc) return null;
  const user = normalise(doc);

  // Opportunistic write-back: outside the response path, guarded so a
  // concurrent newer write is never downgraded, and failure is invisible.
  if ((doc.schemaVersion ?? 1) !== CURRENT) {
    setImmediate(() => users.updateOne(
      { _id: doc._id, schemaVersion: { $lt: CURRENT } },   // guard: never go backwards
      { $set: user },
    ).catch((err) => logger().warn({ err, id }, 'lazy migration write-back failed')));
  }
  return user;
}
Lazy migration alone never finishes

Documents nobody reads are never upgraded, so STEPS[1] becomes permanent code that you cannot delete and juniors cannot explain. Worse, the read path is not the only reader: aggregation pipelines, exports, the analytics job and the next service to touch this collection all see raw v1 documents and quietly produce wrong numbers — $group on firstName simply drops every unmigrated row rather than failing. Use lazy migration to make the change safe immediately, then run a slow background sweep behind it. The sweep is what earns you the right to delete the branch.

Migrations belong in source control

A schema change performed by pasting into mongosh has no author, no review, no record of having run, and no way to be replayed onto a fresh environment. migrate-mongo is the standard answer: timestamped files in the repository, a changelog collection recording what has been applied and when, and up/down pairs. The tool is small; the discipline around it is the valuable part.

javascript · migrations/20260214093000-orders-total-cents.js
// Rules this file obeys, all of them learned the hard way:
//
//  1. NO IMPORTS FROM src/. Your Mongoose models describe the NEW shape and
//     carry hooks, validators and required fields. A migration runs against the
//     OLD shape and must not be re-broken every time the model changes. Raw
//     driver only. A migration that imports a model is a time bomb: it works
//     today and throws ValidationError when replayed on a fresh database in six
//     months, because the model has moved on and the migration has not.
//  2. IDEMPOTENT. Safe to run twice, safe to resume after a partial failure.
//  3. SMALL AND FAST. Anything touching more than ~50k documents does NOT live
//     here — a migration blocks the deploy. It creates the index and the
//     validator; a separate Job does the backfill.
//  4. It runs from a deploy step or a one-shot Job. NEVER from application boot:
//     with three replicas you get three concurrent migrations racing.

module.exports = {
  async up(db) {
    // Additive and idempotent: createIndex on an existing identical index is a
    // no-op. A DIFFERENT index with the same name throws IndexOptionsConflict.
    // Note the filter indexes what is DONE, not what is pending: a partial index
    // cannot express `$exists: false`.
    await db.collection('orders').createIndex(
      { schemaVersion: 1 },
      { name: 'cents_backfill_progress',
        partialFilterExpression: { schemaVersion: { $gte: 2 } } },
    );
    // Bounded by an indexed predicate, so this is milliseconds, not minutes.
    await db.collection('orders').updateMany(
      { totalCents: { $exists: false }, createdAt: { $gte: new Date('2026-02-01') } },
      [{ $set: { totalCents: { $toInt: { $round: [{ $multiply: ['$total', 100] }, 0] } },
                 schemaVersion: 2 } }],   // pipeline update: can reference other fields
    );
  },

  async down(db) {
    // A `down` that has never been executed is a comment, not a rollback. Run
    // `up; down; up` in CI against a restored production snapshot, or write
    // "irreversible — restore from backup" here and mean it.
    await db.collection('orders').dropIndex('cents_backfill_progress');
    await db.collection('orders').updateMany({}, { $unset: { totalCents: '' } });
  },
};
The three commands

migrate-mongo status before every deploy — it lists pending migrations and is the only honest answer to “is prod up to date?”. migrate-mongo up in the deploy pipeline, as a step that must succeed before the new image receives traffic. migrate-mongo down rolls back exactly one migration, which is why each file must contain exactly one logical change. In CI, restore last night's snapshot and run up, down, up; a rollback you have never executed is not a rollback.

Server-side validators, introduced without breaking history

Application validation does not run for the migration script, the bulk write, the other service, or the engineer in a shell. A $jsonSchema validator on the collection does. The problem is that turning one on over a collection full of historic documents in their pre-migration shape would reject every subsequent write to them. The two knobs that solve this are validationLevel and validationAction, and the rollout is a diagonal walk across them.

Roll left to right, then top to bottom. Never start at strict + error on a live collection.
validationAction: 'warn'validationAction: 'error'
level: 'moderate'Start here. Violations are logged, nothing is rejected. Grep the server log for real counts before you commit.Rejects non-conforming writes only to documents that already conform. Historic documents remain writable, so nothing breaks mid-backfill.
level: 'strict'Logs every violation including writes to historic documents. Useful to size the remaining tail.End here, after countDocuments on the pending predicate has been 0 for a full rollback window.
javascript · mongosh — collMod on a live collection, and hiding an index before dropping it
// collMod applies to the collection in place. It does NOT rewrite documents and
// does NOT scan — it is metadata only, so it is safe on a 200M collection.
db.runCommand({
  collMod: 'orders',
  validator: { $jsonSchema: {
    bsonType: 'object',
    required: ['tenantId', 'status', 'totalCents'],
    properties: {
      tenantId:   { bsonType: 'objectId' },
      status:     { enum: ['draft', 'placed', 'shipped', 'cancelled'] },
      // Integer minor units. bsonType 'int' rejects the float you are migrating
      // away from, which is exactly the regression you want caught at the server.
      totalCents: { bsonType: 'int', minimum: 0 },
      schemaVersion: { bsonType: 'int', minimum: 2 },
    },
    additionalProperties: true,   // start permissive; tightening this is its own migration
  } },
  validationLevel: 'moderate',    // historic documents stay writable
  validationAction: 'warn',       // phase 1: observe only
});

// Dropping an index is irreversible and rebuilding one on a large collection is
// hours. Hide it first (4.4+): the planner ignores it, but it stays maintained,
// so un-hiding is instant if p99 moves.
db.runCommand({ collMod: 'orders', index: { name: 'tenant_total_idx', hidden: true } });
// ...watch dashboards for a week, THEN:
db.orders.dropIndex('tenant_total_idx');

Index changes are migrations too

Since 4.2 an index build takes an exclusive lock only briefly at the start and the end, and the rest runs alongside traffic — but “alongside traffic” on a collection larger than cache still means sustained read amplification and a measurable p99 hit for the duration. For anything genuinely large, build it rolling instead.

  1. One secondary at a time. Stop mongod, restart it as a standalone on a different port with no replSetName, build the index there, stop it, restart it back in the set. It catches up from the oplog — which is why the oplog window matters more than usual during index work.
  2. Repeat for every secondary. Never take two out at once; you need a majority to acknowledge w: 'majority' writes, and losing it stalls every write in the application.
  3. Step down the primary with rs.stepDown(), wait for the election, then treat the former primary as the last secondary. The election costs you a few seconds of write errors, so do it in a quiet window and make sure the driver retries.
  4. Verify. db.orders.getIndexes() on every member, and check explain() actually picks the new index before you rely on it.

Four changes, four recipes

ChangeThe one-liner that causes the incidentThe recipe
Rename a fieldupdateMany({}, { $rename: { a: 'b' } })Textbook expand/contract. $rename is also a trap in a pipeline update — it is a no-op on documents where the source is missing, so a partial run leaves no trace of how far it got.
Change a type$set the same field to the new typeNew field, new name, always. A field with mixed types in one index answers range queries wrong, not slowly.
Add a required fieldrequired: true in the Mongoose schemaShip it as optional with a default, backfill, verify the count is 0, then make it required and add the validator. required does not break reads — it breaks the next save() on a legacy document, in a code path that is unrelated to your change.
Split a collectioncopy, then switch readsDual-write both collections, backfill, compare counts and a sampled checksum, switch reads behind a flag, then stop the old write. Deleting the source collection is a phase-6 decision, weeks later.
Drop a field$unset and delete the codeDelete the readers first and ship. Wait a rollback window. Then $unset in a throttled job, not an updateMany.
Mixed types in one indexed field return wrong answers, silently

Half your documents have price: 150 and half still have price: "150". BSON has a total ordering across types and numbers sort before strings, so find({ price: { $gte: 100 } }) matches the numbers and excludes every string. No error, no warning, no slow query — just a report that is quietly missing half the rows, and a sort order that interleaves by type. This is the single strongest argument for never changing a field's type in place: give the new type a new name, and let the index for each stay homogeneous.

Which goes first, the code or the data?

Always the artefact that tolerates both shapes. In practice that means additive database changes — creating an index, adding a collection, installing a validator in warn mode — can go before the deploy, because code that does not know about them cannot be broken by them. Everything subtractive goes after the last code that reads the old shape has been out of production for at least one rollback window.

That window is the real unit of scheduling, and most teams have never written it down. If your rollback procedure is “redeploy last week's image”, then last week's image must still work against today's database — which means a field cannot be dropped until every image you would roll back to has stopped reading it. Pick the number, publish it, and make phase 6 a scheduled ticket rather than something that rides along with a feature.

Interview follow-up

“Rename a field on a 200-million-document collection with no downtime.” Lead with the invariant — at every instant, running code must read every shape present — then name the six phases and say which one is reversible and which is not. The three things that separate a senior answer: the backfill is a separately deployed, resumable job with a checkpoint collection and a lag-aware throttle, not a line in the migration file; the rollback constraint is that yesterday's image must work against today's database, which is what sets the delay before the destructive step; and the gate for phase 6 is a specific query — countDocuments({ oldField: { $exists: true } }) reading 0, and staying 0 for a full rollback window. If they push on why not just updateMany, do not say “it's slow”. Say it floods the oplog, pushes a secondary into RECOVERING and a full initial sync, and cannot be resumed or reversed.

29

Replication, elections & failover

A replica set is not a backup and it is not free high availability — it is a distributed log with a voting protocol bolted on. Everything that hurts in production (rollbacks, stale reads, ten seconds of failed writes at 03:00) follows from that one sentence.

driver ──writes──▶ ┌─────────────┐ applies the op, appends one entry │ PRIMARY │ to the capped collection local.oplog.rs └──────┬──────┘ │ secondaries tail the oplog — they PULL, the primary never pushes ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ │ SECONDARY │ │ SECONDARY │ │ ARBITER │ │ votes, data │ │ votes, data │ │ votes, NO data│ └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘ electable hidden:true a vote you cannot priority:0 read from and cannot → backups, BI fail over to heartbeats every 2 s · no primary heard for electionTimeoutMillis (10 s) ──▶ election a candidate needs a strict majority of VOTING members — not of surviving members

Anatomy: who votes, who serves, who lies to you

Primary
The single member accepting writes. There is exactly one, or there is none. Every write is applied locally and written to the oplog in the same operation.
Secondary
Tails the primary's oplog and replays it. Readable, electable, and by default invisible to your driver unless you ask for it with a read preference.
Arbiter
Votes, holds no data, cannot be elected, cannot be read. Its only purpose is to make a two-data-node set able to elect. It is almost always the wrong answer — see the callout below.
Hidden member
priority: 0, hidden: true. Replicates fully but is never returned by server selection, so no read preference can accidentally route traffic to it. The correct home for backups, BI extracts and full-collection scans.
Delayed member
A hidden member with secondaryDelaySecs (named slaveDelay before 5.0). It is deliberately N seconds behind, so a bad migration or an updateMany with a missing filter can be recovered from a live node. The delay must be shorter than your oplog window, or the member can never catch up again.
priority / votes
priority: 0 means "never become primary". votes: 0 means "no say in elections" and requires priority: 0. A set may have 50 members but only 7 voting ones; everything beyond that must be votes: 0.
Why arbiters cause outages (PSA sets)

In a primary-secondary-arbiter set, lose the secondary and you still have a primary — the set looks healthy. But the majority commit point can no longer advance, because a majority is 2 of 3 and only one data-bearing node remains. Consequences, all at once: every w: "majority" write blocks until wtimeout; every readConcern: "majority" read and every transaction stalls; and WiredTiger must retain all history since the last majority-committed snapshot, so cache pressure climbs and the history store grows until the disk fills. You degrade from "one node down" to "cluster down" on a single failure. Add a third data-bearing node instead — the cost of one more instance is much less than the incident.

The oplog, and why idempotency is the whole trick

The oplog is a capped collection in the local database. Secondaries open a tailable cursor on it and replay entries in order. The critical design decision is that MongoDB does not replicate your statement — it replicates the effect, rewritten to be idempotent. A { $inc: { views: 1 } } becomes, in the oplog, a $set to the resulting value. An upsert becomes an explicit insert or update. Applying the same entry twice therefore produces the same document, which is exactly what a secondary needs when it crashes mid-batch and restarts from its last durable point, and what initial sync needs when it replays oplog entries recorded while it was copying collections.

The practical consequence is the oplog window: the wall-clock span between the oldest and newest entry. If a secondary is offline for longer than that window, the entry it needs has already been overwritten, it goes RECOVERING with too stale to catch up, and your only recovery is a full initial sync — hours of disk and network on a node you needed. The oplog window is not a storage setting, it is a maximum tolerable node downtime, and it shrinks under write load exactly when you can least afford it.

Oplog window
≥ 24 h72 h if you ever patch nodes by hand
Replication lag
< 2 spage above 10 s; investigate at 30 s
electionTimeoutMillis
10 000detection delay before an election even starts
Write outage per failover
5–15 sMongoDB's own guidance: median under 12 s
javascript · mongosh: the two commands to run before you size anything
rs.printReplicationInfo()
// configured oplog size:   51200 MB
// log length start to end: 187245 secs (52.01 hrs)   <-- THE number. Not the MB.
// oplog first event time:  Tue Sep 02 2025 04:11:07 GMT+0000
// oplog last event time:   Thu Sep 04 2025 08:11:52 GMT+0000

rs.printSecondaryReplicationInfo()
// source: mongo-2.internal:27017
//   syncedTo: Thu Sep 04 2025 08:11:51 GMT+0000
//   1 secs (0 hrs) behind the primary          <-- healthy
// source: mongo-3.internal:27017
//   syncedTo: Thu Sep 04 2025 07:44:03 GMT+0000
//   1668 secs (0.46 hrs) behind the primary    <-- this node cannot save you

// Resize the oplog online — no restart, no downtime, since 4.4:
db.adminCommand({ replSetResizeOplog: 1, size: 102400 })   // MB, double it

// Better than guessing at a size: pin a MINIMUM RETENTION and let the
// collection grow to satisfy it. Entries are only truncated once they are
// both older than this AND the collection is over its size cap.
db.adminCommand({ replSetResizeOplog: 1, size: 102400, minRetentionHours: 48 })
The default oplog size is a guess about your disk, not your workload

MongoDB defaults the oplog to 5% of free disk (floor 990 MB, cap 50 GB) at the moment the node is first initialised. A node provisioned with a small data volume that later grows keeps the tiny oplog it was born with. Measure the window with rs.printReplicationInfo() on your busiest hour, not at 04:00, and alert on it — an oplog window falling below 12 hours is a silent countdown to an unrecoverable secondary.

Replication lag: causes, measurement, blast radius

A secondary applies oplog entries in parallel batches, but it must apply all of them — it cannot skip the expensive ones. Lag therefore appears when the secondary's ability to apply is lower than the primary's ability to produce. Four causes account for nearly everything: sustained write volume above what the secondary's disk can absorb (the classic case of an under-provisioned secondary, or a smaller instance class chosen to save money); a long-running operation on the secondary holding locks that the applier needs; a foreground index build; and a large unbatched write — an updateMany touching a million documents becomes a million oplog entries the secondary must replay one at a time, having taken the primary a single command.

What breaks as lag grows, in order: secondary reads start serving visibly stale data (a user updates their profile and sees the old value); maxStalenessSeconds begins excluding nodes so your read load concentrates back onto the primary; the majority commit point falls behind, so w: "majority" latency rises for everyone; and finally the lagging node exceeds the oplog window and needs a full resync. A node lagging by 20 minutes is not a degraded replica — it is a node that cannot be elected without losing 20 minutes of writes.
