javascript · export replication lag as a metric, not a dashboard glance
// rs.status() is the source of truth. Lag = primary optime - secondary optime.
// Run this from a small side-car or a scheduled job against the ADMIN db,
// with readPreference primaryPreferred so it still reports during a failover.
export async function collectReplicationLag(client, gauge) {
  const status = await client.db('admin').command({ replSetGetStatus: 1 });
  const primary = status.members.find((m) => m.stateStr === 'PRIMARY');
  if (!primary) {                       // mid-election: this IS the signal
    gauge.set({ member: 'none' }, Number.NaN);
    logger().error({ set: status.set }, 'no primary in replSetGetStatus');
    return;
  }
  for (const m of status.members) {
    if (m.stateStr !== 'SECONDARY') continue;
    // optimeDate is the timestamp of the last oplog entry APPLIED by that member.
    const lagSeconds = (primary.optimeDate - m.optimeDate) / 1000;
    gauge.set({ member: m.name, hidden: String(m.hidden ?? false) }, lagSeconds);
  }
}

// The trap: on an idle set, lag appears to grow because no new oplog entries
// exist to advance the secondary's optime. The server writes a periodic no-op
// every 10 s precisely to stop this, but if you compute lag against
// Date.now() instead of the PRIMARY's optime you will page yourself at 3 a.m.
// for a perfectly healthy, quiet cluster.

Elections: majority, and why even numbers are a trap

  1. Detection. Members heartbeat every 2 s. A secondary that has not heard from a primary within electionTimeoutMillis (default 10 000) declares the primary gone. This delay dominates your failover time — lowering it speeds up failover and makes spurious elections on a jittery network far more likely.
  2. Candidacy. The secondary increments the term and requests votes. It only stands if its own oplog is at least as current as the members it can see; a lagging node cannot win.
  3. Majority. It needs a strict majority of configured voting members. In a 5-voter set that is 3 — regardless of how many are currently reachable. A minority partition can never elect, which is what prevents split brain.
  4. Catch-up. The winner does not accept writes immediately; it first pulls any entries it is missing from the most up-to-date reachable secondary, governed by catchUpTimeoutMillis. This is the phase that minimises data loss.
  5. Rollback on the loser. When the old primary rejoins, any writes it applied that the new primary never received are rolled back — see the write-concern section.

The arithmetic of majorities is why an even number of voting members is a defect, not a neutral choice. Four voters need three to elect: you tolerate one failure, exactly the same as three voters, while adding a fourth thing that can break and a fourth vote to split. Two voters need two: you tolerate zero failures, and losing either node leaves the survivor permanently secondary and read-only. Always configure an odd number of voting members, and spread them across three failure domains — a 2+2+1 layout across three availability zones survives the loss of any one zone; a 2+2 layout across two zones does not survive losing the zone with two.

Failover as the Node driver actually experiences it

Your application does not receive "the primary changed". It receives a burst of errors, and what happens next depends entirely on client options you set at startup. The driver's topology monitor marks the old primary Unknown, immediately clears the connection pool for that server (so in-flight sockets are not reused against a stale node), and begins re-scanning aggressively — every 500 ms rather than the usual 10 s. Meanwhile every operation you issue enters server selection: it blocks, waiting for a suitable server to appear, until serverSelectionTimeoutMS expires. That blocking is the feature. With the right settings a 9-second failover shows up as elevated p99 latency; with the wrong ones it shows up as a wall of 500s.

javascript · client options and observability that make a failover a latency blip
const client = new MongoClient(uri, {
  // Retry ONE time on a retryable error after re-selecting a server. Both
  // default to true in driver 4.x+ — do not turn them off "for safety".
  retryWrites: true,
  retryReads: true,

  // How long an operation waits for a usable primary to appear. This is your
  // failover shock absorber: it MUST comfortably exceed a normal election
  // (~10-15 s). Setting it to 5 s to "fail fast" converts every routine
  // failover into an outage.
  serverSelectionTimeoutMS: 30_000,

  // Per-operation ceiling once a server IS selected. Guards against a hung
  // socket to a node that is up but wedged. Keep it BELOW your HTTP timeout.
  socketTimeoutMS: 45_000,
  connectTimeoutMS: 10_000,

  // Bound the work itself, so a slow query cannot outlive the request that
  // wanted it. Applies server-side; survives the client giving up.
  timeoutMS: 10_000,               // driver 6.x CSOT — replaces ad-hoc maxTimeMS

  writeConcern: { w: 'majority', wtimeoutMS: 5_000 },
  readPreference: 'primary',
});

// SDAM events are the only way to KNOW a failover happened. Log them; you will
// read them during the post-incident review and nothing else will explain the
// 11-second gap.
client.on('serverDescriptionChanged', (e) => {
  if (e.previousDescription.type !== e.newDescription.type) {
    logger().warn({ address: e.address, from: e.previousDescription.type,
                    to: e.newDescription.type }, 'sdam server type changed');
  }
});
client.on('topologyDescriptionChanged', (e) => {
  const hasPrimary = [...e.newDescription.servers.values()]
    .some((s) => s.type === 'RSPrimary');
  primaryUpGauge.set(hasPrimary ? 1 : 0);      // alert on this being 0 for > 30 s
});
client.on('connectionPoolCleared', (e) => {
  poolClearedCounter.inc({ address: e.address });   // expected once per failover
});
What the driver retries, and what it hands you
Error you will seeCodeRetried?
MongoServerError: not primary10107yes — reselect, retry once
PrimarySteppedDown189yes
InterruptedDueToReplStateChange11602yes
MongoNetworkError on write—yes, if retryWrites and the op is retryable
updateMany / deleteMany during failover—no — multi-document writes are never retryable
MongoServerSelectionError: Server selection timed out—no — the election outlasted your timeout
MongoWriteConcernError: waiting for replication timed out64no — and the write may still commit
Retryable writes are exactly-once, but only once

The driver attaches a session id and a monotonic transaction number to each retryable write, and the server records it in config.transactions. A retry of the same statement returns the original result rather than applying it twice — that is what makes it safe. But the retry happens once. If the election is still in progress when the retry is attempted, you get the error. Your own idempotency layer is still required; retryable writes shorten the window, they do not remove it. And note that updateMany and deleteMany are excluded entirely, so a bulk cleanup job needs its own resume logic.

Read preferences and the staleness trap

ModeRoutes toThe trap
primaryprimary onlyDefault. Errors outright during an election — correct, but means every read shares the failover outage.
primaryPreferredprimary, else any secondarySilently degrades to stale reads exactly when the cluster is unhealthy and lag is highest. Fine for a dashboard, wrong for a balance.
secondarysecondaries onlyErrors when all secondaries are down even though a healthy primary exists. Reads are always lagged by some amount.
secondaryPreferredsecondaries, else primaryThe load-shedding you wanted becomes a thundering herd onto the primary the moment a secondary drops out.
nearestlowest-latency member, primary included"Nearest" means network round-trip within localThresholdMS (15 ms), not freshest. A nearby, badly lagged secondary wins.
javascript · bounding staleness and pinning analytics to dedicated nodes
import { ReadPreference } from 'mongodb';

// maxStalenessSeconds tells the driver to EXCLUDE members estimated to be
// further behind than this. Minimum 90 — the driver throws below it, because
// the estimate is built from heartbeats (10 s) plus the server's idle no-op
// write period (10 s) and anything tighter would be noise.
const listings = db.collection('listings', {
  readPreference: new ReadPreference('secondaryPreferred', null, {
    maxStalenessSeconds: 120,
  }),
});

// Tag sets route to SPECIFIC members. Tag the node in the replica set config
// ({ 'nodeType': 'ANALYTICS' } — Atlas analytics nodes carry exactly this tag),
// then send every heavy aggregation there. The tag list is ordered: the driver
// tries each document in turn and uses the first that matches any member.
const analytics = db.collection('orders', {
  readPreference: new ReadPreference('secondary', [
    { nodeType: 'ANALYTICS' },   // preferred: the dedicated node
    {},                          // fallback: ANY secondary — remove this if you
                                 // would rather fail than crush an OLTP replica
  ]),
});

// Or entirely in the URI, for a second client dedicated to reporting:
// mongodb+srv://…/app?readPreference=secondary&readPreferenceTags=nodeType:ANALYTICS
//   &maxStalenessSeconds=300&appName=reporting

// THE TRAP: maxStalenessSeconds is an estimate, not a guarantee, and it is
// only meaningful with 3+ members. It stops you reading from a node that is
// minutes behind; it does NOT give you read-your-own-writes. For that you need
// a causally consistent session, which carries the cluster time forward so a
// secondary waits until it has applied your write before answering.
const session = client.startSession({ causalConsistency: true });

Write concern, and the rollback nobody reads

Write concern is where replication stops being an operations concern and becomes a correctness one. w: 1 means the primary acknowledged the write in memory and nothing more. If that primary fails before a secondary has replicated the entry, the new primary never had it, and when the old node rejoins the set it must undo those writes to converge on the new primary's history. That is a rollback: the affected documents are written out as BSON files under <dbpath>/rollback/<db>.<collection>/, the node resumes as a secondary, and your application is never told. You returned POST 201 Created to a customer for an order that no longer exists.

w: "majority" is the fix, because a write acknowledged by a majority is, by the same arithmetic that governs elections, guaranteed to be present on any node that can win an election. It cannot be rolled back. It costs one network round trip to the second-fastest data-bearing node — on a same-region replica set, typically single-digit milliseconds.

wtimeout does not undo the write

{ w: 'majority', wtimeoutMS: 5000 } returns MongoWriteConcernError (code 64) if replication has not reached a majority in five seconds. The write is still on the primary and will very likely replicate a moment later. Treating that error as a failure and retrying gives you two orders; treating it as a success gives you an order that may still roll back. The only correct handling is an idempotency key on the write itself, so the retry is a no-op, plus a metric on error 64 — a sustained rate of it means a secondary is down or lagging and you are one primary failure away from data loss. Conversely, omitting wtimeout entirely means the operation blocks indefinitely when a majority is unreachable, quietly consuming your connection pool until the whole service stalls.

Rehearse it, on purpose, in business hours

A failover path that has never been exercised is a hypothesis. Step a primary down deliberately, under load, and watch what your own dashboards say. Since 4.2 replSetStepDown also kills in-progress operations on the stepping-down node, so this is a genuine rehearsal rather than a polite handover.

bash · the failover drill
# 1. Put steady load through the real API first — this is a test of your app,
#    not of MongoDB. MongoDB will pass.
k6 run --vus 50 --duration 3m load/checkout.js &

# 2. Step down the current primary for 60 s, allowing 10 s for a secondary to
#    catch up first. Without the catch-up period you are also testing rollback.
mongosh "$MONGO_URI" --eval 'rs.stepDown(60, 10)'

# 3. Watch the set converge. stateStr goes PRIMARY -> SECONDARY on the old
#    node and SECONDARY -> PRIMARY on the winner, usually within 3-12 s.
watch -n1 'mongosh "$MONGO_URI" --quiet --eval \
  "rs.status().members.map(m => m.name + \" \" + m.stateStr).join(\"\\n\")"'

# 4. On Atlas, use the managed equivalent so the drill matches production:
#    Cluster -> ... -> Test Failover, or
curl -u "$PUB:$PRIV" --digest -X POST \
  "https://cloud.mongodb.com/api/atlas/v2/groups/$GROUP/clusters/$NAME/restartPrimaries"

# PASS criteria — decide these BEFORE you run it:
#   * zero 5xx responses to clients (elevated p99 is expected and fine)
#   * MongoServerSelectionError count == 0
#   * connectionPoolCleared fires once per node, then stops
#   * write error rate returns to baseline within 20 s
#   * no rollback files: find /var/lib/mongodb/rollback -type f
Two settings that turn a failover into a non-event

Set serverSelectionTimeoutMS comfortably above your election time (30 s), and set your HTTP request timeout above that for write endpoints, so the request survives long enough for the retry to land. If your load balancer kills the request at 10 s, the driver's careful 30-second wait is wasted — the client already saw a 504. Failover survival is an end-to-end timeout budget, not a database setting.

Interview follow-up

"Walk me through what happens to an in-flight POST when the primary dies." The strong answer is layered and names mechanisms: the socket errors or the server returns not primary (10107); the driver marks that server Unknown, clears its pool and re-scans every 500 ms; the operation re-enters server selection and blocks up to serverSelectionTimeoutMS; a secondary detects the missing heartbeat after electionTimeoutMillis, wins a majority vote, catches up, and starts accepting writes — typically 5 to 15 seconds end to end; the driver then retries the write once, and because the retry carries the same session id and transaction number, the server deduplicates it rather than applying it twice. Then close the loop on the two things that decide whether the user notices: the write must have been w: "majority", or it can be rolled back into a BSON file nobody reads; and the whole retry only helps if your HTTP timeout is longer than your server-selection timeout. Finish by mentioning that updateMany is not retryable, which is why bulk jobs need their own resume point.

30

Sharding

Sharding is horizontal partitioning on a key you choose once and can only change by rewriting the entire collection. It is the only way past the ceiling of a single primary — and the fastest way to turn a healthy database into a permanently lopsided one, where one shard runs at 90% CPU and the other five idle.

Shard later than you think

Most clusters that get sharded early should not have been. Sharding multiplies your operational surface by the number of shards, makes unique indexes nearly impossible, turns cheap transactions into two-phase commits, and permanently constrains how you may query. Work down this ladder first.

  1. Fix the indexes. The overwhelming majority of "we need to shard" tickets are a collection scan, an in-memory sort, or an unbounded $lookup. Read the slow-query log before you read the sharding docs.
  2. Scale up. Vertical scaling on managed MongoDB goes a very long way — hundreds of gigabytes of RAM and NVMe storage on a single instance. A larger instance is a maintenance window; a shard key is forever.
  3. Move reads off the primary. Analytics, exports and dashboards run on secondaries or a dedicated analytics node with readPreference: 'secondaryPreferred' and a tag set. This buys read capacity, never write capacity.
  4. Archive and tier. Most collections are 5% hot and 95% cold. Move closed orders older than 18 months to an archive collection or object storage, and the working set — not the total size — collapses.
  5. Split by domain. Events into their own cluster, sessions into Redis, files into S3. Several unsharded clusters are far easier to run than one sharded one.
  6. Then shard. Only when a single replica set genuinely cannot hold the working set or absorb the write rate.

There are two honest triggers. The first: the working set — indexes plus the documents actually being touched — no longer fits in the WiredTiger cache on the largest instance you can buy, so reads hit disk and eviction pressure never subsides. The second: sustained write throughput exceeds what one primary can commit, because every write in a replica set goes to exactly one node. Storage size alone is not a trigger — a 4 TB collection with a 20 GB working set is fine.

Working set ÷ cache
< 1.0above 1.0 the cache thrashes and p99 read latency climbs with no query change
Targeted queries
> 95%share of ops that carry the shard key; below this, adding shards adds latency
Default range size
128 MBthe unit the balancer moves; smaller means more migrations, more metadata
Minimum cluster
13 processes3 shards × 3 nodes + 3 config servers + at least one mongos

The moving parts

Three tiers, and worth being able to draw from memory. Your application never talks to a shard; it talks to mongos, a stateless router that owns no data and caches the routing table. The config servers hold that table — which ranges of shard key live on which shard — as an ordinary replica set. Each shard is itself a replica set, so a three-shard cluster is three independent failure domains with three primaries and three elections.

app (mongoose / driver) one connection pool per mongos │ ▼ mongos mongos mongos stateless routers, no data, cache the routing table │ │ │ pool to EVERY shard: app × mongos × shards connections ├──────┴──────┴──── reads metadata ──► config servers (CSRS) │ replica set holding config.chunks, │ config.collections, config.settings ▼ ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐ │ shard A │ shard B │ shard C │ each a full replica set (P + 2S) │ P S S │ P S S │ P S S │ each with its own primary and elections └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘ ▲ └── the balancer migrates ranges between shards to equalise data size Lose the config servers and you have not lost data — you have lost the map. Without it the shards are a pile of disjoint partial collections.

Data is divided into contiguous ranges (historically "chunks") of shard key values, bounded by MinKey and MaxKey at the extremes. The balancer, which runs on the config server primary in modern versions, moves ranges to equalise data size — not range count, which is what older releases balanced on and which could leave shards wildly uneven. Ranges split as they grow and are merged back when adjacent ranges are small.

A jumbo range has grown past the range size but cannot be split, because every document in it shares one shard key value — there is nowhere to put a split point. The balancer skips it, the shard holding it keeps growing, and sh.status() prints jumbo: true beside it. Jumbo ranges are not a balancer bug; they are a shard key bug with a delayed fuse.

The shard key is the whole decision

Everything else in a sharded cluster is operational and reversible. The shard key is neither. Judge a candidate on three properties, in this order.

Cardinality
How many distinct values exist? This is a hard cap on how many ranges can exist, and therefore on how many shards you can ever use. A key of country across 12 countries gives you at most 12 ranges — a 20-shard cluster is impossible, no matter how much data arrives.
Frequency
How evenly are documents spread across those values? High cardinality with a skewed distribution is still fatal. customerId looks perfect until one enterprise customer accounts for 30% of the collection: that single value cannot be split, so it becomes a jumbo range that pins 30% of your data to one shard.
Monotonicity
Does the value always increase? _id (an ObjectId embeds a timestamp in its high-order bytes), createdAt, and any sequence counter are monotonically increasing. Every insert lands in the top range, which lives on exactly one shard, so 100% of your write traffic hits one primary while the rest of the cluster does nothing but receive migrations.
What each bad property actually looks like in production
Candidate keyFailsSymptom you will see
{ _id: 1 } (ObjectId)monotonicityOne shard at 95% CPU on writes, others near idle. The balancer runs constantly, moving ranges away from the hot shard, and the migration traffic makes it slower still.
{ createdAt: 1 }monotonicityIdentical, plus all recent-data reads also land on the same shard — the hot range is both the write target and the working set.
{ status: 1 }cardinalitySix values, six ranges, five of them jumbo. sh.status() shows the whole collection on two shards and the balancer refusing to move anything.
{ tenantId: 1 }frequencyFine for a year, then your biggest tenant outgrows the range size. A single unsplittable range, and a shard whose disk fills while the others are half empty.
{ userId: 'hashed' }nothing, if you never range-queryEven distribution, but every query by createdAt range becomes scatter-gather and zone sharding by region becomes impossible.
{ tenantId: 1, _id: 1 }—Usually the right answer for multi-tenant SaaS. See below.

Hashed versus ranged

A hashed shard key stores the hash of the field in the routing metadata, so consecutive values land on different shards. It solves monotonicity outright and is the standard escape hatch for _id. The price is that adjacency is destroyed: a query for a createdAt window has no contiguous range to target and must be broadcast everywhere. Hashed keys also rule out zone sharding on that field, and hashing a field that is missing from some documents hashes null for all of them — every one lands in the same range.

Ranged keys preserve locality: nearby key values live together, so a range query touches one or two shards — which is exactly what makes them vulnerable to monotonic inserts. The resolution is rarely to pick one or the other, but to make the prefix of a compound key non-monotonic and let the suffix supply splittability.

Compound keys and the tenantId-first pattern

For multi-tenant systems, { tenantId: 1, _id: 1 } is the workhorse. Because tenantId prefixes the key, every query that filters on it — which, if you have followed the tenancy chapter, is every query you issue — routes to a bounded set of shards. Small tenants live inside one range on one shard, so their queries are single-shard and their documents physically colocated. A tenant that outgrows a range is still splittable, because the _id suffix supplies split points inside it. Writes spread because tenants spread.

The failure case is one tenant that dominates the collection: inside it _id is monotonic, so its writes concentrate on its highest range. If you expect a whale, make the suffix non-monotonic — a stored hash of the document id, or a bucket field written at insert time.

javascript · mongosh: enabling sharding, with the traps annotated
// 1. The shard key MUST be backed by an index that starts with it.
//    Create it BEFORE sharding; on an empty collection shardCollection will
//    create it for you, on a populated one it will simply refuse.
db.orders.createIndex({ tenantId: 1, _id: 1 });

// 2. Shard it. The key is immutable from this moment on — refineCollectionShardKey
//    can APPEND suffix fields later, but it can never change or remove a prefix.
sh.enableSharding('app');
sh.shardCollection('app.orders', { tenantId: 1, _id: 1 });

// Hashed alternative, for a collection with no natural non-monotonic prefix.
// numInitialChunks pre-splits an EMPTY collection so the first hour of writes
// is already distributed — without it every insert goes to one shard until the
// balancer catches up, which on a launch-day import is the whole import.
sh.shardCollection('app.events', { deviceId: 'hashed' }, false, { numInitialChunks: 64 });

// 3. Verify distribution — do this a week after go-live, not on day one.
db.orders.getShardDistribution();
// Shard shard-a: 34.1% data, 33.9% docs, avg obj size 1.4 KB
// Shard shard-b: 33.2% ...
// A shard above ~45% in a three-shard cluster means the key is skewed.

// 4. Find jumbo ranges before they find you.
db.getSiblingDB('config').chunks.countDocuments({ jumbo: true });
sh.status(true);   // prints the offending range bounds

// TRAPS, in the order teams hit them:
// - Unique indexes are only supported on the shard key or a prefix of it.
//   You CANNOT enforce { email: 1 } unique on a sharded collection unless
//   email prefixes the shard key. _id uniqueness is guaranteed per shard only.
// - Every document must have the shard key field. A missing field is treated
//   as null and all such documents collapse into one range.
// - Changing a document's shard key value is possible in modern versions, but
//   only inside a transaction or retryable write, and never for _id.
Outage: the hot shard that a bigger cluster made worse

A team shards an events collection on { createdAt: 1 } to "spread the write load". Every insert has a createdAt of now, so every insert targets the single top range on a single shard. That primary saturates; the balancer notices the size imbalance and starts migrating ranges off it; the migrations compete with the writes for the same disk and the same cache. Write latency triples. The instinctive fix — add a fourth shard — makes it worse, because the new shard triggers another migration wave off the same overloaded primary. No configuration change recovers this: the exits are a full reshard or a dual-write migration into a new collection.

Targeted versus scatter-gather

This is what decides whether sharding helps you at all. A query carrying the shard key prefix is targeted: mongos consults the routing table, sends it to the one shard that can hold matches, and streams the result back at roughly unsharded latency. A query without it is scatter-gather: mongos broadcasts to every shard and waits for all of them, so its latency is the slowest shard's, not the average, and it burns a connection and a cursor on every shard. Add shards and scatter-gather queries get slower, because you have added more chances to be slow.

A sorted scatter-gather is worse than it looks. Each shard sorts its own matches and returns its top skip + limit documents; mongos merge-sorts those streams. So .sort({ createdAt: -1 }).skip(10000).limit(20) on six shards asks each for 10,020 sorted documents — 60,120 crossing the network to return 20. An aggregation with a blocking stage splits: the shard half runs everywhere, the merging half on a designated merging shard or on mongos, under a 100 MB limit before it spills or errors.

QueryRoutingCost on a 6-shard cluster
{ tenantId: 'T1', status: 'open' }targeted, 1 shard1 network hop, 1 index seek
{ tenantId: { $in: ['T1','T2'] } }targeted, ≤ 2 shardsparallel, latency = slower of two
{ status: 'open' }scatter-gather6 hops, 6 cursors, latency = slowest shard
{ status: 'open' } + sort + skip 10kscatter-gather + merge sort6 × 10,020 docs to return 20
countDocuments({})scatter-gatherfull broadcast; use $collStats for an estimate instead
javascript · proving a query is targeted, and keeping it that way
// explain() on a sharded cluster reports which shards were consulted.
// This is the single check that matters; run it on every new query.
const plan = await orders.find({ tenantId, status: 'open' })
  .sort({ _id: -1 }).limit(50)
  .explain('queryPlanner');

const shards = Object.keys(plan.queryPlanner.winningPlan.shards ?? {});
// Targeted   -> shards.length === 1  and  plan.queryPlanner.winningPlan.stage
//               is SINGLE_SHARD
// Broadcast  -> stage is SHARD_MERGE / SHARD_MERGE_SORT and shards.length is N

// Turn that into a test, not a habit. This runs in CI against a 2-shard
// docker cluster and fails the build when someone drops the tenant filter.
test('every hot-path query is single-shard', async () => {
  for (const q of HOT_PATH_QUERIES) {
    const p = await db.collection(q.coll).find(q.filter).explain('queryPlanner');
    const n = Object.keys(p.queryPlanner.winningPlan.shards ?? {}).length;
    expect(n, `${q.name} broadcast to ${n} shards`).toBe(1);
  }
});
javascript · the repository layer is where the shard key is enforced
// On a sharded cluster, "forgot the tenant filter" stops being a data-leak bug
// and becomes a performance bug as well: it broadcasts. Make it impossible to
// express, rather than reviewing for it.
export function orderRepo(db, tenantId) {
  if (!tenantId) throw new Error('tenantId is required: it is the shard key prefix');
  const coll = db.collection('orders');
  const scope = (filter = {}) => ({ ...filter, tenantId });   // always the prefix

  return {
    // Targeted: tenantId prefixes the shard key, so mongos routes to one shard.
    findOpen: (limit = 50) =>
      coll.find(scope({ status: 'open' })).sort({ _id: -1 }).limit(limit).toArray(),

    // Targeted UPDATE. On a sharded collection, updateOne/deleteOne/replaceOne
    // that cannot be routed used to be rejected outright; they are still far
    // more expensive, because they become a broadcast plus a two-phase write.
    close: (id) =>
      coll.updateOne(scope({ _id: id }), { $set: { status: 'closed' } }),

    // Inserts MUST carry the shard key. A document missing tenantId is not
    // rejected in a helpful way — it is filed under null, on one shard, forever.
    insert: (doc) => coll.insertOne({ ...doc, tenantId }),

    // Cross-shard transactions work, but they are a two-phase commit across
    // primaries with a 60-second default lifetime. Keep every transaction
    // inside one tenant, and it stays inside one shard.
    withinTenant: (fn) => fn(coll, scope),
  };
}
Interview follow-up

"How would you pick a shard key for a multi-tenant order system?" Said out loud: "First I would check whether we need to shard at all — working set against RAM, and write rate against one primary, because those are the only two triggers. If we do, { tenantId: 1, _id: 1 }. tenantId is high cardinality, non-monotonic and present in every query we issue, so reads stay single-shard; the _id suffix gives split points inside a tenant that outgrows a range. The risk is a whale tenant, where _id is monotonic again — so I would look at the tenant size distribution first and swap the suffix for a hashed field if the top tenant is more than a few percent of the collection. I would not hash tenantId itself, because that kills zone sharding, which we will want the first time a customer asks for EU residency."

Zones, for residency rather than performance

Zone sharding pins ranges of shard key values to named groups of shards. Its real use is not load balancing but regulation: if region prefixes the shard key, every EU customer's documents live only on shards physically in Frankfurt, and you can prove it. The constraint is that a zone range is expressed in shard key terms, so the field you want to pin must be in the key, near the front.

javascript · mongosh: data residency and a balancer window
// Shard key must lead with the field you intend to pin.
sh.shardCollection('app.customers', { region: 1, tenantId: 1 });

sh.addShardToZone('shard-fra-1', 'EU');
sh.addShardToZone('shard-fra-2', 'EU');
sh.addShardToZone('shard-iad-1', 'US');

// Ranges are half-open [min, max). MinKey/MaxKey cover the suffix entirely.
sh.updateZoneKeyRange('app.customers',
  { region: 'eu', tenantId: MinKey },
  { region: 'eu', tenantId: MaxKey }, 'EU');
sh.updateZoneKeyRange('app.customers',
  { region: 'us', tenantId: MinKey },
  { region: 'us', tenantId: MaxKey }, 'US');

// Confine migrations to a quiet window. Migrations are foreground work on both
// the donor and the recipient primary; running them during peak competes with
// user traffic for the same cache and the same disk.
db.getSiblingDB('config').settings.updateOne(
  { _id: 'balancer' },
  { $set: { activeWindow: { start: '01:00', stop: '05:00' } } },
  { upsert: true },
);
sh.getBalancerState();   // and sh.isBalancerRunning() to see live activity
A zone with nowhere to go

If a document's shard key falls outside every defined zone range, the balancer has no home for it and leaves it wherever it landed — possibly on a shard in the wrong jurisdiction. Define a catch-all zone and validate region at the API boundary with an enum. Equally, a range whose zone has no shards assigned is simply stranded: sh.status() reports it, and nothing alerts on it.

Resharding, and why it is still the last resort

Modern MongoDB can reshard a live collection with reshardCollection — a genuine improvement on the alternative, which was a dual-write migration you wrote yourself. It is not cheap. It builds a complete second copy of the collection under the new key while replicating ongoing writes into it, so you need roughly 1.2× the collection's size in free space on every shard and enough spare I/O to copy terabytes without starving production. It runs for hours or days. At the end comes a critical section — typically a couple of seconds — during which writes to that collection block while the routing metadata is swapped.

Check first whether refineCollectionShardKey is enough: it appends suffix fields to an existing key, fixing low cardinality and jumbo ranges without moving any data, provided the existing key stays a valid prefix. { tenantId: 1 } to { tenantId: 1, _id: 1 } is a refine. { createdAt: 1 } to anything is a reshard.

javascript · mongosh: refine first, reshard only if you must
// CHEAP: append a suffix. Requires an index on the full new key first.
// Fixes jumbo ranges and cardinality; does not move a single document.
db.orders.createIndex({ tenantId: 1, _id: 1 });
db.adminCommand({ refineCollectionShardKey: 'app.orders',
                  key: { tenantId: 1, _id: 1 } });

// EXPENSIVE: full rewrite under a new key. Budget days, not minutes.
db.adminCommand({ reshardCollection: 'app.events', key: { deviceId: 'hashed' } });

// Watch it. currentOp is the only honest progress indicator; the estimate in
// remainingOperationTimeEstimatedSecs is unreliable early on.
db.getSiblingDB('admin').aggregate([
  { $currentOp: { allUsers: true, localOps: false } },
  { $match: { type: 'op', 'originatingCommand.reshardCollection': { $exists: true } } },
  { $project: { shard: 1, totalCopyTimeElapsedSecs: 1, approxDocumentsToCopy: 1,
                documentsCopied: 1, remainingOperationTimeEstimatedSecs: 1 } },
]);

// Abort is safe up to the critical section and discards the temporary copy.
db.adminCommand({ abortReshardCollection: 'app.events' });

Operational reality

A sharded cluster costs more than the sum of its shards: three shards means three replica sets to patch, monitor, back up and page on, plus a config server replica set, plus routers. Connection counts multiply, because every mongos keeps a pool to every shard — app instances × routers × shards is the number that has to stay under the connection limit, and a fleet of 40 pods against 6 shards exhausts default pool settings long before it exhausts the database.

Backups are the part teams get wrong. A logical dump of the shards is not a backup: the config servers hold the metadata that makes those dumps mean anything, and the shards are not consistent with each other at any instant. A restorable backup is a coordinated snapshot of every shard and the config servers with the balancer stopped, or a managed backup product that does that coordination. Test the restore — a cluster you have never restored has backups you should assume do not work.

The four things to alert on

Per-shard data size skew (alert above ~20% over the mean); jumbo range count, which should be zero — alert on the first one; the ratio of scatter-gather to targeted operations, which drifts upward every time someone ships a query without the shard key; and balancer failures, which are silent by default and surface only in sh.status() and the config server log. Add config server replication lag: a lagging config primary makes routing metadata stale and produces intermittent StaleConfig retries cluster-wide.

Sharding buys write throughput and working-set capacity, and charges you in operational complexity, query freedom, and one decision you cannot take back. Pick the key with the tenancy model in front of you, prove the hot paths are single-shard with explain, and defend that property in CI. Everything else about a sharded cluster is recoverable.

31

Performance tuning & profiling

Tuning is a loop with a fixed order — is the box saturated, which queries cost the most, why is this one slow, did the fix work — and every step has an instrument that answers it. Guessing at indexes because a page "feels slow" is how teams end up with forty indexes and the same latency.

serverStatus is the BOX saturated? cache % full · application-thread evictions · tickets available │ if yes, no query fix helps until it is not ▼ system.profile which queries COST most? group by queryHash → Σ millis, not max millis │ ▼ explain("executionStats") why is THIS one slow? totalDocsExamined : nReturned · SORT stage · IXSCAN vs COLLSCAN │ ▼ fix, then re-profile same window, same load a fix you did not re-measure is a guess

The cache decides everything else

MongoDB with WiredTiger gives the storage engine a cache of max(50% of (RAM − 1 GB), 256 MB) by default; inside a container it reads the cgroup limit, not the host's RAM. The filesystem cache gets most of the rest. The number that matters is not the cache size but whether your working set — the documents and index pages actually touched by live traffic, not the whole database — fits inside it. When it does, reads are memory reads. When it does not, every query pulls pages off disk, those pages evict other pages, and the eviction victims are immediately needed again. Performance does not degrade gradually at that boundary; it falls off a cliff, and the query that trips over the edge is rarely the query that caused it.

javascript · mongosh: the four numbers that say whether the box is healthy
const s = db.serverStatus();
const c = s.wiredTiger.cache;

const used  = c['bytes currently in the cache'];
const max   = c['maximum bytes configured'];
const dirty = c['tracked dirty bytes in the cache'];

print(`cache      ${(used  / max * 100).toFixed(1)}%`);  // steady state should sit BELOW 80%
print(`dirty      ${(dirty / max * 100).toFixed(1)}%`);  // 5% is the eviction target, 20% and writes stall

// The single most useful counter in the whole of serverStatus. Eviction is supposed to be
// done by background workers. When the cache passes the 95% trigger, MongoDB conscripts the
// threads that are serving your queries into doing eviction instead. This number MUST be flat.
print(`app-evict  ${c['pages evicted by application threads']}`);

// Rate, not total: sample twice 10 s apart and take the delta. This is your disk read rate,
// and it is the primary saturation signal — a sustained non-zero delta means the working set
// no longer fits and you are paying an SSD round trip per page.
print(`read-in    ${c['pages read into cache']}`);

// Tickets: the storage engine admits a bounded number of concurrent operations. The field moved
// to queues.execution in 7.0, where the limit is also sized dynamically.
const q = s.queues?.execution ?? s.wiredTiger.concurrentTransactions;
printjson({ readOut: q.read.out, readAvail: q.read.available,
            writeOut: q.write.out, writeAvail: q.write.available });
// available reaching 0 means operations queue BEFORE they touch data. It is a symptom of slow
// operations holding tickets, never the cause — raising the limit makes the thrashing worse.
The cliff, as it actually presents

An index grows past the cache overnight. Nothing is deployed. In the morning, p50 is unchanged and p99 has gone from 40 ms to 3 s, pages read into cache is a straight diagonal line, and pages evicted by application threads — flat for six months — is climbing. Every query looks slow because every query is now doing storage work. Do not go index-hunting in this state: shrink the working set (projections, a TTL on cold data, an archive collection, dropping a redundant index) or add RAM. Then re-measure.

Cache fill
< 80%above the eviction target, query threads start evicting
docs : returned
≈ 1:110:1 means a missing or badly ordered index
Pool checkout p99
< 1 msanything more is queueing, not database time
slowms
50 msthe 100 ms default hides most API-latency offenders

The profiler finds the queries you did not suspect

Every engineer has a theory about which endpoint is slow, and the theory is usually about the endpoint they wrote most recently. The profiler settles it. Level 1 writes an entry to system.profile for any operation over slowms; the same threshold controls the Slow query lines in the mongod log, so if you cannot enable profiling you can parse durationMillis out of the logs instead and get most of the way there.

Then rank the workload. The instinct is to sort by the slowest single operation; that is almost always wrong. A 4 ms query executed 50 000 times a minute costs the cluster far more than a 3 s report run hourly, and it is the one whose index will change your p99.

javascript · enable profiling, then rank the real offenders
// Per-DATABASE and per-NODE, and it does NOT survive a restart unless you also set
// operationProfiling in mongod.conf. Enabling it on the primary tells you nothing about
// the secondary your reporting traffic reads from — enable it there too.
db.setProfilingLevel(1, { slowms: 50, sampleRate: 1.0 });

// system.profile is a CAPPED collection, 1 MB by default: roughly thirty seconds of traffic
// on a busy API, after which your evidence has already wrapped around. Resize it FIRST.
db.setProfilingLevel(0);
db.system.profile.drop();                                    // must be off before dropping
db.createCollection('system.profile', { capped: true, size: 256 * 1024 * 1024 });
db.setProfilingLevel(1, { slowms: 50 });

// On a very hot cluster, sample instead of raising slowms — you keep the tail visible.
db.setProfilingLevel(1, { slowms: 20, sampleRate: 0.1 });

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
db.system.profile.aggregate([
  { $match: {
      ts: { $gt: new Date(Date.now() - 3600e3) },
      op: { $in: ['query', 'command', 'getmore', 'update', 'remove'] },
      ns: { $not: /system\.profile/ },        // do not profile your own profiling
  } },
  { $group: {
      // queryHash groups shape-identical queries regardless of their literal values —
      // this is what turns 400 000 rows into a list of twelve query shapes.
      _id: { ns: '$ns', shape: '$queryHash', plan: '$planSummary' },
      n:        { $sum: 1 },
      totalMs:  { $sum: '$millis' },
      worstMs:  { $max: '$millis' },
      examRatio: { $avg: { $divide: [ { $ifNull: ['$docsExamined', 0] },
                                      { $max: [ { $ifNull: ['$nreturned', 1] }, 1 ] } ] } },
      blockingSorts: { $sum: { $cond: ['$hasSortStage', 1, 0] } },   // in-memory sort
      spilledToDisk: { $sum: { $cond: ['$usedDisk', 1, 0] } },       // blew the 100 MB limit
      replans:       { $sum: { $cond: ['$replanned', 1, 0] } },      // plan cache thrash
      sample:        { $last: '$command' },
  } },
  { $sort: { totalMs: -1 } },      // TOTAL cost, not worst case
  { $limit: 20 },
]);
Level 2 is not a debugging setting

setProfilingLevel(2) profiles every operation. On a collection taking 8 000 ops/s that is 8 000 extra writes per second into a capped collection on the same disk, and a 256 MB buffer that wraps in under a minute — you add load and still lose the evidence. It has taken production down. Use level 1 with a low slowms and a sampleRate, and turn it off when you are finished: db.setProfilingLevel(0) is part of the fix, not an optional tidy-up.

serverStatus
Is the machine saturated? Cache, eviction, tickets, connections. Answers "will tuning a query help at all".
system.profile
What did it cost, over a window? The only honest ranking of your workload.
$currentOp
What is running now, and how long has it been running? The only instrument that helps during an incident.
explain("executionStats")
Why is this one operation slow? Actually executes the query and reports what it touched.
$indexStats
Which indexes are being used, and which are pure write tax.

What is running right now, and killing it

javascript · find the runaway, then stop it
db.getSiblingDB('admin').aggregate([
  { $currentOp: { allUsers: true, idleConnections: false } },
  { $match: { active: true, secs_running: { $gte: 3 }, ns: /^shop\./ } },
  { $project: { opid: 1, secs_running: 1, op: 1, ns: 1, planSummary: 1, numYields: 1,
                appName: 1, client: 1, waitingForLock: 1, 'command.comment': 1 } },
  { $sort: { secs_running: -1 } },
]);
// This is why every aggregation in this book carries `comment` — without it you are looking at
// an anonymous 90-second COLLSCAN and cannot tell which service or job issued it.

db.adminCommand({ killOp: 1, op: 92814 });
// On a sharded cluster the opid is shard-prefixed and must be passed as a string:
db.adminCommand({ killOp: 1, op: 'shard02:92814' });
killOp is cooperative, maxTimeMS is prevention

killOp marks the operation as interrupted; it dies at its next yield point, which for a blocking $group may be seconds away, and it does not un-evict the cache pages it already trampled. It is an incident tool. The fix is that every query and pipeline carries maxTimeMS, so a runaway kills itself and the caller gets a MaxTimeMSExpired error it can turn into a 504 — see the chapters on error design and aggregation.

The loop, and the ratio that drives it

  1. Rank. Profiler over a representative hour. Take the top shape by totalMs, not the one someone complained about.
  2. Reproduce the shape. Copy the command from the profile entry verbatim — literal values matter, because plan choice depends on them.
  3. Explain it. .explain('executionStats') on the real node, with production data. Read totalDocsExamined, totalKeysExamined, nReturned, and the stage list.
  4. Compute the ratio. totalDocsExamined / nReturned should approach 1, and 0 for a covered query. 200 examined to return 20 is a wrong index; 200 000 to return 20 is no usable index at all.
  5. Change one thing. One index, one projection, one bound. Two changes at once and you learn nothing about either.
  6. Re-measure in the same window. Clear the profile collection, run the same traffic, compare totalMs for that shape. Then check the write side: an index you added is a cost paid on every insert and every update to that field.
Profiler symptom → cause → fix
What the entry showsCauseFix
planSummary: COLLSCAN, docsExamined ≈ collection countNo usable index for this predicateCreate it, then confirm with explain that the planner actually chose it
keysExamined ≫ nreturned, IXSCANCompound index in the wrong order — the selective field is not doing the workReorder to equality, sort, range (see the chapter on indexes and query plans)
hasSortStage: true, sometimes usedDisk: trueIn-memory blocking sort. Over 100 MB it fails outright with "Sort exceeded memory limit of 104857600 bytes, but did not opt in to external sorting"An index whose key order supplies the sort. allowDiskUse is a workaround, not a fix — it trades an error for spilling to disk
responseLength near 16 MB, huge nreturnedUnbounded result set: a filter with no limit, or a tenant that grewEnforce a hard limit and range-based pagination in the repository layer, not in the caller
High docsExamined, tiny responseLength40 KB documents fetched whole to serve two fieldsProjection, ideally covered by the index so no document is fetched at all
IXSCAN but keysExamined ≈ index sizeUnanchored or case-insensitive regex — /acme/ and /^acme/i both scan the whole index; only /^acme/ seeksAnchor the prefix, or store a normalised lowercase field, or use a collation-aware index / Atlas Search for real text matching
Slow despite an index, filter uses $ne, $nin, $notNegations are non-selective: the engine must scan everything the index contains to prove exclusionRewrite as a positive predicate (status: { $in: [...] }) or add a partial index over the rows you actually query
$in with thousands of valuesOne index seek per point, plus a command approaching the 16 MB limitChunk into batches of a few hundred, or fix the model so the ids do not have to be shipped back in
Slow update on a document with a large arrayWiredTiger rewrites the whole document. A 6 MB document appended to once per event costs 6 MB of write and cache churn per appendBucket the array into bounded sub-documents, or split into a child collection
replanned: true recurring on one shapePlan cache thrash — two indexes are close enough that the winner flipsDrop the redundant index, or pin with hint and document why

The connection pool is part of query latency

Application-side latency and database-side latency are different numbers, and the gap between them is the pool. If the driver's maxPoolSize is 100 and 140 requests want a connection, forty of them are waiting before MongoDB has seen a byte — the profiler shows a healthy 3 ms operation while your route handler reports 900 ms. Measure checkout time separately or you will spend a week tuning indexes that were never the problem.

javascript · instrumenting checkout, the metric nobody exports by default
const client = new MongoClient(uri, {
  maxPoolSize: 100,          // per MongoClient, per process. With 8 cluster workers that is 800
  minPoolSize: 10,           // pre-warm: connection setup + TLS + auth is 30–80 ms you do not want on p99
  maxIdleTimeMS: 60_000,
  waitQueueTimeoutMS: 5_000, // fail fast rather than pile up — an unbounded wait queue IS the outage
});

let queued = 0;
client.on('connectionCheckOutStarted', () => poolQueue.set(++queued));
client.on('connectionCheckedOut', (e) => {
  poolQueue.set(--queued);
  // durationMS is provided by Node driver 6.7+; on older drivers, the queue gauge above is
  // the signal — sustained depth > 0 means the pool, not the database, is your bottleneck.
  if (e.durationMS !== undefined) checkoutSeconds.observe(e.durationMS / 1000);
});
client.on('connectionCheckOutFailed', (e) => { poolQueue.set(--queued); poolTimeouts.inc({ reason: e.reason }); });

// The error you will actually see in the log, and what it means:
//   "Timed out while checking out a connection from connection pool: maxPoolSize: 100,
//    connections in use by cursors: 0, connections in use by transactions: 0,
//    connections in use by other operations: 100"
// "in use by cursors" being high means someone is holding cursors open across awaits —
// an unclosed cursor holds its connection for its entire lifetime.

Fetch less, and stop paying for round trips

javascript · projections, batches, and bulk writes
// Projection is a cache-pressure decision, not a tidiness one. Every field you do not project
// is still read from the page, serialised to BSON, sent over the wire, parsed by the driver
// and allocated in the V8 heap.
const cursor = coll.find(
  { tenantId, status: 'placed' },
  { projection: { _id: 0, sku: 1, 'totals.totalCents': 1 } },
).batchSize(1000).maxTimeMS(10_000);
// Cursor mechanics: the first batch is 101 documents, subsequent batches fill up to 16 MB.
// Raising batchSize cuts getMore round trips on a large export; LOWERING it bounds how much
// arrives in one chunk when documents are fat and Node's heap is the constraint.

// ❌ N round trips. At 0.8 ms RTT inside one availability zone, 5 000 updates is four seconds
//    of pure waiting, and 5 000 sequential pool checkouts.
for (const row of rows) await coll.updateOne({ _id: row.id }, { $set: { price: row.price } });

// ✅ One round trip per 1 000 operations. Typically 20–50× faster for the same work.
for (const chunk of chunks(rows, 1000)) {
  try {
    await coll.bulkWrite(chunk.map(r => ({
      updateOne: { filter: { _id: r.id }, update: { $set: { price: r.price } } },
    })), { ordered: false });   // unordered: one duplicate key does not abandon the other 999
  } catch (err) {
    // THE TRAP: an unordered bulkWrite still THROWS at the end if any write failed, and the
    // successful writes are already committed. Swallowing this loses data silently.
    if (err.name !== 'MongoBulkWriteError') throw err;
    log.warn({ failed: err.writeErrors.length, ok: err.result.nModified }, 'partial bulk write');
  }
}

Atlas Performance Advisor mines the same slow-query data and proposes indexes, and it is a genuinely good first pass — it will spot the shape you never looked at. Treat every suggestion as a hypothesis. It reasons per query shape, so it happily recommends {a:1,b:1} when {a:1,b:1,c:1} already serves it as a prefix; it does not know what the write path costs, it does not know which of those queries runs eleven times a day, and it cannot see the index you are about to add for a different endpoint. Run explain before and after, check $indexStats a week later, and drop it if nothing used it.

Benchmarking honestly

Most benchmarks in pull request descriptions are wrong in the same four ways, and all four flatter the change.

Cold versus warm cache

The first run pages the working set in from disk; the second reads memory. Ten times faster is often just the second run. Report both, or run five and discard the first — and say which you did.

Volume

A 1 000-document collection fits in a handful of pages. A collection scan wins, the planner may legitimately choose one, and your index proves nothing. Benchmark against production-order-of-magnitude data or do not claim a result.

Distribution

Uniformly random test data makes every index look selective. Production has one tenant holding 40% of the rows and a status field that is 'placed' 92% of the time. Generate skew deliberately.

Concurrency

A single-threaded loop measures latency at concurrency one, where tickets, eviction and the pool are all invisible. Drive a fixed arrival rate, not a closed loop that throttles itself when the system slows, and read p99 rather than the mean.

One more discipline: change one variable and keep the query shape identical between runs, because the plan cache is state. A benchmark whose first iteration pays for multi-planning and whose remaining iterations use the cached plan is measuring two different systems and averaging them.

Interview follow-up

"A page has got slow. Walk me through it." The weak answer starts adding indexes. The strong answer states the order and why: first confirm it is the database at all by comparing application-side timing against the driver's command duration and the pool checkout gauge; then check whether the box is saturated, because if the working set has outgrown the cache then every query is slow and tuning one is pointless; then rank by profiler over a window, sorting by total time so a hot cheap query beats a rare expensive one; then explain the top shape and look at documents examined per document returned; then change exactly one thing and re-measure the same window, including the write-side cost of any index added. Finish with the honest caveat: "and I would check whether the slow query is even on the critical path — a 300 ms aggregation running behind a cache with a 99% hit rate is not the problem."

32

Backups & disaster recovery

Backups are not a storage problem, they are a restore problem. Every decision below — method, frequency, retention, region, permissions — follows mechanically from two numbers you must agree with the business before you buy anything.

Start with RPO and RTO, in numbers

RPO — recovery point objective
How much data you are allowed to lose, measured in time. It sets your backup frequency and whether you need continuous oplog capture at all. A nightly dump is an RPO of 24 hours, whatever the slide deck says.
RTO — recovery time objective
How long you are allowed to be down, measured from the decision to restore to serving traffic again. It sets your restore method and, far more than anything else, how often you rehearse.
Actual RPO / RTO
What your last drill measured. Until you have a measurement, both numbers are aspirations, and aspirations do not survive an outage.

Write them per data class rather than per cluster. The orders collection and the ingest-metrics collection do not deserve the same money. A typical SaaS settles on something like: financial and customer data at RPO 5 minutes / RTO 1 hour; derived analytics at RPO 24 hours / RTO 1 day, because it can be recomputed; session and cache data at no backup at all, because restoring it is worse than losing it.

RPO target
5 mindrives capture frequency — only continuous oplog gets you here
RTO target
60 mindrives restore method and rehearsal cadence, not backup tooling
Oplog window
≥ 48 hbelow this, point-in-time recovery between snapshots is impossible
Restore drill
monthlyan unrehearsed restore has an RTO of "unknown"

The four mechanisms, and what each one actually gives you

Pick by consistency guarantee and restore speed, not by convenience of the command line.
MethodConsistencyPractical RPORestore costUse for
mongodump / mongorestorefuzzy across collections unless --oplog; never consistent across shardsthe dump intervalslowest — index rebuild dominatesdata sets under ~100 GB, extracting one collection, moving between major versions
Volume / filesystem snapshotcrash-consistent per member if the journal shares the volume; otherwise needs fsync+lock1–24 hminutes to attach, then lazily hydratedthe default above roughly 200 GB
Continuous oplog capture (Atlas, Ops Manager, Percona Backup for MongoDB)an exact instant, cluster-wide, including shardedseconds to ~1 minsnapshot restore + replay timeanything where the RPO is measured in minutes
Delayed replica memberlive, exactly N seconds behindn/a — it is not a backupfastest of all: copy one collection out, or promotehuman error only; it dies with the cluster and with a region

Logical dumps

mongodump walks documents through the WiredTiger cache, so running it against the primary evicts your working set and the application gets slower for the whole duration. Point it at a hidden secondary. The other trap is that a dump is not an instant: collections are read one after another, so without --oplog you get orders from 02:00 and payments from 02:41, which reconcile to nothing.

bash · a logical backup that is actually point-in-time
# --oplog tails the oplog for the duration of the dump and writes oplog.bson beside
# the data, so the restore can roll everything forward to ONE instant: the end of
# the dump. Without it the archive is a fuzzy snapshot and cross-collection
# invariants (order ⇄ payment ⇄ ledger) will not hold.
mongodump \
  --uri="mongodb+srv://backup@cluster.example/?readPreference=secondary" \
  --oplog \                                  # NOT valid with --db/--collection: full dump only
  --gzip \
  --numParallelCollections=4 \
  --archive=/backup/app-$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).archive.gz

# Restore. --oplogReplay applies oplog.bson at the end; omitting it silently
# gives you the fuzzy version, with no warning and no error.
mongorestore \
  --uri="mongodb://scratch-cluster:27017" \
  --archive=/backup/app-20260904T020000Z.archive.gz --gzip \
  --oplogReplay \
  --numInsertionWorkersPerCollection=4 \
  --noIndexRestore          # land the data first, then build indexes in parallel

# Restore wall-clock is dominated by index builds, not by inserts. A 400 GB dump
# that takes 3 h to produce routinely takes 9–12 h to restore. That number, not the
# dump duration, is the one that belongs in your RTO.
mongodump against mongos is not a cluster backup

On a sharded cluster --oplog is unsupported through mongos, and per-shard dumps are taken at different instants while the balancer may be migrating chunks between them. The result can contain a document twice, or not at all. Stop the balancer and use a coordinated tool (Ops Manager, Atlas, PBM) — or accept that you have no consistent backup of a sharded cluster.

Snapshots

bash · volume snapshots, and when you must freeze
# Case 1 — data files and journal on the SAME volume. A snapshot is crash-consistent
# and WiredTiger replays the journal on start-up. Nothing to freeze, no write stall.
aws ec2 create-snapshot --volume-id vol-0abc --description "mongo-sec-2 $(date -u +%FT%TZ)"

# Case 2 — journal on a separate volume, or an LVM set spanning volumes. The
# per-volume snapshots are taken at different instants and do NOT reconcile.
mongosh --host mongo-sec-2 --eval 'db.adminCommand({ fsync: 1, lock: true })'
#   ... snapshot every volume here. Seconds, not minutes. ...
mongosh --host mongo-sec-2 --eval 'db.adminCommand({ fsyncUnlock: 1 })'

# Rules that are not optional:
#  * Snapshot a HIDDEN SECONDARY. fsyncLock on a primary stalls every write in the
#    application; the API sees connection-pool exhaustion, not a database error.
#  * fsyncLock also halts replication on that member. Hold it long enough and the
#    member falls off the primary's oplog window and needs a full initial sync.
#  * Sharded: sh.stopBalancer() first, or a half-migrated chunk lands in the snapshot.
A restored EBS volume is not a warm volume

Snapshot-backed volumes hydrate lazily from S3: the first read of every block pays a round trip to object storage. A cluster restored in four minutes can then run at a fraction of its normal throughput for an hour, which is exactly the hour you cannot afford. Either pre-warm (fio or dd across the device), pay for Fast Snapshot Restore, or measure warm-up as part of RTO. A drill that stops at "mongod started" is measuring the wrong thing.

The delayed member

A hidden, non-voting, priority-0 secondary configured with secondaryDelaySecs: 3600 (called slaveDelay before 5.0) is the cheapest insurance against human error that exists. When someone drops a collection, that member still has it — for another hour. It costs one node and recovers a mistake in minutes rather than hours. It is not a backup: it replicates corruption faithfully once the delay elapses, it dies with the cluster and with the region, and it cannot serve compliance retention. Keep it alongside real backups, never instead of them — and note that the primary's oplog must span the delay plus a normal replication buffer, so a one-hour delayed member on a 90-minute oplog window will need a full resync the first time write traffic spikes.

Point-in-time recovery, mechanically

snapshot S bad op now timeline ───────●──────────────────────────────────────●──────────────▶ │ 02:00:00 │ 14:22:07 │ │ local.oplog.rs │ [ insert ][ update ][ update ][ … ] │ [ drop ][ … ] │ └──────────── replay these ──────┘ ▲ │ └─ --oplogLimit is exclusive ▼ restore S into a scratch cluster ──▶ mongorestore --oplogReplay --oplogLimit=1772719327:3

Every managed PITR product is this loop with a UI on top: a base snapshot, plus the oplog from that snapshot forward, replayed up to but not including the operation you regret. Doing it by hand is worth knowing because the managed product will one day not cover the namespace you need, or the restore window will have expired by twelve minutes.

bash · hand-rolled PITR after a dropped collection
# 1. Find the exact operation. A drop is a command op against the <db>.$cmd namespace.
mongosh --quiet --eval '
  db.getSiblingDB("local").oplog.rs
    .find({ ns: "app.$cmd", "o.drop": "orders" }, { ts: 1, o: 1, wall: 1 })
    .sort({ ts: -1 }).limit(5).toArray()'
# => { ts: Timestamp({ t: 1772719327, i: 3 }), o: { drop: "orders" }, wall: ISODate(...) }

# 2. Confirm the oplog still reaches back past the snapshot you intend to use.
#    If it does not, PITR is off the table and your RPO is the snapshot interval.
mongosh --quiet --eval 'rs.printReplicationInfo()'

# 3. Export the oplog segment covering [snapshot .. incident]. mongorestore expects
#    the file to be named oplog.bson in the directory you hand to --oplogReplay.
mongodump --uri="mongodb://mongo-sec-2:27017" --db=local --collection=oplog.rs \
  --query='{ "ts": { "$gte": { "$timestamp": { "t": 1772700000, "i": 1 } } } }' \
  --out=/restore/seg
mkdir -p /restore/replay && mv /restore/seg/local/oplog.rs.bson /restore/replay/oplog.bson

# 4. Restore the snapshot into a SCRATCH cluster, then roll forward to one op before
#    the drop. --oplogLimit is exclusive: the entry AT this timestamp is not applied.
mongorestore --uri="mongodb://scratch:27017" --oplogReplay \
  --oplogLimit="1772719327:3" /restore/replay

# 5. Move only what is missing back, into a shadow namespace first. Never --drop a
#    live collection during an incident; a mistake here is unrecoverable.
mongodump --uri="mongodb://scratch:27017" --db=app --collection=orders --archive=/restore/orders.bson
mongorestore --uri="mongodb://prod:27017" --archive=/restore/orders.bson \
  --nsFrom='app.orders' --nsTo='app.orders_restored'
Your PITR window is the oplog window, not the retention setting

Snapshots every 24 hours with a 6-hour oplog means there is no recoverable point between 06:00 and the next snapshot — an 18-hour hole nobody notices until the incident. Size the oplog (replSetResizeOplog) so rs.printReplicationInfo() reports at least twice your snapshot interval, alert when the window drops below 48 hours, and re-check it after any change that raises write volume: a bulk migration can compress a two-day oplog window into twenty minutes.

A backup you have never restored is not a backup

The failure mode is boring and near-universal: the cron job exits 0 every night for fourteen months, the dashboard is green, and the artefact is a 4 KB file containing an authentication error, or a dump of an empty database because a role change removed find on one collection. Monitoring the job tells you nothing. Monitor the artefact and the restore.

bash · the weekly restore drill, run in CI, fails loudly
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
start=$(date +%s)

# Choose the artefact the way an on-call engineer would at 03:00 — newest, by listing.
latest=$(aws s3 ls s3://acme-mongo-backups/prod/ | sort | tail -1 | awk '{print $4}')
aws s3 cp "s3://acme-mongo-backups/prod/$latest" /scratch/dump.archive.gz

# Restore into a throwaway cluster whose URI cannot be confused with production.
mongorestore --uri="mongodb://drill:27017" --archive=/scratch/dump.archive.gz \
  --gzip --oplogReplay --drop

# Assert the restore is USABLE, not merely present. Row counts, index count, and one
# business-level invariant. The newest document's timestamp is your ACTUAL RPO.
mongosh "mongodb://drill:27017/app" --quiet --eval '
  const n = db.orders.countDocuments({});
  if (n < 1000000) { print("FAIL orders=" + n); quit(1); }
  if (db.orders.getIndexes().length < 7) { print("FAIL missing indexes"); quit(1); }
  const newest = db.orders.find().sort({ createdAt: -1 }).limit(1).next().createdAt;
  print("measured_rpo_seconds " + Math.round((Date.now() - newest.getTime()) / 1000));'

# Publish the measured RTO. If this number drifts above the SLO, that is a defect
# with a ticket, not a nice-to-have.
echo "restore_drill_seconds $(( $(date +%s) - start ))" \
  | curl --data-binary @- http://pushgateway:9091/metrics/job/restore_drill

Run the full-cluster version quarterly with a human doing it from the runbook and a stopwatch running, because the parts that make restores slow are never the commands: finding who has the credentials, discovering that the KMS key is in the account that was compromised, waiting on a support ticket to raise a quota, and arguing about whether to restore at all.

Retention, regions and residency

A tiered policy: each tier answers a different question, and each has a different cost driver.
TierKeptWhereAnswersCost driver
Delayed member1 h rollingsame cluster"undo the last hour of human error"one extra node
Oplog / PITR window72 hbackup account, same region"restore to any second yesterday"oplog volume × write churn
Daily snapshot35 daysbackup account, object-locked"the corruption started three weeks ago"changed blocks per day
Monthly snapshot13 monthssecond region"we lost the region"full size × copies
Annual / legal hold7 yearsarchive class, compliance lockaudit, litigation holdcheap to store, hours to restore

Cross-region copies are what make the difference between a bad day and a company-ending one, and they are also where compliance bites. If a tenant's contract or GDPR posture says their data stays in the EU, a snapshot replicated to us-east-1 is a personal-data transfer, and so is the encryption key if it lives in the wrong region's KMS. Pick the DR region inside the same residency boundary — eu-west-1 to eu-central-1 — and make the residency tag a property of the backup pipeline, not a note in a spreadsheet. If you have per-tenant residency, you need per-residency backup destinations, which is an argument for splitting the cluster by region rather than by tenant.

Protecting the backups themselves

Modern ransomware does not encrypt your database. It waits, finds the credentials your application already has, deletes the backups first, and only then encrypts. If one compromised set of credentials can both read production and delete the backup bucket, you have one copy of your data.

  • Separate account, separate blast radius. Backups land in a different cloud account or subscription. The production role can PutObject and nothing else — no DeleteObject, no PutBucketPolicy, no lifecycle changes. Restores use a different, human-approved role.
  • Immutability. S3 Object Lock in compliance mode (or the equivalent immutable-blob policy) for the retention period. Compliance mode means the root account cannot delete it either, which is the entire point; governance mode is a suggestion.
  • Encryption, plus key survivability. Encrypt at rest with a customer-managed key — and back the key arrangement up somewhere the incident cannot reach. A backup you cannot decrypt is not a backup, and a KMS key scheduled for deletion in the compromised account takes every snapshot with it.
  • Least privilege inside Mongo. The application's database user has no business holding dropCollection or dropDatabase. Grant migrations a separate role, used only by the migration runner. Most "someone dropped a collection" incidents are a permission defect first and a human error second.
  • Alert on absence and on shape. Alert when no artefact appeared in the expected window, and when today's size deviates more than ~20% from the trailing median. Both catch the silent-empty-dump failure that job-exit-code monitoring never will.

The application-level layer

Most data loss is not a cluster failure; it is one tenant's records removed by a bug or a support action. Restoring a 600 GB cluster to recover forty documents is absurd, so build the cheap recovery path into the data model: never hard-delete on the request path.

javascript · soft delete with a TTL grace period, and the audit trail
// Deletion is a state change. Nothing in the API layer ever calls deleteOne().
const now = new Date();
await orders.updateOne(
  { _id, tenantId, deletedAt: null },
  { $set: {
      deletedAt: now,
      deletedBy: actor.id,
      // Jitter the purge so a bulk delete does not expire 40 M documents in one
      // minute — TTL deletes replicate as ordinary deletes and will otherwise
      // shred your oplog window, and with it your PITR capability.
      purgeAt: new Date(now.getTime() + 30 * 864e5 + Math.random() * 36e5),
  } },
);

// expireAfterSeconds: 0 means "expire at the date stored in this field". Documents
// with no purgeAt are never touched, which is what makes the grace period opt-in.
await orders.createIndex({ purgeAt: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 0, name: 'ttl_purge' });

// The TTL monitor wakes roughly every 60 s and deletes in batches, on the primary
// only. Expiry is "some time after", never "at" — no business rule may depend on it.

// Every read path must exclude soft-deleted rows. Put it in the repository, once,
// and make the indexes partial so the tombstones cost nothing.
await orders.createIndex(
  { tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 },
  { partialFilterExpression: { deletedAt: null }, name: 'tenant_status_created_live' },
);

// The audit record is written in the same transaction as the change, and the
// audit collection's role has insert only — no update, no remove.
await audit.insertOne({
  at: now, actor: actor.id, tenantId, action: 'order.delete',
  ns: 'app.orders', docId: _id,
  before: previous,          // enough to reconstruct without a database restore
  requestId, ip: actor.ip,
}, { session });

The third application-level source is the outbox. If you already publish domain events transactionally (see the chapter on the outbox pattern) and retain them for longer than your incident-detection time, then rebuilding a downstream projection after a bad migration is a replay, not a restore: re-read the outbox from the last good offset and re-apply. This only holds if the events carry enough state to be replayed and the consumers are idempotent — which is a design decision you make long before the incident, not during it.

Rename, do not drop, in migrations

A migration that must remove a collection should renameCollection it to orders_deprecated_20260904 and schedule the real drop for two weeks later. The rename is instant, reversible, and costs nothing; the drop is instant and irreversible. The same applies to destructive field updates: write the new field, ship, verify, and only then remove the old one in a separate release.

Runbook: someone dropped a collection in production

  1. Stop the bleeding before you restore. Note the wall-clock time. Disable the write paths that touch the namespace (feature flag, or revoke write on that collection) so recovery does not race new inserts and so nobody re-creates the collection empty. A collection that a driver has auto-created is harder to reason about than one that is absent.
  2. Establish the exact instant. Find the operation in local.oplog.rs and record its ts. Every subsequent decision references that timestamp. In the same breath run rs.printReplicationInfo() — if the oplog no longer reaches your last snapshot, the options narrow immediately and you need to know now, not in an hour.
  3. Try the cheapest source first. Delayed member: if the delay exceeds the elapsed time, the collection is still there — copy it out immediately, because that window is closing in real time. Then managed PITR. Then snapshot plus oplog replay. Work down that list; do not start with the slowest option because it is the one in the wiki.
  4. Restore into scratch, never into production. A restore command aimed at the production URI, because someone copied a line from the runbook and forgot to change the host, has turned a recoverable incident into an unrecoverable one more than once. The scratch cluster's hostname should be visibly different.
  5. Verify in scratch before touching production. Document count against yesterday's metric, index count, newest document timestamp, and one known business record fetched by id. Announce the measured gap: "we can recover to 14:21:58, which loses roughly nine minutes of writes."
  6. Merge back, additively. Restore into a shadow namespace, then insert only the missing ids, or rename into place if the collection is genuinely empty. Do not use --drop against a live namespace.
  7. Reconcile the gap explicitly. Writes between the recovery point and now are replayed from the outbox, the audit log, or the upstream producer — or they are declared lost, in writing, to whoever owns the data. An unstated gap becomes a mystery bug next quarter.
  8. Close the permission hole in the same week. Remove dropCollection from the application role, record the measured RTO against the target, and fix the SLO document if the target was fiction.
Interview follow-up

"How do you back up MongoDB?" The weak answer names a tool. The strong one starts with the numbers: "RPO and RTO first, per data class, because they decide everything else. For a five-minute RPO I need continuous oplog capture, not nightly dumps — so snapshots of a hidden secondary every six hours, plus oplog to give point-in-time recovery to the second, with the oplog window alerted at twice the snapshot interval. Copies go to a second region inside the same residency boundary, into a separate account with object-lock so a compromised production credential cannot delete them. I keep a one-hour delayed member as well, because most real incidents are human error and that recovers in minutes rather than hours. And the part that matters most: an automated weekly restore into a scratch cluster that asserts row counts and indexes and publishes the measured RTO as a metric — because an untested backup has an unknown restore time, and unknown is not a number you can put in an SLO."

33

Database security

Almost every MongoDB breach on record is a configuration failure, not an exploit: a port reachable from the internet, a role granted far too widely, a production password sitting in a developer's .env. Security here is five layers, and each one is designed on the assumption that the layer above it has already failed.

the internet │ │ 0.0.0.0:27017 with authorization off — how every ransom note starts ▼ 1 · network private subnet, security group, private endpoint. No route from outside. 2 · authN SCRAM-SHA-256, x.509 or MONGODB-AWS — who are you 3 · authZ one least-privilege role per service — what may you touch 4 · crypto requireTLS in transit, encrypted volumes, field-level for the crown jewels 5 · audit immutable record of auth failures, role grants and DDL ▼ documents Layer 3 exists because layer 2 leaks. Layer 5 exists because layers 1-4 all eventually fail, and the only question left is how long it takes you to notice.

Layer 1 — the port that must never be reachable

Unauthenticated, internet-exposed MongoDB is one of the most exploited misconfigurations in the history of the internet. In the wave that began in January 2017, tens of thousands of instances were emptied within days and replaced with a single document demanding Bitcoin; the 2020 “Meow” campaign wiped thousands more without even asking for money. Scanning the entire IPv4 space for port 27017 takes minutes with off-the-shelf tooling, so a freshly exposed instance is typically found within the hour. There is no scenario in which the database tier needs a public IP.

Two defaults conspire against you. bindIp has defaulted to 127.0.0.1 since MongoDB 3.6, but container images, Helm charts and quick fixes routinely set it to 0.0.0.0. And security.authorization is still off by default in a self-managed mongod — a fresh instance with no users accepts every connection as an unauthenticated superuser. That is not a bug; it is the localhost exception that lets you create the first administrator. It becomes a breach the moment the bind address changes.

The outage that has no recovery path

The classic incident is not a clever attack. It is a “temporary” analytics replica spun up on a public subnet with a permissive security group and no --auth, discovered by a scanner, dropped, and ransomed. It is temporary, so it is not in Terraform, not in the backup policy, and not on the monitoring dashboard. If your recovery plan for any database is “restore from backup”, prove the backup exists and restores — for the temporary instances too.
