  • Data-tier subnets have no route to an internet gateway. Outbound patching goes through a NAT gateway; inbound goes nowhere.
  • Security groups reference the application's security group, not a CIDR. A CIDR outlives the instance that justified it; a group reference does not.
  • For Atlas, use PrivateLink or VPC peering, and keep the IP access list to bastion and CI egress addresses. 0.0.0.0/0 in an Atlas access list is exactly as bad as a public mongod, because the credential is now the only control left.
  • Temporary access gets an expiry, not a reminder. Atlas access-list entries accept a deleteAfterDate; use it for every human.
yaml · mongod.conf, the parts that matter
net:
  port: 27017
  bindIp: 10.20.4.11,127.0.0.1        # the private NIC only. Never 0.0.0.0.
  tls:
    mode: requireTLS                  # NOT preferTLS — preferTLS still accepts plaintext,
                                      # so one misconfigured client silently downgrades
    certificateKeyFile: /etc/ssl/mongod.pem
    CAFile: /etc/ssl/ca.pem
    disabledProtocols: TLS1_0,TLS1_1

security:
  authorization: enabled              # OFF by default in a self-managed mongod
  clusterAuthMode: x509               # replica-set members authenticate with certificates,
                                      # not a shared keyfile that lives forever in git
  javascriptEnabled: false            # kills $where, $function, $accumulator and mapReduce
                                      # server-side — a defence the app layer cannot undo
  enableEncryption: true              # WiredTiger encryption at rest (Enterprise)
  kmip:
    serverName: kmip.internal.example
    clientCertificateFile: /etc/ssl/kmip-client.pem

setParameter:
  authenticationMechanisms: SCRAM-SHA-256   # drop SCRAM-SHA-1 once every client supports 256
  enableLocalhostAuthBypass: false          # close the door once the first admin exists

auditLog:
  destination: file
  format: JSON
  path: /var/log/mongodb/audit.json
  filter: '{ "atype": { "$ne": "authCheck" } }'   # placeholder — the real filter is below

Layer 2 — authentication, and how to stop shipping passwords

A password is a secret you now have to store, distribute, rotate and eventually explain to an auditor. The mechanisms that have no shared secret at all are strictly better, and both are boring to operate once configured.

Choosing a mechanism
MechanismWhat the client presentsUse it when
SCRAM-SHA-256Username and password, salted and challenged — the password never crosses the wireThe default. Fine, provided the password comes from a secrets manager and is rotated.
MONGODB-X509A client certificate; the subject DN is the username, in the $external databaseSelf-managed clusters with an internal CA. Short-lived certs make rotation automatic.
MONGODB-AWSA signed request derived from the pod or instance IAM roleAtlas on AWS. No credential exists to leak; the driver refreshes the temporary token itself.
MONGODB-OIDCA workforce or workload OIDC tokenHuman access through your existing IdP, with MFA and group mapping already solved.
PLAIN (LDAP)Username and password proxied to LDAP — in cleartext over the connectionOnly with requireTLS, and only when central directory management is a hard requirement.
javascript · one client, three ways to prove who you are
import { MongoClient } from 'mongodb';

// 1. SCRAM. Note the credential is NOT in the URI: connection strings end up in
//    logs, in error messages, in APM spans and in `ps` output. Pass them separately.
const scramClient = new MongoClient(
  'mongodb+srv://shop.abcd.mongodb.net/?authSource=admin&retryWrites=true&w=majority',
  {
    auth: { username: 'svc-orders-prod', password: await secrets.get('mongo/orders') },
    tls: true,
    serverSelectionTimeoutMS: 5_000,
  },
);
// Trap if you DO embed it: a password containing @ / : or ? breaks URI parsing with
// `MongoParseError: Password contains unescaped characters`, and the usual "fix" is
// someone choosing a weaker password rather than calling encodeURIComponent().

// 2. x.509 — no password anywhere. The username is the certificate subject DN.
const x509Client = new MongoClient(
  'mongodb://mongo-0.svc.internal:27017/?authMechanism=MONGODB-X509&authSource=%24external',
  { tls: true, tlsCertificateKeyFile: '/run/secrets/client.pem', tlsCAFile: '/run/secrets/ca.pem' },
);
// %24external, not $external: the moment that URI passes through a shell, a compose
// file or an entrypoint script, a bare $ is expanded to the empty string and you get
// `MongoServerError: Authentication failed.` with no hint as to why.

// 3. IAM — the strongest option on Atlas. Nothing to store, nothing to rotate.
const iamClient = new MongoClient(
  'mongodb+srv://shop.abcd.mongodb.net/?authSource=%24external&authMechanism=MONGODB-AWS',
  { tls: true },   // credentials come from the pod's IRSA role / instance metadata
);
The two-character security incident

A certificate rotation goes wrong and every pod starts failing with MongoServerSelectionError: unable to verify the first certificate. Under pressure, someone appends &tlsAllowInvalidCertificates=true — or worse, tlsInsecure=true — the deploy goes green, and the change never gets reverted. TLS is now decorative: any host that can win a DNS or routing race reads and rewrites your traffic. Ban both options in CI by grepping the rendered config, and fix the actual problem, which is almost always a missing intermediate in the bundle or a stale tlsCAFile.

Layer 3 — authorisation: the app user must not be able to drop a collection

Built-in roles are convenience wrappers, and the one everybody reaches for is wider than its name suggests.

RoleWhat it really grantsVerdict for an app user
readfind, listIndexes, listCollections, cursor management on one databaseGood for reporting and analytics users.
readWriteread, plus insert/update/remove — and createCollection, dropCollection, createIndex, dropIndexNot least privilege. Your API can delete a collection.
dbAdminIndex and schema administration, profiling, dropDatabaseMigration jobs only, never the API.
userAdminCreate users and roles in that databaseRoot-equivalent: the holder can grant themselves anything. Treat it as such.
readWriteAnyDatabasereadWrite on every database on the clusterEvery service reads every other service's data. The usual cause of a “small” breach becoming total.
clusterAdmin / rootReplica-set reconfiguration, shutdown, everythingBreak-glass only, with an alarm on use.

The distinction that matters at 3 a.m.: with readWrite, a migration script pointed at the wrong environment, or an injected command that reaches the driver, can execute drop. With a properly scoped custom role, the same code produces MongoServerError: not authorized on shop to execute command { drop: "orders" } and an alert, and nothing is lost. That error line is the entire return on this section.

javascript · mongosh — a role the application can live inside
// Roles are created on `admin` so they can be granted anywhere; the PRIVILEGES below
// are still pinned to one database and to named collections.
db.getSiblingDB('admin').createRole({
  role: 'app_shop_rw',
  privileges: [
    { resource: { db: 'shop', collection: 'orders' },
      actions: ['find','insert','update','remove','listIndexes','collStats','changeStream'] },
    { resource: { db: 'shop', collection: 'customers' },
      actions: ['find','insert','update','listIndexes','collStats'] },   // no remove: soft-delete only
    { resource: { db: 'shop', collection: 'audit_events' },
      actions: ['insert','find'] },                                       // append-only
  ],
  roles: [],
});
// Deliberately absent: dropCollection, dropDatabase, createIndex, dropIndex, collMod,
// killAnyCursor, and any resource outside `shop`. readWrite would have granted the first four.
//
// Privileges are ADDITIVE and there is no deny rule. A wildcard grant on
// { db: 'shop', collection: '' } would silently re-grant update and remove on
// audit_events — enumerate collections whenever one of them must be narrower.

// Index changes belong to the migration job, which runs once and then has no running process.
db.getSiblingDB('admin').createRole({
  role: 'app_shop_migrate',
  privileges: [{ resource: { db: 'shop', collection: '' },
                 actions: ['createIndex','dropIndex','createCollection','collMod','listIndexes','find'] }],
  roles: [],
});

// One user per service, per environment. The name is what shows up in the audit log,
// in currentOp and in Atlas access history — make it identify a deployment, not a human.
db.getSiblingDB('admin').createUser({
  user: 'svc-orders-prod',
  pwd: passwordPrompt(),
  roles: [{ role: 'app_shop_rw', db: 'admin' }],
  // Defence in depth: this credential is useless from anywhere but the app subnet,
  // so a leaked password in a laptop .env or a CI log is not on its own an incident.
  authenticationRestrictions: [{ clientSource: ['10.20.0.0/16'] }],
});

// Verify what you actually granted, not what you meant to grant:
db.getSiblingDB('admin').getUser('svc-orders-prod', { showPrivileges: true });
Credentials are not shared

One credential per service and per environment, never one per team. Shared credentials make the audit log useless (“the app did it” is not an answer), make rotation a cross-team negotiation, and mean a compromise in the least important consumer is a compromise of everything. If two services genuinely need the same data, that is a signal about your service boundaries, not a reason to share a password.

Rotating a credential without an outage

Drivers read credentials once, when the MongoClient is constructed, and reuse them for every pooled connection and every reconnection after a failover. Changing a password in place therefore does not fail at deploy time — it fails hours later, during the next election or pool refresh, as a storm of MongoServerError: Authentication failed. (code 18) across every pod at once. Rotate by adding a user, not by editing one.

  1. Create the successor. svc-orders-prod-2026q3 with the identical role and restrictions. MongoDB has no two-password user, so two users is how you get an overlap window.
  2. Publish the new secret. A new version in the secrets manager, read at process start. The old version stays valid.
  3. Roll the deployment. Each replacement pod builds a fresh client with the new credential. No in-place reload, no SIGHUP — a new process is the only reliable way to change a driver's credentials.
  4. Watch for stragglers. Query the audit log (or Atlas access history) for authentications by the old username. Batch jobs, cron, the analytics box and that one lambda nobody owns will show up here, and only here.
  5. Drop the old user after a window long enough to cover your slowest scheduled job — a week if you have weekly reports. Dropping it is what makes the rotation real; a disabled-but-present user is a rotation that never happened.

Layer 4 — encryption, and what each kind actually defends against

In transit
requireTLS on the server, tls: true and a pinned CA on the client. Defends against network position: a compromised node in the VPC, a mirrored port, a hostile sidecar.
At rest
WiredTiger encryption with a KMIP key, or encrypted cloud volumes and snapshots. Defends against a stolen disk, a leaked snapshot, a decommissioned SAN — and satisfies auditors. It defends against nothing that authenticates successfully, which is how most breaches happen.
In use
Client-side field-level encryption and Queryable Encryption: the driver encrypts before the bytes leave your process, so the server, the backup, the DBA and the cloud provider only ever hold ciphertext.

Reserve field-level encryption for fields where the threat model genuinely includes your own operators and your own backups — national identifiers, payment instruments, clinical notes — because the cost is permanent and structural, not a tuning knob.

javascript · Queryable Encryption, with the costs written down
import { MongoClient } from 'mongodb';

// The DEK lives in a key-vault collection and is itself wrapped by a CMK in KMS.
// Anyone who can read BOTH the key vault and use the CMK can decrypt everything, so
// they must be different principals in different accounts. Otherwise this is theatre.
const keyVaultNamespace = 'encryption.__keyVault';
const kmsProviders = { aws: {} };   // empty = use the instance/pod IAM role, no static keys

const encryptedFieldsMap = {
  'shop.patients': {
    fields: [
      { path: 'ssn',   bsonType: 'string', keyId: dekId, queries: { queryType: 'equality' } },
      { path: 'dob',   bsonType: 'date',   keyId: dekId, queries: { queryType: 'range' } },
      { path: 'notes', bsonType: 'string', keyId: dekId },   // no `queries`: stored, never searched
    ],
  },
};

const client = new MongoClient(uri, {
  autoEncryption: {
    keyVaultNamespace, kmsProviders, encryptedFieldsMap,
    // The shared crypt library, not the deprecated mongocryptd sidecar process.
    extraOptions: { cryptSharedLibPath: '/usr/lib/mongo_crypt_v1.so' },
  },
});

// Create the collection THROUGH this client so the metadata collections are made too:
await client.db('shop').createCollection('patients', { encryptedFields: encryptedFieldsMap['shop.patients'] });

// What you permanently give up on an encrypted field:
//  - no $regex, no prefix or substring search, no text index, no sort
//  - no aggregation arithmetic or $group on the ciphertext
//  - storage grows several-fold: QE maintains enxcol_.patients.esc and .ecoc alongside
//  - rotation means rewrapManyDataKey() plus a rewrite pass, so plan it before go-live
//  - and it does NOT protect against RCE in your own service: that process holds the plaintext

Layer 5 — an audit log the database administrator cannot edit

The audit log answers the questions asked after an incident, when the interesting evidence is the sequence of privileged actions rather than the data itself. Capture failed authentications (a brute-force or a stale credential looks identical until you count), every user and role change, and every DDL operation. Add authCheck only for the namespaces a regulator will ask about — auditing authCheck globally multiplies log volume by your query rate and costs real throughput.

json · auditLog filter — worth shipping, cheap enough to run
{
  "$or": [
    { "atype": "authenticate", "result": { "$ne": 0 } },

    { "atype": { "$in": ["createUser", "dropUser", "updateUser", "createRole", "dropRole",
                          "grantRolesToUser", "grantPrivilegesToRole", "dropAllUsersFromDatabase"] } },

    { "atype": { "$in": ["dropCollection", "dropDatabase", "createCollection", "renameCollection",
                          "createIndex", "dropIndex", "shutdown", "replSetReconfig"] } },

    { "atype": "authCheck",
      "param.ns": { "$in": ["shop.patients", "shop.payment_methods"] },
      "param.command": { "$in": ["find", "aggregate", "update", "delete"] } }
  ]
}

Then get it off the box. A log written to local disk by a process running as mongod, on a host where the DBA has root, is a record of what the DBA chose to leave behind. Forward it — syslog to a collector, or the file tailed to a different account's bucket with S3 Object Lock in compliance mode — so that write access belongs to a role nobody on the database team holds. Retention of around 400 days is the usual target: PCI DSS requires twelve months with the most recent three immediately searchable.

Injection, once more, at the driver level

Validation at the boundary is covered in its own chapter; what belongs here is the half of the defence that lives on the database. Server-side JavaScript is the sharpest edge: $where, $function, $accumulator and mapReduce execute attacker-influenced code inside the server, and a single expensive expression stalls a whole node. Almost no production application needs them, so set security.javascriptEnabled: false and remove the class of bug from the cluster rather than from one service. The second edge is the regular expression: a user-supplied pattern passed through to $regex runs on the server, where catastrophic backtracking is a denial-of-service you pay for in primary CPU. Escape it, anchor it, or reject it. The third is shape rather than content — a query value that arrives as an object instead of a string turns { token: value } into { token: { $ne: null } }, which is a login bypass. Cast to a primitive before the value is ever near a filter.

The developer-laptop threat

Assume one laptop in your organisation is compromised right now — by a phished session, a malicious postinstall script in a transitive dependency, or a bag left on a train. The blast radius is exactly the set of credentials and data on it. So there are none: no production connection string in .env, no production dump in ~/Downloads, no shared admin password in the team vault labelled “prod mongo”.

  • Analytics gets its own identity. A read-only user on one database, connected with readPreference=secondaryPreferred and a maxTimeMS so a careless aggregation cannot take the primary down. Better still, point it at an analytics node or a downstream warehouse copy where the answer to a bad query is a slow report rather than an incident.
  • Shell access goes through a bastion. SSM Session Manager (aws ssm start-session --target i-0abc --document-name AWS-StartPortForwardingSession) or an equivalent proxy: no inbound SSH, IAM-controlled, session-recorded. The database's authenticationRestrictions then pin its credentials to that host, so they are worthless anywhere else.
  • Human access is time-boxed and attributable. OIDC or LDAP-backed users mapped to your IdP groups, elevated for a window, revoked automatically. If a person's access cannot expire on its own, it will not expire.
  • Break glass is loud. The root credential is sealed, its use pages someone, and using it is expected to be followed by a written explanation. Nobody objects to that policy until they need it, which is the point.
  • Local development uses seeded, synthetic data. The moment restoring a production snapshot locally is normal, every laptop is a copy of the customer database and every one of them is in scope for the next regulator's letter.

The hardening checklist

Public IPs on the data tier
0including “temporary” 0.0.0.0/0 access-list entries
Roles per service
1scoped to one database, with no drop actions
Credential age
< 90 daysor none at all, with x.509 or IAM auth
Audit retention
400 daysPCI wants 12 months, 3 immediately searchable
  1. No public route to the database. Private subnets, group-to-group rules, private endpoints. Verify from outside, do not assume.
  2. Authorisation enabled, localhost bypass off. Assert it in a startup check so a rebuilt node cannot come up open.
  3. requireTLS, and no tlsInsecure anywhere. Grep the rendered config in CI; fail the build on a match.
  4. A custom role per service. No readWrite, no *AnyDatabase, no userAdmin outside break-glass. Index changes belong to the migration identity.
  5. authenticationRestrictions on every service user, so a leaked password alone is not an incident.
  6. Credentials from a secrets manager, injected at runtime, rotated by adding a user and rolling the deployment.
  7. Server-side JavaScript disabled, and user input cast to primitives before it reaches a filter.
  8. Encryption at rest on volumes and snapshots; field-level encryption for the handful of fields where your own operators are in the threat model.
  9. Audit log filtered, shipped off-box, and immutable to the database team.
  10. Zero production credentials on laptops. Read-only analytics users, a recorded bastion, expiring human access.
Say this in an interview

“How would you secure MongoDB?” is a breadth question, and the weak answer is “enable authentication and TLS”. Answer in layers, and lead with the two statements that show you have operated this: the application credential must not be able to drop a collection — which rules out readWrite and forces a custom role — and ideally there is no long-lived password at all, because x.509 or IAM auth removes the rotation problem instead of scheduling it. Then close the loop on detection: least privilege turns a catastrophe into a not authorized line in the log, and the audit log is only evidence if it lives somewhere the database administrator cannot edit.

34

Capacity planning & back-of-envelope

“We are going to have a million users” is a sentence, not a requirement. Twenty minutes of arithmetic turns it into peak requests per second, gigabytes of working set, a count of Node processes and a line item on the cloud bill — numbers you can provision against, alert on, and be provably wrong about. Done honestly the estimate lands within a factor of two, which is close enough to choose between a bigger instance and a shard key you can never take back.

From “a million users” to a number you can buy

Every capacity estimate is the same short chain, computed in one direction with no link skipped. Each link is an assumption owned by a named person; everything downstream is arithmetic anyone can redo on a whiteboard. That separation is the discipline: when reality disagrees with the model, exactly one assumption is wrong, and you can find out which in an afternoon instead of re-architecting on a hunch.

DAU, never registered users
Registered users include everyone who signed up in 2021 and never returned. Daily actives generate load. If product only reports MAU, DAU is typically 10–30% of it for a consumer app and 40–60% for a tool people open every morning.
API requests per active user per day
Count requests, not intentions. “Open the dashboard” is routinely six calls: session, profile, feed, unread counts, flags, telemetry. Teams under-count this by 3–5× because they count screens. Open the network tab and count for real, then add the polling you forgot.
Peak-to-average ratio
The average is a billing number; the peak is the sizing number. Single-time-zone consumer apps run 3–5×; a global B2B tool runs 2–3×; anything driven by push notifications or a scheduled sync runs 10× or worse for sixty seconds at a time.
Read:write ratio
Decides whether replicas help, whether a cache can pay for itself, and whether the write path needs its own capacity story. Take it from route-level request counts, not from feel.
Payload size
The p50 response body after compression, measured at the edge. Multiplies into egress bandwidth and cost, and — more painfully — into serialisation CPU on the single thread.
Bytes written per day
Only inserts grow storage; in-place updates mostly do not. Splitting writes into the two is the difference between a sane disk forecast and a panicked one.
The only constant worth memorising

There are 86 400 seconds in a day, which for estimation purposes is 100 000. So one request per user per day at 1M DAU is about 12 RPS. Everything else is multiplication: 1M DAU × 40 requests × 4× peak ≈ 12 × 40 × 4 ≈ 1 900 RPS. You can do that in your head while the interviewer is still writing on the board, and it is within 3% of the exact answer.

Latency numbers, and how a budget gets spent

Sizing tells you how much iron to buy; latency numbers tell you which design choices are free and which are catastrophic. Know these to an order of magnitude without looking them up — the useful skill is not recall, it is instantly noticing that a proposal has put a 1 ms operation inside a loop that runs two hundred times.

Round figures for commodity cloud hardware. The right-hand column is what actually matters: how many main-memory reads you could have done instead.
OperationTypicalRelative to one memory read
L1 / L2 cache reference1–4 ns0.01–0.04× — free, and the reason arrays beat linked structures
Main memory reference100 ns1× — the unit
Read 1 MB sequentially from RAM~30 µs300× — copying a big buffer is not free on a single thread
NVMe SSD random 4 KB read~50 µs500× — the cost of one WiredTiger cache miss
Read 1 MB sequentially from NVMe~150 µs1 500×
Round trip within one availability zone~0.3–0.5 ms~4 000× — your floor for any Redis or Mongo call
Round trip cross-AZ, same region~1 ms10 000× — and billed in both directions
Spinning-disk seek~10 ms100 000× — why the working set must fit in cache
Round trip Virginia → Ireland~75 ms750 000× — one such hop eats a third of a 200 ms budget
Round trip Virginia → Singapore~230 ms2 300 000× — no amount of tuning survives this; move the data

Two rules fall straight out. First, sequential dependent calls multiply the RTT floor: twelve awaited queries cross-AZ cost 12 ms of pure network before the database does any work, and Promise.all collapses that to 1 ms for free. Second, a cache miss is 500 memory reads and a cross-region hop is eight million — which is why the geography of your data decides your p99 far more often than your index choices do.

p99 budget for GET /feed — 200 ms as the user experiences it ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── client → CDN edge 30 ms mobile radio wake + TLS 1.3 resumption. Not yours to fix. edge → origin region 20 ms one cross-region RTT. Two, if you forgot keep-alive. ALB → node (cross-AZ) 1 ms 0.5 ms each way wait in the event loop 10 ms at 65% utilisation. At 90% this line alone is 60 ms. Redis GET session 1 ms the network is the cost; the lookup is 100 ns Mongo · 2 indexed reads 10 ms awaited in sequence. Parallel makes it 5 ms. serialise + gzip 6 KB 3 ms pure CPU, and it blocks every other request ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── accounted 75 ms unaccounted 125 ms where every real p99 investigation ends up

A worked example: 1M DAU, end to end

Put the model in the repository next to the code it sizes. A spreadsheet goes stale in a month and nobody can diff it; a forty-line module gets reviewed, gets its assumptions corrected in a pull request, and can run in CI to fail the build when measured CPU per request drifts past the number the fleet was sized on.

javascript · capacity.js — assumptions above the line, arithmetic below
// Every value in A is an ASSUMPTION with an owner and a source. Nothing below
// the line is negotiable -- it is multiplication. When the estimate is wrong,
// exactly one of these constants is wrong, and that is a tractable question.
const A = {
  dau:              1_000_000, // product analytics, 28-day trailing. NOT signups.
  reqPerUserDay:    40,        // MEASURED: devtools count over the 3 main journeys
  peakToAverage:    4,         // MEASURED: max 60s bucket / daily mean, one week of ALB logs
  readWrite:        20,        // reads per write, from route-level counters
  respBytes:        6 * 1024,  // p50 response body AFTER compression, at the edge
  cpuMsPerReq:      12,        // MEASURED under load, not on a laptop -- see next block
  insertShare:      0.4,       // 60% of writes are in-place updates and grow nothing
  docBytes:         1_500,
  secondaryIndexes: 4,
  indexEntryBytes:  60,        // key + 12-byte record id, before prefix compression
  hotWindowDays:    14,        // how far back reads ACTUALLY go. Ask the query, not product.
  targetCpu:        0.65,      // headroom for GC pauses, deploys and being wrong
  azCount:          3,         // must still serve peak with one AZ gone
};

const SECONDS_PER_DAY = 86_400;
const reqPerDay = A.dau * A.reqPerUserDay;              //  40,000,000
const avgRps    = reqPerDay / SECONDS_PER_DAY;          //     463 rps
const peakRps   = avgRps * A.peakToAverage;             //   1,852 rps
const peakWrites = peakRps / (A.readWrite + 1);         //      88 writes/s
const peakMbps  = (peakRps * A.respBytes * 8) / 1e6;    //      91 Mbit/s

// FLEET. A process pinned at 100% CPU serves 1000/cpuMs requests per second.
// Never size at 100%: queueing delay scales as rho/(1-rho), so the last 20% of
// utilisation costs more latency than the first 80% put together.
const rpsPerProcess     = (1000 / A.cpuMsPerReq) * A.targetCpu;   // 54 rps
const survivingFraction = (A.azCount - 1) / A.azCount;            // 0.667
const processes = Math.ceil(peakRps / rpsPerProcess / survivingFraction); // 52

// STORAGE. Only inserts grow the collection.
const insertsPerDay  = (reqPerDay / (A.readWrite + 1)) * A.insertShare;   // 762k/day
const docGbYear      = (insertsPerDay * 365 * A.docBytes) / 1e9;          // 417 GB
const indexGbYear    = (insertsPerDay * 365 * A.secondaryIndexes * A.indexEntryBytes) / 1e9; // 67 GB

// WORKING SET. WiredTiger holds data pages UNCOMPRESSED in cache, so use raw
// bytes here -- never storageSize. Snappy hides a 3-4x factor and sizing RAM
// from the on-disk figure is the classic way to under-provision by 4x.
const workingSetGb = (insertsPerDay * A.hotWindowDays * A.docBytes) / 1e9  // 16 GB hot docs
                   + indexGbYear * 0.4;                                    // 27 GB hot index
// Default cache is max(50% of (RAM - 1GB), 256MB), so RAM is roughly 2x cache.
const ramGb = 2 * workingSetGb + 8;                                        // ~94 -> buy 128

console.table({ avgRps, peakRps, peakWrites, peakMbps, processes,
                docGbYear, indexGbYear, workingSetGb, ramGb });
Peak RPS
1 9001M DAU × 40 requests × 4× peak — the only number the whole design hangs on
Working set
43 GBfits one 128 GB replica set; sharding is years away, not months
Node processes
52sized to carry peak with one availability zone of three gone
Peak egress
91 Mbit/s7 TB/month ≈ $650 at $0.09/GB — the CDN pays for itself immediately

Read the tiles for what they rule out as much as what they prescribe: 1 900 RPS is one large machine's worth of work, and nothing here justifies a distributed data architecture on capacity grounds — only on availability grounds. The bandwidth line also hides the real bill. With three AZs, roughly two-thirds of internal service-to-service calls cross an AZ boundary and are charged in both directions, so a chatty internal fan-out routinely costs more per month than all the customer-facing egress combined.

The peak you measured at the wrong granularity

A team computed peak-to-average from hourly CloudWatch buckets and got 2.4×, then sized a fleet with 30% headroom. The marketing push went out at 09:00 to 400 000 devices; the app wakes on receipt and syncs. The sixty-second peak was 11× the daily average and completely invisible in hourly data. The fleet saturated, the mobile client retried on timeout without jitter, and the retries arrived as a second wave larger than the first. Always compute peak-to-average from 60-second or finer buckets, and treat every scheduled fan-out — push campaigns, cron-driven syncs, an hourly poll in the client — as its own capacity event with its own number.

Little's Law, and what “halving latency” actually buys

Concurrency, latency and throughput are related by one equation: L = λW. Requests in flight equals arrival rate times time in the system. It is not a model with assumptions to argue about — it is an accounting identity, true of any stable queue.

At 1 900 RPS and 250 ms mean latency, 1900 × 0.25 = 475 requests are in flight across the fleet, about 9 per process. That is a memory budget as much as a concurrency budget. If each in-flight request holds a 2 MB response buffer and one dependency slows from 250 ms to 2 s, in-flight jumps to 3 800 — 73 per process, 146 MB of extra heap each — and the fleet dies of JavaScript heap out of memory during what the dashboard shows as a latency blip. Little's Law is why latency incidents become crash loops.

Rearranged as λ = L / W, it says that at a fixed concurrency limit, halving residence time doubles throughput for free. True — and the highest-leverage optimisation available — but only for the resource that is actually constrained. In Node that distinction is sharp.

Halving CPU time doubles fleet capacity

Requests per process is 1000 / cpuMsPerRequest. Take 12 ms down to 6 ms — stop re-serialising the same object, drop a schema validation on a hot path, cache a compiled template — and 52 processes become 26. The saving is linear and immediate.

Halving I/O wait buys slots, not throughput

If 238 ms of that 250 ms is spent awaiting Mongo, the event loop is idle throughout and the process is not CPU-bound. Halving it frees connection-pool slots, sockets and heap — real wins that raise your ceiling under stress — but it does not add a single RPS to a CPU-saturated process.

So: identify the constrained resource first, then halve the latency at that resource. Halving the wrong one produces a beautiful flame graph and an unchanged bill.

Sizing the Node fleet from a measured number

cpuMsPerRequest is the single input to the fleet model that you must measure rather than assume, and it is the one people measure wrongly.

javascript · cpu-per-request.js — and the trap in process.cpuUsage()
const { performance, monitorEventLoopDelay } = require('node:perf_hooks');

// THE TRAP: process.cpuUsage(start) wrapped around one handler looks right and
// is wrong. cpuUsage() is PROCESS-wide. With 30 requests in flight, the delta
// you attribute to this request includes everyone else's CPU during its awaits,
// so it over-reports by roughly the concurrency factor -- and the error grows
// exactly when you care, under load.
//
// The honest measurement is aggregate: CPU burned in a window / requests
// completed in that window. No attribution, no per-request bookkeeping.
let completed = 0;
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  // 'finish' = response fully handed to the socket. 'close' also fires on
  // client aborts, which would inflate the denominator with work never done.
  res.on('finish', () => { completed += 1; });
  next();
});

const loopDelay = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 10 });
loopDelay.enable();

let lastCpu = process.cpuUsage();
let lastElu = performance.eventLoopUtilization();
let lastAt  = process.hrtime.bigint();

setInterval(() => {
  const cpu    = process.cpuUsage(lastCpu);              // microseconds, user + system
  const elu    = performance.eventLoopUtilization(lastElu);
  const now    = process.hrtime.bigint();
  const wallMs = Number(now - lastAt) / 1e6;
  const cpuMs  = (cpu.user + cpu.system) / 1000;
  const n      = completed;

  // Feed this straight into the fleet model: rpsPerProcess = 1000 / this.
  metrics.gauge('node.cpu_ms_per_request', n ? cpuMs / n : 0);

  // A value above 1.0 means work is happening off the main thread -- worker
  // threads, or the libuv pool doing crypto/zlib/fs. Sizing on ELU alone would
  // have missed all of it, because ELU only ever describes the main thread.
  metrics.gauge('node.cpu_cores_used', cpuMs / wallMs);

  // ELU is the fraction of loop time not spent idle. It is the only honest
  // utilisation signal for a single-threaded runtime: container CPU% reads low
  // whenever the process is blocked on I/O, and reads low right up to collapse.
  metrics.gauge('node.event_loop_utilization', elu.utilization);
  metrics.gauge('node.event_loop_delay_p99_ms', loopDelay.percentile(99) / 1e6);

  loopDelay.reset();
  completed = 0;
  lastCpu = process.cpuUsage();
  lastElu = performance.eventLoopUtilization();
  lastAt  = now;
}, 10_000).unref();

Then size in three multiplications, in this order. Capacity per process: 1000 / 12 = 83 RPS at full saturation. Headroom: multiply by 0.65, giving 54 RPS, because a process at 95% utilisation has a queue nineteen times its service time and a single 40 ms major GC pause becomes a visible p99 cliff. Failure domain: divide by the fraction of the fleet that survives your largest correlated loss. Across three AZs that is two-thirds, so 1 852 / 54 / 0.667 = 52 processes — 13 four-vCPU instances, or 15 if you want the AZs to divide evenly.

The consequence is that on an ordinary Tuesday the fleet sits near 40% CPU, and someone will offer to save money by halving it. The answer is that you are not paying for CPU, you are paying for the AZ you are allowed to lose. Size for peak, degraded, not for average, healthy.

Why 65% and not 90%. M/M/1 queueing delay is ρ/(1−ρ) multiples of service time; multi-core servers are kinder than this, but the knee sits in the same place.
Utilisation ρQueue wait, as a multiple of service timeWhat you see
0.501.0×Flat latency. Feels like the box is idle.
0.651.9×Still flat. Absorbs a GC pause and a deploy without a graph moving.
0.804.0×p99 detaches from p50. First complaints, no obvious cause.
0.909.0×Latency doubles on a 5% traffic increase. This is the cliff.
0.9519×Timeouts, retries, and retries becoming the traffic.

Sizing MongoDB: the working set is the whole answer

For a well-indexed workload there is exactly one sizing question — does the working set fit in the WiredTiger cache? — because the answer is worth a factor of 500 in read latency. Above 1.0 the cache thrashes, every page fault becomes a 50 µs NVMe read plus Snappy decompression on a path that used to be 100 ns, and p99 moves from 3 ms to 60 ms with no query change and no deploy to blame.

javascript · mongosh — measure the working set instead of arguing about it
const wt = db.serverStatus().wiredTiger.cache;
const GB = 1024 ** 3;
({
  configuredGb: wt['maximum bytes configured'] / GB,
  inCacheGb:    wt['bytes currently in the cache'] / GB,
  dirtyGb:      wt['tracked dirty bytes in the cache'] / GB,   // sustained > 5% means eviction is behind
  // THE signal. A healthy server reads pages in during warm-up and then almost
  // never again. Sample this counter twice, 60s apart: a steady non-zero rate
  // in steady state IS the working set overflowing, and it is visible weeks
  // before p99 moves enough for anyone to open a ticket.
  pagesReadIn:  wt['pages read into cache'],
  evictedClean: wt['unmodified pages evicted'],
});

// size: UNCOMPRESSED bytes -- what the cache must hold.
// storageSize: compressed on disk -- what you pay AWS for.
// Sizing RAM from storageSize under-provisions by the compression ratio, 3-4x
// for typical JSON. This one confusion causes more Mongo latency incidents
// than every slow query put together.
db.getCollectionNames().map((name) => {
  const s = db[name].stats({ scale: 1024 * 1024 });   // a shell helper over $collStats
  return {
    name,
    docs:      s.count,
    dataMb:    s.size,             // cache pressure
    diskMb:    s.storageSize,      // disk bill
    indexMb:   s.totalIndexSize,   // index pages keep prefix compression IN cache
    avgObjB:   s.avgObjSize,       // sanity-check your docBytes assumption here
  };
});

Index size is the half people forget. Estimate it as documents × indexes × (key bytes + 12) for the record id, then halve it for prefix compression on the low-cardinality prefixes. In the worked example that is 67 GB a year of index against 417 GB of documents — but the index is hot in its entirety far more often than the documents are, which is why the working set came out at 43 GB when only 16 GB of it was document data.

Which leaves the escalation ladder. Buy a bigger instance while the working set still fits one — a terabyte of RAM is a phone call, sharding is a permanent architectural commitment, and the crossover is far higher than people assume. Add secondaries when reads dominate and specific routes tolerate staleness; this does nothing for writes and nothing for a working set that no longer fits. Shard only when the hot data genuinely exceeds the cache of the largest instance you can buy, or sustained writes exceed what one primary absorbs. At 88 writes per second the example is three orders of magnitude from that second condition.

The capacity plan that sized everything except connections

The plan covered CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth, and the fleet scaled cleanly to 52 processes. Nobody multiplied 52 by the driver's default maxPoolSize of 100. That is 5 200 potential connections to one primary. Mid-tier Atlas clusters cap concurrent connections in the low thousands and reject the rest with connection refused because too many open connections; a self-managed mongod is worse, because it will accept them and spend roughly 1 MB of thread stack on each — 5 GB taken directly out of the WiredTiger cache you spent this chapter sizing. The fix is arithmetic, not tuning: maxPoolSize × processes must stay under half the server limit, leaving room for a deploy that briefly runs both fleets. At 88 writes and 1 800 reads per second, maxPoolSize: 20 is generous — Little's Law again, since 1 800 reads/s at 5 ms needs 9 concurrent connections, not 100.

Queue depth is the leading indicator

Utilisation makes a poor alarm because it saturates: once a resource reads 100% it cannot say whether you are 5% or 500% over capacity. Latency is worse — it is the output of saturation, so by the time p99 moves, the queue that produced it has been growing for minutes. Queue depth is unbounded and moves first, which is the only property an early-warning signal needs. Three queues sit in the path, all cheap to export.

  • The kernel accept queue. ss -lnt on the listening socket reports Recv-Q as completed connections waiting for accept() and Send-Q as the backlog limit. Non-zero Recv-Q means the process is not accepting fast enough; connections dropped past the limit appear only in netstat -s under the listen-overflow counters.
  • The event loop. ELU is the honest saturation measure for a single-threaded runtime; loop delay p99 is that queue in milliseconds. Alert on ELU above 0.75 sustained, never on container CPU, which reads low right up to the moment the process stops answering health checks.
  • The connection pool. Checkout wait is the purest leading indicator in the system: it goes non-zero the instant demand exceeds the pool, while every latency percentile is still flat. Recent 6.x Node drivers put durationMS on connectionCheckedOut; otherwise track outstanding checkouts as a gauge from the CMAP events.

One rule for all three: alert on the queue, page on its derivative, keep latency on the dashboard rather than in the alert. A queue that is deep but flat is a system running hot. A queue that is shallow but growing linearly is an outage in about four minutes.

What to cache, decided by arithmetic

The read:write ratio tells you how many reads amortise each invalidation; the miss cost tells you whether the hop is worth taking. Both must clear their bar — one alone is not an argument.

Origin load after caching is (1 − h) × readRate, so it is the miss rate that matters and the improvements are reciprocal rather than linear: 90% to 99% cuts database load tenfold, while 50% to 90% only cuts it fivefold. Effective read latency for a read-through cache is t_cache + (1 − h) × t_origin, because a miss pays the probe too. At 95% with a 0.6 ms Redis hop in front of an 8 ms aggregation that is 0.6 + 0.05 × 8 = 1.0 ms — eightfold, and 1 900 reads per second reduced to 95 reaching Mongo.

The counter-example is the more useful half. A 2 ms indexed point read, behind a 0.6 ms Redis hop, at 3:1 reads to writes: you save 1.4 ms on three-quarters of calls and buy a new failure domain, a coherence bug waiting to be written, and a stampede risk on expiry. Don't. The rule is to cache when read:write is at least 10:1 and the miss costs at least 10× the hit. Mechanics — invalidation, stampede protection, layer choice — belong to the chapter on caching; capacity planning only decides whether, and it decides with those two ratios.

Doing this out loud in a design interview

The estimate is not the deliverable. The deliverable is showing that you know which numbers are assumptions, which are consequences, and which one you would go and measure on Monday morning.

SIX MOVES, IN ORDER
  1. State every assumption before using it. “I'll assume 1M DAU, 40 requests per user per day, and a 4× peak because this is consumer and single-time-zone.” An interviewer who disagrees corrects you now — worth far more than a correct answer to the wrong problem.
  2. Round aggressively, and say so. 86 400 becomes 100 000; 1 852 becomes 2 000. The estimate is good to a factor of two, so carrying three significant figures is a claim you cannot support.
  3. Compute the chain in one direction. DAU → requests/day → average RPS → peak RPS → split by read:write → bandwidth, storage, fleet. Never reach for an architecture before peak RPS is on the board.
  4. Sanity-check against something known. “2 000 RPS is about one large machine's worth of work, so nothing here forces a distributed design on capacity grounds.” Anchors worth carrying: 1 Gbit/s is 125 MB/s; a real Node handler does 500–2 000 RPS per process; a cache-resident Mongo primary does tens of thousands of point reads per second.
  5. Size for peak, degraded. Say “and this must hold with one availability zone gone”, then divide. Most candidates size for peak; almost none subtract a failure domain, and that is the line between a plan and a diagram.
  6. Name the assumption you would verify first. Requests per user per day — the most under-counted input in the model, and everything downstream scales linearly with it.
Say this in an interview

“A million DAU at forty requests each is forty million requests a day. There are about a hundred thousand seconds in a day, so that is roughly four hundred RPS average; at a 4× peak, call it two thousand. Twenty-to-one reads, so nineteen hundred reads and a hundred writes at peak — a hundred writes per second is nothing, one primary handles that with room to spare, so this is a read-scaling problem and a working-set problem, not a sharding problem. I measured twelve milliseconds of CPU per request, which is eighty RPS per process saturated, fifty-four at a sixty-five percent target; two thousand over fifty-four is thirty-seven processes, and I need to survive losing one AZ of three, so fifty-two. On the database side the hot fourteen days plus the resident index is about forty gigabytes uncompressed, so I want a hundred-and-twenty-eight gigabyte instance and I am memory-resident with headroom. I would alert on WiredTiger pages-read-into-cache, event loop utilisation and connection-pool checkout wait rather than on p99, because all three move before latency does. And the assumption I would verify first is requests per user per day — everything above scales linearly with it, and teams routinely under-count it by three.”

35

Stateless services & horizontal scale

Horizontal scale is not a thing you buy when traffic arrives; it is a property you either preserved or quietly destroyed months earlier. The whole property reduces to one testable sentence — any request must be servable by any instance, with no memory of any previous request — and every violation of it has a name, a symptom and a known externalised replacement. This chapter is the audit, the replacements, and the three operational problems that remain even after you pass it: duplicate cron, cold starts and scale-in.

The rule, and how to test it

The rule has a cheap falsification test that costs nothing to run and that most services fail on the first attempt: start three instances behind a round-robin balancer with sticky sessions disabled, and run your integration suite against them. Nothing else — no chaos tooling, no load generator. Login will break, or the rate limiter will let three times the configured traffic through, or an upload will 404 on the follow-up GET. Each failure points at a variable that lives in one process's heap and should not.

The reason to care is not elegance. A stateless tier gives you four concrete operational powers: you can deploy by replacing instances one at a time; you can lose an instance without losing user work; you can add capacity in the ninety seconds before a spike lands rather than the ninety minutes after; and you can run identical code in three availability zones without a coherence protocol between them. Every piece of in-process state you keep takes back one of those four.

The audit: in-process state, the symptom under N instances, and where it goes instead
What lives in the heapSymptom once N > 1Externalised replacement
express-session with the default MemoryStoreRandom logouts, roughly (N−1)/N of requests. Node itself warns: MemoryStore is not designed for a production environment.Redis session store, or a signed stateless token. A token moves the state to the client and gives up cheap revocation; a Redis store keeps revocation and costs one round trip.
Rate-limit counters in a MapEffective limit is N× the configured one, and it changes every time the fleet scales. Attackers with keep-alive off spread across instances for free.Redis counters (see the rate-limiting chapter). Do not "fix" it by dividing the limit by N — that breaks the moment an instance dies or the balancer is not perfectly even.
An in-memory cache that must be coherentStale reads on 1/N of requests after a write. Users see the old value, refresh, see the new one, refresh, see the old one again.Shared cache, or keep the local cache and make it tolerant: short TTL plus a pub/sub invalidation fan-out. Local caches are fine when staleness is bounded and acceptable — never when a write must be visible immediately.
Uploads written to local disk or /tmpThe follow-up request lands elsewhere and 404s. Multipart chunked uploads fail unless every chunk hits the same box.Stream to object storage, ideally with a pre-signed PUT so the bytes never touch your tier at all. Local disk is a scratch buffer within one request, never across two.
node-cron / setInterval inside the API processEvery job runs N times. Invoices double-charge, digest emails send twice, reconciliation jobs fight each other.Leader lock, a single-replica scheduler deployment, or a real scheduler. All three are covered below.
WebSocket / SSE connection registryio.emit() reaches only the clients pinned to that instance. Messages arrive for some users and not others, and it looks like a client bug.A pub/sub adapter (@socket.io/redis-adapter) so a publish on any instance fans out to all. The socket itself stays pinned — that is unavoidable — but the routing must not be.
Feature flags loaded once at bootA flag flip takes effect on instances started after it. During a rollout the fleet is split-brain and the A/B numbers are meaningless.A flag SDK with streaming updates, or a short TTL plus a version check. Log the flag config hash on every instance so you can prove they agree.
Idempotency keys, OTPs, nonces, in-flight de-dupe setsA retried payment goes through twice because the retry landed on an instance that never saw the first one.Redis SET NX with a TTL, or a unique index in MongoDB. This is the class where the bug costs money.
The one that survives the audit and still bites

Module-scope variables look stateless because they are never written to at request time. const config = await loadConfig() at boot, a memoised JWKS key set, a compiled regex cache, a lazily-populated tenant lookup — all fine, until one of them is populated from the first request that needs it. Now instance behaviour depends on which request each instance saw first, and you have a heisenbug that only appears after a scale-out. Grep for let and Map at module scope and ask of each: would a fresh process serve this request identically?

Sticky sessions: a workaround with an itemised bill

Stickiness pins a client to an instance so in-process state keeps working. It is a real tool and it is sometimes correct, but it is a payment plan, not a fix, and the instalments are these. Load stops being even: hashing distributes clients, not work, so one instance ends up with the tenant that runs reports all day and sits at 90% while its neighbours idle at 20%. With ip_hash behind a corporate NAT or a mobile carrier gateway, thousands of users share one hash bucket. Deploys get worse: replacing an instance evicts every session on it, so a rolling deploy is a rolling logout unless the state is also persisted — at which point you did not need stickiness. Instance failure is now user-visible: losing one of six instances stops being a 17% capacity event and becomes a 17% data loss event. Scale-out is slow to take effect: new instances only receive new clients, so a fresh replica sits nearly idle during exactly the spike you added it for.

Stickiness is still acceptable in three places. Long-lived connections — WebSocket, SSE, HTTP/2 streams — are pinned by physics; the connection is the affinity, and the fix is to keep the routing shared rather than to fight it. Resumable multipart uploads that buffer chunks locally need affinity for the duration of the upload and must not need it afterwards. And deliberate pinning for canary or A/B assignment is stickiness used as a feature, where the pin carries no state that matters if it moves. If you use it, prefer an explicit application cookie (hash $cookie_sid consistent; in nginx, or an application-controlled ALB stickiness cookie) over ip_hash: it survives client IP changes and it is visible in logs, so you can prove where a request went.

Scheduled jobs when there is more than one of you

The duplicate-cron problem is the single most common way a stateless-looking service turns out not to be. node-cron inside the API process is a one-line addition that works perfectly on one instance and silently multiplies by the replica count. There are exactly three good answers, in ascending order of cost and descending order of cleverness.

  1. A leader lock with a TTL. Every instance runs the schedule; each tick, all of them race for SET job:nightly-invoices <token> NX PX 300000 and only the winner executes. Cheap, no new deployment, and the TTL means a leader that dies mid-job cannot block the next run forever. The cost is that the lock is advisory — see the honest limits below — so the job itself must still be safe to run twice.
  2. A dedicated scheduler deployment with one replica. Same image, different entrypoint, replicas: 1 and — critically — strategy: Recreate rather than RollingUpdate, because a rolling update briefly runs two pods and reintroduces the exact bug you deployed this to avoid. Simple, auditable, and the honest trade is that the schedule has a gap while the pod restarts. Fine for a nightly job, not for a thirty-second poll.
  3. A real scheduler. A Kubernetes CronJob, BullMQ repeatable jobs backed by Redis, or a workflow engine. The schedule becomes data rather than code, you get history, retries, backoff, concurrency policy (concurrencyPolicy: Forbid is the one you want) and an answer to "did last night's run actually happen?". This is where you end up once the number of jobs passes about five.
Production failure: 3× the invoices, 3× the emails

A team scaled the API from one instance to three on a Friday to absorb a campaign. The nightly billing job was a node-cron entry in app.js. At 02:00 on Saturday, three instances independently read the same batch of unbilled subscriptions and issued three charges each — the payment provider accepted them because each carried a fresh idempotency key generated per run. The alert that fired was not "duplicate charges"; it was a support queue on Monday. The lesson that survives: a lock reduces the probability of duplicate work; only an idempotency key derived from the work itself makes duplication harmless. Key the charge on {subscriptionId, billingPeriod} with a unique index, and three concurrent runs produce one charge and two E11000 duplicate key errors, which is a correct outcome rather than an incident.

javascript · lock.js — SET NX PX, unique token, Lua compare-and-delete
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

// Release must be a compare-and-delete, executed atomically. Redis runs Lua scripts
// single-threaded to completion, so GET-then-DEL inside a script cannot interleave.
const RELEASE = `
if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
  return redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1])
else
  return 0
end`;

// Extending has the same requirement: only the holder may push the deadline out.
const EXTEND = `
if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
  return redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[2])
else
  return 0
end`;

export function makeLocker(redis) {
  redis.defineCommand('releaseLock', { numberOfKeys: 1, lua: RELEASE });
  redis.defineCommand('extendLock',  { numberOfKeys: 1, lua: EXTEND });

  return async function withLock(key, ttlMs, fn) {
    const token = randomUUID();          // unique per ACQUISITION, not per process:
                                         // a process that reacquires must not be able
                                         // to release the attempt it previously lost.

    // NX = only if absent (the mutual exclusion). PX = expiry in ms (the liveness
    // guarantee). Both in ONE command: SETNX followed by EXPIRE is a bug, because a
    // crash between them leaves a lock with no TTL and the job never runs again.
    const ok = await redis.set(key, token, 'PX', ttlMs, 'NX');
    if (ok !== 'OK') return { ran: false };

    // Heartbeat at TTL/3. Without it you must guess the job's worst-case duration and
    // set a TTL longer than it, which means a crashed holder blocks the job for that
    // long. With it, the TTL can be short (30 s) and still cover a 20-minute job.
    const beat = setInterval(() => {
      redis.extendLock(key, token, ttlMs).catch(() => {});
    }, Math.floor(ttlMs / 3));
    beat.unref();                        // never hold the event loop open on shutdown

    try {
      return { ran: true, value: await fn() };
    } finally {
      clearInterval(beat);
      // THE TRAP: a naive `redis.del(key)` here is a correctness bug. If fn() overran
      // the TTL, the lock already expired and another instance holds it — a bare DEL
      // deletes SOMEONE ELSE'S lock and you now have two runners with no mutual
      // exclusion at all. Compare-and-delete makes the stale release a no-op.
      await redis.releaseLock(key, token).catch(() => {});
    }
  };
}

The honest limits of a Redis lock

A lock built this way is correct against the failure modes it can see, and unsound against the ones it cannot. Three of them matter in production. A stop-the-world GC pause or a descheduled container can suspend the holder for longer than the TTL; it wakes up believing it still holds the lock and writes anyway, having never observed the expiry. Clock skew and TTL expiry are not the same clock as your Date.now() checks, which is why the lock must never be validated by comparing timestamps in application code. And Redis replication is asynchronous: if the primary fails over after acknowledging your SET NX but before the replica has it, the new primary hands the same lock to someone else. Redlock across independent nodes narrows the window and does not close it — the published critique of it is worth reading before you cite it in a design review.

The senior answer

“Is a Redis lock safe?” The answer that lands: “It is safe enough for efficiency and not sufficient for correctness. I use it to stop N instances doing the same expensive work at once — that is a cost and load argument, and a rare double-execution is acceptable. I never use it as the only thing standing between me and a double charge, because a GC pause longer than the TTL, or a failover between primary and replica, will hand two holders the same lock and neither of them can detect it. For correctness I need the protected resource itself to reject the second writer: a unique index on {subscriptionId, period}, a conditional update with expectedVersion, or a fencing token that the storage layer refuses to accept if it is lower than the last one it saw. Redis cannot give me fencing because it does not participate in the write. So: lock for efficiency, idempotency key for correctness, and if the design only has the lock, that is the finding I would raise.”

Warm-up: why the first request after a scale-out times out

A freshly started Node process is measurably slower than the same process ninety seconds later, and the gap is bigger than most people expect. Four things are cold at once. The connection pools are empty — the MongoDB driver establishes minPoolSize connections in the background, but the first queries can queue behind TCP plus TLS plus SCRAM authentication, roughly 3–15 ms per connection and more cross-AZ. DNS and TLS to upstreams are unresolved and unhandshaked. V8 has not tiered up: your route handlers run in the Ignition interpreter until they are hot enough for Sparkplug and TurboFan, which is typically several thousand invocations; JSON serialisation of a large document can be 5–10× slower on the first pass. And lazily-required modules — the AWS SDK client you only construct on first use, an ajv schema compiled on first validation — pay their cost on a user's request.

Measured on a typical service, a first request lands at 400–900 ms against a warm p50 of 10–15 ms. That would be a curiosity except for what the load balancer does with it: a new instance is added to the pool and immediately given its full 1/N share of traffic. During a spike-driven scale-out, the pod that exists specifically to absorb the spike is the one serving 900 ms responses, and if your upstream timeout is 1 s, it serves errors. This is a self-inflicted latency spike that appears every time you scale up.

The fix is that readiness must mean warm, not alive. Do the warm-up work before the readiness probe passes, and let orchestration hold traffic back until it does. Note the flag reality here: nginx open source has no slow_start — that directive is nginx Plus only — whereas an AWS target group does, via slow_start.duration_seconds (30–900 s). If you are on open-source nginx, the readiness gate is your only ramp, so it has to be honest.

javascript · warmup.js — readiness that does not lie
import { performance, monitorEventLoopDelay } from 'node:perf_hooks';

let state = 'starting';   // starting | ready | draining
export const isReady = () => state === 'ready';
export const setDraining = () => { state = 'draining'; };

export async function warmUp({ mongo, redis }) {
  const t0 = performance.now();

  // 1. Force the pools open. minPoolSize fills in the background; a ping per intended
  //    connection makes the process WAIT for them instead of a user's request waiting.
  await Promise.all(
    Array.from({ length: 10 }, () => mongo.db().command({ ping: 1 })),
  );
  await redis.ping();

  // 2. Touch the hot query paths so V8 tiers them up and, just as importantly, so the
  //    query planner cache and the index pages are populated. Use a query shape that
  //    matches production traffic — a count(*) warms nothing that matters.
  for (let i = 0; i < 50; i++) {
    await mongo.db().collection('products')
      .find({ status: 'active' }).sort({ rank: -1 }).limit(20).toArray();
  }

  // 3. Serialise a representative payload. JSON.stringify on a 40 KB object is one of
  //    the biggest first-request costs and it is trivially warmable.
  const sample = await mongo.db().collection('products').findOne({});
  for (let i = 0; i < 200; i++) JSON.stringify(sample);

  state = 'ready';
  console.log(JSON.stringify({ msg: 'warm', ms: Math.round(performance.now() - t0) }));
}

// Liveness and readiness are DIFFERENT questions and must be different endpoints.
// Liveness: is this process wedged and in need of a kill? Answer from the event loop.
// Readiness: should this process receive traffic right now? Answer from warm + not draining.
const lag = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 10 });
lag.enable();

export function mountProbes(app) {
  app.get('/livez', (_req, res) => {
    // Deliberately NOT checking Mongo here. A dependency outage must not make every
    // pod fail liveness and get killed — that turns a database blip into a full
    // CrashLoopBackOff outage with no capacity left when the database recovers.
    const wedged = lag.mean / 1e6 > 2000;
    res.status(wedged ? 500 : 200).json({ loopMeanMs: +(lag.mean / 1e6).toFixed(1) });
  });

  app.get('/readyz', (_req, res) => {
    res.status(isReady() ? 200 : 503).json({ state });
  });
}

Autoscaling on a signal that means something

CPU is the default autoscaling metric and it is close to useless for an I/O-bound Node service. A process that spends its time awaiting MongoDB and Redis sits at 25–35% CPU while its event loop is fully saturated and its latency is climbing — because the work is waiting, not computing. Scaling on targetCPUUtilizationPercentage: 70 means you never scale out until something else has already broken, and it also means a GC-heavy moment triggers a scale-out that does not help. Pick a signal that tracks the queue rather than the arithmetic.

Autoscaling signals for a Node API tier, ranked by how early they move
SignalUse it whenTrap
RPS per podDefault choice. Requests are roughly uniform in cost. Load-test to find the RPS at which p95 breaks, then target 60–70% of it.Mixed workloads. One expensive report endpoint at 2 RPS can saturate a pod that handles 400 RPS of reads, and the metric shows the pod as idle.
Event loop utilisationMixed request costs. It is the truest measure of "how busy is this process really". Scale above 0.6–0.7.It saturates at 1.0 and stays there, so it tells you that you are overloaded but not by how much. Pair it with RPS for the scaling magnitude.
Queue depth (jobs waiting, pool checkout waiters)Worker tiers and anything consuming a broker. Depth is linear in the deficit, so the controller's arithmetic actually works.Scaling workers when the bottleneck is the database just moves the queue from the broker into the connection pool.
p95 latencyAs a guardrail, never as the primary signal.It is a lagging, non-linear signal: flat from 40% to 70% utilisation, then vertical. And it rises when a dependency is slow, where adding pods makes things worse.
CPUGenuinely CPU-bound work — image processing, crypto, heavy serialisation.Everything above. Also container CPU is throttle-based: a pod hitting its CFS quota shows 100% of limit, not of the node.

Whatever the signal, the policy is asymmetric: scale out fast, scale in slowly. Adding a pod that turns out to be unnecessary costs a few cents; removing one thirty seconds before the next peak costs an outage. A stabilisation window of 0 on scale-up and 300 s on scale-down, with a scale-down policy capped at roughly 10% of the fleet per minute, stops the flapping that otherwise emerges when the metric oscillates around the target.

yaml · hpa + pod lifecycle — the parts that actually matter
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment, name: api }
  minReplicas: 6          # 3 AZs x 2, so losing one AZ never drops below 4
  maxReplicas: 60         # a real ceiling: 60 pods x 20 conns = 1200 Mongo connections
  metrics:
    - type: Pods          # RPS per pod, from the Prometheus adapter
      pods:
        metric: { name: http_requests_per_second }
        target: { type: AverageValue, averageValue: "180" }   # 65% of the 280 RPS breaking point
    - type: Pods          # event-loop utilisation catches the expensive-request case
      pods:
        metric: { name: nodejs_eventloop_utilization }
        target: { type: AverageValue, averageValue: "650m" }  # 0.65
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 0     # react to the spike immediately
      policies: [{ type: Percent, value: 100, periodSeconds: 30 }]   # can double every 30 s
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300   # must be calm for 5 minutes before shrinking
      policies: [{ type: Percent, value: 10, periodSeconds: 60 }]    # bleed off slowly
---
# The pod spec half. Without the preStop sleep, the HPA scaling IN causes 502s.
spec:
  terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 60     # MUST exceed preStop sleep + longest in-flight request
  containers:
    - name: api
      readinessProbe:
        httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 8080 }
        periodSeconds: 2
        failureThreshold: 2             # out of rotation ~4 s after we start draining
      livenessProbe:
        httpGet: { path: /livez, port: 8080 }
        periodSeconds: 10
        failureThreshold: 6             # 60 s of wedge before a kill; do NOT make this tight
      lifecycle:
        preStop:
          exec: { command: ["sleep", "15"] }   # see the race described below

Scale-in safety: draining without dropping requests

Scaling out is the easy direction. Scaling in — and every rolling deploy is a scale-in followed by a scale-out — is where a stateless tier still loses requests, because of one race that is not obvious from the documentation. When a pod is deleted, two things happen concurrently and unordered: the kubelet sends SIGTERM to the container, and the endpoints controller removes the pod from the Service, which then has to propagate to every kube-proxy, every ingress controller and every load balancer target group. Propagation takes seconds. If the process exits on SIGTERM in 50 ms, traffic keeps arriving at a socket nobody is listening on for the next five to fifteen seconds, and the balancer returns GET 502 Bad Gateway — a burst of them, on every deploy, blamed on "the deploy" and never fixed.

t=0 kubelet sends SIGTERM ─┬─ endpoints controller starts removing the pod │ (these two are concurrent and unordered) t=0 preStop sleep 15 begins │ readiness flips to 503 └──► kube-proxy / ALB still sending traffic ◄── the race t≈4 s probe failed twice ─► pulled from rotation t≈6 s ALB target marked draining, new connections stop t=15 s sleep ends ─► server.close() ─► no new connections, in-flight allowed to finish t≈16 s closeIdleConnections() ─► keep-alive sockets with no request in flight are cut (without this, server.close() waits for the client's 5 s keepAliveTimeout) t≈18 s last in-flight response written ─► close Mongo, Redis, flush metrics ─► exit 0 t=60 s terminationGracePeriodSeconds ─► SIGKILL (if you are here, you have a bug)

The sequence has one non-negotiable ordering rule: fail readiness first, wait, then stop accepting. The preStop sleep exists purely to give endpoint removal time to propagate; it is not a hack, it is the documented remedy for an eventually-consistent control plane. Fifteen seconds is a reasonable default and should be measured — turn on access logging at the balancer and find the last request that reached a terminating pod.

preStop sleep
15 scovers endpoint propagation to every proxy and target group
Readiness out
~4 speriodSeconds 2 × failureThreshold 2
In-flight drain
p99.9 + 2 smeasure it; do not guess the longest request
Grace period
60 smust exceed sleep + drain, or SIGKILL truncates responses
javascript · shutdown.js — the ordering, and the keep-alive trap
import { setDraining } from './warmup.js';

export function installShutdown({ server, mongo, redis, log }) {
  let shuttingDown = false;

  async function drain(signal) {
    if (shuttingDown) return;          // SIGTERM can be delivered more than once
    shuttingDown = true;
    log.info({ signal }, 'drain: start');

    // STEP 1 — stop being ready. This is the ONLY thing that happens immediately.
    // The process keeps serving normally; it is just telling the orchestrator to
    // take it out of rotation. Doing anything else first is what causes 502s.
    setDraining();

    // STEP 2 — do nothing, on purpose, while endpoint removal propagates. In k8s the
    // preStop sleep covers this; keep a shorter belt-and-braces wait here for
    // environments (bare ECS, a hand-rolled ASG) where there is no preStop hook.
    await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, Number(process.env.DRAIN_DELAY_MS ?? 5000)));

    // STEP 3 — stop accepting new connections. server.close() does NOT abort in-flight
    // requests; it stops the listener and fires the callback when the last one ends.
    await new Promise((resolve) => {
      server.close((err) => { if (err) log.warn({ err }, 'close'); resolve(); });

      // THE TRAP: server.close() also waits for IDLE keep-alive sockets, which linger
      // until the client's keepAliveTimeout (5 s by default, often much longer behind
      // a proxy). Node >= 18.2 exposes closeIdleConnections() — without it, shutdown
      // routinely hangs for the full grace period and ends in SIGKILL.
      server.closeIdleConnections();
      // Re-run it as requests finish, so sockets that go idle mid-drain are also cut.
      const sweep = setInterval(() => server.closeIdleConnections(), 500);
      sweep.unref();

      // Hard ceiling. If in-flight work has not finished, closeAllConnections() cuts
      // it — deliberately, and with a log line, rather than being SIGKILLed silently.
      setTimeout(() => {
        log.error('drain: in-flight requests exceeded budget, forcing close');
        server.closeAllConnections();
      }, 20_000).unref();
    });

    // STEP 4 — only now tear down dependencies. Closing the Mongo client before
    // in-flight requests finish turns a clean drain into a burst of
    // "MongoNotConnectedError: Client must be connected before running operations".
    await Promise.allSettled([mongo.close(false), redis.quit()]);
    log.info('drain: complete');
    process.exit(0);
  }

  process.on('SIGTERM', () => drain('SIGTERM'));
  process.on('SIGINT',  () => drain('SIGINT'));
}
Two settings that quietly cause the last 502s

Set server.keepAliveTimeout higher than the load balancer's idle timeout (AWS ALB defaults to 60 s, so use 65 s) and server.headersTimeout higher still. If Node closes an idle keep-alive socket the balancer still considers usable, the balancer sends a request into a closing connection and reports 502. This is the classic residual 502 rate — a fraction of a percent, constant, uncorrelated with load — that survives every other fix on this page.

Put together, the property is simple to state and cheap to keep: no request depends on a previous request having touched this process; every schedule has exactly one owner; readiness means warm; and shutdown drains before it closes. Services that hold that line scale by editing one number. Services that do not, scale by planning a migration.

36

Load balancing

A load balancer does not spread traffic evenly; it makes a routing decision thousands of times a second using information that is always slightly stale. Everything that hurts — the fleet ejected by its own health check, the 502 nobody logged, the retry storm that turned a slow query into an outage — comes from a mismatch between what the balancer believes about a backend and what is true.

Layer 4 and layer 7: what each is allowed to know

The distinction is not about speed, it is about what the balancer can read before it commits. An L4 balancer sees a TCP connection: five-tuple, SYN, bytes. It picks a backend at connection time and every byte thereafter goes to that backend, whatever those bytes turn out to be. An L7 balancer terminates the connection, parses HTTP, and picks a backend per request — which means it can route on path, method, header or cookie, retry a failed request on another backend, and rewrite what it forwards.

That per-request granularity is the whole game once you have HTTP/2 or gRPC. A single HTTP/2 connection multiplexes hundreds of streams; under an L4 balancer they all land on one backend for the life of the connection. Ten clients and twenty backends will happily leave fifteen backends idle while three saturate. The fixes are all L7-shaped: put an L7 proxy in the path so streams are balanced individually, or force connection churn server-side (gRPC's MAX_CONNECTION_AGE plus a grace period, typically 30 minutes with 5 seconds of grace) so the client periodically re-resolves and redistributes.

The same three questions, answered differently at each layer. "Cannot" here means cannot without terminating and re-implementing the layer above.
L4 (NLB, IPVS, HAProxy in mode tcp)L7 (ALB, nginx, Envoy, HAProxy in mode http)
Unit of balancingThe connection. One decision, then it is bound for minutes.The request. A new decision every time, including inside one keep-alive connection.
Can it retry?No. A backend that resets mid-response gives the client a broken connection.Yes, if the request is idempotent and nothing has been written to the client yet.
SeesIP, port, TLS SNI (bytes only). Client IP preserved natively.Method, path, headers, cookies, body size. Client IP must be re-injected as X-Forwarded-For.
CostMicroseconds, millions of connections per node, no TLS work.Hundreds of microseconds to low milliseconds, plus TLS termination and buffers.
Health check depthTCP connect succeeded. Says nothing about whether the app can serve.An HTTP status on a path you choose — the only check worth trusting.
Right forNon-HTTP protocols, extreme packet rates, static ports, TLS passthrough to the app, preserving source IP for free.Anything HTTP. This is the default answer for a Node API.

An L4 TCP health check is the sharpest edge here. It proves that the kernel accepted a SYN, which a Node process will do while the event loop is blocked, while Mongoose is buffering every query, and while every response is a 500. Ports answer long after services stop working.

Algorithms, and the failure each one owns

Every algorithm optimises for one assumption. Pick the one whose assumption matches your traffic and you get a flat p99 across the fleet; pick the wrong one and you get one instance at 90% CPU next to one at 12%.

AlgorithmAssumption it makesWhere it breaksKnob
Round robinAll requests cost roughly the same.The moment they do not. A CSV export and a GET on a cached key are one tick each; an instance that received three exports in a row is saturated while its neighbour idles. Also blind to instance size unless weighted.nginx default; ALB default
Least connections (least outstanding requests)Outstanding work is the best available proxy for load.A backend that fails instantly has zero outstanding requests, so it wins every decision — see the black-hole warning below. Also degrades to round robin when responses are uniformly fast.least_conn; balance leastconn
Least response time (EWMA)Recent latency predicts near-future latency.Reacts to noise, and a backend that has just been ejected and re-added has no samples. Needs damping; a bare average over a 5-second window oscillates.least_time (nginx Plus); Linkerd's default
Power of two choicesTwo random samples get you nearly the best of N with O(1) state.Almost nothing — this is why Envoy's LEAST_REQUEST is P2C rather than a global scan. With many independent balancer nodes, an exact global least-conn causes herding; P2C does not.Envoy choice_count: 2
Weighted variantsYou know the relative capacity of each instance.Weights are set once and go stale after the next instance-type change. Automate them from CPU count or let slow start handle new arrivals instead.weight=3
Consistent hashRouting the same key to the same backend is worth more than even distribution.Distribution is balls-in-bins, so a hot key is a hot instance and no algorithm can move it. See below.hash $k consistent

For a Node API serving a mix of cheap reads and expensive aggregations, least outstanding requests is the correct default and it is not close. Node's concurrency is bounded by one thread; the number of requests currently in flight on an instance is its queue depth, which is exactly what you want to minimise. Round robin cannot see that queue at all.

The fail-fast black hole

One instance loses its Mongo connection and starts returning 500 in 3 ms while healthy peers take 40 ms. Under least connections it now has the fewest outstanding requests by a wide margin, so the balancer preferentially routes to it — the broken instance attracts traffic in proportion to how badly it is broken.
