Error rate goes to 30% with 1 instance out of 20 unhealthy, and the health check has not fired yet because its interval is 30 seconds. The defence is not a different algorithm; it is outlier detection (eject on consecutive 5xx, not on a periodic probe) plus never returning an error faster than you return a success on the path the balancer measures.

Consistent hashing: the ring, virtual nodes, and why a modulo is not enough

Hash the key, take modulo N, done — and then N changes from 8 to 9 and roughly 89% of your keys map somewhere new. For cache affinity that is a full cold start on every deploy; for a sharded stateful tier it is a reshuffle of nearly everything. Consistent hashing exists to make that number 1/N instead.

The construction is small: treat the 32-bit hash space as a ring, place each backend at several positions on it, and route a key to the first backend position clockwise from the key's own hash. Remove a backend and only the arcs it owned become vacant; those keys move to the next node clockwise, and every other key is untouched. The virtual nodes matter more than people expect — with one position per backend, arc lengths are wildly uneven and you routinely see 2× between the busiest and quietest node. With 150–200 positions each, the spread tightens to a few per cent.

javascript · ring.js — a hash ring you can also use for client-side sharding
const { createHash } = require('node:crypto');

// MD5 is used here for speed and uniform distribution, not for security. What
// matters is that it is STABLE: every balancer, every worker and every client
// must compute an identical ring, so never use a randomly seeded hash and never
// use Node's object iteration order as an input.
const h32 = (s) => createHash('md5').update(s).digest().readUInt32BE(0);

class HashRing {
  // 160 virtual nodes per backend is the common choice (memcached clients ship
  // with it). With vnodes = 1 the arc lengths are exponentially distributed and
  // the hottest backend takes ~2x the coldest; at 160 the spread is ~5%.
  constructor(nodes = [], vnodes = 160) {
    this.vnodes = vnodes;
    this.ring = [];                 // sorted [{ pos, node }]
    for (const n of nodes) this.add(n, /* rebuild */ false);
    this.#sort();
  }

  add(node, rebuild = true) {
    for (let i = 0; i < this.vnodes; i++) {
      // The vnode label must be derived only from the node's STABLE identity.
      // Hashing a pod IP means every reschedule reshuffles the ring - use the
      // StatefulSet ordinal, the shard name, or a registered node id.
      this.ring.push({ pos: h32(`${node}#${i}`), node });
    }
    if (rebuild) this.#sort();
  }

  remove(node) {
    this.ring = this.ring.filter((e) => e.node !== node);
    // No re-sort needed: filtering preserves order. Only this node's arcs are
    // freed, so ~1/N of the keyspace moves and it SCATTERS across survivors
    // rather than piling onto one neighbour - that is the whole point.
  }

  get(key) {
    if (this.ring.length === 0) return null;
    const p = h32(key);
    // Binary search for the first position >= p, wrapping at the end of the ring.
    let lo = 0, hi = this.ring.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
      const mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
      if (this.ring[mid].pos < p) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
    }
    return this.ring[this.ring[lo].pos < p ? 0 : lo].node;
  }

  #sort() { this.ring.sort((a, b) => a.pos - b.pos); }
}

// Verify the reshuffle claim rather than believing it. Removing 1 of 8 nodes
// should move ~12.5% of keys; a modulo scheme moves ~89%.
const ring = new HashRing(['a', 'b', 'c', 'd', 'e', 'f', 'g', 'h']);
const keys = Array.from({ length: 100_000 }, (_, i) => `user:${i}`);
const before = keys.map((k) => ring.get(k));
ring.remove('d');
const moved = keys.filter((k, i) => ring.get(k) !== before[i]).length;
console.log(`${(moved / keys.length * 100).toFixed(1)}% moved`);   // ~12.4%

At the balancer you rarely write this yourself: nginx gives you hash $cookie_sid consistent or hash $arg_tenant consistent, HAProxy balance hash with hash-type consistent, and Envoy offers two rings — RING_HASH (tunable, minimum_ring_size defaults to 1024 which is too small for even distribution; raise it) and MAGLEV (a fixed 65 537-entry lookup table, constant-time and cheaper to build, at the cost of slightly more disruption on membership change). You do write it yourself the moment you shard a cache tier, a WebSocket fan-out, or a set of stateful workers from inside Node.

One caveat that is easy to miss: plain consistent hashing has no notion of capacity, so a viral tenant pins to exactly one backend and stays there. Envoy and HAProxy implement bounded-load variants (Envoy's hash_balance_factor, default 100 meaning "no host may exceed the mean") that spill overflow to the next node on the ring. Turn it on; affinity is an optimisation, and an optimisation should never be the reason one box melts.

Health checks: three different questions

Most fleets have one endpoint called /health wired to everything, and it is the wrong number of endpoints. There are three questions with three different consequences.

Liveness — "is this process wedged?"
The only correct response to failure is restart. It must therefore test nothing but the process itself: can the event loop still schedule a callback. A liveness probe that touches Mongo turns a database blip into a fleet-wide restart, which discards every warm connection pool and JIT-optimised code path at exactly the worst moment.
Readiness — "should this instance receive traffic right now?"
The response to failure is remove from rotation, reversibly. This is what the load balancer polls. It reflects the ability to serve: booted, pool established, not draining, not overloaded past a shed threshold.
Startup — "has it finished booting?"
Exists purely to stop liveness from killing a slow starter. It permits a long total window (30 failures × 2 s) while keeping the liveness period tight afterwards, so a genuinely wedged process is still killed in 30 seconds rather than 60.
Dependency status — "what does this instance think of the world?"
Reported on a separate, unauthenticated-but-internal endpoint that nothing automated acts on. This is for humans and dashboards. It is the deep check, and it must never be the routing check.
How a deep health check takes down a fleet that was working

/health runs db.admin().ping() and a SET against Redis. Mongo has a 20-second election. Every one of the 40 instances fails its readiness probe in the same two-second window because they all check the same dependency, so the target group empties and the balancer has nothing to route to. Meanwhile 90% of your endpoints were served entirely from cache and would have been fine. Two extra bites: the probe traffic itself is amplified — ALB health-checks come from every load-balancer node in every enabled subnet, so a 10-second interval across 6 nodes is a check every 1.7 s per target, and a check that opens a database connection multiplies that into the connection pool — and recovery is slower than failure, because healthy_threshold is usually higher than unhealthy_threshold. The one mercy is that most balancers fail open: ALB routes to all targets when every target in a group is unhealthy, and Envoy has a healthy_panic_threshold (default 50%) below which it ignores health status entirely. Know whether yours does, because that behaviour is the difference between degraded and dark.

javascript · health.js — three endpoints, three answers, none of them a database call
const { monitorEventLoopDelay } = require('node:perf_hooks');
const loop = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 20 });
loop.enable();

let phase = 'starting';        // starting -> ready -> draining
let inflight = 0;
app.use((req, res, next) => { inflight++; res.on('close', () => inflight--); next(); });

// Driver topology events give us dependency state for FREE and asynchronously.
// The probe then reads a variable instead of issuing I/O, so it cannot queue
// behind a saturated pool and cannot add load to a struggling database.
let mongoUp = false;
mongoClient.on('topologyDescriptionChanged', (e) => {
  mongoUp = [...e.newDescription.servers.values()]
    .some((s) => s.type === 'RSPrimary' || s.type === 'Standalone' || s.type === 'Mongos');
});

// LIVENESS. No dependencies, no auth, no middleware that can throw. The only
// failure that should be reported here is one a restart actually fixes: an
// event loop that has been blocked long enough that this process is useless.
app.get('/healthz', (req, res) => {
  const maxLagMs = loop.max / 1e6;               // histogram is in nanoseconds
  loop.reset();
  if (maxLagMs > 5_000) return res.status(503).send('event loop blocked');
  res.type('text/plain').send('ok');
});

// READINESS. What the load balancer polls. Local facts only. Note that Mongo
// appears here but as a CACHED flag, and only because this service cannot serve
// a single useful response without it - if 80% of your traffic is cache hits,
// leave it out and let those responses keep flowing.
app.get('/readyz', (req, res) => {
  if (phase !== 'ready')   return res.status(503).json({ phase });
  if (!mongoUp)            return res.status(503).json({ reason: 'no primary' });
  // Deliberate self-shedding: past this depth we are queueing, not serving, and
  // a balancer with other options should use them. Set it well ABOVE normal
  // peak concurrency, and never let every instance cross it at once - that is
  // the fleet-ejection failure mode wearing a different hat.
  if (inflight > 400)      return res.status(503).json({ reason: 'overloaded', inflight });
  res.json({ ok: true, inflight });
});

// DEEP CHECK. Humans and dashboards only. Never wire this to a probe, and give
// every dependency its own short timeout so the endpoint itself cannot hang.
app.get('/internal/dependencies', async (req, res) => {
  const probe = (name, p) => Promise.race([
    p.then(() => ({ name, ok: true }), (e) => ({ name, ok: false, err: e.message })),
    new Promise((r) => setTimeout(() => r({ name, ok: false, err: 'timeout' }), 800)),
  ]);
  res.json({
    checks: await Promise.all([
      probe('mongo', mongoClient.db().admin().ping()),
      probe('redis', redis.ping()),
      probe('payments', fetch(PAYMENTS_HEALTH, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(700) })),
    ]),
  });
});
Probe hygiene that pays for itself

Exempt the probe paths from rate limiting, from authentication, and from the access log (access_log off for that location) — otherwise 6 balancer nodes × 40 pods × 2 checks/second is 480 log lines a second of nothing. Bind them so they are unreachable from the internet, or you have published your dependency topology. And check the probe's own timeout against reality: a default 5-second health-check timeout on an endpoint that is served by the same single thread as everything else will fail during a GC pause, so measure the p99 of /readyz like any other endpoint.

Draining: the last five minutes of an instance's life

Removing an instance is not an event, it is a protocol, and the balancer owns half of it. The failure mode is universally the same — 502s and truncated responses during every deploy — and it is always an ordering bug.

  1. Fail readiness first, keep serving. On SIGTERM the process flips phase = 'draining' and starts returning 503 from /readyz, but continues to serve normal requests. Closing the listener first is the classic mistake: the balancer is still sending you traffic and now nothing answers it.
  2. Wait out the detection window. The balancer needs interval × unhealthy_threshold to notice, and then its own propagation delay. With ALB defaults (30 s interval, threshold 2) that is up to 60 seconds during which requests still arrive. This is why the drain sleep is not optional and why 5 seconds is not enough.
  3. Stop accepting, then close idle sockets. server.close() stops new connections but leaves established keep-alive sockets open until they idle out — on Node 18.2+ call server.closeIdleConnections(), and set Connection: close on responses while draining so the balancer stops reusing the socket for its next request.
  4. Tune the deregistration delay to your longest request, not to the default. ALB's deregistration_delay.timeout_seconds defaults to 300 s. Too long and every scale-in and deploy holds capacity for five minutes; too short and the balancer forcibly closes an in-flight download or SSE stream. Set it to just above your p99.9 request duration — typically 30–60 s for an API, and much higher only if you stream.

Timeouts must decrease inwards

There is one rule, and violating it is the most reliable way to convert a slow dependency into an outage: every hop's timeout must be shorter than the timeout of the hop that called it. When an inner timeout exceeds an outer one, the caller gives up and retries while the original work is still running. You now have two copies of the same query, then four, then eight. The database gets slower, so more requests hit the timeout, so more retries are issued — a retry storm, and it is self-sustaining well after the original trigger has cleared.

correct — each hop gives up before its caller does browser 30 s ──► CDN 20 s ──► ALB idle 60 s / target 15 s ──► node deadline 8 s │ ┌───────────────────────────┴────────────┐ upstream HTTP 2.5 s mongo maxTimeMS 2 s (1 retry, only if >3 s of budget remains) socketTimeout 3 s broken — the inner hop outlives its caller ALB target timeout 10 s ──► node mongo query no maxTimeMS (runs 40 s, unstoppable) ▲ │ └── client retries at 10 s, 20 s, 30 s ────────┘ 4 copies of one query, all still running
Retry amplification
27×3 retries at 3 layers; each layer looks reasonable alone
Retry budget
10%cap retries as a share of traffic, not as a per-call count
ALB idle default
60 sNode's keepAliveTimeout must exceed it — see below
Deregistration
300 sthe default; almost always wrong in both directions

Per-call retry counts multiply; retry budgets do not. Envoy's retry_budget and Finagle's original design both cap retries at a percentage of successful traffic (10% is the usual figure), so a broadly failing dependency cannot be retried into the ground no matter how many call sites ask. Combine it with three rules: only retry idempotent requests, only retry when the remaining deadline exceeds the p99 of the call, and always add jitter — synchronised retries from 40 instances arrive as a single spike.

javascript · deadline.js — one budget per request, decremented at every hop
// The app's own ceiling. MUST be below the balancer's response timeout, so that
// WE produce the 504 (with a request id, a log line and a metric) rather than
// the balancer producing one we cannot correlate with anything.
const REQUEST_BUDGET_MS = 8_000;

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  // Honour an inbound deadline from an upstream caller, and NEVER extend it.
  // Without this, a chain of 5 services each allowing 8 s permits 40 s of work
  // for a client that walked away after 10.
  const inbound = Number(req.get('x-deadline-ms'));
  const budget = Number.isFinite(inbound) && inbound > 0
    ? Math.min(inbound, REQUEST_BUDGET_MS)
    : REQUEST_BUDGET_MS;
  req.deadline = Date.now() + budget;
  next();
});

// Reserve leaves room to serialise and flush the response. Timing out with zero
// reserve means the work finishes and the client still gets nothing.
const remaining = (req, reserve = 200) => Math.max(0, req.deadline - Date.now() - reserve);

async function callPricing(req, body) {
  const ms = remaining(req);
  if (ms < 300) throw Object.assign(new Error('no budget left'), { status: 504 });

  return fetch(PRICING_URL, {
    method: 'POST',
    body: JSON.stringify(body),
    // Propagate the SHRINKING budget so the callee cannot outlive us.
    headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json', 'x-deadline-ms': String(ms) },
    signal: AbortSignal.timeout(Math.min(ms, 2_500)),
  });
}

// maxTimeMS is the only timeout the SERVER honours: it aborts the operation
// inside mongod. socketTimeoutMS merely hangs up on the client side while the
// query keeps burning CPU on the primary - which is exactly how a retry storm
// stacks four copies of the same collection scan.
const order = await Order.findById(id).maxTimeMS(Math.min(remaining(req), 2_000));

// The keep-alive contract with the balancer. The RECEIVER of a pooled socket
// must hold it open LONGER than the sender is willing to reuse it. Node's
// default keepAliveTimeout is 5 s; ALB's idle timeout is 60 s. That mismatch
// means ALB regularly writes a request into a socket Node is closing in the
// same millisecond - the client gets a 502, and there is NOTHING in the app log
// because the request never reached a handler.
const server = app.listen(3000);
server.keepAliveTimeout = 65_000;   // strictly greater than the LB idle timeout
server.headersTimeout   = 66_000;   // must exceed keepAliveTimeout
server.requestTimeout   = 30_000;   // hard cap on a single request's lifetime
The 502 with no stack trace

If you see HTTPCode_ELB_502_Count rising with a flat HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count, a matching climb in TargetResetCount, and no errors in the application log at all, this is the idle-timeout race and nothing else. It presents as a low, steady 0.01–0.5% error rate that correlates with traffic volume rather than with any deploy, so it survives for months as "flaky clients". The rule generalises beyond AWS: nginx keepalive_timeout in the upstream block, an Envoy cluster's idle_timeout, and a Kubernetes ingress annotation all have to sit below Node's keepAliveTimeout. Check the ordering after every infrastructure change, because raising the balancer's idle timeout to 120 s to support long polling silently re-opens the hole.

TLS termination, re-encryption, and mTLS between hops

Terminating TLS at the balancer is nearly always right: it moves handshakes off Node's single thread (an RSA-2048 handshake is a few milliseconds of pure CPU, and at 2 000 new connections per second that is more than one core doing nothing else), it centralises certificate rotation, and it is the only way an L7 balancer can read a request at all. What matters is what happens on the second hop.

Terminate and forward plaintext

Fine when the hop is a loopback interface or a VPC you control and your compliance regime allows it. The app must then bind to a private address only, and trust proxy must be set to an exact hop count — with plaintext behind the balancer, anything that can reach the port can forge X-Forwarded-For and X-Forwarded-Proto.

Terminate and re-encrypt

The balancer opens a new TLS session to the backend. You pay the handshake twice, but the backend certificate can be a long-lived internal one and most balancers do not verify it by default — ALB explicitly does not validate the target's certificate, so re-encryption without a trust store buys confidentiality, not authenticity.

Passthrough

L4 only. The app terminates, so you keep end-to-end TLS and lose every L7 capability: no path routing, no retries, no header injection, and client IP now requires the PROXY protocol. Choose it for regulatory reasons or for non-HTTP protocols, not for convenience.

mTLS between hops is the version that actually proves identity: each side presents a certificate and verifies the other, so a compromised pod cannot impersonate the payments service simply by being on the network. Doing it by hand means a certificate authority, short-lived leaf certificates (SPIFFE-style identities expire in hours, not years), rotation without restarts, and revocation — which is precisely the bundle a service mesh automates, and precisely why meshes are usually adopted for security rather than for traffic management.

Slow start, outlier detection, and ejection

A newly added instance is the slowest instance in the fleet — cold connection pools, unoptimised JIT tiers, empty local caches. Least-connections sees an instance with zero outstanding requests and rewards it with a disproportionate share of traffic at the exact moment it can least handle it. The result is a latency spike on every scale-out and every deploy, blamed on "the deploy" rather than on the routing policy.

Slow start fixes it by ramping a new backend's weight from near zero to full over a window: ALB's slow_start.duration_seconds (30–900 s, disabled by default), nginx Plus's slow_start=30s, Envoy's slow_start_config. Thirty to sixty seconds is the usual figure for a Node service, and it composes with the readiness gate rather than replacing it — readiness decides whether, slow start decides how fast.

yaml · envoy — passive ejection is what actually catches a bad instance
clusters:
  - name: orders
    connect_timeout: 0.25s              # connecting is fast or it is broken
    lb_policy: LEAST_REQUEST            # P2C, not a global scan
    least_request_lb_config:
      choice_count: 2
      slow_start_config:
        slow_start_window: 60s          # ramp a new endpoint in over a minute

    # ACTIVE checking: a periodic probe. Necessary, but it detects a bad host in
    # interval x unhealthy_threshold = 10s at best, during which real user
    # requests keep landing on it.
    health_checks:
      - timeout: 1s
        interval: 5s
        unhealthy_threshold: 2
        healthy_threshold: 3            # slower to trust than to distrust
        http_health_check: { path: /readyz }

    # PASSIVE checking: judges hosts by the traffic they are already serving, so
    # it reacts in milliseconds, not in probe intervals. This is what stops the
    # fail-fast black hole.
    outlier_detection:
      consecutive_5xx: 5
      interval: 10s
      base_ejection_time: 30s           # doubles on each successive ejection
      # The critical knob. Without a cap, a bad DEPENDENCY (every host returning
      # 503 because Mongo is down) ejects the entire cluster and you have turned
      # a degradation into an outage. 20% means you always keep 4 in 5.
      max_ejection_percent: 20
      # Distinguish "the app returned 503" from "the connection failed", so a
      # network fault and an application fault are not counted together.
      split_external_local_origin_errors: true
      consecutive_local_origin_failure: 3

    # Below this share of healthy hosts, Envoy ignores health status and routes
    # to EVERY host. Counter-intuitive and correct: if 70% of your fleet looks
    # unhealthy, the checker is more likely wrong than the fleet.
    common_lb_config:
      healthy_panic_threshold: { value: 50 }

    circuit_breakers:
      thresholds:
        - priority: DEFAULT
          max_connections: 1024
          max_pending_requests: 64      # the queue that sheds. The DEFAULT is
                                        # 1024, which is unbounded queueing and
                                        # shows up as p99 latency, not as errors
          max_retries: 3                # fleet-wide concurrent retries, not
                                        # per-request attempts

Global load balancing: anycast and GeoDNS

Once there is more than one region, something must choose between them, and the two mechanisms available have very different failure stories.

Anycast

One IP announced by BGP from many points of presence; the internet's own routing delivers packets to the topologically nearest one. Failover is withdrawing a route — tens of seconds, with no client involvement and no cache to wait out. Route changes mid-connection can break an established TCP session, which is rare in practice and is why anycast is universal for DNS and CDNs. It needs your own address space and BGP presence, so in practice you rent it from a CDN.

GeoDNS / latency-based routing

The resolver's location (not the client's, unless EDNS Client Subnet is in play) selects an answer. Cheap and available to anyone with Route 53 or Cloudflare. Failover costs a TTL — and TTLs are honoured badly: resolvers round short TTLs up, browsers pin a resolution for ~60 s regardless, and connection pools in your own SDKs may hold an IP for hours. Budget health-check detection + TTL + client cache, which is realistically 2–5 minutes even with a 60-second TTL.

The practical arrangement for most teams is both: anycast at the CDN edge for the front door and instant failover, GeoDNS for the regional API hostnames behind it, and a regional balancer inside each region doing all the work described above. Then test the failover, because the untested half is always the same — the failed-over region has a cold cache, a database replica in the wrong role, and a connection pool sized for its own share of traffic rather than for everyone's. A failover that shifts 100% of traffic to a region provisioned for 50% is a second outage with better paperwork.

When a service mesh earns its complexity

Everything in this chapter — P2C balancing, retry budgets, outlier detection, mTLS, slow start, per-route timeouts — is exactly what a mesh gives you uniformly across every service without each team implementing it in their own HTTP client. The question is only whether you need it uniformly.

The honest cost: a sidecar per pod at roughly 50–150 MB of memory and a fraction of a core, 0.5–2 ms of added p99 per hop and per side (so 1–4 ms for one service-to-service call), a control plane that is now a new outage surface, and a debugging vocabulary your on-call has to learn — an Envoy 503 with response flag UF (upstream connection failure), UO (circuit-breaker overflow), UC (upstream terminated the connection) or NR (no route configured) is a different investigation each time. There is a startup ordering trap too: an app container that opens connections before its sidecar is ready gets ECONNREFUSED at boot, which is why Istio has holdApplicationUntilProxyStarts and why Kubernetes 1.29's native sidecar containers matter.

It is justified when you have tens of services in more than one language (so a shared client library cannot be the answer), when mTLS everywhere is a compliance requirement rather than a preference, or when you need traffic splitting for canaries and per-service retry budgets as configuration rather than as code. It is not justified for six Node services that already share one HTTP client module. Start with an L7 balancer and a well-written client; adopt a mesh when you can name the specific capability you are buying.

Interview follow-up

“Traffic doubles and one instance out of twenty starts failing. Walk me through what the load balancer does.” The weak answer is “the health check removes it”. The strong answer names the gap: “The health check is the slow path — 30-second interval, threshold of 2, so up to a minute of user requests still land on a broken box. Worse, if it is failing fast — 500 in 3 ms — then under least-outstanding-requests it has the fewest in-flight requests of anything in the fleet and the balancer actively prefers it. So the mechanism I actually rely on is passive outlier detection: eject on five consecutive 5xx, base ejection 30 s, and crucially max_ejection_percent around 20, because if the failure is a shared dependency then every host trips at once and an uncapped ejection turns a degradation into a total outage. That is also why the readiness probe has to be shallow — cached driver state, not a live ping — and why the deep dependency check goes on a separate endpoint that nothing automated acts on. On the way back in, slow start ramps the host over 30 to 60 seconds, otherwise least-connections dumps traffic on the coldest instance in the fleet. And underneath all of it the timeout ordering has to hold: the client above the balancer, the balancer above the app deadline, the app deadline above maxTimeMS — with a deadline header shrinking at each hop and a retry budget capped at 10% of traffic, because three retries at three layers is 27× amplification and that is what turns a slow query into an incident.”

The balancer is where your architecture's assumptions are enforced or exposed. It is the only component that sees every instance at once, so it is the only one that can route around a failure — and the only one that can, through one deep health check or one inverted timeout, amplify a single sick dependency across the whole fleet in seconds. Configure it as if it were application code: shallow readiness, passive ejection with a cap, decreasing timeouts, slow start on the way in and a real drain on the way out. Every one of those is a line of configuration, and every one of them has an outage attached to its default.

37

Nginx & the reverse proxy tier

The reverse proxy exists so that your single-threaded process only ever sees a complete, well-formed request arriving on a warm connection from a fast client. Everything else — the handshake, the half-sent header, the 3 MB upload trickling in at 200 bytes a second, the client on hotel wifi that takes ninety seconds to read your response — is C code with an event loop you did not write and do not have to defend.

The division of labour, and why it is not arbitrary

The rule is one sentence: anything that costs wall-clock time but no decision belongs in front of Node. A TLS handshake, a gzip pass, a slow socket read — none of them need to know who the user is, and all of them either burn CPU or hold a request open. In Node, holding a request open is cheap but burning CPU is not, and the failure mode is not "that request is slow", it is "every request on that process is slow".

The proxy is not a performance optimisation. It is a blast-radius boundary around the event loop.
ConcernWhereWhy there and not the other place
TLS terminationnginxBoth use OpenSSL, but nginx keeps one shared:SSL session cache across all workers, staples OCSP once, and does the ECDHE maths on a thread that is not also running your handlers. A full P-256 handshake is roughly 0.5–1 ms of CPU; at 2 000 new connections/s that is a core, taken directly out of your request budget.
HTTP/2 and HTTP/3nginxh2 multiplexing and h3/QUIC over UDP are protocol work with no application content. Terminate them at the edge and speak boring HTTP/1.1 with keep-alive to Node, which is the transport its http module is fastest and least surprising on.
CompressionnginxNode's zlib runs on the libuv threadpool — four threads by default, shared with fs and DNS. Gzipping a 200 KB JSON response is ~2–3 ms; do four of those concurrently and your readFile and DNS lookups queue behind them. In nginx it is per-worker and never touches your I/O.
Static assetsnginxsendfile plus tcp_nopush is a kernel-space copy with zero user-space buffers. express.static is a stream through V8. One nginx worker serves tens of thousands of small files per second per core; Express does single-digit thousands and pollutes your latency histogram while it does.
Request/response bufferingnginxThe whole point. See below — this is the single most valuable thing the tier does.
Crude rate and connection limitsnginxA 429 that costs one leaky-bucket increment in shared memory instead of a full trip through your middleware stack, JWT verification and a Redis round trip.
Identity-aware limits, quotas, billingNodeAnything keyed on a tenant, plan or token needs to parse the token. Do not put auth logic in ngx_http_auth_request or Lua; you will end up with two implementations of your permission model. See the chapter on rate limiting.
Routing by path prefixEither — pick oneSplitting routes across location blocks and your Express router is how a 404 becomes a two-hour investigation. Keep the proxy's map coarse: one location / per service, no more.
client ──TLS──▶ ┌───────────────────── nginx worker ─────────────────────┐ │ 1 handshake ssl_session_cache, OCSP stapled │ │ 2 read headers client_header_timeout 5s ◀── slowloris │ │ 3 match host/loc SNI, normalise X-Forwarded-* │ │ 4 limit_req/conn 429 here costs Node nothing │ │ 5 read body client_max_body_size 2m ──▶ 413 │ │ buffered complete before upstream connect│ │ 6 proxy_cache HIT ──▶ response, upstream never woken │ └────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘ ▼ keepalive pool, HTTP/1.1 node :3000 sees only step 6 onwards

A production http block, annotated

nginx · /etc/nginx/nginx.conf — the http block
worker_processes auto;               # one per core; each has its own accept loop
worker_rlimit_nofile 65535;          # MUST exceed worker_connections x 2 (client + upstream
                                     # socket per request) or you get "worker_connections are
                                     # not enough" and nginx starts refusing accepts silently

events {
    worker_connections 16384;        # default is 512-1024, which is absurdly low for a proxy.
    multi_accept on;
}

http {
    # ---- client-side timeouts: the slowloris defence ----------------------------
    client_header_timeout 5s;        # default 60s. Time to send the ENTIRE request line +
    client_body_timeout   10s;       # headers, and the time between two body reads.
    send_timeout          10s;       # time between two successful writes TO the client.
    keepalive_timeout     65s;       # idle browser connection. Must be >= the LB idle timeout
    keepalive_requests    1000;      # in front of it, or the LB reuses a socket nginx just shut.
    reset_timedout_connection on;    # RST instead of FIN on timeout: frees the socket
                                     # immediately rather than parking it in FIN_WAIT.

    client_max_body_size    2m;      # default 1m. Exceeding it is a 413, generated by nginx
    client_body_buffer_size 128k;    # without ever contacting Node. Bodies larger than this
                                     # spill to client_body_temp on disk -- watch that mount.

    # ---- upstream-side timeouts -------------------------------------------------
    proxy_connect_timeout 2s;        # TCP connect only. If this ever fires, the box is gone;
                                     # 60s (the default) means 60s of held connections first.
    proxy_send_timeout    10s;
    proxy_read_timeout    30s;       # gap between two reads from Node. This is your real
                                     # request deadline at the edge -- keep it just above the
                                     # app's own timeout so the app wins and can log the reason.

    # ---- response buffering (see the section below before touching this) --------
    proxy_buffering        on;
    proxy_buffer_size      8k;       # holds the RESPONSE HEADERS ONLY. Too small and you get
                                     # "upstream sent too big header while reading response
                                     # header from upstream" -> 502. Fat JWT cookies do this.
    proxy_buffers          16 16k;   # 256k of body buffered in memory per request
    proxy_busy_buffers_size 32k;
    proxy_max_temp_file_size 0;      # never spool a huge response to disk; apply backpressure

    # ---- header normalisation ---------------------------------------------------
    proxy_http_version 1.1;
    proxy_set_header Connection "";  # THE line people forget. Without it nginx sends
                                     # "Connection: close" upstream and the keepalive pool
                                     # below is dead weight -- a fresh TCP handshake per request.
    proxy_set_header Host              $host;
    proxy_set_header X-Real-IP         $remote_addr;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For   $remote_addr;   # OVERWRITE at the trust boundary.
                                     # $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for APPENDS to whatever the
                                     # client sent, so a client can prepend a fake IP and
                                     # defeat your app-tier rate limiter. Only use the _add_
                                     # form on an INNER hop whose peer you control.
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Host  $host;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Port  $server_port;
    proxy_set_header X-Request-Id      $request_id;    # nginx's own 32-hex id; log it here
                                                       # AND in Node to stitch the two together.
    proxy_next_upstream error timeout; # NEVER add non_idempotent: it replays POSTs on a
                                       # different backend after a timeout. Double charges.

    # ---- compression ------------------------------------------------------------
    gzip on;
    gzip_comp_level 5;               # default is 1. Level 5 is the knee: ~3% worse ratio than
                                     # 9 for a third of the CPU. Above 6 you are buying nothing.
    gzip_min_length 512;             # default 20 bytes. Compressing a 100-byte JSON error
                                     # makes it BIGGER and costs a syscall.
    gzip_vary on;                    # emits Vary: Accept-Encoding -- without it any shared
                                     # cache will hand a gzipped body to a client that said
                                     # it could not decode one.
    gzip_proxied any;
    gzip_types application/json application/javascript text/css text/plain
               image/svg+xml application/manifest+json;   # text/html is always included
    gzip_static on;                  # serve foo.js.gz from disk if it exists (see static)
    # brotli on; brotli_comp_level 5; brotli_static on;   # ngx_brotli, ~15-20% smaller than
                                     # gzip on JSON. Not in mainline: build it or use a distro
                                     # package that has it. brotli_static is the real win.

    # ---- limiter zones ----------------------------------------------------------
    map $remote_addr $rl_key {
        default                    $binary_remote_addr;  # IPv4: 4 bytes, exact host
        "~^(?<p>([0-9a-f]+:){4})"  $p;   # IPv6: first four hextets = the /64. One user gets a
    }                                    # whole /64, so limiting per-address limits nothing.
                                         # Compressed forms fall through to exact-host, which
                                         # is the safe direction to be wrong in.
    limit_req_zone  $rl_key zone=api:10m   rate=20r/s;   # ~160k states per 10m
    limit_req_zone  $rl_key zone=login:10m rate=1r/s;
    limit_conn_zone $rl_key zone=perip:10m;
    limit_req_status  429;           # default is 503, which tells your monitoring you had an
    limit_conn_status 429;           # outage when you actually had a working rate limiter.
    limit_req_log_level warn;

    # ---- cache ------------------------------------------------------------------
    proxy_cache_path /var/cache/nginx/api levels=1:2 keys_zone=api_cache:100m
                     max_size=10g inactive=60m use_temp_path=off;
                     # 100m of keys_zone ~= 800k cached objects. use_temp_path=off avoids a
                     # cross-filesystem rename on every store, which is pure wasted I/O.

    log_format json escape=json '{"t":"$time_iso8601","id":"$request_id",'
        '"ip":"$remote_addr","m":"$request_method","u":"$uri","st":$status,'
        '"rt":$request_time,"urt":"$upstream_response_time","cache":"$upstream_cache_status",'
        '"bytes":$body_bytes_sent,"ua":"$http_user_agent"}';
        # $request_time includes reading the client's body and writing the response to a slow
        # client. $upstream_response_time does not. When they diverge, the client is the
        # problem, not your code -- that single comparison ends most "the API is slow" tickets.
    access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log json buffer=64k flush=5s;

    sendfile on; tcp_nopush on; tcp_nodelay on;
    server_tokens off;               # stop advertising the exact patch version to scanners
    include /etc/nginx/conf.d/*.conf;
}

The server block: TLS, HTTP/3 and the upstream pool

nginx · conf.d/api.conf
upstream api {
    least_conn;                      # round robin assumes uniform request cost. It is not.
    server 10.0.1.11:3000 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
    server 10.0.1.12:3000 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;

    keepalive 64;                    # idle upstream connections kept PER WORKER. With 8
                                     # workers that is up to 512 sockets to the backends --
                                     # size it against the backend's own connection budget.
    keepalive_timeout  60s;          # must be SHORTER than Node's server.keepAliveTimeout,
    keepalive_requests 1000;         # or Node closes a socket nginx is about to reuse and you
}                                    # get intermittent 502s with no application log line.

server {
    listen 443 ssl reuseport;        # reuseport gives each worker its own accept queue and
    listen 443 quic reuseport;       # removes the thundering herd on accept
    listen [::]:443 ssl;
    http2 on;                        # 1.25.1+ directive; older builds use `listen ... http2`
    http3 on;
    server_name api.example.com;

    ssl_certificate     /etc/letsencrypt/live/api.example.com/fullchain.pem;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/letsencrypt/live/api.example.com/privkey.pem;
    ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;
    ssl_prefer_server_ciphers off;   # TLS 1.3 clients order better than your cipher list does
    ssl_session_cache shared:SSL:50m;# ~200k sessions, shared across workers. Without `shared`
    ssl_session_timeout 1d;          # each worker caches separately and resumption rate drops
    ssl_session_tickets off;         # by 1/N. Tickets need a rotating key distributed across
    ssl_stapling on;                 # every node; off is the safe default for a small fleet.
    ssl_stapling_verify on;
    resolver 127.0.0.53 valid=300s ipv6=off;

    add_header Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains" always;
    add_header Alt-Svc 'h3=":443"; ma=86400' always;   # how a browser learns h3 exists

    location / {
        limit_req  zone=api burst=40 nodelay;
        limit_conn perip 20;         # caps ONE client's concurrent sockets. A browser opens 6;
                                     # a badly written SDK opens 200 and starves everyone else.
        proxy_pass http://api;
    }

    location = /auth/login {         # exact match: cheaper and unambiguous
        limit_req zone=login burst=5 nodelay;
        proxy_pass http://api;
    }

    location /v1/stream/ {           # SSE and chunked exports
        proxy_buffering off;         # forward every chunk immediately (see next section)
        proxy_cache off;
        proxy_read_timeout 1h;       # a long-lived SSE stream is idle by design
        chunked_transfer_encoding on;
        gzip off;                    # gzip buffers to fill a block; it will swallow your events
        proxy_pass http://api;
    }

    location = /healthz {
        access_log off;              # a probe every 2s from 6 LB nodes is 260k lines a day
        proxy_pass http://api;
    }

    location = /metrics {            # Prometheus scrape target: never public
        allow 10.0.0.0/8; deny all;
        proxy_pass http://api;
    }

    location = /nginx_status {       # stub_status: active connections, accepts, handled
        stub_status;                 # "handled" < "accepts" means you hit worker_connections
        allow 127.0.0.1; deny all;
        access_log off;
    }
}
The 502 storm that a deploy causes and a reload fixes

Hostnames inside an upstream block are resolved once, at start-up or reload, and never again — even when the DNS TTL is 30 seconds. Point nginx at api.internal backed by ECS, a Kubernetes headless Service or an internal NLB, redeploy, and nginx keeps opening connections to IP addresses that no longer exist. You get uniform 502s, nothing in the Node logs, and the "fix" is someone running nginx -s reload at 2 a.m. The workarounds, in order of preference: put a stable IP in front (an internal load balancer), use a variable so resolution happens per request — resolver 10.0.0.2 valid=10s; set $up http://api.internal:3000; proxy_pass $up$request_uri; (note that a variable in proxy_pass switches off automatic URI passing, so you must append $request_uri yourself, and you lose the keepalive pool), or template the IP list from service discovery and reload on change. NGINX Plus has server ... resolve;, which is precisely the feature you are paying for.

Buffering: the setting that decides whether slow clients can hurt you

With proxy_buffering on, nginx reads the response from Node as fast as Node can write it, into proxy_buffers and then to a temp file, and releases the upstream connection the moment the last byte is read. The client can then take ninety seconds to dribble that response down a 2G link, and Node has already moved on. With buffering off, nginx forwards chunk by chunk, which means the client's socket backpressure propagates all the way into your Node stream. A hundred slow clients become a hundred parked responses, a hundred sockets, and a hundred sets of pinned response buffers in your heap.

The same asymmetry exists on the request side: proxy_request_buffering on (the default) means a 50 MB upload is entirely absorbed by nginx before Express sees a byte. Turn it off only when you are streaming an upload straight to object storage and genuinely want the backpressure end to end.

Do not disable buffering globally to fix streaming

The usual sequence: someone ships Server-Sent Events, sees events arriving in 4 KB clumps instead of immediately, and sets proxy_buffering off; in the http block. Streaming works. Three weeks later p99 latency doubles under mobile traffic and nobody connects the two, because the metric that moved is $request_time while $upstream_response_time is unchanged. Scope the change to the streaming location, or better, let the application decide per response: nginx honours an X-Accel-Buffering: no header from upstream and disables buffering for that response only. Also disable gzip for those routes — the compressor holds bytes back waiting to fill a block, which looks exactly like buffering and survives every other fix you try.

javascript · the Node side of the contract
import express from 'express';
const app = express();

// One hop, and it is ours. `true` means "trust the leftmost X-Forwarded-For entry",
// which is attacker-supplied. `1` means "the last hop added it" -- since our nginx
// OVERWRITES the header, req.ip is now the real peer and nothing else.
app.set('trust proxy', 1);

// nginx already gzips. Doing it again in Node burns a libuv thread for nothing --
// and if you leave compression() mounted, responses get double-encoded whenever
// someone bypasses the proxy in staging. Delete the middleware, don't disable it.

// Adopt the proxy's request id so one grep spans both access logs.
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  req.id = req.get('x-request-id') ?? crypto.randomUUID();
  res.set('X-Request-Id', req.id);
  next();
});

app.get('/v1/stream/events', (req, res) => {
  res.set({
    'Content-Type': 'text/event-stream',
    'Cache-Control': 'no-cache, no-transform',   // no-transform also stops CDNs rewriting
    'X-Accel-Buffering': 'no',                   // per-response opt-out; nginx consumes this
  });                                            // header and does not forward it to the client
  res.flushHeaders();                            // send the 200 NOW, before the first event --
                                                 // otherwise nginx waits and so does the browser
  const t = setInterval(() => res.write(`data: ${Date.now()}\n\n`), 1000);
  // 'close' fires when the CLIENT goes away. nginx propagates that abort upstream
  // (proxy_ignore_client_abort is off by default) and logs status 499 -- a synthetic
  // code that means "client hung up", not an error you should page on.
  res.on('close', () => clearInterval(t));
});

const server = app.listen(3000);
// Must EXCEED the upstream keepalive_timeout in nginx (60s above). If Node closes first,
// nginx hands a request to a socket that is already going away: 502, no app log.
server.keepAliveTimeout = 75_000;
server.headersTimeout   = 80_000;   // must be greater than keepAliveTimeout or you have
                                    // simply moved the race, not fixed it

Slowloris, and why the default timeouts are an invitation

Slowloris opens a connection, sends GET / HTTP/1.1 and one header, then sends another header every forty seconds — forever. Against a stock nginx (client_header_timeout 60s) each connection costs the attacker almost nothing and holds a slot for as long as they like; against a bare Node server it holds a socket and a parser. The defence is not clever: give the client a short, absolute budget to finish talking, and make the connection cheap to reap.

Every timeout below is a gap timer, not a total-duration timer. A client sending one byte every four seconds never trips client_body_timeout 10s — that is what limit_conn and limit_rate_after are for.
DirectiveDefaultSet toWhat it stops
client_header_timeout60s5sSlowloris. No legitimate client takes five seconds to emit its request headers.
client_body_timeout60s10sSlow POST (R-U-Dead-Yet). Raise it only on genuine upload routes.
send_timeout60s10sA client that stops reading your response and leaves the socket buffer full.
keepalive_timeout75s65sIdle sockets. Must stay above the idle timeout of the LB in front (ALB defaults to 60s) so nginx is never the side that closes first.
limit_connnone20/IPThe real slowloris cap: one address cannot hold more than twenty slots however slowly it talks.
proxy_read_timeout60s30sA wedged Node handler holding an upstream socket open indefinitely.
worker_shutdown_timeoutunset (∞)30sOld workers refusing to die after a reload because a WebSocket is still attached.
bash · verify the tier rather than trusting the config
# 1. Syntax + file permissions, ALWAYS before a reload. Non-zero exit = do not proceed.
nginx -t && nginx -s reload

# 2. Slowloris: 1000 connections, one header every 10s. Watch nginx_status "active"
#    and confirm real requests still complete during the run.
slowhttptest -c 1000 -H -i 10 -r 200 -t GET -u https://api.example.com/ -x 24 -p 3

# 3. Prove keepalive to the upstream is actually on. If Connection:"" is missing you will
#    see a SYN per request here instead of a steady, small connection count.
ss -tnp state established '( dport = :3000 )' | wc -l

# 4. Confirm HTTP/3 is really negotiated (needs a curl built with HTTP/3 support).
curl -sI --http3-only https://api.example.com/healthz | head -1

# 5. Header size ceiling -- reproduce "upstream sent too big header" before a user does.
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' https://api.example.com/v1/me \
  -H "Cookie: session=$(head -c 12000 /dev/urandom | base64 -w0)"

# 6. Cache behaviour, read straight off the wire.
curl -sI https://api.example.com/v1/countries | grep -i -E 'x-cache|age|cache-control'

Static assets: pre-compressed, fingerprinted, immutable

Compressing the same bundle on every request is the most common wasted CPU in a web tier. Compress once at build time, ship app.4f9c2b1e.js, app.4f9c2b1e.js.gz and app.4f9c2b1e.js.br, and let gzip_static/brotli_static pick the encoding the client advertised. Brotli level 11 offline gives roughly 20% smaller output than gzip 9 and costs nothing at runtime — the compression level you would never dare use live is exactly the one to use in CI.

Because the filename contains a content hash, the response is safe to cache for a year and the immutable directive tells browsers not to revalidate even on a hard refresh. The one file that must never get this treatment is index.html: it is the map to the fingerprints, and if it is cached your users are pinned to a dead build.

nginx · static + cache-control, with the add_header trap defused
location /assets/ {
    root /srv/app/public;
    gzip_static  on;                 # serve .gz if present, else compress live
    brotli_static on;                # .br wins when the client sends Accept-Encoding: br
    access_log off;

    # Fingerprinted filenames -> the content can never change under this URL.
    add_header Cache-Control "public, max-age=31536000, immutable" always;
    # TRAP: add_header does NOT merge with the server block. Any location containing its
    # OWN add_header discards every inherited one -- so the HSTS header defined at server
    # level silently vanishes for /assets/ unless it is repeated here. Repeat it, or use
    # the headers-more module, or accept that your scanner will find it eventually.
    add_header Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains" always;

    try_files $uri =404;             # no directory listing, no fallthrough to the SPA
}

location = /index.html {
    root /srv/app/public;
    add_header Cache-Control "no-cache" always;   # NOT no-store: revalidate, don't refetch.
                                                  # A 304 is 150 bytes; a refetch is 4 KB.
    etag on;
}

Proxy caching, and the two directives that stop a stampede

Caching at the proxy is cheap because the response never wakes Node at all — an api_cache HIT is a file read and a header rewrite, tens of microseconds. It is dangerous for exactly one reason: the cache key is whatever you say it is, and if you omit a dimension that varies the response, you will serve one tenant's data to another. Never cache a route that reads Authorization unless the key contains it, and prefer to cache only the routes that are the same for everyone.

proxy_cache_lock and proxy_cache_use_stale are the two settings that turn a cache from an optimisation into a shield. Without the lock, a popular key expiring means every concurrent request for it misses simultaneously and all of them are forwarded — a thundering herd, at exactly the moment the origin is least able to absorb one. Without use_stale, a backend blip converts every cached URL into a 502 despite a perfectly good copy sitting on disk.

nginx · a cacheable read path
location /v1/public/ {
    proxy_cache api_cache;
    proxy_cache_key "$scheme$request_method$host$request_uri$http_accept_encoding";
        # $request_uri includes the query string. Include Accept-Encoding or you will hand a
        # brotli body to a gzip-only client. Do NOT include the whole Vary set by reflex --
        # every extra dimension divides your hit rate.

    proxy_cache_valid 200 301  60s;
    proxy_cache_valid 404      10s;   # cache negatives briefly or a scanner hitting missing
                                      # ids becomes a full-rate origin load test
    proxy_cache_min_uses 2;           # don't fill the cache with one-hit-wonder URLs

    proxy_cache_lock          on;     # one request per key goes upstream; the rest WAIT
    proxy_cache_lock_timeout  5s;     # ...but no longer than this, then they go too
    proxy_cache_lock_age      5s;

    proxy_cache_use_stale error timeout updating
                          http_500 http_502 http_503 http_504;
        # `updating` = serve the stale copy while ONE request refreshes it. This plus
        # background_update is stale-while-revalidate: nobody ever waits for a refresh.
    proxy_cache_background_update on;

    proxy_cache_bypass $http_authorization $cookie_session;  # authed request -> always fresh
    proxy_no_cache     $http_authorization $cookie_session;  # ...and never stored. BOTH are
                                                             # needed; they are different verbs.
    proxy_ignore_headers Set-Cookie;   # a stray Set-Cookie from Node otherwise disables
                                       # caching for the whole route, silently
    proxy_hide_header Set-Cookie;      # and must never be stored in a shared entry

    add_header X-Cache-Status $upstream_cache_status always;  # HIT/MISS/EXPIRED/STALE/UPDATING/
                                                              # BYPASS -- graph this ratio
    proxy_pass http://api;
}
HIT cost
~40 µsfile read + header rewrite; Node is never woken
gzip level 5
~1.4 msper 100 KB JSON, per worker, off your loop
10m req zone
~160k IPs64-byte state each; size it to your real client count
Reload
0 droppedold workers drain, bounded by worker_shutdown_timeout

Rate limiting: burst, nodelay, and what each one feels like

limit_req is a leaky bucket, not a fixed window. rate=20r/s is enforced as one request every 50 ms, so two requests 10 ms apart already violate it — which is why a bare limit_req zone=api; rejects normal browser traffic and gets ripped out again within the hour. burst=40 adds a forty-slot queue in front of the bucket. The queue's behaviour is then decided by one word:

burst=40 (no modifier)
Excess requests are delayed until the bucket drains. Nobody is rejected, but request 40 in a burst waits two seconds. For an API this is worse than a 429: the client cannot tell the difference between a rate limit and a slow backend, and your latency graph absorbs the lie.
burst=40 nodelay
All 40 are forwarded immediately, and the slots refill at 20/s. Bursty, well-behaved clients never notice; a sustained abuser exhausts the queue in two seconds and gets a clean 429 from then on. This is the default you want.
burst=40 delay=20
The first 20 go straight through, slots 21–40 are throttled, past 40 is a 429. Useful when you want to slow a client before you refuse it — a browser doing parallel page loads passes cleanly while a script is progressively squeezed.

When the limiter fires, nginx writes limiting requests, excess: 0.720 by zone "api", client: 203.0.113.9 to the error log at your limit_req_log_level. Alert on the rate of that line, not on 429s at the app tier — by definition the app never sees these requests.

Reloading without dropping a request

nginx -s reload sends SIGHUP to the master. The master re-reads the configuration, starts new workers with it, and tells the old workers to stop accepting. Old workers keep serving their in-flight requests and only exit when the last one completes; listening sockets are never closed, so not a single SYN is lost. This is genuinely zero-downtime, with two caveats. First, always gate it on nginx -t — the reload of a broken config fails and leaves the old workers running, but the reload of a config that is valid but wrong (a certificate path that does not exist for the worker user) fails at worker start and you lose capacity quietly. Second, a worker with an attached WebSocket or SSE stream never finishes; after a few reloads you have a dozen shutting-down workers holding memory. worker_shutdown_timeout 30s; gives them a hard deadline, at the cost of cutting those long-lived connections — which is the right trade, because clients reconnect and leaked workers do not.

nginx, the cloud load balancer, or both

Cloud LB only

ALB/GCLB gives you TLS with managed certificates, health checks, WAF integration and cross-zone balancing with no boxes to patch. It cannot do: response caching, arbitrary header rewriting, request buffering you control, pre-compressed static files, or per-IP connection limits. If your traffic is all dynamic JSON and your static assets are on a CDN, this is enough and you should stop here.

nginx only

One or two VMs you own end to end. Cheapest, most capable, and you are now responsible for certificate renewal, kernel tuning, capacity, and the fact that a single box is a single point of failure. Reasonable for a self-hosted product or an internal service; hard to justify for a public API in a cloud that will do the boring half for you.

Both (the usual answer)

An L4 NLB or an L7 ALB fronting an autoscaling group of nginx, each nginx fronting local or nearby Node. The cloud LB does availability, zone spread and the public certificate; nginx does buffering, caching, compression and limits. Cost: one extra hop (~0.3 ms) and one more place a header can be rewritten.

The alternatives are not nginx-with-extra-steps; they optimise for different things. Caddy gets automatic ACME right, has a far smaller configuration surface, and speaks HTTP/3 out of the box — the correct choice for a small team that wants TLS to be a non-event, with the caveat that its caching story needs a third-party module. Traefik reconfigures itself from container labels or Kubernetes CRDs, which is the entire point: if your backends come and go every deploy, it dissolves the stale-DNS problem described above. Envoy is a different class of tool — per-request load balancing over HTTP/2, outlier detection, retry budgets, gRPC transcoding, a dynamic control plane — and it is what you graduate to when the proxy tier needs to be programmable rather than configured. It is also strictly more operational work than nginx, and it does not serve static files nearly as well. If the ingress controller in front of your Kubernetes cluster is already ingress-nginx, note that it is nginx with a config generator on top: everything in this chapter applies, but you set it via annotations and a ConfigMap, and a bad annotation regenerates the config for every ingress in the cluster.

The four graphs that make this tier legible

$request_time minus $upstream_response_time as a p99 (client slowness, isolated from application slowness); $upstream_cache_status as a ratio (a cache whose hit rate silently fell to 4% after a key change); active connections from stub_status against worker_connections (how close you are to refusing accepts); and the count of limiting requests lines in the error log. All four are free, and each one answers a question your application metrics structurally cannot.

Interview follow-up

"Node can terminate TLS, gzip, and serve static files. Why put nginx in front of it at all?" The weak answer is "nginx is written in C so it's faster". The strong one names the mechanism: "It's not about throughput, it's about what shares the event loop. Every one of those jobs is CPU or wall-clock time with no application decision in it, and in Node they contend with request handling on the same thread — a gzip on the libuv pool queues behind three other gzips and now my fs reads are slow too. The specific thing I care about most is buffering. With proxy_buffering on, nginx absorbs the whole response and releases the upstream socket immediately, so a client on a bad mobile link takes ninety seconds to read it and my process has been free for eighty-nine of them. Turn that off — which people do to make SSE work — and the client's TCP backpressure propagates into a Node stream and I'm holding heap for every slow reader. So I scope it per location, or better, let the app send X-Accel-Buffering: no for the routes that stream. The same argument covers request bodies: a slow POST is absorbed by nginx, and slowloris dies against client_header_timeout 5s plus limit_conn rather than against my request handler." If they push on what goes wrong in practice, the two answers that land are the keepalive ladder — proxy_http_version 1.1 with Connection: "", and nginx's upstream keepalive_timeout strictly below Node's server.keepAliveTimeout, or you get 502s with no application log — and stale upstream DNS, where nginx resolves a backend hostname once at reload and keeps talking to IPs that were recycled three deploys ago.

38

Caching: layers & patterns

A cache is a deliberate decision to be wrong for a bounded period in exchange for latency and origin load, and every caching incident you will ever run is the moment that bound turned out to be unbounded. The work is not installing Redis; it is choosing which layer answers the request, naming the value so precisely that the key cannot lie, and deciding — before the pager goes off — what happens in the second after the cache is empty.

The ladder, priced by blast radius rather than latency

Caching is usually taught as a latency ranking, cheapest first. Latency is the least interesting axis. The layers differ by three orders of magnitude in who can see the value, and that is what decides what may live there. A personalised response in an in-process Map is wasteful. The same response in a CDN is a data breach with a press release attached.

Where a value belongs, and what it costs to get it wrong
LayerHit latencyShared withBelongs hereNever here
Browser0 msone userHashed asset URLs with max-age=31536000, immutable; short private, max-age=60 on personalised JSONAnything you may need to revoke; you cannot reach into a browser
CDN / edge10–30 mseveryone in a regionAnonymous GET responses, images, public product pages, s-maxage + stale-while-revalidateAnything varying by identity unless the identity is in the cache key or Vary
Reverse proxy micro-cache1–5 msone site1–5 s on the hottest handful of endpoints; collapses a spike into one origin callLong TTLs — this layer is a shock absorber, not a store
In-process LRU< 1 µsone podFeature flags, JWKS, compiled validators, tenant config, tiny and rarely changingAnything needing prompt invalidation; you cannot invalidate 40 pods reliably
Redis0.3–1 ms same-AZthe whole fleetThe shared working set: sessions, rendered fragments, expensive aggregations, rate countersValues cheaper to recompute than to fetch across the network
Database buffer cache~0.1 msone clusterMongoDB's WiredTiger cache, sized at 50% of RAM minus 1 GB by defaultNothing — you size it, you do not populate it, and it is not yours to reason about per key

Two rules follow from the table and they settle most design arguments. First, push the value as high up the ladder as its privacy allows: a request answered at the edge never touches your VPC, your event loop, your connection pool or your bill. Second, the higher the layer, the weaker your invalidation. You can UNLINK a Redis key in a millisecond; a CDN purge takes seconds to tens of seconds; a browser cache cannot be purged at all, which is why asset URLs carry a content hash instead of a TTL you will later regret.

The in-process tier, and the drift you are agreeing to

An lru-cache in the Node heap answers in under a microsecond, has no network to fail and no serialisation cost, and it is one copy per pod with no reliable way to invalidate forty of them. That makes it right for values whose staleness window you can state out loud: JWKS, feature flags, tenant configuration, compiled validators. An L1 in front of Redis with a 2–5 s TTL is often the largest single win available, because it removes most of the Redis traffic for the hottest keys while bounding drift to seconds. Redis pub/sub invalidation (SUBSCRIBE cache:invalidate) narrows that window without ever closing it — pub/sub is fire-and-forget, and a pod that was reconnecting when the message went out never receives it. The short TTL stays the guarantee; the message is only an accelerator.

Size it against the container, not the machine. Every pod pays for the full L1, so 500 MB across forty pods is 20 GB of RAM bought forty times, charged to the memory limit rather than to Redis — which is why an oversized L1 surfaces as an OOMKilled restart loop and an inexplicably cold cache, not as anything a cache dashboard shows you.

Vary is not a suggestion

The classic edge breach is a GET that is normally anonymous but returns a personalised body when a session cookie is present. The CDN keys on the URL, stores user 8814's response, and serves it to everyone for the next hour. The fix is structural, not a header tweak: personalised responses must be served from a path the edge is configured never to cache, or the cache key must include the identity. Relying on Vary: Cookie alone gives you a cache keyed on every distinct cookie string in existence — a 0% hit ratio, which is at least safe, right up to the day someone “optimises” it away.

Answering above your own front door

The cheapest request is the one Node never sees, and all that stands between you and it is a response header written on purpose. A handler that sets no Cache-Control is not opting out of caching; it hands the decision to heuristic caching, where an intermediary guesses a freshness lifetime from Last-Modified. Be explicit on every public route and on the negative case too — private, no-store for anything personalised, because no-cache does not mean “do not cache”, it means “store it, then revalidate before reuse”. The directive worth learning properly is stale-while-revalidate: stale-serve, standardised, where the CDN keeps handing out the expired copy at edge latency while one request refreshes behind it. With stale-if-error beside it, an origin that goes slow or disappears costs you staleness instead of 502s.

javascript · routes/catalogue.js — validators, directives and surrogate keys
router.get('/api/public/products/:id', async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    // The cheap validator comes FIRST, and this is the part everyone gets wrong.
    // Express's built-in ETag is a hash of the body computed AFTER you rendered
    // it, so a 304 saves bandwidth and not one millisecond of database work. To
    // save the WORK, derive a validator from something you can read cheaply — a
    // version counter, an updatedAt, a monotonic revision on the document.
    const stamp = await catalogue.versionOf(req.params.id);  // ~0.3 ms, cached
    if (stamp === null) return next();                       // falls through to 404

    // The namespace version rides inside the validator, so the deploy that
    // changes the serialiser also invalidates every copy in every browser —
    // the one cache tier you can never purge.
    const etag = `W/"${V}-${stamp}"`;
    res.set('ETag', etag);   // Express only generates its own if you set none
    if (req.headers['if-none-match'] === etag) return res.status(304).end();

    const body = await getProduct(req.params.id);   // cache-aside from here down

    res.set({
      // max-age governs the browser; s-maxage governs shared caches only and
      // overrides it there. 60 s in the user's pocket, 10 minutes at the edge.
      // stale-while-revalidate is stale-serve at the CDN: expired copies keep
      // being served at edge latency while ONE request refreshes behind them.
      'Cache-Control':
        'public, max-age=60, s-maxage=600, stale-while-revalidate=86400, stale-if-error=86400',
      // Tag-based invalidation at the edge (Fastly Surrogate-Key, Cloudflare
      // Cache-Tag). Same idea as the Redis tag set, but the purge is an API call
      // with seconds of propagation — an optimisation on top of s-maxage, never
      // the guarantee. Never emit a tag you cannot enumerate at write time.
      'Surrogate-Key': `product-${req.params.id} catalogue`,
      // Vary on encoding only. Every value you add here multiplies the number of
      // stored variants; Vary: Cookie divides your hit ratio by your user count.
      Vary: 'Accept-Encoding',
    });
    res.json(body);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// The route that must never be shared. no-store, not no-cache: no-cache still
// writes the body to disk and still lets a misconfigured proxy hold a copy.
router.get('/api/me/orders', requireAuth, async (req, res) => {
  res.set('Cache-Control', 'private, no-store');
  res.json(await ordersFor(req.user.id));   // never carries an ETag or a tag
});

Five patterns, and what each one does when it fails

Pick the pattern by its failure mode, not by its diagram. All five look identical when everything works.

Cache patterns and their consistency/failure profile
PatternWho writes the cacheOn cache failureOn origin failureUse when
Cache-aside (lazy)The application, after a missSlow request; correctness intactRequest fails; nothing lostDefault. Choose this unless you can articulate why not
Read-throughThe cache library/proxySame, but hidden inside the clientSameYou want one place to enforce single-flight and metrics
Write-throughThe write path, synchronouslyWrites fail or you skip the cache — decide whichWrite fails, cache untouchedRead-after-write must be exact and the value is cheap to serialise
Write-behindThe write path, asynchronouslyAcknowledged writes are lostBacklog grows silentlyAlmost never in a system of record. Fine for counters and analytics
Refresh-aheadA background refresher before expiryFalls back to a normal missStale value keeps servingA small set of very hot, very expensive keys

Cache-aside is the default because its worst day is a slow request. Write-behind is the one that ends careers: you have told the client 201 Created, the value exists only in a Redis instance with appendonly no, and a failover discards it. If you use write-behind, the durable write must already have happened somewhere else.

Write-through has a subtler trap. Writing the new value into the cache on every update looks like it guarantees freshness, but two concurrent writers can interleave — writer A writes the database, writer B writes the database, B writes the cache, A writes the cache — and the cache now holds the older of two committed values, permanently, until the TTL saves you. Delete on write is safer than update on write. A deletion cannot be reordered into a wrong value; it can only cause a miss.

javascript · cache/aside.js — the 90% path
// Everything else in this chapter is a modification of this function,
// not a replacement for it.
// Sentinel for "the origin genuinely has nothing". It must be a string no real
// serialised value can produce, or a user can poison their own cache entry.
const NEGATIVE = '\u0000nil';

// The ioredis client MUST be constructed with these. The defaults are the trap:
// no command timeout at all, and an offline queue that buffers commands during a
// failover so your p99 becomes "however long the failover took".
//   new Redis({ commandTimeout: 50, enableOfflineQueue: false,
//               maxRetriesPerRequest: 1 })

async function cacheAside(name, key, ttlSec, loader, { negativeTtl = 30 } = {}) {
  let raw;
  try {
    raw = await redis.get(key);
  } catch (err) {
    // FAIL OPEN. A cache outage must degrade to a slow site, never a broken one.
    // The corollary you are signing up for: the origin has to survive 100% miss.
    metrics.increment('cache.error', { name, op: 'get' });
  }

  if (raw != null) {
    metrics.increment('cache.hit', { name });
    return raw === NEGATIVE ? null : JSON.parse(raw);
  }

  metrics.increment('cache.miss', { name });
  const t0 = process.hrtime.bigint();
  const value = await loader();                    // the real cost of a miss
  metrics.timing('cache.miss_cost_ms',
    Number(process.hrtime.bigint() - t0) / 1e6, { name });

  // Negative caching: a null result gets a SHORT ttl of its own. Without this,
  // a scripted scan of /api/users/:id for ids that do not exist bypasses the
  // cache entirely and turns straight into database load. That is penetration.
  const ttl = jitter(value === null ? negativeTtl : ttlSec);

  // Not awaited: the write-back is not on the correctness path, and awaiting it
  // adds a network round trip to the slowest requests you have. Do attach a
  // catch — an unhandled rejection here kills the process under Node 15+.
  redis.set(key, value === null ? NEGATIVE : JSON.stringify(value), 'EX', ttl)
    .catch(() => metrics.increment('cache.error', { name, op: 'set' }));

  return value;
}

// ±15% jitter on EVERY ttl, no exceptions. See the avalanche section.
const jitter = (s) => Math.max(1, Math.round(s * (0.85 + Math.random() * 0.3)));

Naming and invalidating are the same problem

“There are only two hard things in computer science: cache invalidation and naming things” survives as a joke, but it is one observation, not two. A cache key is a name, and invalidation is only hard when the name is incomplete. If a rendered price depends on the product, the currency, the tenant's discount tier and the schema version of your serialiser, and the key is product:42, then no invalidation strategy can save you: two different correct values are competing for one name. Fix the name and most invalidation bugs stop existing.

TTL
Simple, self-healing, always right eventually. The only strategy that survives a code path you forgot about. Every key gets one — an entry with no expiry is not a cache entry, it is undeclared state.
Explicit invalidation on write
Precise and immediate, and it fails silently the day someone adds a second write path — a bulk import, an admin tool, a migration script, a Mongo updateMany in a cron job. Treat it as an optimisation on top of a TTL, never as the guarantee.
Versioned key prefixes
Put a version in the namespace: v7:product:42. Bump it in the same commit that changes the shape of the value and the entire namespace becomes unreachable at deploy time, with no SCAN and no purge. The old keys die of their own TTL. This is the cheapest mass invalidation that exists.
Tag-based invalidation
Maintain a set per entity — tag:product:42 — holding every key derived from it, and delete the set's members on write. Precise across derived views (search results, category pages, sitemaps), at the cost of a second data structure that can itself grow unbounded.
javascript · versioned namespaces and tag fan-out
// The version belongs in the code, not in config. It must move atomically with
// the serialiser it describes, and a rollback must take the old keys back with it.
const V = 'v7';

// Every input that changes the BODY must appear in the key. Sort the entries:
// {b:1,a:2} and {a:2,b:1} are the same query and must not be two cache entries.
function keyFor(kind, id, variant = {}) {
  const suffix = Object.keys(variant).sort()
    .map((k) => `${k}=${variant[k]}`).join('&');
  return suffix ? `${V}:${kind}:${id}:${suffix}` : `${V}:${kind}:${id}`;
}

// Tag fan-out. Register the key against every entity it was derived from,
// with a ttl on the TAG SET slightly longer than the longest member ttl,
// otherwise the set outlives its members and grows for ever.
async function remember(tags, key, ttlSec) {
  const p = redis.pipeline();
  for (const t of tags) {
    p.sadd(`${V}:tag:${t}`, key);
    p.expire(`${V}:tag:${t}`, ttlSec + 300);
  }
  await p.exec();
}

async function invalidateTag(tag) {
  const setKey = `${V}:tag:${tag}`;
  // SMEMBERS on a huge set is O(N) on Redis's single thread. Above a few
  // thousand members use SSCAN in batches; above a few hundred thousand,
  // your tag is too coarse and you should be bumping a namespace version.
  const keys = await redis.smembers(setKey);
  if (keys.length) {
    // UNLINK, not DEL: DEL frees memory synchronously and a large batch will
    // stall every other client on the instance. UNLINK frees on a background
    // thread. Same visibility, no latency spike.
    await redis.unlink(...keys, setKey);
  }
}

// NEVER: redis.keys('v7:product:*'). KEYS is O(N) over the entire keyspace and
// blocks the single thread — on 8M keys that is roughly two seconds during which
// every request in your fleet that touches Redis is stopped. SCAN is safer but
// still walks everything and races with concurrent writes.

The three ways a cache takes down the thing behind it

12 000 rps hit ─────────────────► 0.4 ms, no origin work │ ▲ ▼ │ ┌───────────────┐ key present? ───┘ │ cache lookup │ └───────┬───────┘ key absent │ stampede: 12 000 rps × 200 ms origin ▼ = 2 400 concurrent queries against a single-flight pool whose maxPoolSize is 100 lock holder ──► origin ──► set + release everyone else ──► serve stale (or wait 20 ms and re-read) One expiring key. No code changed. The database is now the incident.

Thundering herd is the arithmetic above: the moment a hot key expires, every concurrent request misses at once, and they all run the same expensive query. The Mongo driver's default maxPoolSize of 100 means the first hundred run and the rest queue until waitQueueTimeoutMS fires — you will see MongoServerSelectionError or connection-pool timeouts on endpoints that never touch the slow collection, because the pool is shared.
