Three mitigations, in increasing order of effort: a single-flight lock so exactly one caller recomputes; serving the stale value to everyone else while it happens; and probabilistic early expiry so the recompute is scheduled slightly before expiry rather than triggered by it.

Cache penetration is traffic for keys that do not exist and therefore can never be cached — an id enumeration scan, a broken client requesting a deleted product in a loop. Every request is a guaranteed miss followed by a guaranteed origin query. Negative caching with a short TTL (30–60 s) fixes the common case for the price of being wrong about newly created ids for one TTL. If the keyspace is enormous and mostly absent — checking whether an email has been seen, whether a hash is known — a Bloom filter in front (Redis's BF.EXISTS from RedisBloom, or an in-process filter rebuilt hourly) answers “definitely not present” in microseconds with no false negatives.

Cache avalanche is a correlated expiry: a large set of keys written at the same moment, with the same TTL, expiring in the same second. Jitter every TTL by ±10–20% and the correlation disappears. The second flavour of avalanche is a cold cache — a Redis failover, a FLUSHALL, a maxmemory eviction storm, a deploy that bumped the namespace version — where the hit ratio goes to zero for everything at once.

The 02:00 avalanche, and the flush that followed it

A nightly job warms 400 000 product keys at 02:00 with EX 86400. For months this is invisible. Then traffic grows, and at 02:00:03 the following night every one of those keys expires within the same second while the warm job is still starting. Origin QPS goes from 900 to 40 000, the connection pool saturates, health checks time out, and the orchestrator starts killing pods — which drops the in-process caches too. The engineer on call reaches for FLUSHALL to “clear the bad state” and converts a five-minute degradation into a forty-minute outage, because nothing in the system can serve a fully cold cache at peak. Two lines of prevention: jitter the TTL, and answer the capacity question honestly before you need to — can the origin serve 100% of peak traffic with the cache empty? If not, you do not have a cache, you have an undeclared single point of failure. Find out by flushing it in staging under load, not at 02:00.

javascript · single-flight + stale-while-revalidate + early expiry
// The envelope carries a LOGICAL expiry; the Redis ttl is longer, so the value
// is still physically present (stale) after it stops being fresh. That grace
// window is what lets you serve a stale answer instead of a stampede.
//   envelope = { v: value, exp: unixSeconds, delta: secondsTheRecomputeTook }

// Release the lock only if we still own it. A plain DEL can delete the lock a
// LATER holder acquired after ours expired, which un-serialises the whole thing.
const RELEASE = `if redis.call("get", KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1]
                 then return redis.call("del", KEYS[1]) else return 0 end`;

async function readThrough(key, ttlSec, loader, { graceSec = 120, beta = 1 } = {}) {
  const raw = await redis.get(key);
  const now = Date.now() / 1000;

  if (raw) {
    const env = JSON.parse(raw);
    // XFetch (Vattani et al., "Optimal Probabilistic Cache Stampede Prevention").
    // -ln(random()) is a positive exponential draw; scaling it by how long the
    // last recompute took means expensive keys start refreshing earlier and
    // cheap ones barely refresh early at all. Probability rises smoothly toward
    // expiry, so ONE unlucky request refreshes while the rest keep hitting.
    const early = env.delta * beta * -Math.log(Math.random());
    if (now + early < env.exp) return env.v;                 // fresh enough
  }

  // Either stale or missing. Exactly one caller gets to do the work.
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  const won = await redis.set(`lock:${key}`, token, 'EX', 30, 'NX');

  if (!won) {
    if (raw) return JSON.parse(raw).v;   // stale-while-revalidate: instant, slightly old
    // Genuine cold miss with no stale copy: wait briefly for the winner rather
    // than joining the herd. Bounded — never loop until the lock is free.
    await sleep(25 + Math.random() * 25);
    const second = await redis.get(key);
    if (second) return JSON.parse(second).v;
    return loader();                     // give up on coordination, not on the user
  }

  try {
    const t0 = Date.now();
    const value = await loader();
    const delta = (Date.now() - t0) / 1000;
    // ONE jitter draw, used for BOTH the logical expiry and the physical ttl.
    // Drawing twice can hand you a physical ttl shorter than the logical one and
    // silently delete the grace window this whole function exists to provide.
    const ttl = jitter(ttlSec);
    const env = { v: value, exp: now + ttl, delta };
    await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(env), 'EX', ttl + graceSec);
    return value;
  } finally {
    // If the recompute threw, releasing lets the next caller retry immediately
    // instead of everyone serving stale for the full 30 s lock ttl.
    await redis.eval(RELEASE, 1, `lock:${key}`, token).catch(() => {});
  }
}

The same three ideas exist at the proxy tier and cost you four directives rather than eighty lines. If the response is cacheable by URL alone, do it there and let Node never see the traffic.

nginx · micro-cache with request collapsing
proxy_cache_path /var/cache/nginx/api levels=1:2 keys_zone=api:64m
                 max_size=4g inactive=10m use_temp_path=off;

location /api/public/ {
    proxy_cache api;
    proxy_cache_key "$scheme$request_method$host$request_uri";
    proxy_cache_valid 200 5s;        # a micro-cache: 5 s collapses a spike into
    proxy_cache_valid 404 30s;       # one origin call. 404s cached = penetration fix.

    proxy_cache_lock on;             # single-flight, at the proxy. Without it, N
    proxy_cache_lock_timeout 5s;     # concurrent misses become N upstream requests.

    # Serve stale rather than propagate an origin failure, and refresh in the
    # background so the request that triggered the refresh is not the one that pays.
    proxy_cache_use_stale error timeout updating http_500 http_502 http_503 http_504;
    proxy_cache_background_update on;

    # Never cache a response that carries a session. Belt and braces alongside
    # the application's own Cache-Control.
    proxy_no_cache     $http_authorization $cookie_session;
    proxy_cache_bypass $http_authorization $cookie_session;

    add_header X-Cache-Status $upstream_cache_status always;  # HIT/MISS/STALE/UPDATING
    proxy_pass http://node_api;
}

The arithmetic that tells you whether the cache is worth having

Let t_c be cache latency, t_o origin latency and h the hit ratio. Mean latency with the cache is t_c + (1 − h) · t_o, because a miss pays both. That is better than the uncached t_o exactly when h > t_c / t_o. The break-even hit ratio is simply the ratio of the two latencies, and the consequence is the useful part: against a 400 ms aggregation, a 1 ms cache pays for itself at a hit ratio of 0.25%, so a 60% hit rate is an enormous win. Against a 3 ms indexed primary-key lookup, you need 33% just to break even, and a cross-AZ Redis at 2 ms needs 67% before it stops making the endpoint slower.

Break-even hit ratio
t_c / t_o1 ms vs 400 ms origin → 0.25%. 1 ms vs 3 ms origin → 33%.
90% → 95%
2× headroomorigin load halves. The miss ratio is the quantity that matters, not the hit ratio.
98% → 96%
2× loada two-point drop doubles database traffic. Alert on the delta, not on a threshold.
TTL jitter
±15%the cheapest outage prevention in this chapter; one function, applied everywhere

Latency is only half the return. The other half is origin protection, and there the relevant figure is the miss ratio: 60% hit means the database sees 40% of traffic, a 2.5× capacity multiplier. Going from 90% to 95% halves origin load again; 99% to 99.5% halves it once more. All the money is in the last few points, which is also why a small regression is a genuine emergency rather than a dashboard curiosity.

Measuring, so the numbers mean something

  • Hit ratio per cache name, not per instance. Redis's own keyspace_hits/keyspace_misses are cumulative since boot and aggregated across every workload sharing the instance; they will cheerfully average a 99% session cache with a 20% search cache into a meaningless 84%. Emit your own counters, tagged, as in the first listing.
  • Miss cost as a histogram. Hit ratio alone cannot tell you where to invest. Miss rate × p50 miss cost is the latency the cache is actually buying, and it is the number that goes in the design review.
  • Latency tagged by outcome. One timer spanning hits and misses is bimodal and its p99 is noise. Time cache.get and cache.miss_cost separately.
  • Evictions. evicted_keys rising means maxmemory is your real TTL and keys are vanishing early; hit ratio is capacity-bound and no amount of TTL tuning will help. Choose allkeys-lru deliberately — the default noeviction turns a full instance into OOM command not allowed when used memory > 'maxmemory' on every write.
  • Stale-serve rate. If you implemented stale-while-revalidate, the fraction served stale rises minutes before the origin's own alerts fire. It is the best leading indicator you own.
  • Edge status. Log X-Cache-Status and the CDN's equivalent. A hit ratio you cannot break down by layer is a single number hiding three problems.

What not to cache

  1. Per-user data in a shared cache without the user in the key. Not a performance bug — an authorisation bug. If the body varies by identity, the identity is part of the name, all the way up to the CDN.
  2. Anything whose correctness requirement is stronger than the TTL. Balances, remaining stock at checkout, permission and role checks, anything a regulator will ask about. Read those through, or write down that you have chosen to be wrong for up to one TTL and can defend it.
  3. Values cheaper to compute than to fetch. A 2 ms indexed lookup does not want a 2 ms network round trip in front of it. Measure both before adding a layer.
  4. Write-heavy keys. Below roughly a 3:1 read:write ratio, every cached write is wasted work and every read is stale anyway. The cache is pure overhead with extra failure modes.
  5. Values only one client will ever ask for. One-off report exports and long-tail search permutations fill memory, evict genuinely hot keys and are never hit again. This is how a healthy cache quietly becomes an expensive random-eviction machine.
The three lines that prevent most cache incidents

Set an explicit commandTimeout (50 ms) on the client; wrap every cache call so an error is logged and treated as a miss; and put a jittered TTL on every key without exception. If a value genuinely must never expire, it is not cache — it is state, and it belongs in the database.

Interview follow-up

“How would you cache this?” is answered badly by naming Redis. Answer with the trade and the failure you are planning for: cache-aside by default, because its worst day is a slow request rather than a lost write; delete-on-write rather than update-on-write, because a deletion cannot be reordered into a permanently wrong value; the key contains every input that changes the body, behind a version prefix so I can invalidate a whole namespace with a deploy instead of a SCAN; a jittered TTL on everything so a warm-up job cannot expire 400 000 keys in the same second; a lease-based single-flight lock with serve-stale so one hot key expiring cannot become 2 400 concurrent queries against a pool of 100; and negative caching so an id-enumeration scan does not walk straight past the cache into the database. Then produce the number that proves you have operated one: break-even hit ratio is cache latency over origin latency, which is why 60% against a slow aggregation is a win and 60% in front of a 3 ms lookup is a regression. Finish with the capacity question — can the origin serve peak traffic with the cache completely cold? — because that is the difference between a cache and a dependency you have not written down.

39

Redis in depth

Most services use about four per cent of Redis: GET, SET, DEL and a TTL. The other ninety-six per cent is the part that deletes concurrency bugs rather than working around them — a counter that cannot lose an increment, a queue that cannot hand the same job to two workers, a distinct-visitor count that costs 12 KB whether you have a thousand visitors or a billion. Redis is a single-threaded server of data structures, and both halves of that sentence decide how your service behaves at three in the morning.

Choose the structure and the race condition disappears

The reflex to store JSON.stringify(user) under user:1042 costs three things at once. Updating one field rewrites the whole value, so two writers touching two different fields silently lose one update. Reading one field transfers the whole document. And the value is opaque, so the server can do nothing useful with it: no atomic increment, no partial read, no comparison. HINCRBY user:1042 credits -1 fixes all three, is atomic by construction, and returns the new value in one round trip. Almost every Redis design question reduces to this: which structure makes the mutation a single server-side command?

the question you are actually asking structure the command that answers it cost ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── "how many times has this happened?" string INCR / INCRBY / INCRBYFLOAT O(1) "what does this object look like now?" hash HSET / HGET / HINCRBY / HRANDFIELD O(1) per field "what are the 20 most recent?" list LPUSH + LTRIM 0 19 O(1) amortised "is this one of them? how many overlap?" set SADD / SISMEMBER / SINTERCARD O(1) / O(N) "top 10 · what is due · oldest first" zset ZADD / ZRANGE / ZPOPMIN O(log N) "did each of 5M users do X today?" bitmap SETBIT / BITCOUNT / BITOP O(1) / O(bytes) "roughly how many distinct?" HLL PFADD / PFCOUNT / PFMERGE O(1), 12 KB flat "replay events, per consumer group, with acks" stream XADD / XREADGROUP / XACK O(1) append "what is near this point?" geo GEOADD / GEOSEARCH BYRADIUS O(N + log M) "give me every key matching order:*" KEYS never. SCAN, or maintain a set yourself. The structure decides whether a mutation is one atomic server-side command or a read-modify-write race living in your Node process.
Same-AZ round trip
0.2–0.5 msthis is the unit of cost; ten sequential calls is 5 ms of pure waiting
Simple ops per instance
~100k/sone core executes commands; more traffic means sharding, not a bigger box
Overhead per key
~60–100 B10M keys costs ~800 MB before a single byte of your data
HyperLogLog
12 KBfixed, for any cardinality, at 0.81% standard error

Strings, hashes and lists, in one round trip

Round trips dominate. A page that needs a profile, the last twenty views and a membership check is 1.5 ms of network if you await three times, and 0.5 ms if you pipeline. Pipelining is not batching for tidiness; it is the difference between a p99 you can defend and one you cannot. It is also not atomic — other clients' commands interleave with yours — which is fine when the commands are independent and fatal when they are not.

javascript · lib/redis/profile.js — four structures, one round trip
const { Redis } = require('ioredis');

// enableAutoPipelining batches commands issued in the same event-loop tick
// automatically. It is the single highest-leverage ioredis option: on a
// handler that issues 6 independent reads it turns 6 RTTs into 1.
const redis = new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL, {
  enableAutoPipelining: true,
  maxRetriesPerRequest: 2,      // fail fast; do not let a dead Redis hold requests
  // TRAP: ioredis defaults to queueing commands while disconnected. Under a
  // Redis outage that queue grows without bound and you OOM the Node process
  // long before Redis comes back.
  enableOfflineQueue: false,
});

async function loadProfilePage(userId, postId) {
  // .pipeline() = one TCP write, one read, N replies, NOT a transaction.
  const [[, profile], [, recent], [, isFollower], [, views]] = await redis
    .pipeline()
    // Hash: partial read of a partial object. HGETALL on a 300-field hash is
    // O(N) on the single command thread - name the fields you need.
    .hmget(`user:${userId}`, 'name', 'avatar', 'plan')
    // List as a capped recent-items buffer. LPUSH then LTRIM is the pattern;
    // without the LTRIM this list grows forever and becomes the big key that
    // takes 400 ms to serialise when someone finally runs LRANGE 0 -1 on it.
    .lrange(`recent:${userId}`, 0, 19)
    // Set membership: O(1), and the answer is one byte on the wire.
    .sismember(`followers:${postId}`, userId)
    // String + INCR: the only counter that cannot lose a write under
    // concurrency, because the read and the write are the same instruction.
    .incr(`views:${postId}`)
    .exec();

  return { profile, recent, isFollower: isFollower === 1, views };
}

// Writing the recent-items buffer. Two commands that MUST be adjacent, so
// this one is a real transaction rather than a pipeline.
async function recordView(userId, postId) {
  await redis.multi()
    .lpush(`recent:${userId}`, postId)
    .ltrim(`recent:${userId}`, 0, 199)     // hard cap: 200 entries, forever
    .expire(`recent:${userId}`, 60 * 60 * 24 * 30)
    .exec();
}

Two encoding facts pay for themselves. A hash with fewer than hash-max-listpack-entries (128) fields, each shorter than hash-max-listpack-value (64) bytes, is stored as a flat listpack — a few hundred bytes rather than a hashtable with per-field overhead. Cross either threshold and the hash converts permanently, and memory for that key can jump five-fold. The same applies to small sets and sorted sets. Splitting one million-field hash into ten thousand hundred-field hashes is a routine, and enormous, memory win.

Sorted sets are three different products

The sorted set is the structure worth genuinely learning, because one implementation answers three unrelated production problems depending on what you put in the score.

  • Score is a value. A leaderboard. ZADD lb 4210 user:9, then ZREVRANGE lb 0 9 WITHSCORES for the top ten and ZREVRANK lb user:9 for one player's position — O(log N) for a rank that would be a full sort in Mongo.
  • Score is a timestamp, members are events. A sliding window. ZREMRANGEBYSCORE k -inf (now-60000) then ZCARD k is an exact sixty-second rate limit with no fixed-window boundary burst. It costs one member per request, so use it for small limits on expensive endpoints, not for 5000/minute.
  • Score is a due time or a priority, members are jobs. A delayed queue and a priority queue with the same commands. ZADD due:jobs 1767225600000 job:abc, and a worker polls for members scored at or below now.

The third one is where teams introduce their first real bug. Reading due jobs and then removing them is a read-modify-write across two round trips; with four workers polling, the same job is delivered four times. WATCH/MULTI/EXEC fixes correctness but degrades badly — every worker aborts and retries on every conflict, so throughput falls as you add workers. Lua is the right tool: the whole claim runs inside the server as one indivisible step.

javascript · lib/queue/claim.js — atomic claim with a visibility lease
// Both keys carry the same hash tag {jobs} so Redis Cluster maps them to the
// same slot. Without it this script fails on a cluster with
// "CROSSSLOT Keys in request don't hash to the same slot" - and it will pass
// every test you run against a single node before it does.
const DUE = 'due:{jobs}';         // zset: score = due epoch ms
const LEASED = 'leased:{jobs}';   // zset: score = lease expiry epoch ms

const CLAIM = `
local now   = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local limit = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local lease = tonumber(ARGV[3])

-- Reclaim work whose lease expired: a worker was OOM-killed mid-job. This is
-- the at-least-once guarantee; handlers must therefore be idempotent.
local dead = redis.call('ZRANGEBYSCORE', KEYS[2], '-inf', now, 'LIMIT', 0, limit)
for i = 1, #dead do
  redis.call('ZREM', KEYS[2], dead[i])
  redis.call('ZADD', KEYS[1], now, dead[i])
end

local jobs = redis.call('ZRANGEBYSCORE', KEYS[1], '-inf', now, 'LIMIT', 0, limit)
if #jobs == 0 then return {} end

-- unpack() is Lua 5.1 and blows the C stack somewhere around 8000 arguments
-- with "too many results to unpack". Keep limit small (32-128); a worker that
-- claims 5000 jobs at once is also a worker whose crash strands 5000 jobs.
redis.call('ZREM', KEYS[1], unpack(jobs))
for i = 1, #jobs do
  redis.call('ZADD', KEYS[2], now + lease, jobs[i])
end
return jobs
`;

const sha = require('node:crypto').createHash('sha1').update(CLAIM).digest('hex');

async function claim(redis, { limit = 64, leaseMs = 30_000 } = {}) {
  const args = [String(Date.now()), String(limit), String(leaseMs)];
  try {
    // EVALSHA, not EVAL. Shipping 900 bytes of Lua on every poll from 40
    // workers is pointless bandwidth and a re-parse on the server each time.
    return await redis.evalsha(sha, 2, DUE, LEASED, ...args);
  } catch (err) {
    if (!String(err.message).startsWith('NOSCRIPT')) throw err;
    return await redis.eval(CLAIM, 2, DUE, LEASED, ...args);   // reload once
  }
}

// Completion is a single ZREM on the lease set. Do it only after the side
// effect has committed, never before - that is the difference between
// at-least-once and at-most-once, and you almost always want the former.
const complete = (redis, jobId) => redis.zrem(LEASED, jobId);
The Lua script that took the cluster down

A script is atomic because nothing else runs while it does. A loop over a 200 000-member zset inside Lua stops every other client for the duration; a script with an accidental infinite loop stops them permanently. After busy-reply-threshold (5000 ms, formerly lua-time-limit) Redis starts answering everything else with BUSY Redis is busy running a script, and your entire fleet's health checks fail at once. SCRIPT KILL works only if the script has not yet written; once it has, the sole recovery is SHUTDOWN NOSAVE — deliberately discarding whatever is not on disk. Keep scripts to tens of commands with bounded loops, and pass every key you touch in KEYS rather than constructing key names inside Lua.

Counting things without storing what you counted

Three structures exist to answer aggregate questions in constant memory, and each one trades a specific guarantee for that.

Bitmaps are strings addressed by bit. SETBIT dau:2026-09-05 1042 1 marks user 1042 active; BITCOUNT dau:2026-09-05 is the daily active count; BITOP AND wk dau:... across seven days gives users active every day of the week. Five million dense user IDs cost 625 KB per day. The trap is sparsity: the offset is the allocation, so SETBIT k 4000000000 1 immediately allocates 500 MB. Bitmaps require dense, small, integer IDs — keep a monotonic internal counter, never use a Mongo ObjectId.

HyperLogLog answers "how many distinct" in 12 KB with 0.81% standard error, and is the right answer for unique visitors, distinct search terms and distinct IPs at any scale. PFADD, PFCOUNT, and crucially PFMERGE, which lets you keep hourly keys and roll them into a day or a month without double-counting — something a plain counter can never do. You give up membership: you can never ask whether a specific user was in it.

Geospatial is a sorted set whose score is a 52-bit geohash. GEOADD drivers -0.1276 51.5072 driver:88 then GEOSEARCH drivers FROMLONLAT -0.12 51.50 BYRADIUS 3 km ASC COUNT 10 gives the ten nearest, sorted. Because it is a zset underneath, ZREM removes a driver and ZCARD counts them; because it is a zset underneath, there is no TTL per member, so a driver who goes offline uncleanly stays in the index forever unless you sweep it.

Streams, when pub/sub is not enough

Pub/sub is fire-and-forget: a subscriber that is disconnected for 200 ms misses everything published in that window, permanently and silently. Streams are the durable alternative — an append-only log with IDs, consumer groups, per-consumer pending lists and explicit acknowledgement. They are the right tool for a work queue that must survive a worker restart, and the wrong tool for ephemeral fan-out where you would rather drop than store.

javascript · workers/events.js — a consumer group that recovers stuck work
const STREAM = 'events:orders';
const GROUP  = 'billing';
const ME     = `${process.env.HOSTNAME}-${process.pid}`;   // stable per worker

async function start(redis) {
  // MKSTREAM creates the stream if absent; '$' means "from now". Use '0' to
  // replay everything. BUSYGROUP is the expected error on every restart.
  await redis.xgroup('CREATE', STREAM, GROUP, '$', 'MKSTREAM')
    .catch((e) => { if (!String(e.message).includes('BUSYGROUP')) throw e; });

  let cursor = '0-0';
  for (;;) {
    // Every ~30s, adopt entries pending longer than 60s on ANY consumer -
    // i.e. work claimed by a pod that has since been evicted. Without this,
    // a rolling deploy silently strands whatever was in flight.
    const [next, claimed] = await redis.xautoclaim(
      STREAM, GROUP, ME, 60_000, cursor, 'COUNT', 20,
    );
    cursor = next;
    for (const entry of claimed) await handle(redis, entry);

    // BLOCK holds THIS connection for up to 5s. A blocking command occupies a
    // whole connection - it cannot serve anything else - so give blocking
    // consumers their own client, never the one your HTTP handlers share.
    const res = await redis.xreadgroup(
      'GROUP', GROUP, ME, 'COUNT', 20, 'BLOCK', 5000,
      'STREAMS', STREAM, '>',                 // '>' = never-delivered entries
    );
    if (!res) continue;
    for (const [, entries] of res) {
      for (const entry of entries) await handle(redis, entry);
    }
  }
}

async function handle(redis, [id, fields]) {
  try {
    await process(fields);
    await redis.xack(STREAM, GROUP, id);
  } catch (err) {
    // No ack: the entry stays in the pending list and XAUTOCLAIM will bring
    // it back. Track delivery count via XPENDING and route poison messages to
    // a dead-letter stream after N attempts, or you will retry forever.
    log.error({ id, err }, 'event handler failed');
  }
}

// TRAP: XACK removes an entry from the pending list, it does NOT delete it.
// Acked entries live in the stream until trimmed. Trim on write, approximately
// (the ~ lets Redis trim on macro-node boundaries and is far cheaper):
const publish = (redis, data) =>
  redis.xadd(STREAM, 'MAXLEN', '~', 1_000_000, '*', ...Object.entries(data).flat());

Atomicity: four tools, one correct default

The four mechanisms are routinely confused in interviews and in code review, and they are not interchangeable.

What each concurrency tool actually guarantees
ToolAtomic?Logic between commands?Use it for
PipelineNo — other clients interleaveNoIndependent commands. Purely a round-trip optimisation
MULTI/EXECYes — executed as one blockNo — commands are queued blindA fixed set of writes that must land together, like LPUSH + LTRIM
WATCH + MULTIYes, optimisticallyIn your process, between round tripsRare conflicts only. Under contention the retry loop is the bottleneck
Lua / FunctionsYes — nothing else runsYes, server-sideThe default for read-modify-write. One RTT, no retry loop

Two details people get wrong. MULTI is not a database transaction: a command that fails at runtime (say INCR on a key holding a list) does not abort the rest and nothing rolls back, because Redis has no rollback. And WATCH's EXEC returns nil on conflict rather than throwing, so an await that ignores the return value produces a write that silently did not happen. Redis 7 Functions (FUNCTION LOAD) are the modern form of Lua: they persist in the RDB and replicate, so you never see NOSCRIPT after a failover.

Interview follow-up

Asked "how do you implement a counter with a cap in Redis?", the weak answer is GET, compare, SET. The strong one names the race out loud: "Read-modify-write across two round trips is lost-update at any real concurrency — both clients read 99, both write 100, and the cap leaks by the number of in-flight requests. I'd use a Lua script so the check and the increment are one server-side step, pass the key in KEYS so it works on Cluster, dispatch with EVALSHA and handle NOSCRIPT by reloading, and keep the script to a handful of commands because it blocks the whole instance while it runs. WATCH/MULTI also works, but it converts contention into retries and gets slower exactly when you need it." That answer covers correctness, dispatch, cluster-safety and blast radius in four sentences.

Expiry, eviction, and one instance per job

Keys expire two ways: lazily, when touched, and by an active cycle that samples twenty keys per database roughly ten times a second and repeats while more than a quarter of the sample was expired. Neither is a scheduler. A key with a five-minute TTL may occupy memory well beyond five minutes if nothing touches it and the sampling is unlucky, which matters when you sized the instance assuming prompt reclamation. Note also that TTLs apply to the top-level key only — hash fields had no independent expiry until HEXPIRE arrived in Redis 7.4.

When maxmemory is reached, maxmemory-policy decides what happens next, and there are exactly eight answers.

The eight maxmemory-policy values, and when each is correct
PolicyCandidate keysChoose it when
noevictionnone — writes fail with OOM command not allowedQueues, sessions, locks, rate-limit state. Losing a key is a bug, so refuse the write and page someone
allkeys-lruevery key, approximated LRUA pure cache. The safe default when every key is regenerable
allkeys-lfuevery key, frequency-basedA cache with a stable hot set; survives a full scan that would flush an LRU
allkeys-randomevery key, uniformlyUniform access with no hot set; cheapest to compute, rarely the best answer
volatile-lruonly keys with a TTLA single instance holding cache and durable data — workable, and see the warning below
volatile-lfuonly keys with a TTLAs above, frequency-weighted
volatile-randomonly keys with a TTLRarely; you almost always know something about access patterns
volatile-ttlTTL keys, shortest remaining firstValues whose worth genuinely decays with their TTL, such as short-lived tokens

Redis LRU and LFU are both approximate: eviction samples maxmemory-samples keys (default 5) and evicts the best candidate in the sample, not the global best. Raising it to 10 gets close to true LRU at a measurable CPU cost. LFU additionally decays counters over lfu-decay-time minutes, so yesterday's hot key does not stay hot forever, and OBJECT FREQ lets you inspect the counter.

One instance, two jobs, one incident

The most common self-inflicted Redis outage is a single instance holding the response cache and the session store and the job queue under allkeys-lru. A traffic spike fills memory, the cache and the queue compete, and Redis dutifully evicts whatever was least recently touched — which is the queued jobs nobody has read yet and the sessions of users who stepped away. Every user is logged out and a few thousand jobs vanish with no error anywhere, because eviction is not an error. Run at least two instances: a cache with maxmemory plus allkeys-lru, and a data instance with noeviction and alerting on memory. The volatile policies exist for people who cannot yet split, and they only help if every cache key genuinely has a TTL — if none do, volatile-* behaves exactly like noeviction.

Persistence is a recovery aid, not a durability guarantee

Say this plainly to anyone proposing Redis as a system of record. RDB takes point-in-time snapshots by forking; the child writes the dump while the parent keeps serving, so a crash costs you everything since the last snapshot — with the default save 900 1, up to fifteen minutes. AOF appends every write command and replays it on restart. appendfsync everysec, the sane default, means up to one second of acknowledged writes are lost on a hard kill, and closer to two in the pathological case. appendfsync always makes that near-zero and costs you an fsync per write, which on network storage takes throughput from six figures to low thousands.

The second, larger hole is replication. Replicas are asynchronous: the primary acknowledges your write and streams it afterwards, so a failover promotes a replica that is milliseconds behind and those writes are gone. WAIT numreplicas timeout confirms replicas received the data (not that they fsynced it), and WAITAOF in Redis 7 confirms the fsync — both at a latency cost that removes most of the reason you chose Redis. Fork itself is the operational trap: copy-on-write means a 20 GB instance under heavy write load can approach 40 GB of RSS during a background save, and the fork call alone stalls the command loop for tens to hundreds of milliseconds on a large heap. If losing a second of writes is unacceptable, the data belongs in Mongo and Redis holds a derived copy.

plaintext · redis.conf — the lines that decide your failure modes
# ---- CACHE INSTANCE ------------------------------------------------------
maxmemory 6gb                 # ~75% of the box: leave room for COW + buffers
maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru
maxmemory-samples 5           # 10 is closer to true LRU, at a CPU cost
save ""                       # no snapshots: a cache is regenerable by design
appendonly no

# ---- DATA INSTANCE (queues, sessions, locks) -----------------------------
maxmemory 6gb
maxmemory-policy noeviction   # fail the write loudly; never lose a job
appendonly yes
appendfsync everysec          # 'always' costs ~50x throughput on netdisk
auto-aof-rewrite-percentage 100
no-appendfsync-on-rewrite no  # 'yes' avoids latency spikes but risks 30s loss

# ---- SAFETY --------------------------------------------------------------
rename-command KEYS ""        # remove the footgun rather than documenting it
rename-command FLUSHALL ""
rename-command CONFIG "CONFIG_a7f3"
maxclients 10000
timeout 300                   # reap idle clients; each costs a file descriptor
tcp-keepalive 300

# Free big objects on a background thread so DEL/eviction/expiry of a
# 2M-element key does not stall the command loop for hundreds of ms.
lazyfree-lazy-eviction yes
lazyfree-lazy-expire yes
lazyfree-lazy-server-del yes
replica-lazy-flush yes

# Slow log threshold in MICROseconds. 10ms on a single-threaded server is
# already an outage in progress - this is your primary diagnostic.
slowlog-log-slower-than 10000
slowlog-max-len 512

Keyspace notifications are not a scheduler

Enabling notify-keyspace-events Ex publishes an event when a key expires, and the pattern that follows — set a key with a TTL, act when it expires — is proposed at least once in every team's history. It is unsound for three reasons. Notifications ride pub/sub, so they are at-most-once and fire-and-forget: no subscriber connected means the event is discarded with no trace. The expired event fires when the key is actually deleted, not when it logically expired, so with lazy expiry it can lag by minutes. And the event carries only the key name, never the value — the value is already gone. Notifications are acceptable for cache-invalidation hints and useless as a durable trigger. If you need a delayed job, use the sorted set above; if you need a durable event, use a stream.

Replication, Sentinel, and Cluster's one hard constraint

Replication
Asynchronous primary to replicas. Buys you read scale and a warm standby, not durability. Replicas are read-only by default; a replica that reconnects after a long gap and falls out of the backlog (repl-backlog-size) triggers a full resync, which forks the primary and ships the whole dataset.
Sentinel
Three or more processes that monitor the primary, agree it is down by quorum, elect a new primary and rewrite the replicas' configuration. Failover takes roughly 10–30 seconds by default (down-after-milliseconds plus election). Clients must be Sentinel-aware, because the endpoint moves — a plain REDIS_URL pointing at the old primary now talks to a read-only replica and every write fails with READONLY You can't write against a read only replica.
Cluster
16 384 hash slots spread across primaries; the slot is CRC16(key) mod 16384. Clients cache the slot map and follow MOVED and ASK redirects. This is the only option that scales memory and write throughput past one machine, and it costs you multi-key operations.
Hash tags
If a key contains {...}, only the substring between the first { and the following } is hashed. user:{1042}:profile and user:{1042}:sessions therefore land on the same node, so MGET, MULTI and Lua across them are legal.

Cluster's constraint is worth stating precisely because it is a rewrite if you discover it late: every key touched by a single command, transaction or script must hash to the same slot, otherwise you get CROSSSLOT Keys in request don't hash to the same slot. That kills unqualified MGET across arbitrary keys, SINTER between two unrelated sets, and every Lua script that touches more than one key family. There is also only database 0 — no SELECT. Over-tagging is the opposite failure: hash-tag everything with {tenant:42} and your largest tenant's entire dataset sits on one node, which is a hot shard you cannot rebalance away. Tag the smallest unit that genuinely needs co-location, usually a single entity.

The traps that page you

Every one of these is a consequence of the same fact: one thread executes commands, so any slow command is a full stop for every other client. A 500 ms command on a server handling 50 000 ops/s queues twenty-five thousand requests behind it.

  1. KEYS in production. O(N) over the entire keyspace with the command loop held. On eight million keys this blocks for several seconds; your health checks fail, the load balancer removes every app node, and the incident looks like an app outage. Use SCAN with COUNT, or better, maintain an index set yourself so you never need to enumerate. Delete the command with rename-command.
  2. Big keys. A 500 MB value or a two-million-element list is legal and ruinous: LRANGE 0 -1 serialises the lot, DEL frees it synchronously, and cluster resharding cannot split a single key across slots. Find them with redis-cli --bigkeys, size individuals with MEMORY USAGE key, and always UNLINK rather than DEL for anything large.
  3. Hot keys. A single key taking 40% of traffic pins one core, and no amount of clustering helps because the key lives on exactly one node. Fix it by splitting into counter:{n} shards summed on read, or by caching that one value in-process for a second.
  4. Unbounded growth. Every list, set, zset and stream needs a cap at write time — LTRIM, ZREMRANGEBYRANK, XADD MAXLEN ~ — or a TTL. The producer that outruns its consumer is how an unbounded list becomes an OOM at 04:00.
  5. Blocking commands on a shared connection. BLPOP, BLMOVE, BZPOPMIN and XREAD BLOCK block the connection, not the server. Issue one on the client your HTTP handlers share and every request behind it waits for the timeout. Blocking consumers get their own connection, always.
  6. Assuming O(1). SMEMBERS on 400 000 members, HGETALL on a wide hash, ZRANGE 0 -1, SORT, SUNIONSTORE across large sets and FLUSHALL without ASYNC are all O(N) with the loop held. Read the complexity line in the docs; it is there for exactly this reason.
bash · the six commands that diagnose a Redis incident
# Is Redis actually slow, or is it the network? --latency measures round trip
# from here; --intrinsic-latency runs ON the Redis box and measures the kernel
# scheduler alone. If intrinsic is 8ms you have a noisy-neighbour VM, not a
# Redis problem, and no amount of tuning will help.
redis-cli --latency -h redis.prod -p 6379
redis-cli --intrinsic-latency 30          # run this on the server itself

# What was slow, in microseconds, with the exact arguments. First stop, always.
redis-cli SLOWLOG GET 25

# Find the big keys. Runs SCAN under the hood so it is safe on a live primary,
# but it samples one key per type - run --memkeys for a memory-weighted view.
redis-cli --bigkeys
redis-cli MEMORY USAGE recent:1042 SAMPLES 0    # exact bytes for one key

# Find the hot keys. REQUIRES maxmemory-policy allkeys-lfu (it reads the
# access-frequency counter); returns nothing useful under an LRU policy.
redis-cli --hotkeys

# Blocked? Who is connected, what are they running, how long have they idled.
redis-cli CLIENT LIST | awk '{print $1, $17, $18}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn
redis-cli INFO clients        # blocked_clients, client_recent_max_input_buffer

# The four INFO numbers worth alerting on:
#   used_memory / maxmemory        -> approaching the eviction cliff
#   evicted_keys                   -> non-zero on a noeviction instance = bug
#   keyspace_hits / keyspace_misses-> your real hit rate, not the one you assume
#   rdb_last_bgsave_status         -> "err" means you have no backup at all
redis-cli INFO stats | grep -E 'evicted|expired|keyspace|rejected'
The habit that prevents most of this

Before adding any Redis call, answer three questions in the pull request description: what is the maximum size this key can reach, what deletes it, and what happens to the request if Redis is down. A key with no bound and no TTL is an incident with a delay fuse; a call with no timeout and no fallback turns a Redis blip into a full outage. Set commandTimeout on the client, wrap cache reads so a failure degrades to the origin, and let writes to the durable instance fail loudly rather than silently.

Redis rewards precision and punishes casual use with unusual symmetry. The right structure makes a hard concurrency problem into one atomic command; the wrong one makes a trivial counter into a lost-update bug you will only see at peak. Separate the cache from the data, cap every collection at write time, put read-modify-write in Lua, and treat persistence as a way to restart quickly rather than a promise that nothing is lost.

40

Rate limiting & load shedding

A rate limiter is not an anti-abuse feature that happens to also help under load; it is the mechanism that keeps arrival rate below service rate, and a system where arrivals exceed service does not degrade gracefully — its queue grows without bound and its latency goes to infinity. Every service has a concurrency past which it is no longer serving anybody, and the only question is whether you reject the excess in two milliseconds with a number the client can act on, or accept all of it and discover thirty seconds later that you have three copies of every request in flight and a goodput of zero.

Why this is a correctness feature

Little's Law is the whole argument. Concurrency equals arrival rate multiplied by residence time, so if you do not bound concurrency at the door, residence time is whatever the queue makes it. A service that can commit 800 requests per second and is offered 1 200 does not serve 800 and drop 400 — it accepts all 1 200, queues 400 of them, and the queue never drains, so every request including the ones you could have served comfortably now sits behind a growing backlog. Latency climbs linearly with elapsed time, the client timeout fires, the client retries, and the retry is offered load you have not counted. This is why overload looks like a cliff rather than a slope.

Bounding the input turns that cliff back into a slope you chose. It also does three things that are hard to buy any other way. It makes your capacity plan enforceable instead of aspirational, because a tenant's documented 50 rps is now a fact rather than a hope. It converts a noisy neighbour from an outage into a 429 on one customer's dashboard. And it makes retry storms survivable, because a limiter rejects in about a millisecond of CPU and can absorb an order of magnitude more rejections than the work it is protecting.

Limiter cost
< 1 msone Redis round trip; a 5 ms limiter is a tax on every request you serve
Shed threshold
ELU 0.90above this the queue grows faster than the loop drains it
Retry-After floor
1 snever emit 0 — that instructs a client to retry immediately
Client retry budget
10%of total requests; past that, retries are the incident

Five algorithms, ranked by what each one gets wrong

All five are a few lines of code. Pick by failure mode, because they are indistinguishable when traffic is well behaved.

The algorithms and the price of each
AlgorithmState per keyBurst behaviourWhat it gets wrongRight for
Fixed windowOne integer with a TTLEverything at once, then nothingThe boundary: a 100/min limit permits 100 requests at 00:59.9 and 100 more at 01:00.1 — 200 in 200 msCrude edge limits where cheapness beats precision
Sliding window logA sorted set of timestampsExact; no boundary artefactMemory. A member costs 60–100 bytes past listpack encoding, so a 1 000-request window over 200 000 active keys is 12–20 GBSmall limits where exactness is contractual — billing, OTP sends
Sliding window counterTwo integersSmooth, approximateA small, bounded error; Cloudflare measured roughly 0.003% of requests classified differently from an exact logThe pragmatic default when you do not need bursts
Token bucketToken count + last-refill timestampBurst up to bucket size, then the sustained rateOutput is not smooth — a downstream with a hard rate still gets a spikePublic APIs. This is the usual answer
Leaky bucket (queue)A bounded queue drained at a fixed rateNone — output rate is constant by constructionAdds latency, and an unbounded queue is just a slower way to run out of memoryProtecting a downstream with a contractual rate: SMTP, SMS, a partner API

The sliding window counter is worth knowing because it is one line: estimate = prev × (1 − elapsed/window) + current, where prev and current are two fixed-window counters and elapsed is how far you are into the current window. Two INCRs and a multiplication buy you almost all of the accuracy of a log at almost none of the memory. Use it when you want a hard ceiling with no bursting.

Token bucket wins for APIs because it encodes two independent numbers that customers actually care about: a sustained rate and a tolerance for bursts. A client that batches ten calls on page load is not abusive, and a limiter that rejects it will be worked around rather than obeyed. Bucket size is the largest burst you are prepared to absorb; refill rate is the number in the contract. Crucially, refill is computed on read, never scheduled — there is no timer, no cron, and an idle bucket costs nothing.

Token bucket in Redis, atomically

Read-modify-write across the network is a lost-update bug waiting for enough traffic. GET, decide, SET lets two nodes both observe one remaining token and both spend it, and under exactly the load where the limiter matters, that race fires constantly. The whole operation has to be one round trip and one atomic step, which means Lua.

javascript · limits/bucket.js — one round trip, cluster-safe, self-expiring
// ioredis registers the script once and handles EVALSHA plus the NOSCRIPT
// retry after a Redis restart. numberOfKeys: 1 means every bucket lives in a
// single hash slot, so this is safe on Redis Cluster without a hash tag.
redis.defineCommand('takeTokens', { numberOfKeys: 1, lua: `
  local rate  = tonumber(ARGV[1])   -- sustained tokens per second
  local burst = tonumber(ARGV[2])   -- bucket size = largest burst permitted
  local cost  = tonumber(ARGV[3])   -- this request's price; not always 1

  -- The clock comes from Redis, not from Node. Twenty app servers have twenty
  -- clocks, and 200 ms of NTP skew lets a client alternate between two of them
  -- to refill early. Redis has one clock. Scripts replicate by EFFECTS since
  -- Redis 5, so calling TIME here is legal and does not diverge on the replica.
  local t   = redis.call('TIME')
  local now = tonumber(t[1]) * 1000 + math.floor(tonumber(t[2]) / 1000)

  local st     = redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tk', 'ts')
  local tokens = tonumber(st[1])
  local ts     = tonumber(st[2])
  if tokens == nil then tokens = burst; ts = now end   -- unseen key starts full

  -- Refill is computed, never scheduled. No timers, no sweeper job.
  tokens = math.min(burst, tokens + ((now - ts) / 1000) * rate)

  local allowed, retry = 1, 0
  if tokens >= cost then
    tokens = tokens - cost
  else
    allowed = 0
    retry   = math.ceil(((cost - tokens) / rate) * 1000)  -- ms until affordable
  end

  redis.call('HSET', KEYS[1], 'tk', tokens, 'ts', now)
  -- Every bucket MUST expire, and the TTL is the time to refill from empty.
  -- Without this line the keyspace grows by one hash per IP address that has
  -- ever touched you, for ever; the first symptom is an eviction storm in the
  -- instance that also holds your sessions.
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil((burst / rate) * 1000) + 1000)

  return { allowed, math.floor(tokens), retry }
`});

function limit(policy, { keyOf, cost = () => 1 } = {}) {
  return async function rateLimit(req, res, next) {
    const id = keyOf(req);
    if (id === null) return next();                  // signed internal caller
    const key = `rl:${policy.name}:${id}`;

    let allowed, remaining, retryMs;
    try {
      [allowed, remaining, retryMs] = await redis.takeTokens(
        key, policy.rate, policy.burst, cost(req));
    } catch (err) {
      // FAIL OPEN, deliberately, with a metric and an alert. See the callout:
      // a limiter that fails closed turns a two-second Redis failover into a
      // total outage, and every rejected client then retries.
      metrics.increment('ratelimit.error', { policy: policy.name });
      return next();
    }

    // Emit on EVERY response, not only on the 429. A client that can see it has
    // three left will pace itself; a client that only learns at the wall will
    // sprint into it every single time.
    const resetSec = Math.ceil((policy.burst - remaining) / policy.rate);
    res.set({
      // draft-ietf-httpapi-ratelimit-headers, draft 8 form. Pick one draft and
      // document it; the legacy X-RateLimit-Reset is a UNIX epoch at GitHub and
      // a delta-seconds elsewhere, and clients have slept for decades over it.
      'RateLimit-Policy': `"${policy.name}";q=${policy.burst};w=${Math.ceil(policy.burst / policy.rate)}`,
      RateLimit: `"${policy.name}";r=${Math.max(0, remaining)};t=${resetSec}`,
    });

    if (allowed) return next();

    metrics.increment('ratelimit.blocked', { policy: policy.name, route: req.route?.path });
    res.set({
      'Retry-After': String(Math.max(1, Math.ceil(retryMs / 1000))),  // never 0
      // A CDN or shared proxy that caches a 429 serves it to every other
      // customer in that PoP. This header is not optional on an error response.
      'Cache-Control': 'no-store',
    });
    res.status(429).json({
      error: {
        code: 'rate_limited',
        // Name WHICH policy was hit. "You are rate limited" produces a support
        // ticket; "you exceeded the per-key hourly budget" produces a fix.
        policy: policy.name,
        retryAfterMs: retryMs,      // ms, because machines read this, not humans
        message: `Exceeded the ${policy.name} limit. Retry in ${Math.ceil(retryMs / 1000)}s.`,
      },
    });
  };
}
The limiter that caused the outage

Two defaults conspire. ioredis ships with no commandTimeout and an offline queue that buffers commands during a failover, so when the primary steps down, every request in the fleet blocks in the limiter for the length of the failover — your p99 becomes “however long the election took”, and the limiter you installed to survive load is now the thing amplifying it. If the catch block returns 429 or 503 instead of calling next(), it is worse: 100% of traffic is rejected, every client retries, and the retries keep the limiter's own dependency saturated after Redis recovers. Construct the client with commandTimeout: 50, enableOfflineQueue: false and maxRetriesPerRequest: 1, fail open on error, and alert on ratelimit.error — because failing open silently means you are unprotected and do not know it.

Choosing the key, and layering the limits

The key is the policy. Everything else is arithmetic. Per-IP is the weakest identity you can pick and the one everybody reaches for first: a corporate office, a university and an entire mobile carrier all arrive as one address under CGNAT, so a per-IP limit strict enough to matter will lock out thousands of legitimate users, and one loose enough not to is trivially evaded with a $30/month residential proxy pool. IP limiting belongs at the edge as a volumetric backstop, not in your application as the real control.

Prefer an identity you issued. An API key is best, because you can attach a tier and revoke it; a user id is next; an IP is the fallback for genuinely anonymous traffic. Never fall through to IP for a request that carried a credential, or a stolen key ends up sharing a bucket with the office it was stolen from.

javascript · identity, IPv6 prefixes, cost, and layering
function identityOf(req) {
  if (req.apiKey) return `k:${req.apiKey.id}`;   // strongest: we issued it
  if (req.user)   return `u:${req.user.id}`;
  return `ip:${ipKey(req)}`;                     // weakest: see below
}

function ipKey(req) {
  const ip = req.ip;                 // requires app.set('trust proxy', 1)
  if (!ip.includes(':')) return ip;
  // IPv6 must be truncated to the /64. A residential customer is delegated a
  // /64 or a /56, so limiting per /128 is not a limit: the client picks a fresh
  // address out of 18 quintillion per request and never sees a 429.
  return ip.split(':').slice(0, 4).join(':') + '::/64';
}

// Cost-based limiting: price the endpoint by what it costs YOU, in the same
// units the customer's quota is denominated in. One flat limit either starves
// the cheap endpoints or under-prices the ruinous ones.
const COST = {
  'GET /api/products':    1,    // indexed lookup, ~2 ms
  'GET /api/search':      5,    // fans out to Atlas Search, 40-80 ms
  'POST /api/auth/login': 20,   // ~250 ms of bcrypt on a 4-thread pool
  'POST /api/reports':   200,   // minutes of aggregation, then writes a file
};
const priceOf = (req) => COST[`${req.method} ${req.route?.path}`] ?? 1;

// Layers, checked narrowest first so the 429 names the limit the client can
// actually do something about. Note the honest wart: a request that passes the
// burst layer and fails the hourly one has already spent a burst token. Either
// refund it (one more HSET) or accept that a throttled client is charged
// slightly twice — but know which you chose.
router.post('/api/reports',
  limit(P.burst,    { keyOf: identityOf }),                 // 20 rps, burst 40
  limit(P.hourly,   { keyOf: identityOf, cost: priceOf }),  // 5 000 units/hour
  limit(P.tenant,   { keyOf: (r) => `t:${r.tenantId}` }),   // one tenant's ceiling
  limit(P.reports,  { keyOf: identityOf }),                 // 3 per hour, hard
  createReport);
trust proxy is a two-sided trap

Behind an ALB or nginx, req.ip is the proxy's address until you set trust proxy, so every user in the world shares one bucket and the site 429s itself. express-rate-limit v7 detects this and throws ERR_ERL_UNEXPECTED_X_FORWARDED_FOR — “the X-Forwarded-For header is set but the Express trust proxy setting is false”. The overcorrection is worse: app.set('trust proxy', true) tells Express to believe the leftmost entry of a header the client supplies, so an attacker sends X-Forwarded-For: 1.2.3.4, gets a fresh bucket per request, and your limiter is decorative. Set it to the number of proxies you actually run — app.set('trust proxy', 1) — or to an explicit CIDR list, and assert it in a startup check.

A 429 the client can obey

A rejection is an instruction, and most 429s do not contain one. The client learns it must stop but not for how long, so it does the only thing it can: retries immediately, in a loop, along with every other client you just rejected. Three things make the difference between a limiter that sheds load and one that generates it — Retry-After with a real integer, RateLimit headers on successful responses so a well-behaved client can self-pace before it hits the wall, and a body that names which policy was breached.

Distinguish 429 from 503. A 429 means you exceeded your quota and the fix is on the client's side; a 503 means we are out of capacity and the client did nothing wrong. They demand different client behaviour, they belong on different dashboards, and conflating them guarantees an hour of an incident spent arguing about whose fault it is.

javascript · the client contract you have to document
// Retry these and nothing else. A 400 or a 422 will still be a 400 or a 422 on
// the fourth attempt: retrying it is a slower way to fail that consumes someone
// else's capacity on the way down. 429 and 503 are retryable; 401 is not, and a
// client that retries a 401 in a loop is how accounts get locked out.
const RETRYABLE = new Set([408, 425, 429, 500, 502, 503, 504]);

async function call(url, init, { attempts = 4, baseMs = 200, capMs = 20_000 } = {}) {
  for (let i = 0; ; i++) {
    // POST is only safe to retry when it carries an idempotency key. Without
    // one, a retry after a timeout can charge a card twice.
    const res = await fetch(url, init);
    if (res.ok || !RETRYABLE.has(res.status) || i >= attempts - 1) return res;

    const ra = Number(res.headers.get('retry-after'));
    const waitMs = Number.isFinite(ra) && ra > 0
      // Obey the server's number. It knows when the bucket refills; your
      // exponential guess is at best a coincidence.
      ? ra * 1000
      // FULL jitter — random over [0, backoff) — not "exponential plus noise".
      // Equal jitter still clusters the fleet; full jitter spreads N clients
      // uniformly and is the difference between a recovery and a second outage.
      : Math.random() * Math.min(capMs, baseMs * 2 ** i);

    await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, waitMs));
  }
}
Publish the numbers

Put the limits, the units, the header names and the backoff policy in your API documentation with worked examples, and emit them in the sandbox environment too. Undocumented limits are discovered in production by the customer's overnight batch job, at 03:00, by their on-call engineer, who will open a P1 because from where they are standing your API just broke.

Load shedding: admission control when the limiter was not enough

Rate limits are per-identity and configured in advance. They cannot know that a slow index build has halved your capacity this afternoon, and they do nothing about ten thousand distinct clients each behaving perfectly. Admission control is the layer that reads your own health and refuses work when there is none to give.

client edge your process downstream ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── ◀── 429 Retry-After ─ WAF / edge limit per-IP, volumetric, absorbs floods for free ◀── 429 RateLimit-* ──────────────────── token bucket per identity, per tenant, per cost ◀── 503 Retry-After ──────────────────── admission control ELU · queue depth · request age │ ├─ handler ──▶ semaphore(20) ──▶ Mongo └─ priority: checkout > search > analytics reject here ────────────────────────────────────────────────────▶ and it costs 1 ms of CPU reject here ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────▶ and you paid for it all

The signal to shed on is not CPU and not p99 latency — both are lagging. The leading indicators in Node are event loop utilisation (the fraction of wall time the loop spent doing work, from performance.eventLoopUtilization()) and event loop delay (monitorEventLoopDelay(), a histogram in nanoseconds). ELU above about 0.90 means the loop is saturated and every additional request lengthens the queue rather than getting served; loop delay at p99 above 200–250 ms means requests are already waiting for the runtime before they wait for anything else.

javascript · middleware/admission.js — priority shedding on loop health
const { monitorEventLoopDelay, performance } = require('node:perf_hooks');

const h = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 10 });
h.enable();
let last = performance.eventLoopUtilization();
let elu = 0, delayP99 = 0, inFlight = 0;

// Sample on an interval, never per request: the histogram read is not free and
// you want a stable signal, not one that oscillates with a single slow query.
setInterval(() => {
  last = performance.eventLoopUtilization(last);  // delta since the last call
  elu = last.utilization;
  delayP99 = h.percentile(99) / 1e6;              // histogram is nanoseconds
  h.reset();
  metrics.gauge('loop.elu', elu);
}, 1000).unref();

// Shed cheap work first. Losing the recommendations carousel is a worse page;
// losing checkout is a worse quarter. Tag routes at definition time so the
// decision is data, reviewed in a pull request, not a guess in an incident.
const SHED_AT = { critical: 0.98, standard: 0.92, background: 0.75 };
const MAX_IN_FLIGHT = 300;         // measured, not guessed: the point where p99 leaves SLO

function admission(req, res, next) {
  const cls = req.route?.priority ?? 'standard';
  const hot = elu > SHED_AT[cls] || delayP99 > 250 || inFlight > MAX_IN_FLIGHT;

  if (hot && cls !== 'critical') {
    metrics.increment('shed', { cls, route: req.route?.path });
    // Spread the return: a constant Retry-After returns the entire shed
    // population in the same second and you shed them all again.
    res.set('Retry-After', String(1 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 5)));
    res.set('Cache-Control', 'no-store');
    // 503, not 429 — the client did nothing wrong. Keep them separable.
    return res.status(503).json({ error: { code: 'overloaded' } });
  }

  inFlight++;
  // 'close' fires on aborts too. 'finish' does not, so a client that hangs up
  // mid-response leaks a slot with 'finish' and your counter drifts upward
  // until the process restarts — a shedder that sheds everything, for ever.
  res.on('close', () => { inFlight--; });
  next();
}

// The other half: work already queued may be worthless by the time it starts.
// Under overload, LIFO beats FIFO — the newest request is the one whose client
// is still waiting, and serving it means one happy user rather than N timeouts.
function stillWorthDoing(req) {
  return Date.now() - req.startedAt < 2000;   // client already gave up? drop it
}

Shedding early beats timing out late, and the arithmetic is stark. A request rejected at admission costs a TCP read, a route match and a JSON write — call it a millisecond. The same request timed out at thirty seconds has consumed a socket for thirty seconds, a MongoDB pool connection, whatever CPU the handler burned before it stalled, and it has already been retried twice by a client that gave up at ten. That is three in-flight copies of a request whose result nobody will read. Under sustained overload with retries, useful throughput collapses to a fraction of capacity while the machine reports itself fully busy — the classic congestion collapse signature, and the reason a shed rate of 20% can restore a p99 that a 0% shed rate had destroyed.

Backpressure, all the way down

Admission control at the front door only holds if every hop inside also refuses to accept unbounded work. The discipline is that every queue is bounded, every outbound call has a timeout shorter than the one your caller gave you, and every pool exhaustion is a fast rejection rather than an unbounded wait. A bounded semaphore in front of an expensive downstream is the cheapest version: twenty concurrent calls permitted, a short acquisition timeout, and a 503 rather than a queue. Combined with a circuit breaker, a downstream that is failing stops being asked, which is the only thing that lets it recover.

The tell that you have got this wrong is a timeout budget that grows inward instead of shrinking: the edge waits 30 s, the app waits 30 s on Mongo, Mongo's serverSelectionTimeoutMS is 30 s. Every layer holds resources for the full duration of a request the client abandoned at second ten.

The endpoints that need a budget of their own

A per-user limit of 60 requests per minute is meaningless if one of those requests costs four CPU-seconds. These five need explicit, separate ceilings.

Search

Unbounded query strings, deep pagination and leading wildcards turn one request into a full index scan. Cap the offset (skip past 10 000 is a refusal, not a slow query), reject queries below a minimum length, price it at 5–10 tokens, and give it its own connection semaphore so a search spike cannot starve checkout of pool connections.

Export and report generation

These are jobs pretending to be requests. Never run them inline: accept, return POST 202 Accepted with a status URL, and enqueue. Then limit concurrency per tenant in the worker — three per hour per account, one running at a time — because the natural client behaviour when a report is slow is to click the button again.

Password hashing

bcrypt at cost 12 is roughly 250 ms of pure CPU by design. The native bcrypt module runs on the libuv threadpool, which is four threads by default, so your absolute ceiling is about 16 logins per second per process no matter how many cores you own — and every other threadpool user (fs, dns.lookup, zlib) is queued behind it. Pure-JS bcryptjs is worse: it blocks the event loop outright, so 30 concurrent logins stalls every request in the process. Limit login and registration hard, per-IP and per-account.

Email, SMS and webhooks

Outbound to a third party with its own contractual rate. This is the one case where leaky bucket is right: queue and drain at a fixed rate so a burst of 5 000 signups does not get your sending domain throttled or blocked. Password-reset and OTP endpoints also need a strict per-account fixed window — five per hour — because the abuse target is the recipient's inbox, not your CPU.

Limits are worthless if they are not observed

Export three series from day one: rejections per policy, rejections per identity, and utilisation of the limit as a ratio. Without the third you cannot answer “is this customer about to be throttled?” and you will learn the answer from their escalation. A step change in ratelimit.blocked concentrated on one key is a broken client loop; the same step spread evenly across thousands of keys means you have shipped a limit that is simply too low, and the fix is a config change, not a conversation about abuse.

Interview follow-up

“How would you rate limit this API?” is answered weakly by naming an algorithm. Answer with the key, the layers and the failure mode: “Token bucket in Redis, in a single Lua script so the check is atomic in one round trip, with the clock taken from Redis rather than from the app servers because NTP skew across twenty nodes is exploitable. The key is the API key or the user id — per-IP only at the edge, because CGNAT means one address is a whole mobile carrier and a proxy pool defeats it anyway. Layered: a burst bucket, an hourly cost budget where search costs five and a report costs two hundred, and a per-tenant ceiling so one customer cannot spend the fleet. On rejection, 429 with Retry-After, RateLimit headers on every response including the successful ones, and Cache-Control: no-store so a CDN cannot cache the rejection for a whole PoP. The limiter fails open on a Redis error with a metric, because failing closed converts a two-second failover into an outage and then the clients retry.” Then volunteer the part most candidates never reach, because it is what marks someone who has been on call: “Rate limits are per-identity and static, so they cannot protect me from ten thousand well-behaved clients on the afternoon my capacity halved. Behind them I want admission control on event loop utilisation and in-flight count, shedding by request priority — analytics before search, search before checkout — and returning 503 rather than 429 because the client did nothing wrong. Rejecting at the door costs a millisecond; timing out at thirty seconds costs a socket, a pool connection and two client retries, so under overload the cheap rejection is what preserves goodput.”

41

Queues, workers & the outbox

Anything slow, anything retryable, and anything the user does not need to wait for belongs on a queue. The hard part is not enqueuing — it is that at-least-once delivery means every handler you write will run twice, and most handlers are not ready for that.

What goes on a queue

WorkQueue it?Why
Sending email, SMS, pushalwaysthird-party latency and failure must not be in the request path
Image/video processing, PDF generationalwaysseconds of CPU; must not share the API process
Webhook delivery to customersalwaysneeds retries with backoff over hours
Search index updatesusuallyeventual consistency is acceptable; a failed index write must not fail the write
Report and export generationalwaysunbounded runtime — return 202 and a status URL
Reading the record you just wroteneveryou have created an eventual-consistency bug for no benefit
Anything the response body depends onneveryou cannot await a queue and still call it async

BullMQ, configured for production

javascript · queue and worker setup
import { Queue, Worker, QueueEvents } from 'bullmq';

const connection = { host: cfg.redisHost, port: cfg.redisPort, maxRetriesPerRequest: null };
// maxRetriesPerRequest MUST be null for BullMQ's blocking commands, or the
// worker throws on any Redis blip instead of reconnecting.

export const emailQueue = new Queue('email', {
  connection,
  defaultJobOptions: {
    attempts: 5,
    backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 2_000 },   // 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s, 32s
    removeOnComplete: { age: 3_600, count: 1_000 },   // BOUND THIS or Redis grows forever
    removeOnFail: { age: 7 * 86_400 },                // keep failures long enough to inspect
  },
});

const worker = new Worker('email', async (job) => {
  // job.data is JSON — it crossed a process boundary, so validate it like any input
  const { messageId, to, template, vars } = emailJobSchema.parse(job.data);

  // IDEMPOTENCY: at-least-once means this may be the second run
  const claimed = await sends.updateOne(
    { _id: messageId, status: 'pending' },
    { $set: { status: 'sending', attemptedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (claimed.matchedCount === 0) {
    logger().info({ messageId }, 'already sent — duplicate delivery, skipping');
    return { skipped: true };
  }

  await job.updateProgress(30);
  const providerId = await mailer.send({ to, template, vars, idempotencyKey: messageId });
  await sends.updateOne({ _id: messageId }, { $set: { status: 'sent', providerId } });
  return { providerId };
}, {
  connection,
  concurrency: 20,                       // per worker process
  limiter: { max: 100, duration: 1_000 },// provider rate limit, enforced across this worker
  lockDuration: 60_000,                  // must exceed the slowest legitimate job
  stalledInterval: 30_000,
  maxStalledCount: 2,                    // after this, the job goes to failed
});

worker.on('failed', (job, err) => {
  const final = (job?.attemptsMade ?? 0) >= (job?.opts.attempts ?? 1);
  logger().error({ jobId: job?.id, attempt: job?.attemptsMade, final, err }, 'job failed');
  if (final) deadLetter.inc({ queue: 'email', reason: classify(err) });
});

// Graceful shutdown: finish the active job, then stop.
process.on('SIGTERM', async () => {
  await worker.close();                  // waits for active jobs, stops taking new ones
  await emailQueue.close();
});
Three settings that cause outages when omitted
  • removeOnComplete/removeOnFail unbounded. Every completed job stays in Redis. A queue doing 200 jobs/second fills a 4 GB instance in under a week, and Redis evicting or refusing writes takes down every other thing using it.
  • lockDuration shorter than the job. The lock expires mid-execution, the job is declared stalled and re-run while the first run is still going. You now have two concurrent executions of a handler that assumed exclusivity.
  • maxRetriesPerRequest left at the default. BullMQ uses blocking Redis commands; the default retry limit makes them throw on a transient blip.

The job states, and where jobs get stuck

waiting ──▶ active ──▶ completed ▲ │ │ ├──▶ failed ──(attempts left)──▶ delayed ──▶ waiting │ │ │ └──▶ stalled ── lock expired while active │ ↳ re-queued, up to maxStalledCount, then failed │ delayed scheduled for the future (backoff, or an explicit delay) paused queue-level stop; jobs accumulate in waiting Where jobs actually get stuck in production: · waiting grows → no worker running, or concurrency too low · active never drains → a handler with no timeout, awaiting a dead dependency · delayed piles up → every job failing; backoff pushing them further out · stalled climbing → lockDuration too short, or the event loop is blocked
Every handler needs its own timeout

BullMQ's lock does not cancel your code — it only lets another worker claim the job. A handler awaiting an HTTP call with no timeout occupies a concurrency slot forever, and the queue silently loses capacity one slot at a time until nothing moves. Wrap external calls in AbortSignal.timeout() and make the handler's total budget shorter than lockDuration.

The transactional outbox

“Save the order, then publish order.placed” is a distributed transaction across two systems, and you cannot have one. If the process dies between the two, you have a state change nobody was told about — or, if you publish first, an event for a state change that never happened.

BROKEN OUTBOX ──────── ────── await orders.insertOne(o) await session.withTransaction(() => { ← crash here = silent data loss orders.insertOne(o, { session }) await queue.add('order.placed', …) outbox.insertOne(evt, { session }) }) ↓ ONE atomic write relay polls outbox → publishes → marks sent ↓ crash anywhere = retry, never loss
javascript · the write side and the relay
// ---- write side: one atomic operation ----
await session.withTransaction(async () => {
  const order = await orders.insertOne({ ...doc, status: 'placed' }, { session });
  await outbox.insertOne({
    _id: new ObjectId(),
    type: 'order.placed',
    payload: { orderId: order.insertedId, tenantId, totalCents },
    createdAt: new Date(),
    publishedAt: null,          // partial index only covers null → tiny index
    attempts: 0,
  }, { session });
});
// index: { publishedAt: 1 }, partialFilterExpression: { publishedAt: null }

// ---- relay: a separate process, or a leader-elected job ----
async function relayOnce() {
  // findOneAndUpdate claims the row atomically, so two relays cannot double-publish
  for (;;) {
    const evt = await outbox.findOneAndUpdate(
      { publishedAt: null, claimedUntil: { $lt: new Date() } },
      { $set: { claimedUntil: new Date(Date.now() + 30_000) }, $inc: { attempts: 1 } },
      { sort: { createdAt: 1 }, returnDocument: 'after' },
    );
    if (!evt) return;

    // jobId makes the enqueue itself idempotent — a retry re-uses the same job
    await bus.add(evt.type, evt.payload, { jobId: evt._id.toString() });
    await outbox.updateOne({ _id: evt._id }, { $set: { publishedAt: new Date() } });
  }
}

// Alternative to polling: a change stream on the outbox collection, which removes
// the poll latency but needs a resume token persisted so a restart does not replay
// from the beginning or skip events.
const stream = outbox.watch([{ $match: { operationType: 'insert' } }],
  { resumeAfter: await loadResumeToken(), fullDocument: 'updateLookup' });
The guarantee you actually get

The outbox gives at-least-once publication of an event that is atomic with the state change. It does not give exactly-once — the relay can publish and then crash before marking the row, so the consumer must be idempotent. That is the whole contract, and stating it precisely is what distinguishes understanding the pattern from having read about it.

Scheduling in a multi-instance world

javascript · one run, not one run per pod
// WRONG: setInterval in the API process. Ten pods = ten executions.
setInterval(sendDigests, 3_600_000);

// Option A — repeatable job. BullMQ deduplicates by repeat key across producers.
await emailQueue.add('digest', {}, {
  repeat: { pattern: '0 9 * * *', tz: 'Europe/London' },
  jobId: 'daily-digest',       // stable id prevents duplicate schedules on redeploy
});

// Option B — leader lock, when you cannot use a queue
async function withLeadership(key, ttlMs, fn) {
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  const won = await redis.set(`lock:${key}`, token, 'PX', ttlMs, 'NX');
  if (!won) return;
  try { return await fn(); }
  finally {
    // Compare-and-delete: never DEL blindly, or you release someone else's lock
    await redis.eval(
      `if redis.call('get',KEYS[1])==ARGV[1] then return redis.call('del',KEYS[1]) end`,
      1, `lock:${key}`, token);
  }
}
// Honest caveat: a GC pause longer than the TTL means two leaders. For anything
// where that is unacceptable, the work itself must be idempotent as well.

What to alert on

Oldest job age
< 5 minthe only metric that catches a silently stopped worker
Failed rate
< 1%rising means a dependency is degraded
Stalled count
0anything above zero means locks are expiring
Active vs concurrency
< 90%pinned at the limit means you need more workers
Interview follow-up

“Why alert on oldest-job age rather than queue depth?” Because depth is ambiguous — a depth of 10 000 is fine if you are draining 5 000 per second, and a depth of 3 is a crisis if nothing has moved for an hour. Age directly measures the thing users experience, and it is the one signal that fires when every worker has quietly died, which is exactly when depth looks stable.

42

Event-driven architecture

Events buy you decoupling and absorbency at the cost of eventual consistency and much harder debugging. That trade is worth making at a specific point, and most teams reach for it several years early.

Queue or log — the decision that matters

Work queue (BullMQ, SQS)Durable log (Kafka, Redpanda)
Consumptiona message is taken by one consumer and deletedevery consumer group reads the whole partition independently
Replayno — once acked it is goneyes — reset an offset and reprocess history
Fan-outone queue per consumer, published N timesone topic, N consumer groups, published once
Orderingbest efforttotal order within a partition
Retentionuntil processeddays, weeks, or compacted forever
Operational costlow — it is just Redisreal: brokers, partitions, rebalances, a team that understands it
Right for"do this work" — email, thumbnails, webhooks"this happened" — many independent consumers, audit, analytics, replay
The decision rule

If the message is an instruction to one worker, use a queue. If it is a fact that several unrelated systems care about, and you would want to replay it after fixing a consumer bug, use a log. A single service with one database needs neither, and adding Kafka to it is one of the most common and most expensive architectural mistakes in this space.

Kafka concepts a backend engineer must be able to explain

Topic
A named log. Not a queue — nothing is removed when read.
Partition
The unit of both ordering and parallelism. Order is guaranteed within a partition and nowhere else. Partition count is the ceiling on consumers in a group, and it is easy to increase and impossible to decrease.
Key
Hashed to choose the partition. All events for one order or one user land in the same partition and therefore stay ordered relative to each other. Choosing the key is choosing your ordering guarantee.
Consumer group
A set of consumers sharing the partitions of a topic. Adding a consumer beyond the partition count gets you an idle consumer.
Rebalance
