Partition reassignment when membership changes. During it, consumption stops. Frequent rebalances — from long processing pauses exceeding max.poll.interval.ms — look exactly like a broken consumer.
Offset
The consumer's position. Committing before processing gives at-most-once; committing after gives at-least-once. There is no third option that removes the need for idempotency.
Compaction
Retain only the latest value per key, forever. Turns a topic into a durable changelog you can rebuild state from.
javascript · a consumer that is safe to restart
import { Kafka } from 'kafkajs';

const consumer = new Kafka({ clientId: 'orders-api', brokers: cfg.brokers })
  .consumer({
    groupId: 'search-indexer',
    sessionTimeout: 30_000,
    heartbeatInterval: 3_000,
    maxBytesPerPartition: 1_048_576,
  });

await consumer.subscribe({ topic: 'order.events', fromBeginning: false });

await consumer.run({
  eachBatchAutoResolve: false,          // WE decide what counts as processed
  autoCommit: false,
  eachBatch: async ({ batch, resolveOffset, heartbeat, isRunning, isStale, commitOffsetsIfNecessary }) => {
    for (const message of batch.messages) {
      if (!isRunning() || isStale()) break;      // rebalance happened — stop, do not commit

      const event = JSON.parse(message.value.toString());

      // Idempotency: a unique index on eventId makes a replay a no-op.
      try {
        await processed.insertOne({ _id: event.id, at: new Date() });
      } catch (e) {
        if (e.code === 11000) { resolveOffset(message.offset); continue; }  // already done
        throw e;
      }

      await indexer.upsert(event.payload);
      resolveOffset(message.offset);             // mark THIS message done
      await heartbeat();                         // long batches must heartbeat or we rebalance
    }
    await commitOffsetsIfNecessary();
  },
});
Why the heartbeat matters

If your handler takes longer than max.poll.interval.ms without heartbeating, the broker decides the consumer is dead and reassigns its partitions. The original consumer then finishes, tries to commit, and is rejected — while another consumer has already reprocessed the same messages. The symptom is duplicated side effects and a consumer group that rebalances continuously, and it is almost always a slow handler rather than a broker problem.

Event design is API design

Event, not command

order.placed states a fact that already happened, in the past tense, and the publisher does not care who listens. send_confirmation_email is a command aimed at one consumer — that belongs on a queue, not a topic.

Thin vs fat

Thin (id only) keeps the contract small but forces every consumer to call back, creating load and a race where the entity has already changed. Fat (full payload) removes the callback but makes the event schema a public contract you must version. Prefer fat for facts, thin for large or sensitive payloads.

Versioning

Published events are a public API. Additive changes only; new consumers tolerate unknown fields; a breaking change means a new topic or an explicit version field, never a redefinition of an existing field.

Envelope

Every event carries id, type, version, occurredAt, tenantId, correlationId and causationId. The last two are what make a distributed flow debuggable at all.

json · the envelope
{
  "id": "01J8ZQ4K9X2N7B3E5H6P",
  "type": "order.placed",
  "version": 2,
  "occurredAt": "2026-04-11T10:14:02.417Z",
  "tenantId": "t_9fa2",
  "correlationId": "req_7c1d",
  "causationId": "cmd_44be",
  "payload": {
    "orderId": "65f1c2...",
    "customerId": "65a03b...",
    "totalCents": 10680,
    "currency": "GBP"
  }
}

Choreography or orchestration

CHOREOGRAPHY — each service reacts to events; nobody owns the flow order.placed ─▶ [payments] ─▶ payment.captured ─▶ [inventory] ─▶ stock.reserved ─▶ [shipping] + services stay decoupled, easy to add a consumer − no single place shows the flow; debugging a stuck order means reading five services − compensations are scattered and easy to get wrong ORCHESTRATION — one saga coordinator owns the sequence and the compensations [saga] ─▶ reserve stock ─▶ capture payment ─▶ create shipment ─▶ done ↳ on failure at step N, run compensations N-1 … 1 in reverse + the flow is one readable state machine; timeouts and compensation are explicit − the coordinator is a dependency and can become a bottleneck

The practical rule: choreography for two or three steps with no rollback semantics; orchestration the moment money, inventory or a legal obligation is involved, because you need a single place that can answer “where is this order and what has already been done to it”.

What gets harder the day you go event-driven
  • Debugging. A user says checkout failed; the failure is three services away and forty seconds later. Without a correlation id on every event and a trace that spans the broker, you are reading logs by timestamp and guessing.
  • Testing. Unit tests still pass while the system is broken, because the contract is now between services. You need contract tests on event schemas.
  • Ordering. Two events about the same entity, on different topics, arriving out of order. Version numbers on the entity and rejecting stale updates is the only reliable defence.
  • Backfill. A consumer bug means reprocessing — which is only possible if you chose a log, and only safe if the consumer is idempotent.
The senior answer

“I introduce events when a second consumer appears for the same fact, or when a synchronous call is coupling my availability to someone else's. Before that, a queue for slow work and direct calls for everything else is simpler and easier to debug. When I do introduce them I insist on three things from day one: an envelope with correlation and causation ids, idempotent consumers backed by a unique index, and the outbox so publication is atomic with the state change. Without those three, an event-driven system is a distributed system with no way to reason about it.”

43

Real-time: WebSockets & SSE

The hard part of real-time is not the protocol — it is that a connection lives on exactly one process, so every message must find the process holding its recipient. Everything else follows from that.

Choosing the transport

PollingLong pollingSSEWebSocket
Directionclient pullsclient pullsserver → clientboth
Transportplain HTTPplain HTTPplain HTTPupgraded TCP
Reconnectn/amanualautomatic, with Last-Event-IDmanual
Proxy friendlinessperfectgoodgood (disable buffering)needs upgrade headers
Cost per clienta request per intervala held requestone held responseone socket
Use forslow-changing datalegacy fallbacknotifications, prices, progress, feedschat, collaboration, games
SSE is underrated

If the client only receives, SSE is the right answer more often than teams assume: it is ordinary HTTP so it passes through every proxy and load balancer you already have, it reconnects by itself, and it resumes from Last-Event-ID without you writing a resume protocol. Reach for WebSockets when the client genuinely needs to send frequently — not because real-time is a synonym for WebSocket.

javascript · SSE done properly
app.get('/v1/stream', requireAuth, (req, res) => {
  res.writeHead(200, {
    'content-type': 'text/event-stream',
    'cache-control': 'no-cache, no-transform',   // no-transform stops proxies buffering
    connection: 'keep-alive',
    'x-accel-buffering': 'no',                   // nginx: disable response buffering
  });
  res.flushHeaders();

  // Resume: the browser sends the last id it saw on reconnect
  const since = req.get('last-event-id');
  if (since) replayFrom(req.principal.id, since).forEach(send);

  function send(evt) {
    // The wire format is line-based; a newline inside data breaks the frame.
    res.write(`id: ${evt.id}\n`);
    res.write(`event: ${evt.type}\n`);
    res.write(`data: ${JSON.stringify(evt.payload)}\n\n`);
  }

  const unsubscribe = bus.subscribe(req.principal.id, send);

  // Comment frames keep intermediaries from closing an idle connection
  const beat = setInterval(() => res.write(': ping\n\n'), 25_000);

  req.on('close', () => { clearInterval(beat); unsubscribe(); });
});

The fan-out problem

A message for user 42 arrives at pod 3. User 42's socket is on pod 7. Pod 3 has no idea. OPTION A — pub/sub broadcast OPTION B — connection registry pod 3 publishes to Redis channel pod 3 reads Redis: user42 → pod 7 every pod receives it pod 3 sends directly to pod 7 each pod checks its local sockets pod 7 delivers + trivial, no registry to keep correct + O(1) network, scales to many pods − every pod handles every message − registry must survive crashes and − at 50 pods that is 50x the work reconnects; stale entries are common Rule of thumb: broadcast until roughly 10-20 pods or high message rates, then move to a registry — or shard channels so a pod only subscribes to the channels it actually holds connections for.
javascript · Redis-backed fan-out with per-user channels
// Subscribe only to the channels this pod actually needs — the middle path
// between broadcasting everything and maintaining a global registry.
const sub = redis.duplicate();
const local = new Map();                     // userId -> Set<socket>

export async function attach(userId, socket) {
  if (!local.has(userId)) {
    local.set(userId, new Set());
    await sub.subscribe(`u:${userId}`);      // first socket for this user on this pod
  }
  local.get(userId).add(socket);
}

export async function detach(userId, socket) {
  const set = local.get(userId);
  if (!set) return;
  set.delete(socket);
  if (set.size === 0) {
    local.delete(userId);
    await sub.unsubscribe(`u:${userId}`);    // stop paying for a user we no longer hold
  }
}

sub.on('message', (channel, raw) => {
  const userId = channel.slice(2);
  const msg = JSON.parse(raw);
  for (const s of local.get(userId) ?? []) deliver(s, msg);
});

// Publishing from anywhere — an API handler, a worker, another service
export const publish = (userId, msg) => redis.publish(`u:${userId}`, JSON.stringify(msg));
Redis pub/sub loses messages by design

It is fire-and-forget: a subscriber that is disconnected at the moment of publish never sees the message, and there is no acknowledgement or replay. That is fine for a presence indicator and wrong for anything the user must not miss. For messages that matter, write to durable storage first and use pub/sub only as a wake-up signal — the client then fetches from a cursor, which also makes reconnection correct.

Authentication, backpressure and half-open connections

javascript · WebSocket concerns that bite in production
import { WebSocketServer } from 'ws';
const wss = new WebSocketServer({ noServer: true });

server.on('upgrade', async (req, socket, head) => {
  // 1. AUTH before the upgrade. Never accept a token in the query string —
  //    it lands in access logs, referrers and proxy caches. Use a cookie, or a
  //    short-lived single-use ticket the client fetches over normal HTTP first.
  const principal = await verifyTicket(req.headers.cookie);
  if (!principal) { socket.write('HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized\r\n\r\n'); return socket.destroy(); }

  // 2. Origin check — the browser does NOT apply CORS to WebSockets.
  if (!cfg.allowedOrigins.includes(req.headers.origin)) {
    socket.write('HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden\r\n\r\n'); return socket.destroy();
  }

  wss.handleUpgrade(req, socket, head, (ws) => { ws.principal = principal; wss.emit('connection', ws, req); });
});

wss.on('connection', (ws) => {
  ws.isAlive = true;
  ws.on('pong', () => { ws.isAlive = true; });

  ws.on('message', (data) => {
    if (data.length > 64 * 1024) return ws.close(1009, 'message too large');
    handle(ws, JSON.parse(data));
  });
});

// 3. Half-open detection. TCP will not tell you the laptop went into a tunnel.
setInterval(() => {
  for (const ws of wss.clients) {
    if (!ws.isAlive) { ws.terminate(); continue; }   // terminate, not close — it is already gone
    ws.isAlive = false;
    ws.ping();
  }
}, 30_000).unref();

// 4. Backpressure. A slow client's messages queue in the process's memory.
function deliver(ws, msg) {
  if (ws.bufferedAmount > 1_048_576) return ws.close(1013, 'too slow');
  ws.send(JSON.stringify(msg));
}

The numbers that size your fleet

Memory per socket
~20–60 kBNode buffers plus your per-connection state
Sockets per pod
10–50 kraise ulimit -n or you hit EMFILE long before memory
Reconnect storm
all at oncea pod restart reconnects every client simultaneously — jitter is mandatory
LB idle timeout
> heartbeata 60 s idle timeout kills a 90 s heartbeat
The reconnect storm

When a pod holding 30 000 connections restarts, 30 000 clients reconnect within a second — to the remaining pods, which are already handling their own load. Without full jitter on the client's backoff and a connection rate limit at the edge, a single rolling deploy becomes a self-inflicted denial of service. Test this deliberately before you find it during an incident.

Interview follow-up

“How do you scale WebSockets horizontally?” The complete answer names the constraint first — a connection is pinned to one process — then the two mechanisms (broadcast pub/sub versus a connection registry) with the crossover point, then the three details that make it correct in production: durable storage with a cursor so a reconnect resumes rather than gaps, heartbeats to detect half-open sockets, and jittered client backoff so a deploy does not stampede.

44

Idempotency & exactly-once

Every network call can fail after the server processed it. The client cannot tell that apart from a call that never arrived, so it must retry — which means the server must make retries harmless. There is no exactly-once delivery; there is at-least-once delivery plus idempotent processing.

client server │ POST /orders ──────────────▶ │ order created ✓ │ │ charge captured ✓ │ ✗ connection reset ◀──────── │ 200 OK (never arrives) │ │ "did that work? I must retry" │ POST /orders ──────────────▶ │ second order, second charge │ The client did nothing wrong. The server did nothing wrong. Without an idempotency key, the customer is charged twice.

Idempotency keys

javascript · middleware that makes any endpoint safe to retry
// index: { tenantId: 1, key: 1 } unique
//        { createdAt: 1 } expireAfterSeconds: 86400
export function idempotent({ required = true } = {}) {
  return ah(async (req, res, next) => {
    const key = req.get('idempotency-key');
    if (!key) {
      if (!required) return next();
      throw new AppError('Idempotency-Key header required', { status: 400 });
    }
    if (!/^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{16,128}$/.test(key)) throw new BadRequest([{ path: 'idempotency-key', message: 'invalid' }]);

    // The fingerprint catches a client reusing a key for a DIFFERENT request,
    // which is a client bug we must surface rather than silently replay.
    const fingerprint = sha256(JSON.stringify({ m: req.method, p: req.path, b: req.body }));

    try {
      // Claim the key. The unique index is what makes two concurrent requests safe.
      await req.repos.idempotency.insertOne({
        tenantId: req.tenant.id, key, fingerprint,
        state: 'in_progress', createdAt: new Date(),
      });
    } catch (e) {
      if (e.code !== 11000) throw e;

      const existing = await req.repos.idempotency.findOne({ tenantId: req.tenant.id, key });
      if (existing.fingerprint !== fingerprint) {
        throw new Conflict('idempotency key reused with a different payload');
      }
      if (existing.state === 'in_progress') {
        // The first request is still running. Do NOT run it again.
        res.set('retry-after', '1');
        throw new AppError('request in progress', { status: 409, type: 'urn:acme:in-progress' });
      }
      // Replay the stored response byte for byte.
      res.set('idempotency-replayed', 'true');
      return res.status(existing.status).json(existing.body);
    }

    // Capture the response so a later retry can replay it.
    const json = res.json.bind(res);
    res.json = (body) => {
      // Only persist a successful outcome; a 500 should be retryable.
      if (res.statusCode < 500) {
        req.repos.idempotency.updateOne(
          { tenantId: req.tenant.id, key },
          { $set: { state: 'complete', status: res.statusCode, body } },
        ).catch((err) => logger().error({ err, key }, 'failed to persist idempotent response'));
      } else {
        req.repos.idempotency.deleteOne({ tenantId: req.tenant.id, key }).catch(() => {});
      }
      return json(body);
    };
    next();
  });
}

r.post('/orders', idempotent(), validate(createOrderSchema), ah(create));
The three details people get wrong
  1. Checking before inserting. findOne then insertOne is a race — two concurrent requests both find nothing and both proceed. The unique index must be the arbiter; you handle E11000, you do not avoid it.
  2. Storing the response on a 5xx. A server error should be retryable. Persisting it means the client retries and gets the same failure forever.
  3. No fingerprint. Without it, a client that reuses a key for a genuinely different request silently receives the wrong response, and the second operation never happens.

Natural idempotency — cheaper than a key

OperationReplay-safe?Make it safe
$set: { status: 'shipped' }yesalready idempotent — the end state is the same
$inc: { stock: -1 }noguard with the expected state, or record the operation id and check it first
$push: { events: e }no$addToSet, or a unique index on the event id
POST create with server-generated idnoaccept a client-supplied id and upsert — PUT semantics
PUT with a client idyesnaturally idempotent; prefer it where the client can generate the id
State transitionyes, if guardedput the current state in the filter, so the second attempt matches nothing
javascript · the guarded transition — a compare-and-swap
// Runs once no matter how many times it is called. The filter IS the lock.
const result = await orders.updateOne(
  { _id: id, tenantId, status: 'placed' },        // only transitions FROM placed
  { $set: { status: 'shipped', shippedAt: new Date() }, $inc: { version: 1 } },
);

if (result.modifiedCount === 1) {
  await notify(id);                                // side effects only on the real transition
} else {
  const current = await orders.findOne({ _id: id, tenantId }, { projection: { status: 1 } });
  if (!current) throw new NotFound('order', id);
  if (current.status === 'shipped') return;        // already done — a retry, not an error
  throw new Conflict(`cannot ship an order in state ${current.status}`);
}

Idempotent consumers

javascript · the effect and the marker in one atomic write
// A queue or a broker will deliver twice. Design for it rather than hoping.
// index: { _id: 1 } is the dedup — the event id IS the primary key.
export async function onPaymentCaptured(event) {
  const session = client.startSession();
  try {
    await session.withTransaction(async () => {
      // Insert the marker FIRST, inside the same transaction as the effect.
      // If this throws E11000 the whole transaction aborts and nothing happened twice.
      await processed.insertOne(
        { _id: event.id, type: event.type, at: new Date() },
        { session },
      );
      await orders.updateOne(
        { _id: event.payload.orderId },
        { $set: { paymentStatus: 'captured', capturedAt: new Date() } },
        { session },
      );
      await ledger.insertOne({ ...entryFor(event) }, { session });
    });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === 11000 || e.errorLabels?.includes('TransientTransactionError') === false && isDuplicate(e)) {
      logger().debug({ eventId: event.id }, 'duplicate event, already processed');
      return;                                     // ack it — this is success, not failure
    }
    throw e;
  } finally {
    await session.endSession();
  }
}
// TTL on `processed` sets your deduplication WINDOW. A 7-day TTL means a
// redelivery 8 days later runs again. Size it against your broker's retention.

What cannot be made idempotent

Some effects leave the system and cannot be recalled: a charge at a payment provider, an email, an SMS, a webhook to a customer. For these there are exactly three strategies, and it is worth naming which one you are using:

Push it to the provider

Most payment APIs accept an idempotency key of their own. Forward yours. The duplicate is then resolved by the system that owns the money, which is the only one that can resolve it correctly.

Claim before acting

Atomically transition a record to sending before calling out. A second attempt finds it already claimed and stops. There remains a window where you claimed and then crashed — which is why the record needs a timeout and a human-visible stuck state.

Make duplicates harmless

Design the email so receiving it twice is merely mildly annoying rather than wrong. “Send exactly once” is not achievable; “a duplicate costs nothing” is a design you can actually deliver.

Test it, do not assume it

One test per unsafe endpoint: fire the identical request twice concurrently (not sequentially — sequential passes while the race still exists) and assert exactly one effect. Sequential-only tests are why idempotency bugs reach production despite a green suite.

javascript · the test that actually catches the race
it('creates one order when the same key arrives twice concurrently', async () => {
  const key = crypto.randomUUID();
  const send = () => request(app).post('/v1/orders')
    .set('idempotency-key', key).set('authorization', auth).send(body);

  const [a, b] = await Promise.all([send(), send()]);

  // One 201; the other is either a 201 replay or a 409 in-progress — both correct.
  assert.ok([a.status, b.status].includes(201));
  assert.ok([a.status, b.status].every(s => [201, 409].includes(s)));
  assert.equal(await db.collection('orders').countDocuments({}), 1);
});
The senior answer

“Exactly-once delivery does not exist over an unreliable network — the sender can never know whether the acknowledgement was lost or the message was. What you build instead is at-least-once delivery plus idempotent processing, and the idempotency always comes from a uniqueness constraint in the database: a unique index on the idempotency key for APIs, on the event id for consumers, or the current state in the filter for a transition. If it is enforced in application code rather than by the storage engine, it is not enforced.”

45

Diagnosing a slow API

“The API is slow” is not a problem statement. This chapter is the decision tree that turns it into one, in the order you should actually run it — cheapest and most discriminating checks first.

Step 0 — establish the facts before touching anything

Who
Everyone, one tenant, or one region? A single tenant points at data volume or a noisy neighbour; one region points at infrastructure.
What
All routes or one? All routes means a shared resource — the process, the database, the network. One route means that route's query or dependency.
When
The exact time it started. Then diff it against the deploy log, the config change log, and any scheduled job. Most “mysterious” slowness started at a deploy.
How slow
p50 or only p99? A p99-only regression is queueing, contention or a long tail of large records. A p50 regression is everything getting uniformly worse.
The highest-yield question

“What changed?” Deploy, config, feature flag, traffic mix, data volume, or a dependency's release. Systems that were fine yesterday and are slow today almost never became slow spontaneously. Check the change log before you open a profiler.

The decision tree

Is it one route or all routes? │ ├─ ONE ROUTE ──▶ that route's DB queries and outbound calls │ ↳ explain() the query · check the index · check docs examined │ ↳ check payload size · check N+1 · check dependency latency │ └─ ALL ROUTES ──▶ something shared is saturated. Check in this order: │ ├─ 1. event loop delay p99 > 20 ms? ──▶ blocked or overloaded │ ↳ CPU high = too much work, or a sync hot path │ ↳ CPU low = blocked on something synchronous │ ├─ 2. GC pause / RSS sawtooth, rising? ──▶ leak or oversized payloads │ ├─ 3. pool checkout time rising? ──▶ pool exhaustion │ ↳ DB op duration FLAT + checkout HIGH = not the database, the pool │ ├─ 4. DB op duration rising? ──▶ index, lock, or cache eviction │ ↳ also check replication lag and WiredTiger eviction rate │ ├─ 5. dependency latency rising? ──▶ upstream; check timeouts │ ↳ and check whether YOUR retries are amplifying their load │ └─ 6. infrastructure CPU throttling, noisy node, network ↳ in Kubernetes, container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds is the tell

Signatures — cause by symptom

What each cause looks like on a dashboard
CauseSignatureConfirm withFix
Missing indexone route slow; DB duration high; docs-examined ≫ returnedexplain('executionStats') shows COLLSCAN or a SORT stagecompound index in ESR order
N+1 queriesroute latency scales with result count; many short identical DB spans in one tracetrace waterfall; command-monitor count per requestone $in query and a map, or an extended reference
Unbounded result setlarge response bytes; GC pressure; occasional OOMresponse size histogram; heap snapshotcap limit; project only needed fields
Pool exhaustionlatency high, DB op duration flat, checkout time highdriver pool metrics; waitQueueTimeoutMS errorsraise pool, reduce per-request queries, or add replicas
Event-loop blockingloop delay spikes; CPU may be flat; all routes affected togethermonitorEventLoopDelay; CPU profile shows one long frameisolate/worker the CPU work; remove sync I/O
GC pressuresawtooth RSS; periodic latency spikes at a fixed interval--trace-gc; heap snapshot diffsmaller payloads; fix the leak; raise heap deliberately
Noisy tenantglobal p99 up, p50 flat; one tenant dominates request or doc countsgroup metrics by tenantper-tenant rate limit and query cost budget
Retry stormrequest count amplified 3–10×; upstream error rate highcompare inbound vs outbound request countsretry budget, jitter, circuit breaker
Cold cachelatency spike immediately after every deploy, decaying over minutescache hit ratio drop correlated with rolloutwarm on start; stagger rollout; stale-while-revalidate
Slow dependency, no timeoutp99 pinned at a suspiciously round number, or unboundedoutbound duration histogramtimeout + circuit breaker + fallback
CPU throttlinglatency correlates with CPU limit, not usagecontainer_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_totalraise the limit or lower concurrency
Replication lagreads stale or slow only on secondary-preferred routesrs.printSecondaryReplicationInfo()route reads to primary; fix the write load
The one that fools everyone

Pool exhaustion looks exactly like a slow database. Request latency is high, so people start tuning queries — but the database's own operation duration is unchanged, because the time is spent waiting for a connection before any query runs. The discriminating metric is checkout time. If it is high while operation duration is flat, the database is fine and you are either running too many queries per request or your pool is too small for your concurrency.

The commands, in order

javascript · instrument first, guess never
// 1. Is the loop healthy?
const h = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 10 }); h.enable();
setInterval(() => logger().info({ p99: h.percentile(99) / 1e6 }, 'loop'), 5_000).unref();

// 2. How many DB round trips is this request making?
client.on('commandStarted',   (e) => { ctx.getStore()?.queries.push(e.commandName); });
client.on('commandSucceeded', (e) => {
  const s = ctx.getStore(); if (!s) return;
  s.dbMs = (s.dbMs ?? 0) + e.duration;
  if (e.duration > 100) logger().warn({ cmd: e.commandName, ms: e.duration }, 'slow op');
});
// Then log queries.length per request. Anything above ~10 on a read endpoint
// is an N+1 until proven otherwise.

// 3. Is the pool the bottleneck?
client.on('connectionCheckOutStarted', () => poolPending.inc());
client.on('connectionCheckedOut',      () => poolPending.dec());
// Export pool size, available, pending. Pending > 0 sustained = too small.

// 4. What is the database actually doing right now?
// db.currentOp({ secs_running: { $gte: 3 }, ns: /^acme\./ })
// db.setProfilingLevel(1, { slowms: 100 })  → db.system.profile.find().sort({ ts: -1 })

The first five minutes of an incident

  1. Mitigate before you diagnose. Roll back the last deploy or disable the last feature flag. Understanding can wait; the users cannot.
  2. Check the change log. Deploys, config, flags, migrations, dependency releases — in the window when it started.
  3. Read the shape. One route or all; p50 or p99; one tenant or everyone. This single observation eliminates most of the tree.
  4. Rule out the process. Loop delay, GC, restarts, CPU throttling. Thirty seconds of looking, and it eliminates a whole branch.
  5. Rule out the pool. Checkout time versus operation duration. Another thirty seconds, and it prevents the most common wrong turn.
  6. Open one trace. A single slow request's waterfall usually names the culprit outright.
  7. Change one thing. Re-measure. Two simultaneous changes mean you learn nothing from the result.
The full-marks answer

“First I'd establish whether it is one endpoint or all of them, and whether p50 moved or only p99 — that alone halves the search space. Then I'd rule the process in or out with event-loop delay and GC, because those affect everything at once and are cheap to check. Then the connection pool, because exhaustion masquerades as a slow database and sends people down the wrong path. Then the database itself: operation duration, the slow-query log, and docs-examined against returned. Then dependencies and my own retry amplification. Finally I'd open a trace on a slow request and read the waterfall. Throughout, I'd change one thing at a time and re-measure — and if this is production, I'd have rolled back first and diagnosed second.”

46

Resilience patterns

Every dependency you call will one day be slow rather than down, and slow is the harder failure. These are the six patterns that turn a degraded dependency into a degraded feature instead of a total outage.

Timeouts — the one that is never optional

javascript · a budget that decreases inwards
// The client gives us 10s. Every hop must fit inside what remains, or the
// caller times out first and all our work is wasted.
export function deadline(req) {
  const total = Number(req.get('x-request-timeout-ms')) || 10_000;
  const startedAt = req.startedAt ?? performance.now();
  return {
    remaining: () => Math.max(0, total - (performance.now() - startedAt)),
    // Reserve headroom so we can still render an error inside the client's budget
    signalFor(ms) {
      const budget = Math.min(ms, this.remaining() - 200);
      if (budget <= 0) throw new AppError('deadline exceeded', { status: 504 });
      return AbortSignal.timeout(budget);
    },
  };
}

const d = deadline(req);
const [profile, entitlements] = await Promise.all([
  fetch(profileUrl,      { signal: d.signalFor(2_000) }),
  fetch(entitlementsUrl, { signal: d.signalFor(1_500) }),
]);
A missing timeout is how one slow service takes down five

Without a timeout, a request waits as long as the dependency takes. Your connection pool fills with waiting requests, new requests queue behind them, your own callers time out and retry, and the retries fill the queue faster. The dependency was merely slow; you converted that into your own total outage. Set a timeout on every outbound call — HTTP, database, cache, queue — and make it shorter than your caller's.

Retries — and the amplification arithmetic

A three-hop chain, each hop retrying 3 times on failure: client ──▶ A ──▶ B ──▶ C C degrades. B retries 3x. A retries 3x per B attempt. 1 client request → 3 × 3 = 9 calls to C. With one more hop, 27. C is already struggling. You have just multiplied its load by an order of magnitude at exactly the moment it could least afford it. Defences: · retry ONLY at the edge closest to the user, or once per chain · a retry BUDGET (e.g. retries ≤ 10% of requests in the window) · full jitter so retries do not arrive in lockstep · a circuit breaker so a failing dependency is not retried at all
javascript · retry with full jitter and a budget
const RETRYABLE = new Set(['ECONNRESET', 'ECONNREFUSED', 'ETIMEDOUT', 'EAI_AGAIN']);
const isRetryable = (e) => RETRYABLE.has(e.code) || [502, 503, 504].includes(e.status);

// A token bucket shared across the process caps total retry traffic.
const retryBudget = new TokenBucket({ capacity: 100, refillPerSec: 10 });

export async function withRetry(fn, { attempts = 3, base = 200, cap = 5_000, idempotent } = {}) {
  if (!idempotent) return fn();            // never retry a non-idempotent call implicitly
  for (let i = 0; ; i++) {
    try {
      return await fn();
    } catch (err) {
      if (i >= attempts - 1 || !isRetryable(err)) throw err;
      if (!retryBudget.tryTake()) { retryBudgetExhausted.inc(); throw err; }
      // FULL jitter: random over the whole window, not base ± a bit.
      // Equal jitter still leaves clients clustered; full jitter spreads them.
      const delay = Math.random() * Math.min(cap, base * 2 ** i);
      await sleep(delay);
    }
  }
}

Circuit breaker

javascript · a breaker you can actually reason about
export class CircuitBreaker {
  #state = 'closed';           // closed → open → half-open → closed
  #failures = 0;
  #successes = 0;
  #openedAt = 0;

  constructor({ name, threshold = 5, resetMs = 30_000, halfOpenSuccesses = 3, onFallback }) {
    Object.assign(this, { name, threshold, resetMs, halfOpenSuccesses, onFallback });
  }

  get state() { return this.#state; }

  async exec(fn) {
    if (this.#state === 'open') {
      if (Date.now() - this.#openedAt < this.resetMs) {
        breakerRejections.inc({ name: this.name });
        return this.#fallback();          // fail FAST — do not queue behind a dead dependency
      }
      this.#state = 'half-open';          // let a trickle through to probe
      this.#successes = 0;
    }
    try {
      const result = await fn();
      this.#onSuccess();
      return result;
    } catch (err) {
      this.#onFailure(err);
      if (this.onFallback) return this.#fallback();
      throw err;
    }
  }

  #onSuccess() {
    if (this.#state === 'half-open' && ++this.#successes >= this.halfOpenSuccesses) {
      this.#state = 'closed'; this.#failures = 0;
      logger().info({ breaker: this.name }, 'circuit closed');
    } else if (this.#state === 'closed') { this.#failures = 0; }
  }

  #onFailure(err) {
    // Half-open failure re-opens immediately — do not wait for the threshold again.
    if (this.#state === 'half-open' || ++this.#failures >= this.threshold) {
      this.#state = 'open'; this.#openedAt = Date.now();
      logger().error({ breaker: this.name, err }, 'circuit opened');
      breakerState.set({ name: this.name }, 1);
    }
  }

  #fallback() {
    if (!this.onFallback) throw new Unavailable(this.name);
    return this.onFallback();
  }
}

const recommendations = new CircuitBreaker({
  name: 'recs', threshold: 5, resetMs: 30_000,
  onFallback: () => popularItemsCache.get(),   // degraded, not broken
});
One breaker per dependency, not per process

A shared breaker means a failing recommendations service opens the circuit for payments too. Scope the breaker to the dependency — and for multi-tenant systems, consider scoping it per upstream host so one bad shard does not trip the whole integration.

Bulkheads

javascript · isolate capacity so one slow dependency cannot consume all of it
// Without bulkheads, 200 concurrent requests to a slow analytics service occupy
// every event-loop slot, socket and pool connection — and checkout stops working.
class Bulkhead {
  #active = 0; #queue = [];
  constructor({ name, concurrency, queueLimit }) { Object.assign(this, { name, concurrency, queueLimit }); }
  async run(fn) {
    if (this.#active >= this.concurrency) {
      if (this.#queue.length >= this.queueLimit) {
        bulkheadRejected.inc({ name: this.name });
        throw new Unavailable(`${this.name} at capacity`);   // shed, do not queue forever
      }
      await new Promise((r) => this.#queue.push(r));
    }
    this.#active++;
    try { return await fn(); }
    finally { this.#active--; this.#queue.shift()?.(); }
  }
}

export const pools = {
  payments:  new Bulkhead({ name: 'payments',  concurrency: 40, queueLimit: 100 }),
  analytics: new Bulkhead({ name: 'analytics', concurrency: 5,  queueLimit: 0 }),
  search:    new Bulkhead({ name: 'search',    concurrency: 20, queueLimit: 50 }),
};
// Analytics gets 5 slots and no queue: if it is slow, analytics degrades and
// nothing else notices. That is the entire point.

Classify every dependency, in writing

DependencyClassTimeoutOn failure
Primary databasecritical2 sfail the request with 503
Auth / token verificationcritical1 sfail with 401; cache JWKS so a brief outage is invisible
Payment providercritical10 sfail, but never retry blindly — poll status instead
Cacheoptional50 msgo to origin; a cache outage must not be an app outage
Searchoptional500 msfall back to a database query with a narrower feature set
Recommendationsoptional300 msserve a static popular list
Analytics / telemetryoptional100 msdrop it; never block a user request on telemetry
Degradation is a designed state

“Optional” only helps if the fallback exists, is tested, and is visible in the UI. Write the table above for your own service, then write one test per optional dependency that makes it time out and asserts the endpoint still returns a useful response. Untested degradation is just a different way to fail.

Worked example: the payment provider slows to five seconds

WITHOUT the patterns checkout waits 5 s → pool fills → all routes slow → LB health checks time out → instances pulled → remaining instances get more load → total outage, caused by a dependency that was still working WITH the patterns timeout 10 s (a payment legitimately takes seconds) bulkhead: 40 slots → the other 160 concurrent requests are unaffected breaker: after 5 failures, checkout fails fast with a clear message fallback: "payments are temporarily unavailable, your basket is saved" result: checkout degraded, browsing and everything else fully working
Interview follow-up

“Which of these would you implement first?” Timeouts, without hesitation — they are one line per call and they prevent the specific failure that turns a slow dependency into an outage. Second, classify dependencies as critical or optional and add fallbacks for the optional ones. Circuit breakers and bulkheads come third; they are valuable but they mitigate a problem that timeouts have already bounded. A candidate who reaches for a circuit-breaker library before setting timeouts has the order backwards.

47

SLOs, alerting & on-call

An SLO turns “is it working?” into a number you can argue about, budget against and alert on. Without one, reliability is a matter of opinion and every alert threshold is somebody's guess.

The three terms, precisely

SLI
A measurement of one aspect of service quality, expressed as a ratio of good events to valid events. “The proportion of requests that returned a non-5xx status.”
SLO
A target for an SLI over a window. “99.9% of requests succeed, measured over 28 rolling days.” Internal, and chosen deliberately below 100%.
SLA
A contract with a customer, with financial consequences. Always looser than your SLO, so that breaching the SLO is an internal warning rather than a refund.

Choosing SLIs that mean something

yaml · SLI definitions as ratios
# Availability — note what is EXCLUDED from "valid"
availability:
  good:  sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status!~"5.."}[5m]))
  valid: sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status!~"4.."}[5m]))
  # 4xx excluded: a client sending malformed requests is not us being unavailable.
  # But 429 SHOULD count against you — that is your capacity, not their bug.

# Latency — a ratio, never an average
latency:
  good:  sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.5",job="api"}[5m]))
  valid: sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{job="api"}[5m]))
  # "99% of requests complete within 500ms", not "average latency is 200ms"

# Freshness — for anything asynchronous
freshness:
  good:  sum(rate(events_processed_total{lag_seconds<="60"}[5m]))
  valid: sum(rate(events_processed_total[5m]))
Why an average latency SLO is useless

Take 100 requests: 99 at 50 ms and one at 30 seconds. The average is 350 ms — comfortably under a 500 ms target — while one user waited half a minute and almost certainly gave up. Averages are dominated by the majority and structurally blind to the users having a bad time. Always express latency as “the fraction of requests under a threshold”.

Where you measure changes the number

Measured atIncludesMisses
Inside the serviceyour own handler timequeueing, LB time, network, and every request that never arrived
Load balancerqueueing and connection setupDNS, client network, total outages of the LB itself
Client / RUMeverything the user experiencesnothing — but it is noisy and includes their bad wi-fi
Synthetic probeavailability from outside, continuouslyreal traffic mix; it is a canary, not an SLI

Measure at the load balancer for the SLO you commit to, and keep client-side RUM alongside it. Service-internal metrics are for diagnosis, not for the SLO — a service that is down measures zero failures, because it measures nothing at all.

Error budgets

99%
7h 18mper 30 days — fine for an internal tool
99.9%
43m 12sthe usual target for a business API
99.95%
21m 36sneeds real redundancy and automated failover
99.99%
4m 19smulti-region, and no manual step in any recovery path

The budget is what you are permitted to spend. Spending none of it means your target is too strict and you are over-investing in reliability at the cost of shipping. The policy that makes the budget real is agreed in advance:

  • Budget healthy — ship freely; take reasonable risks.
  • Budget below 25% — reliability work is prioritised over features until it recovers.
  • Budget exhausted — feature freeze; only fixes and reliability work ship.
  • A single incident consumed more than half the budget — a postmortem with action items is mandatory, not optional.

Burn-rate alerting

A static threshold ("error rate > 1%") pages at 3am for a blip that consumed 0.2% of the month's budget, and stays silent through a slow leak that will exhaust it by Thursday. Burn rate = how fast you are consuming the budget, relative to even spend. burn 1 = you will exactly exhaust the budget at the end of the window burn 14 = you will exhaust a 30-day budget in ~2 days Two windows, so you catch both shapes and avoid false pages: PAGE burn ≥ 14.4 over 1h AND burn ≥ 14.4 over 5m ↳ 2% of the monthly budget in an hour. Something is broken now. ↳ the short window makes it recover quickly when you fix it TICKET burn ≥ 6 over 6h AND burn ≥ 6 over 30m ↳ a real degradation, but nobody needs to wake up
yaml · the alert rules
groups:
  - name: api-slo
    rules:
      # Recording rules keep the alert expressions readable and cheap
      - record: job:slo_error_ratio:rate5m
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          / sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api"}[5m]))
      - record: job:slo_error_ratio:rate1h
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api",status=~"5.."}[1h]))
          / sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="api"}[1h]))

      - alert: ApiErrorBudgetFastBurn
        expr: |
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate1h  > (14.4 * 0.001)
          and
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate5m  > (14.4 * 0.001)
        for: 2m
        labels: { severity: page, slo: api-availability }
        annotations:
          summary: "API burning error budget 14x — 2% of the month in the last hour"
          runbook: "https://runbooks.acme.com/api-availability"
          dashboard: "https://grafana.acme.com/d/api-overview"

      - alert: ApiErrorBudgetSlowBurn
        expr: |
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate6h > (6 * 0.001)
          and
          job:slo_error_ratio:rate30m > (6 * 0.001)
        for: 15m
        labels: { severity: ticket, slo: api-availability }

What deserves to wake someone

SignalActionWhy
SLO fast burnpageusers are affected right now
All instances failing readinesspagetotal outage imminent
Queue oldest-job age > 15 minpagework has silently stopped
Disk > 85% and risingpagepredictable, imminent, and catastrophic when it lands
SLO slow burnticketreal, but daylight is soon enough
Certificate expiring in 14 daysticketplenty of time; paging is cruelty
One pod restarteddashboardKubernetes did its job
CPU > 80%dashboarda cause, not a symptom — alert on the symptom instead
The three tests every alert must pass

Actionable — there is something a human can do right now. Urgent — it cannot wait until morning. Documented — it links to a runbook naming the first three commands. An alert that fails any of the three should be deleted or downgraded. A rotation that fires twenty times a night trains people to ignore the one that matters, which is worse than having no alerting at all.

Postmortems

  1. Timeline first. Timestamped, from the scribe's notes: when it started, when it was detected, when it was mitigated, when it was resolved.
  2. Two gaps are the real findings. Time-to-detect and time-to-mitigate. If detection took forty minutes, the alerting is the problem regardless of what broke.
  3. Contributing factors, not a root cause. Real incidents have several. “The deploy” is never the whole answer; the missing canary gate is closer to it.
  4. Blameless. The question is what made the mistake possible and easy, not who made it. Anything else guarantees the next person hides the next incident.
  5. Action items with owners and dates, tracked in the same backlog as features. Untracked action items are a fiction everyone participates in.
Interview follow-up

“What would you instrument on day one of a new service?” Request rate, error rate and duration per route pattern; event-loop delay; database operation duration and pool checkout time; queue depth and oldest-job age; and a correlation id on every log line. That is roughly an hour of work and it covers most of what you will ever need at 3am. Everything else can be added when a specific question demands it — but you cannot retrofit observability during an incident.

48

Containers, orchestration & autoscaling

Node has three specific pathologies inside a container — PID 1 signal handling, a heap limit that ignores the cgroup, and a CPU count that reports the host's. Each one is a production incident that looks like something else.

A Dockerfile worth copying

dockerfile · multi-stage, non-root, correct signals
# Pin to a digest, not just a tag — tags move and your build stops being reproducible.
FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim AS deps
WORKDIR /app
# Copy ONLY the manifests first. This layer is cached until dependencies change,
# so a source-only edit skips the install entirely.
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev --ignore-scripts && npm cache clean --force

FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --ignore-scripts
COPY . .
RUN npm run build && npm run test:unit

FROM node:22.11-bookworm-slim AS runtime
ENV NODE_ENV=production
# --init gives us a real PID 1 that forwards signals and reaps zombies.
# Without it, SIGTERM never reaches your handler and graceful shutdown silently
# does not happen — you just get SIGKILL after the grace period.
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=deps  --chown=node:node /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build --chown=node:node /app/dist ./dist
COPY --chown=node:node package.json ./

USER node
EXPOSE 3000
# NOT the shell form. Shell form makes /bin/sh PID 1, which does not forward signals.
ENTRYPOINT ["node", "--enable-source-maps", "dist/server.js"]
bash · .dockerignore matters more than people think
node_modules
.git
.env*
coverage
dist
*.log
.github
# Without this, the build context includes .git and node_modules — often hundreds
# of megabytes uploaded to the daemon on every build, and a real risk of baking
# .env into an image layer where it is extractable forever.
Why alpine is not automatically the right choice

Alpine uses musl rather than glibc. Native modules must be rebuilt, some prebuilt binaries do not exist, and there are long-standing reports of noticeably slower DNS resolution and worse performance in some allocation-heavy workloads. -slim images are only tens of megabytes larger and behave like the platform you tested on. Choose alpine deliberately after measuring, not by default because it is smaller.

The three Node-in-Kubernetes traps

TrapSymptomFix
PID 1 signalsrolling deploys drop requests; shutdown logs never appear--init, or tini, or handle signals explicitly and never use the shell form of ENTRYPOINT
Heap vs memory limitpod OOMKilled with no Node heap error; exit code 137--max-old-space-size set to roughly 75% of the memory limit
CPU countcluster forks 16 workers on a 1-core limit; constant throttlingos.availableParallelism(), or read the cgroup quota explicitly
CPU limitsp99 latency spikes with CPU usage well below the limitthrottling — check container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total; consider requests without limits
CPU limits throttle, memory limits kill

They are not symmetrical. Exceeding a CPU limit means the kernel stops scheduling you for the rest of the 100 ms period — your process is not slow, it is paused, and the latency histogram develops a distinctive spiky tail. Exceeding a memory limit means the OOM killer terminates the container immediately, with no chance to flush or log. Many teams set generous CPU requests and no CPU limit at all for latency-sensitive services, while always setting a memory limit.

The Deployment, annotated

yaml · the parts that actually matter
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  replicas: 6
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate: { maxSurge: 2, maxUnavailable: 0 }   # never lose capacity mid-deploy
  template:
    spec:
      # Must exceed drain + in-flight completion, or you get SIGKILL mid-request
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 45
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: ghcr.io/acme/api@sha256:9f3c...        # digest, not a tag
          env:
            - name: NODE_OPTIONS
              value: "--max-old-space-size=768"          # ~75% of the 1Gi limit
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }   # what the scheduler reserves
            limits:   { memory: "1Gi" }                  # no CPU limit: avoid throttling
          lifecycle:
            preStop:
              exec:
                # Endpoint removal is asynchronous. This sleep lets the LB stop
                # sending traffic BEFORE the process starts refusing it — this
                # single line eliminates most rolling-deploy 502s.
                command: ["sh", "-c", "sleep 10"]
          startupProbe:                                  # slow starts are not failures
            httpGet: { path: /livez, port: 3000 }
            failureThreshold: 30
            periodSeconds: 2                             # up to 60s to become live
          livenessProbe:                                 # ONLY "is the process wedged"
            httpGet: { path: /livez, port: 3000 }
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3
          readinessProbe:                                # "can I serve right now"
            httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 3000 }
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 2
javascript · what the two probes must actually return
// LIVENESS: is this process fundamentally broken? Nothing else.
// It must NOT check dependencies — if Mongo blips, Kubernetes would restart
// every pod simultaneously, which turns a database wobble into a total outage.
app.get('/livez', (_req, res) => res.status(200).send('ok'));

// READINESS: can I serve a request right now? Dependencies belong here,
// but only the CRITICAL ones, and with a short timeout and a cached result.
let readyCache = { at: 0, ok: false };
app.get('/readyz', async (_req, res) => {
  if (shuttingDown) return res.status(503).send('draining');   // the key line
  if (Date.now() - readyCache.at < 2_000) {
    return res.status(readyCache.ok ? 200 : 503).send(readyCache.ok ? 'ok' : 'not ready');
  }
  try {
    await db.admin().command({ ping: 1 }, { maxTimeMS: 800 });
    readyCache = { at: Date.now(), ok: true };
    res.status(200).send('ok');
  } catch {
    readyCache = { at: Date.now(), ok: false };
    res.status(503).send('not ready');
  }
});
Never put a dependency check in the liveness probe

Liveness failure means restart this container. If the probe checks the database, a thirty-second database failover restarts your entire fleet — losing all in-flight requests, dropping every connection pool, and hitting the database with a stampede of reconnections at the exact moment it is recovering. Liveness answers “is the process wedged”; readiness answers “should I receive traffic”. Conflating them is one of the most damaging Kubernetes misconfigurations there is.

Autoscaling a Node service

yaml · scale on something that reflects the work
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment, name: api }
  minReplicas: 4          # never below your failure-domain requirement
  maxReplicas: 40
  metrics:
    - type: Pods
      pods:
        metric: { name: http_requests_per_second }
        target: { type: AverageValue, averageValue: "150" }
    - type: Pods
      pods:
        metric: { name: nodejs_eventloop_lag_p99_seconds }
        target: { type: AverageValue, averageValue: "0.02" }
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 0        # react to load immediately
      policies: [{ type: Percent, value: 100, periodSeconds: 30 }]
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300      # be slow and cautious coming down
      policies: [{ type: Percent, value: 25, periodSeconds: 60 }]
Why CPU is a poor autoscaling signal for Node

An I/O-bound Node service can be completely saturated — every event-loop tick queued, p99 in seconds — while CPU sits at 30%, because it is waiting on sockets rather than computing. Scaling on CPU therefore does nothing until the service is already failing. Requests per pod and event-loop delay both track the thing that actually degrades, and event-loop delay in particular is the single best saturation signal Node exposes.

Connection-pool arithmetic when you autoscale

Pods × pool
= DB connections40 pods × 50 = 2 000 — over most managed tier limits
Rule
pool ≈ 10–20size from per-pod concurrency, not optimism
Alert on
connection countbefore the cap, not when writes start failing
Serverless
use a proxyone pool per invocation exhausts the database instantly
Interview follow-up

“Your pods keep getting OOMKilled but Node never reports a heap error — why?” Because V8's default heap limit is derived from the host's memory, not the cgroup limit. Node happily grows past the container's limit and the kernel kills it before V8 ever decides to run a full collection. The fix is --max-old-space-size below the limit so V8 collects first and, if it genuinely cannot free memory, fails with a heap error you can actually diagnose.

49

Zero-downtime deploys

Two versions of your code run simultaneously during every rolling deploy, and they share one database. Almost every deploy-related outage is a failure to design for that overlap.

The shutdown sequence, and the timing that makes it work

t+0.0s SIGTERM arrives ↳ set shuttingDown = true → /readyz starts returning 503 ↳ do NOT stop accepting connections yet t+0.0s (concurrently) Kubernetes begins removing the endpoint — but propagation to kube-proxy on every node is asynchronous t+10s preStop sleep ends. The LB has now noticed. New traffic has stopped. ↳ server.close() — stop accepting, keep existing connections alive t+10-40s in-flight requests complete naturally ↳ close the queue consumer (finish the active job, take no more) t+40s close DB pool, Redis, flush logs and metrics t+41s process.exit(0) terminationGracePeriodSeconds must exceed all of this, or SIGKILL lands mid-request. The single most common bug: calling server.close() at t+0. The endpoint is still in the LB's table for another few seconds, so it sends requests to a socket that is no longer accepting → connection refused → 502.
javascript · the shutdown handler
let shuttingDown = false;

async function shutdown(signal) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;
  logger().info({ signal }, 'shutdown started');

  // 1. Fail readiness FIRST. This is what removes us from rotation.
  //    The preStop sleep in the manifest gives this time to propagate.
  await sleep(cfg.drainMs);                       // belt and braces if no preStop hook

  // 2. Stop accepting new connections; existing ones continue.
  await new Promise((resolve) => server.close(resolve));

  // 3. Finish the active job, refuse new ones.
  await worker.close();

  // 4. Now that nothing needs them, release resources.
  await Promise.allSettled([
    mongoClient.close(false),                     // false: do not force-close in-flight ops
    redis.quit(),
    tracer.shutdown(),
  ]);

  logger().info('shutdown complete');
  logger().flush?.();
  process.exit(0);
}

for (const sig of ['SIGTERM', 'SIGINT']) process.on(sig, () => shutdown(sig));

// A hard deadline, so a hung dependency cannot hold the pod open past the grace period
process.on('SIGTERM', () => {
  setTimeout(() => { logger().error('shutdown timed out, forcing exit'); process.exit(1); },
             cfg.shutdownTimeoutMs).unref();
});

Strategies compared

StrategyExtra capacityRollbackTwo versions live?Use when
Recreatenoneredeploynonever for a user-facing API
RollingmaxSurge onlyminutesyesthe sensible default
Blue/green100%secondsbrieflywhen instant rollback is worth double the infrastructure
Canarysmallsecondsyes, for longerhigh-risk changes; needs automated analysis to be worth it
Rollback speed beats failure avoidance

You cannot test your way to zero bad deploys. What you can do is make the blast radius small and the recovery fast. A team that ships twenty times a day with a sixty-second rollback has better availability than a team that ships monthly after a two-week freeze — because the second team's failures are larger, rarer, and much harder to attribute.

The database rule that makes rollback possible

BREAKING rename `total` → `total_cents` in one deploy v1 pods (still running) read `total` → undefined → NaN → 500s rollback? the column is gone. You cannot go back. EXPAND / CONTRACT three deploys, each individually reversible 1 EXPAND add `total_cents`; write BOTH; read `total` ↳ old code unaffected. Rollback is free. 2 MIGRATE backfill `total_cents`; switch reads to it; still write both ↳ rollback re-reads `total`, which is still being maintained. 3 CONTRACT stop writing `total`; drop it — only once no running version reads it, which in practice means a release later ↳ this is the ONLY irreversible step, and it is isolated
javascript · the dual-write window
// Phase 1 + 2: both fields maintained, read controlled by a flag.
// The flag means the read switch is a config change, not a deploy — so
// reverting it takes seconds rather than a rollback.
function toDocument(order) {
  return {
    ...order,
    total: order.totalCents,        // legacy, still written
    totalCents: order.totalCents,   // new
  };
}

function fromDocument(doc) {
  return flags.readTotalCents
    ? { ...doc, totalCents: doc.totalCents ?? doc.total }   // tolerate both shapes
    : { ...doc, totalCents: doc.total };
}
// The ?? is what lets a rollback work against documents written by the new code.
// Every migration should leave the code able to read both shapes for one release.

The overlap problems nobody anticipates

Queue consumer skew

v2 enqueues a job with a new payload shape; a v1 worker picks it up and crashes or, worse, silently misreads a field. Deploy consumers before producers, and version the job payload so an old worker can reject cleanly rather than misinterpret.

Cache shape changes

v2 writes a new object shape to a key v1 also reads. Include a version in the key (user:v3:123) so the two versions simply miss each other's entries instead of deserialising garbage.

Session compatibility

A user's request hits v1, then v2 on the next click. Session contents must be readable by both, which means additive changes only for the duration of the rollout.

API response shape

A single-page app loaded from v1 keeps calling the API as it rolls to v2. Removing a field mid-rollout breaks the already-loaded client. Additive only, and remove fields a release later.

Migration ordering in the pipeline

  1. Run additive migrations before the deploy. New columns, new indexes, new collections — the old code ignores them.
  2. Deploy the code that writes both shapes and reads the old one.
  3. Backfill as a throttled, resumable job — never as part of the deploy, because a deploy that takes forty minutes is not a deploy.
  4. Flip the read via a feature flag, watch, and be ready to flip back.
  5. Contract in a later release, once no running version reads the old shape. This is the only step you cannot undo, so it gets its own change window.
Never build an index in the deploy path

An index build on a large collection can take minutes to hours and consumes IO the whole time. A deploy that waits for it either times out or blocks the rollout; one that does not wait races the code that assumes the index exists. Build indexes as a separate, monitored operation ahead of the deploy, and have the application tolerate their temporary absence.

Verifying a deploy

bash · what runs immediately after the rollout
kubectl -n prod rollout status deploy/api --timeout=300s

# Smoke: the money paths only, against the real environment
npm run smoke -- --base-url https://api.acme.com

# Compare the canary's error rate and latency against the stable version.
# Both must be evaluated on the SAME traffic window, or a quiet minute reads
# as a healthy canary.
./scripts/canary-verify.sh --baseline stable --candidate canary \
  --max-error-rate-delta 0.5 --max-p95-delta-ms 50 --window 10m

# Rollback is one command, and everyone on the team has run it in a drill.
kubectl -n prod rollout undo deploy/api
Interview follow-up

“How do you roll back a deploy that included a migration?” The honest answer is that you design so the question does not arise: expand/contract means the code is reversible at every step and only the final contraction is one-way, and it happens a release after the code that needed it. If you are already in the bad situation — a destructive migration that shipped with the code — rolling back the code does not restore the data, so the real options are to roll forward with a fix or restore from a backup and replay. That is precisely why the destructive step is separated in the first place.

50

Multi-region & data locality

Most teams adopt multi-region for disaster recovery and then discover they have signed up for the hardest problem in distributed systems. Know which of the three reasons applies to you, because each demands a different architecture.

The three reasons, and what each actually requires

ReasonMinimum architectureReal cost
Disaster recoveryactive-passive: replicas in region B, infrastructure defined as code, tested failovermodest — you are paying for standby capacity and drills, not for consistency
Latency for global usersread-local: CDN, edge caching, read replicas near users; writes still go homemoderate — mostly a caching and routing problem
Data residencytenant-pinned regions with routing and per-region storagehigh — it must be in the data model from day one
Active-active writesconflict resolution, per-region identity, and a consistency model you can explainvery high — do not do this unless the business genuinely requires it

The physics you cannot negotiate with

Same AZ
0.5 mseffectively free
Cross-AZ
1–2 msfree-ish in latency, not in transfer cost
London → Frankfurt
~15 mstolerable inside a request
London → Virginia
~75 msone round trip; a majority write costs this
London → Sydney
~250 msa single cross-region await ruins the request

A write with w: 'majority' is only acknowledged once a majority of voting members have it. If your majority requires a member in another continent, every write in your system now costs that round trip — not during failover, but permanently. This single fact eliminates most naive multi-region designs.

Topologies

ACTIVE-PASSIVE (start here) eu-west-1 us-east-1 ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ │ app ✔ live │ │ app scaled to 0 │ │ mongo PRIMARY│ ──── async ─────▶ │ mongo SECONDARY (P0) │ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ RPO: replication lag (seconds) RTO: minutes, DNS + promotion + simple, cheap, writes stay fast − failover is a real operation you must rehearse READ-LOCAL, WRITE-HOME (latency for a global audience) users ──▶ nearest region ──▶ local read replica for GETs └──▶ home region for all writes + reads are fast everywhere; writes stay consistent − read-your-writes breaks: a user writes to home, reads locally, sees stale ↳ fix: pin a user to their home region for N seconds after a write TENANT-PINNED (residency, and the only sane active-active) tenant A → eu-west-1 (EU data stays in the EU) tenant B → us-east-1 + no cross-region consistency problem AT ALL — each tenant is single-region + satisfies residency by construction − routing must be correct at the edge; a mis-routed tenant is a compliance incident
Tenant pinning is the pragmatic answer

It gives you regional latency, data residency and blast-radius isolation without ever needing multi-master conflict resolution, because each tenant's data is single-region and strongly consistent. The cost is a routing layer that must always send a tenant to their home region, and a migration procedure for moving one. For B2B products this is almost always the right architecture.

MongoDB across regions

javascript · a replica set that survives a region without taxing every write
// eu-west-1: 3 members (majority lives here → writes stay ~1ms)
// us-east-1: 2 members, priority 0 (cannot be elected automatically)
//
// Losing eu-west-1 means losing the majority: the set goes READ-ONLY until a
// human reconfigures it. That is a deliberate trade — automatic failover across
// a 75ms link would mean every majority write pays 75ms in normal operation.
//
// The alternative — 2 + 2 + 1 arbiter in a third region — gives automatic
// failover, but now the majority may include a remote member and writes slow down.
// Pick which property you want and say so out loud; you cannot have both.

const client = new MongoClient(uri, {
  readPreference: 'nearest',
  readPreferenceTags: [{ region: 'eu-west-1' }, {}],   // prefer local, fall back to any
  maxStalenessSeconds: 90,                             // refuse badly lagged secondaries
  writeConcern: { w: 'majority', wtimeoutMS: 5_000 },
});

// Read-your-writes across a region boundary needs a causally consistent session,
// or the user writes in the home region and reads a stale local secondary.
const session = client.startSession({ causalConsistency: true });
await orders.insertOne(doc, { session });
const fresh = await orders.findOne({ _id: doc._id }, { session });   // guaranteed to see it

Routing and failover

javascript · resolve the tenant's home region at the edge
// The routing decision must not require a database read in the region the
// request landed in — that would be a cross-region call to decide where to go.
// Encode the region in the token, the subdomain, or an edge KV lookup.
export function regionRouter() {
  return (req, res, next) => {
    const home = req.principal?.homeRegion ?? tenantRegionFromHost(req.hostname);
    if (home && home !== cfg.region) {
      // 307 preserves the method and body, unlike 302
      return res.redirect(307, `https://${home}.api.acme.com${req.originalUrl}`);
    }
    next();
  };
}
// Better still, do this at the CDN or global load balancer so the request never
// reaches the wrong region at all.
DNS failover is slower than the TTL suggests

A 60-second TTL does not mean 60-second failover. Resolvers ignore short TTLs, corporate networks cache aggressively, browsers pin connections, and some clients cache DNS for the process lifetime. Real observed failover on DNS alone is frequently 5–15 minutes. If your RTO is tighter than that, you need anycast or a global load balancer that shifts traffic at the network layer, not a DNS change.

What multi-region actually costs you

  • Inter-region transfer is charged per gigabyte and is frequently the largest surprise on the bill — replication traffic runs continuously, whether or not you ever fail over.
  • Double infrastructure for the standby, unless you accept a cold start and a longer RTO.
  • Every deploy is now two deploys, with a version-skew window across regions.
  • Every incident is harder — “is it slow everywhere or just in one region” becomes a question you must answer before anything else.
  • Schema migrations must be safe across a replication lag, not just across two code versions.
The senior answer

“I'd start single-region with tested backups and a documented restore, because that covers the failure most teams actually experience — data loss or corruption, not a region disappearing. If the driver is latency, I'd add read replicas and a CDN before I'd add a second write region, since that is where the wins are cheapest. If it is residency, I'd pin tenants to regions from day one, because retrofitting that into an existing schema is a rewrite. I'd only go active-active for writes if the business requires it, and I'd expect to spend most of that project on conflict resolution and on being able to explain the consistency model to the rest of the team.”

51

Cost engineering

Cost is a non-functional requirement like latency, and it degrades the same way — slowly, invisibly, until someone notices the number. The difference is that nobody gets paged for it.

Where the money goes in a Node + MongoDB estate

LineTypical shareThe usual cause of overspend
Database30–50%oversized for a working set nobody measured; indexes and data nobody reads
Observability10–30%every request body logged; high-cardinality metric labels; 90-day retention on debug logs
Compute15–30%provisioned for peak, never scaling in; non-production running 24/7
Data transfer5–20%cross-AZ chatter, NAT gateway charges, uncompressed responses, no CDN
Object storage5–15%no lifecycle policy; original-resolution images kept forever
Observability is the one that surprises people

Logging every request and response body at a few thousand requests per second generates terabytes a month, and ingestion is charged per gigabyte. It is entirely normal for a team to discover their logging bill exceeds their compute bill. It is also the easiest to fix: log identifiers and sizes rather than bodies, sample debug-level output, tier retention (7 days hot, 30 days cold, then delete), and put body logging behind a per-request debug flag.

Unit economics change behaviour

javascript · attribute cost to the things the business understands
// A total spend figure produces a meeting. A per-tenant figure produces a decision.
// Emit the drivers; compute cost from them in your BI layer with current unit prices.
res.on('finish', () => {
  costDrivers.observe({
    tenantId: req.tenant?.id,
    route: req.route?.path ?? 'unmatched',
  }, {
    dbReads: req.timings?.dbOps ?? 0,
    dbDocsExamined: req.timings?.docsExamined ?? 0,
    egressBytes: Number(res.getHeader('content-length') ?? 0),
    computeMs: req.timings?.cpuMs ?? 0,
    logBytes: req.timings?.logBytes ?? 0,
  });
});

// The questions this lets you answer, none of which a total can:
//   · which tenant costs 40x the median, and are they on the right plan?
//   · which endpoint costs more to serve than the feature is worth?
//   · did last week's release change cost per request?
//   · what is the marginal cost of the next 10,000 users?
Per-tenant cost is a product input

Once you can see that 3% of tenants consume 60% of database IO, pricing, rate limits and plan design stop being guesses. It is also the fastest way to find a bug: a tenant costing a hundred times the median is usually not a heavy user but a polling loop or a missing index on their data volume.

The optimisation ladder, in order of return

  1. Delete waste. Orphaned volumes and snapshots, unattached IPs, dead load balancers, forgotten environments, duplicate log pipelines. Costs nothing, breaks nothing, and is usually a double-digit percentage.
  2. Turn off non-production out of hours. Development and staging running nights and weekends is roughly 70% waste on those environments. A scheduled scale-to-zero is an afternoon's work.
  3. Fix the query before resizing the instance. One missing index that causes a collection scan can be the entire reason a database tier was doubled. Index it and the tier goes back down — a permanent saving from a one-line change.
  4. Cut observability volume. Sample, redact, tier retention, drop high-cardinality labels. Large, immediate, and reversible.
  5. Right-size from measurement. Compare requested to actual utilisation. In Kubernetes the requests-versus-usage gap is usually the single largest line of waste in the cluster.
  6. Lifecycle the data. TTL on ephemeral collections, archive cold data to cheap storage, downsample old metrics, stop keeping full-resolution originals of images nobody re-crops.
  7. Commit to a baseline. Reserved instances or savings plans for the capacity you will certainly use. Only after the steps above, or you commit to your own waste for three years.
  8. Spot for stateless workers. Meaningful savings for queue consumers that tolerate interruption. Requires graceful shutdown to already work — which, by this chapter, it does.

Database cost specifically

javascript · find what you are paying to store and never read
// 1. Indexes nobody uses — each one costs storage AND a write on every insert
const stats = await db.collection('orders').aggregate([{ $indexStats: {} }]).toArray();
stats.filter(s => s.accesses.ops === 0)
     .forEach(s => console.log('unused since', s.accesses.since, s.name));
// Check EVERY replica-set member: stats are per-node and reset on restart,
// and a nightly report may be the only consumer of an index that looks dead.

// 2. Collections growing without a retention policy
for (const c of await db.collections()) {
  const s = await db.command({ collStats: c.collectionName });
  console.log(c.collectionName, {
    dataMB: Math.round(s.size / 1e6),
    indexMB: Math.round(s.totalIndexSize / 1e6),
    // Index size approaching or exceeding data size usually means over-indexing
    ratio: (s.totalIndexSize / Math.max(s.size, 1)).toFixed(2),
  });
}

// 3. TTL on anything ephemeral — sessions, idempotency keys, outbox rows,
//    processed-event markers, verification tokens, soft-deleted documents.
await db.collection('idempotency').createIndex({ createdAt: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 86_400 });
await db.collection('processed_events').createIndex({ at: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 7 * 86_400 });

Making cost visible

Tag everything

Service, environment, team, tenant tier. An untagged resource is one nobody will ever claim, and it will still be running in two years. Enforce tags with a policy at creation time, not with a spreadsheet.

Budget alerts to engineers

Not to finance. The person who can fix a runaway job is the one who deployed it. Alert on rate of change, not just absolute spend — a 40% week-on-week rise is a signal long before the monthly total is.

Review it with latency

Cost per request belongs on the same dashboard as p99 and error rate. Reviewed together, it becomes a normal engineering trade-off rather than an annual panic.

The cost failures that arrive as a bill, not an alert
  • A retry loop against a paid API with no budget, running for a weekend.
  • A log line inside a hot loop shipped on Friday; terabytes ingested by Monday.
  • An autoscaler that scales up and never down, because the scale-in policy was never tested.
  • A cross-AZ chatty service — traffic that is free within a zone and charged between them.
  • A forgotten load test left running against production infrastructure.

All five are invisible until the invoice. A daily spend-anomaly alert catches every one of them within hours instead of weeks.

Interview follow-up

“Your cloud bill doubled — how do you find out why?” Start with the cost-explorer breakdown by service and by tag over the period, and diff it against the previous month rather than reading absolute numbers. Correlate the inflection point with the deploy and config timeline. Then check the four usual suspects in order: log and metric ingestion volume, data transfer, a database tier change, and instance count. Most doublings are one of those four, and most are traceable to a specific change on a specific day — which is why tagging and a change log are the actual prerequisites for answering this at all.

52

Blueprint: multi-tenant SaaS API

The shape most backend engineers actually build. The interesting decisions are all about isolation: how you guarantee tenant A never sees tenant B's data, and how you stop tenant A's traffic from degrading tenant B's experience.

Requirements, with numbers

Tenants
5 000largest is 100× the median — plan for the outlier
Peak
2 000 rpsbusiness hours across three time zones
Availability
99.9%43 min/month budget
Isolation
contractuala cross-tenant leak is an incident, not a bug

Decision 1 — the tenancy model

OptionIsolationCost at 5 000 tenantsVerdict
Shared collections, tenantId on every documentlogical — one missing filter leaksone cluster, one pool, one migrationchosen — with the discipline below
Database per tenantstrong5 000 databases; migrations × 5 000; connection pools do not scalerejected at this count
Cluster per tenantstrongestabsurd at this countreserved for a handful of enterprise contracts

Shared collections are chosen because the alternative's operational cost is unbounded in tenant count, while the shared model's single weakness — a forgotten filter — can be eliminated structurally rather than by discipline.

Decision 2 — making a cross-tenant query impossible to write

javascript · the scoped repository, and the lint that enforces it
// Handlers never see the raw collection. There is no unscoped path to reach for,
// so a cross-tenant query is not a mistake you can make by forgetting something —
// it requires deliberately importing the admin repository, which is audited.
export function buildRepos(db, tenantId) {
  const scope = (f = {}) => ({ ...f, tenantId });
  const wrap = (name) => {
    const col = db.collection(name);
    return {
      findOne:   (f, o) => col.findOne(scope(f), o),
      find:      (f, o) => col.find(scope(f), o),
      countDocuments: (f) => col.countDocuments(scope(f)),
      updateOne: (f, u, o) => col.updateOne(scope(f), u, o),
      updateMany:(f, u, o) => col.updateMany(scope(f), u, o),
      deleteOne: (f, o) => col.deleteOne(scope(f), o),
      insertOne: (doc, o) => col.insertOne({ ...doc, tenantId }, o),
      aggregate: (pipeline, o) => {
        // Force the tenant match to be the FIRST stage: it must be the index seek,
        // not a filter applied after a collection-wide $group.
