        return col.aggregate([{ $match: { tenantId } }, ...pipeline], o);
      },
    };
  };
  return { orders: wrap('orders'), customers: wrap('customers'), invoices: wrap('invoices') };
}

app.use(ah(async (req, _res, next) => {
  req.tenant = await resolveTenant(req);          // subdomain, header, or token claim
  if (!req.tenant || req.tenant.status === 'suspended') throw new Forbidden('tenant');
  req.repos = buildRepos(db, req.tenant.id);
  next();
}));
javascript · the test that is generated, not written
// Enumerate the router at test time so a NEW endpoint without isolation fails CI.
// This is the highest-value test suite in a multi-tenant product.
for (const { method, path } of enumerateRoutes(app)) {
  if (!path.includes(':id')) continue;
  it(`${method} ${path} does not leak across tenants`, async () => {
    const a = await seedTenant(db, 'a');
    const b = await seedTenant(db, 'b');
    const resource = await seedResourceFor(db, b, path);

    const res = await request(app)[method.toLowerCase()](path.replace(':id', resource._id))
      .set('authorization', `Bearer ${tokenFor(a.user)}`);

    // 404, never 403 — a 403 confirms the id exists, which is itself a leak
    assert.equal(res.status, 404);
  });
}
Why 404 and not 403

Returning 403 for another tenant's resource tells the caller that the id is real. An attacker enumerating ids learns exactly which ones exist in your system, which is a meaningful disclosure for anything with sequential or guessable identifiers. Return 404 for both “does not exist” and “not yours” — the two cases must be indistinguishable from outside.

Data model and indexes

javascript · tenantId is first in every index, without exception
// tenants
{ _id, slug, name, plan, region, status, limits: { rps, seats, storageGB }, createdAt }

// memberships — a user can belong to several tenants with different roles
{ _id, userId, tenantId, roles: ['admin'], invitedBy, acceptedAt }
// index: { userId: 1, tenantId: 1 } unique   ← "which tenants am I in"
// index: { tenantId: 1, userId: 1 }          ← "who is in this tenant"

// users — global, NOT per tenant (one identity, many memberships)
{ _id, email, passwordHash, mfa, lastSeenAt }
// index: { email: 1 } unique

// business entity
{ _id, tenantId, status, total, createdAt, version }

const INDEXES = {
  orders: [
    // tenantId FIRST: every query becomes a bounded index range rather than a
    // scan across all tenants. This is what stops a large tenant degrading everyone.
    { key: { tenantId: 1, status: 1, createdAt: -1 } },
    { key: { tenantId: 1, customerId: 1, createdAt: -1 } },
    { key: { tenantId: 1, idempotencyKey: 1 }, unique: true,
      partialFilterExpression: { idempotencyKey: { $type: 'string' } } },
  ],
  api_keys: [{ key: { hash: 1 }, unique: true }, { key: { tenantId: 1, revokedAt: 1 } }],
};
The single most important indexing rule here

Every compound index starts with tenantId. Without it, a query for “orders with status placed” scans across all 5 000 tenants and then filters — so one tenant's query cost scales with everyone else's data. With it, each tenant's queries touch only their own slice of the index, and a tenant onboarding a million records does not slow down anyone else.

Decision 3 — the noisy neighbour

One tenant runs a nightly export loop at 500 rps. Without isolation: your pool fills, p99 rises for all 5 000 tenants, and the SLO breach is charged to everyone's error budget. Four defences, applied in order of cost: 1 rate limit per tenant token bucket keyed by tenantId, limit from their plan 2 queue concurrency per-tenant worker concurrency so one tenant's 10 000 queued jobs cannot starve everyone else 3 query cost budget reject or defer requests whose explain() estimate exceeds a threshold; cap page sizes hard 4 shard placement move the top 1% of tenants to their own cluster once they are large enough to be worth isolating
javascript · plan-aware limits and per-tenant fairness
app.use('/v1', rateLimit({
  store: new RedisStore({ sendCommand: (...a) => redis.call(...a) }),
  windowMs: 60_000,
  // The limit comes from the tenant's plan, not a global constant
  limit: (req) => req.tenant?.limits?.rpm ?? 600,
  keyGenerator: (req) => `t:${req.tenant?.id ?? req.ip}`,
  standardHeaders: 'draft-7',
}));

// Per-tenant worker fairness: round-robin across tenants rather than FIFO,
// so a tenant that enqueued 10 000 jobs does not block a tenant with one.
const worker = new Worker('exports', handler, {
  concurrency: 20,
  // group keys give each tenant its own concurrency allowance within the queue
  group: { concurrency: 2 },
});

// Detect the offender before a human has to
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  res.on('finish', () => tenantCost.observe(
    { tenantId: req.tenant?.id },
    { ms: req.timings?.total ?? 0, docs: req.timings?.docsExamined ?? 0 },
  ));
  next();
});
// Alert: any tenant exceeding 20% of total docsExamined over 15 minutes.

Identity, keys and audit

Users are global, membership is scoped

One identity, many tenants, a role per membership. The session carries the active tenant; switching tenants regenerates the session so a stale tenant id cannot leak across.

API keys are tenant-scoped

Store only a hash. Each key carries scopes and its own rate limit, is attributable in the audit log, and is revocable independently of the user who created it.

Audit log is append-only

Actor, tenant, action, resource, before/after, request id, IP. Enterprise buyers ask for it, and it is the only way to answer “who deleted this” six weeks later.

Offboarding is a feature

Full export on request, and a hard delete with a documented retention window. Both must be tested — the first time you run a tenant delete should not be the day a customer asks for one.

The decisions, summarised

DecisionChosenGiven upRevisit when
Tenancyshared collections + tenantIdphysical isolationa contract requires it, or one tenant exceeds ~20% of the cluster
Isolation enforcementscoped repository + generated testsability to write ad-hoc cross-tenant queries in app codenever — route those through an audited admin path
Not-found semantics404 for other tenantsa more precise error for legitimate usersnever
Fairnessper-tenant limits and queue groupsmaximum throughput for the largest tenantwhen a large tenant is moved to dedicated capacity
Identityglobal users, scoped membershipssimplicity of one user per tenantnever — the alternative breaks the moment someone joins two tenants
Interview follow-up

“How do you guarantee tenant isolation?” The weak answer is “we always filter by tenantId”. The strong answer is structural: the handler has no access to an unscoped collection, the tenant filter is applied by the repository rather than by the caller, the aggregation helper forces the tenant match into the first stage, every compound index leads with tenantId, and a generated test proves every id-bearing endpoint returns 404 across tenants. Discipline fails at the twentieth endpoint; structure does not.

53

Blueprint: high-write ingest pipeline

A telemetry endpoint taking 50 000 events per second is not a CRUD API with a bigger instance. Everything about it — validation, acknowledgement, storage layout, even what "success" means — is different.

Requirements

Peak ingest
50 000 eps3× the daily average, sharp evening spike
Ack latency
< 100 msthe client must not block on our storage
Durability
0.01% loss OKbut a burst must never be dropped wholesale
Query shape
aggregatenobody ever fetches one event by id

That last line is the most important requirement in the document. Because reads are aggregate and time-ranged, the storage layout can be optimised entirely for write throughput and range scans, and individual events never need to be addressable.

The architecture

clients ── batched, compressed ──▶ ingest API (stateless, autoscaled) │ validate shape only, no DB call │ 202 Accepted in <10 ms ▼ durable buffer (Kafka / Kinesis) │ absorbs spikes, survives a storage outage │ retention 24h = your replay window ┌───────────┴───────────┐ ▼ ▼ writer workers rollup workers bulk insert raw $merge into 1m / 1h / 1d ordered:false pre-aggregated tables │ │ ▼ ▼ raw events rollups TTL 7 days retained 2 years └──────── read path ────┘ ▲ query API reads ROLLUPS, never raw
The buffer is the load-bearing decision

Without it, a MongoDB failover or an index build stalls ingest, and 50 000 events per second of backpressure reaches the client within seconds. With it, storage can be unavailable for minutes and nothing is lost — the buffer absorbs it and the writers catch up. It also decouples ingest scaling from write scaling, which is what lets the API layer be trivially stateless.

The ingest endpoint

javascript · nothing on the hot path but validation
// Batched: clients send up to 500 events per request. At 50k eps that is
// 100 rps of HTTP rather than 50 000 — a 500x reduction in request overhead.
const batchSchema = z.object({
  events: z.array(z.object({
    t: z.number().int(),                    // client timestamp, ms
    n: z.string().max(64),                  // event name
    p: z.record(z.union([z.string().max(256), z.number(), z.boolean()])).optional(),
    id: z.string().max(64).optional(),      // client-generated, for dedup
  })).min(1).max(500),
}).strict();

app.post('/v1/ingest',
  express.json({ limit: '1mb', inflate: true }),   // accept gzip from the client
  apiKeyAuth(),                                     // key lookup is CACHED, not a DB read
  ah(async (req, res) => {
    const parsed = batchSchema.safeParse(req.body);
    if (!parsed.success) {
      // Reject the batch, but tell them WHICH events so they can drop only those
      return res.status(400).json({ rejected: parsed.error.issues.slice(0, 20) });
    }

    const now = Date.now();
    const records = parsed.data.events.map((e) => ({
      key: req.apiKey.tenantId,                     // partition key: keeps a tenant ordered
      value: JSON.stringify({
        ...e,
        tenantId: req.apiKey.tenantId,
        // Clamp client clocks. Devices are wrong by hours; without this your
        // rollups grow buckets in 2031 and queries mysteriously miss data.
        t: Math.min(Math.max(e.t, now - 7 * 864e5), now + 5 * 60_000),
        rt: now,                                    // received time, always trustworthy
      }),
    }));

    try {
      await producer.send({ topic: 'events.raw', messages: records, acks: 1 });
    } catch (err) {
      // The buffer is full or unreachable. SHED with a clear signal rather than
      // holding the connection — the client can retry with backoff.
      ingestShed.inc({ reason: 'buffer' });
      res.set('retry-after', '2');
      throw new AppError('ingest temporarily unavailable', { status: 503 });
    }

    res.status(202).json({ accepted: records.length });
  }));
Never validate against the database on the hot path

An API-key lookup that hits MongoDB turns 100 rps of ingest into 100 rps of database reads and couples your ingest availability to your database. Cache keys in memory with a short TTL and a background refresh; a revoked key being accepted for thirty more seconds is an acceptable trade for an ingest path that survives a database failover.

Storage layout — the bucket pattern

javascript · one document per tenant per event per minute
// 50,000 individual documents per second means 50,000 index entries per second
// and enormous per-document overhead. Bucketing turns that into ~hundreds of
// upserts per second against dense documents.
const ops = batch.map((e) => ({
  updateOne: {
    filter: {
      tenantId: e.tenantId,
      name: e.n,
      minute: new Date(Math.floor(e.t / 60_000) * 60_000),
    },
    update: {
      $inc: { count: 1, ...sumFields(e.p) },
      $min: { first: e.t },
      $max: { last: e.t },
      // Keep a bounded sample for debugging; NEVER an unbounded array
      $push: { samples: { $each: [slim(e)], $slice: -20 } },
      $setOnInsert: { createdAt: new Date() },
    },
    upsert: true,
  },
}));

// ordered:false lets the driver continue past individual failures and issue
// the rest in parallel — essential for throughput.
const result = await raw.bulkWrite(ops, { ordered: false, writeConcern: { w: 1 } });

// With ordered:false, a partial failure does NOT throw for the whole batch.
// You must inspect writeErrors or you will silently drop events.
if (result.hasWriteErrors?.()) {
  for (const err of result.getWriteErrors()) {
    if (err.code === 11000) continue;                   // duplicate — already counted
    bulkWriteErrors.inc({ code: String(err.code) });
    await deadLetter.insertOne({ op: ops[err.index], err: err.errmsg, at: new Date() });
  }
}
The monotonic shard-key trap

Sharding on { timestamp: 1 } sends every write to whichever shard owns the highest range — one shard takes 100% of the write load while the rest idle, and adding shards changes nothing. The fix is a compound key leading with something well-distributed: { tenantId: 1, minute: 1 } spreads writes across tenants while keeping one tenant's time range contiguous for queries. If one tenant dominates, add a hashed prefix or a small random salt bucket.

Rollups, and why the query API never touches raw data

javascript · incremental $merge on a schedule
// Recompute only the window that changed, and merge rather than replace.
// A chart query then reads a handful of pre-aggregated documents instead of
// scanning millions of raw events.
await raw.aggregate([
  { $match: { minute: { $gte: windowStart, $lt: windowEnd } } },
  { $group: {
      _id: { tenantId: '$tenantId', name: '$name',
             hour: { $dateTrunc: { date: '$minute', unit: 'hour' } } },
      count: { $sum: '$count' },
      value: { $sum: '$value' },
  } },
  { $merge: {
      into: 'rollup_hourly',
      on: '_id',
      whenMatched: [{ $set: {                    // idempotent: recompute, do not add
        count: '$$new.count', value: '$$new.value', updatedAt: '$$NOW',
      } }],
      whenNotMatched: 'insert',
  } },
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 120_000 }).toArray();

// Retention: raw is a debugging aid, rollups are the product.
await raw.createIndex({ minute: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 7 * 86_400 });
// rollup_hourly: no TTL, or a very long one. It is ~1/1000th the size.
Make the rollup idempotent, not additive

Using $inc in whenMatched means re-running the job double-counts — and you will re-run it, after a crash, a backfill or a late-arriving batch. Setting the recomputed value instead makes the job safe to run any number of times over any window, which is what turns "the rollup is wrong" from an incident into a re-run.

Late and out-of-order events

  • Clamp the client clock at ingest (above) — the single highest-value line in the whole pipeline.
  • Keep the received time alongside the event time. When they disagree wildly, that is a signal about the client, not about the data.
  • Reprocess a trailing window. Re-run rollups for the last few hours on each pass so a mobile client that was offline is folded in — cheap because the rollup is idempotent.
  • Declare a cutoff. Events older than the raw TTL cannot be incorporated. Say so in the documentation rather than discovering it during a customer escalation.

Capacity arithmetic

plaintext · the numbers, worked
INGEST
  50,000 eps ÷ 500 per batch          = 100 HTTP rps
  ~200 bytes/event × 50,000           = 10 MB/s in, ~2 MB/s gzipped
  at ~2,000 rps per Node pod          → 1 pod would do it; run 4 for failure domains

BUFFER
  10 MB/s × 86,400 s                  = ~864 GB/day raw
  24h retention, replication factor 3 = ~2.6 TB of broker storage
  partitions: 50,000 eps ÷ ~5,000 eps per consumer = 10+, round to 24 for headroom

STORAGE (bucketed)
  buckets = tenants × event-names × minutes
  5,000 × 20 × 1,440                  = 144 M documents/day IF every combination fires
  realistically ~2% are active        → ~3 M documents/day, ~1.5 KB each = 4.5 GB/day
  7-day TTL                           → ~32 GB working set for raw
  hourly rollups                      → ~130 K docs/day, ~0.2 GB/day, keep for years

READ
  a 30-day chart = 720 hourly documents. Single-digit milliseconds.
  the same query over raw events would scan ~90 M documents. This is the whole point.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
MongoDB unavailablenone to clientsbuffer absorbs; writers lag then catch up; alert on consumer lag
Buffer unavailableingest returns 503shed with Retry-After; clients buffer locally and retry
Writer falls behinddata is late, not lostscale consumers to the partition count; alert on oldest-message age
Rollup job failscharts staleidempotent re-run over the window; alert on rollup freshness
One tenant floodseveryone's lagper-key rate limit at ingest; a dedicated partition for the largest tenants
Bad client sends garbagerejected batchesper-event validation errors returned so they can fix it; a quarantine topic
Interview follow-up

“Why not just write straight to the database?” Because ingest availability would then equal database availability, a failover or an index build would drop customer data, and a spike would have nowhere to go but backpressure onto the client. The buffer converts a hard availability coupling into a soft latency one — the data is late instead of lost — and that is the entire trade. It also gives you a replay window, which is what makes a bad deploy in the writer recoverable rather than permanent.

54

Blueprint: social feed API

The canonical read-heavy design. One decision dominates everything — whether you build each user's timeline when someone posts, or when someone reads — and the correct answer is both, split by a threshold.

Requirements

Users
10 M2 M daily active
Follow edges
500 Mmedian follows 150; tail follows thousands
Read : write
100 : 1reads are the product
Feed p99
< 200 msincluding media URL resolution
Largest account
10 M followersthis single number decides the architecture

The core decision, with the arithmetic

FAN-OUT ON WRITE (push) — build every follower's timeline at post time read: O(1) — one indexed range scan of a precomputed list ✔ fast write: O(followers) a normal user with 150 followers → 150 inserts ✔ fine a celebrity with 10 M followers → 10,000,000 inserts ✘ at 1 post/sec from big accounts, that is a permanent backlog FAN-OUT ON READ (pull) — query posts from everyone you follow, at read time write: O(1) ✔ trivial read: O(following) following 150 accounts → a 150-way merge, per request, 100× more often at 2 M DAU × 20 feed loads/day = ~460 reads/sec × 150 lookups ✘ HYBRID (what every real system does) · accounts under the threshold → PUSH at write time · accounts over the threshold → PULL at read time · read = precomputed list MERGED WITH a small pull for followed celebrities threshold ≈ 10,000 followers. Above it, fan-out cost exceeds the read cost it saves, because so few of those followers are online at any moment.

Data model

javascript · collections and the indexes that make them work
// posts — the source of truth, immutable except for edits and deletes
{ _id: ObjectId, authorId, text, mediaIds: [], createdAt, deletedAt: null, replyTo }
// index: { authorId: 1, createdAt: -1 }     ← a profile page, and the PULL path

// edges — needs BOTH directions, and they are different queries
{ _id, followerId, followeeId, createdAt }
// index: { followerId: 1, followeeId: 1 } unique  ← "who do I follow" + dedup
// index: { followeeId: 1, followerId: 1 }         ← "who follows X" (the fan-out list)

// authors — the celebrity flag lives here, denormalised for a cheap read
{ _id, handle, name, avatarId, followerCount, isLargeAccount }
// isLargeAccount is maintained by a job when followerCount crosses the threshold.
// Reading it must never require counting edges.

// timeline — the precomputed feed. IDS ONLY, never post bodies.
{ _id: { userId, postId }, authorId, createdAt }
// index: { '_id.userId': 1, createdAt: -1 }
// Bodies live once in `posts`; an edit then changes one document rather than
// every copy in ten million timelines.
Store ids, not content

Copying the post body into every follower's timeline seems to save a lookup, but it makes an edit or a delete an O(followers) update, multiplies storage by the average follower count, and means a moderation removal cannot be guaranteed. Store the id, fetch bodies in one batched query, and cache those bodies by post id — the cache hit rate is extremely high because popular posts appear in many feeds.

The write path

javascript · post, then fan out asynchronously via the outbox
export async function createPost(authorId, input) {
  const post = { _id: new ObjectId(), authorId, ...input, createdAt: new Date(), deletedAt: null };

  await session.withTransaction(async () => {
    await posts.insertOne(post, { session });
    // The user's request completes here. Fan-out is somebody else's problem.
    await outbox.insertOne({ type: 'post.created', payload: { postId: post._id, authorId } }, { session });
  });

  return post;   // p99 of the write path is one insert, regardless of follower count
}

// ---- fan-out worker ----
export async function fanOut({ postId, authorId }) {
  const author = await authors.findOne({ _id: authorId }, { projection: { isLargeAccount: 1 } });

  // Celebrities are NOT fanned out. Their followers pull at read time.
  if (author.isLargeAccount) return;

  const cursor = edges.find({ followeeId: authorId }, { projection: { followerId: 1 } })
                      .batchSize(1_000);

  let batch = [];
  for await (const edge of cursor) {
    batch.push({
      insertOne: { document: { _id: { userId: edge.followerId, postId }, authorId, createdAt: new Date() } },
    });
    if (batch.length === 1_000) { await flush(batch); batch = []; }
  }
  if (batch.length) await flush(batch);
}

async function flush(ops) {
  // ordered:false so one duplicate does not abort the batch; duplicates are
  // expected because the worker may be retried after a partial failure.
  await timeline.bulkWrite(ops, { ordered: false }).catch((e) => {
    if (!e.writeErrors?.every((w) => w.code === 11000)) throw e;
  });
  await sleep(5);   // deliberate pacing: never let one fan-out saturate the cluster
}
Rate-limit the fan-out worker

A user with 9 999 followers posting ten times in a minute generates 100 000 writes. Ten such users at once and the fan-out queue is issuing a million writes a minute against the same cluster serving reads — the feed slows down for everyone because someone was enthusiastic. Cap the worker's global write rate, give fan-out its own connection pool (a bulkhead), and let the queue absorb the backlog. A timeline arriving two seconds late is invisible; a degraded cluster is not.

The read path

javascript · merge the pushed list with a pull for large accounts
export async function getFeed(userId, { cursor, limit = 25 }) {
  const before = cursor ? decodeCursor(cursor) : { createdAt: new Date(), postId: null };

  const [pushed, celebrityIds] = await Promise.all([
    // 1. the precomputed timeline — one index range scan
    timeline.find({
      '_id.userId': userId,
      $or: [
        { createdAt: { $lt: before.createdAt } },
        { createdAt: before.createdAt, '_id.postId': { $lt: before.postId } },
      ],
    }).sort({ createdAt: -1, '_id.postId': -1 }).limit(limit + 1).toArray(),

    // 2. which large accounts do I follow? cached per user, changes rarely
    largeFollowsCache.get(userId),
  ]);

  // 3. pull recent posts from those few accounts — bounded, and usually cached
  const pulled = celebrityIds.length
    ? await posts.find({
        authorId: { $in: celebrityIds },
        createdAt: { $lt: before.createdAt },
        deletedAt: null,
      }).sort({ createdAt: -1 }).limit(limit + 1).toArray()
    : [];

  // 4. merge, dedupe, cut
  const merged = [...pushed.map(toRef), ...pulled.map(toRef)]
    .sort((a, b) => b.createdAt - a.createdAt || (b.postId > a.postId ? 1 : -1))
    .slice(0, limit + 1);

  const hasMore = merged.length > limit;
  const page = hasMore ? merged.slice(0, limit) : merged;

  // 5. hydrate bodies in ONE query, then filter what should no longer be visible
  const bodies = await loadPosts(page.map((p) => p.postId));     // cache-first
  const visible = page
    .map((ref) => bodies.get(String(ref.postId)))
    .filter((p) => p && !p.deletedAt && !isBlocked(userId, p.authorId));

  return {
    data: visible,
    page: { hasMore, nextCursor: hasMore ? encodeCursor(page.at(-1)) : null },
  };
}
Filter at read time, not at fan-out time

Deletes, blocks, mutes and moderation removals happen after fan-out has already copied the id into millions of timelines. Chasing those copies is an O(followers) cleanup that races the reader. Instead, filter during hydration — the post is simply absent from the batch fetch or fails the visibility check. Request limit + a margin so filtering does not return a short page, and clean the timeline rows lazily in the background.

Counters

javascript · sharded counters, because one hot document is a bottleneck
// A viral post takes 50,000 likes/second. A single $inc on one document
// serialises every one of them on the same lock. Spread across N shards.
const SHARDS = 32;

export const like = (postId, userId) => Promise.all([
  // idempotent: the unique index means a double-tap counts once
  likes.insertOne({ _id: `${postId}:${userId}`, at: new Date() }).catch(swallowDuplicate),
  counters.updateOne(
    { _id: `${postId}:${crypto.randomInt(SHARDS)}` },
    { $inc: { likes: 1 } },
    { upsert: true },
  ),
]);

// Read: sum the shards, and cache the total for a few seconds. Nobody needs
// a like count to be exact to the millisecond — and pretending otherwise is
// how you build a bottleneck into the most-read number on the page.
export async function likeCount(postId) {
  const cached = await redis.get(`lc:${postId}`);
  if (cached) return Number(cached);
  const [{ total } = { total: 0 }] = await counters.aggregate([
    { $match: { _id: { $regex: `^${postId}:` } } },
    { $group: { _id: null, total: { $sum: '$likes' } } },
  ]).toArray();
  await redis.set(`lc:${postId}`, total, 'EX', 5);
  return total;
}
// A nightly job reconciles the sharded total against the likes collection.

Capacity

plaintext
WRITES
  2 M DAU × 0.5 posts/day        = 1 M posts/day ≈ 12 posts/sec average, ~60 peak
  fan-out: 12 × 150 median       = ~1,800 timeline inserts/sec average
  celebrities excluded           → the tail does not blow this up

READS
  2 M DAU × 20 feed loads        = 40 M feed reads/day ≈ 460/sec, ~2,000 peak
  each = 1 timeline scan + 1 small pull + 1 batched body fetch (mostly cached)

STORAGE
  timeline rows capped at 1,000 per user
  10 M users × 1,000 × ~80 bytes = ~800 GB  ← the dominant cost
  ↳ cap by ACTIVE users instead: build timelines lazily on first login and
    expire them for users dormant 30+ days. Typically an 80% reduction.
  posts: 1 M/day × 500 bytes     = 0.5 GB/day, ~180 GB/year
Do not materialise timelines for dormant users

Most registered users are not active. Fanning out to all 10 million is mostly writing rows nobody will ever read, and it is the single largest line in the storage estimate. Fan out only to users seen in the last 30 days, and build a dormant user's timeline on demand at their next login — a one-off pull that takes a second and then reverts to the fast path.

What breaks first, and in what order you fix it

  1. Fan-out backlog when a mid-size account goes viral. Fix: rate-limit and bulkhead the worker, and lower the celebrity threshold.
  2. Timeline collection size. Fix: cap rows per user, and stop fanning out to dormant accounts.
  3. Hot post reads during a viral moment. Fix: cache bodies by post id — the hit rate approaches 100% for exactly the posts causing the load.
  4. Counter contention. Fix: shard the counters and cache the totals.
  5. The merge cost for users following hundreds of large accounts. Fix: cache the pulled celebrity slice per author for a few seconds; it is identical for every follower.
  6. Single-cluster write throughput. Fix: shard timeline on _id.userId — it partitions perfectly, because a feed read only ever touches one user's rows.
Interview follow-up

“Which do you pick, push or pull?” Neither, and saying so is the answer: push for the overwhelming majority of accounts because reads are 100× more frequent than writes, pull for accounts above roughly ten thousand followers because fanning out to millions of mostly-inactive users costs more than it saves, and merge the two at read time. The threshold is a tunable, not a constant — and the follow-up question is usually about deletes and blocks, where the answer is that you filter at hydration rather than trying to chase copies through millions of timelines.

55

The senior checklist

Not a summary — a set of statements you can verify against a real service. If you cannot say “yes, and here is where” for an item, that is the next thing to fix.

Correctness

  • Every request body, query string and path parameter is validated by a schema that rejects unknown keys, before any handler sees it.
  • No handler can construct a database query without the tenant or owner filter — it is applied by the repository, not by the caller.
  • There is a generated test proving every id-bearing endpoint returns 404, not 403, for another tenant's resource.
  • Uniqueness is enforced by a unique index, and the duplicate-key error is mapped to a 409. There is no read-then-write uniqueness check anywhere.
  • Every non-idempotent endpoint accepts an Idempotency-Key, stores a request fingerprint, and replays the stored response.
  • Every queue handler and event consumer is idempotent, backed by a unique index on the job or event id.
  • Anywhere a lost update would matter, writes are guarded by a version in the filter and return 412 on mismatch.
  • Transactions are used only where a single document cannot express the invariant — and every one has a documented reason.
  • Money is stored as integer minor units or Decimal128. There is no Double anywhere near a balance.
  • Every state transition is guarded by the current state in the filter, so a replay is a no-op rather than a second transition.

Reliability

  • Every outbound call has a timeout — HTTP, database, cache, queue — and the timeouts decrease inwards from the client's budget.
  • Retries exist only for idempotent operations, use full jitter, and are capped by a retry budget.
  • Each external dependency is classified critical or optional in writing, and every optional one has a tested fallback.
  • Circuit breakers are scoped per dependency, and there is a metric for breaker state.
  • Graceful shutdown fails readiness first, waits for the load balancer, drains in-flight requests, closes the consumer, then exits — with a hard deadline.
  • The liveness probe checks nothing but the process. No dependency check can restart the fleet.
  • Rollback is one command, it has been rehearsed, and it takes under a minute.
  • There is no single point of failure in the request path that has not been deliberately accepted and written down.

Performance

  • Every query in a hot path has been run through explain('executionStats'); docs-examined is within a small multiple of docs-returned.
  • Every field exposed as sortable or filterable is covered by an index — and a test asserts the two lists match.
  • No endpoint can return an unbounded result set. Pagination limits are capped server-side, and pagination is keyset, not offset.
  • There is no N+1 in any list endpoint; related data is fetched in one batched query or denormalised.
  • Every cache has a documented invalidation strategy and a jittered TTL.
  • Event-loop delay is a monitored metric with an alert, and no synchronous CPU work over ~10 ms runs on the request path.
  • Connection pool size × replica count is below the database's connection limit, and there is an alert before that ceiling.
  • JSON payloads over roughly 100 kB are parsed off the main thread or streamed.

Security

  • Every item in the OWASP API top ten maps to a named control in this codebase, and you can point at the file.
  • trust proxy is a hop count or a subnet, never true.
  • Rate limits are distributed (not in-process), keyed per user or tenant, and much tighter on login, signup, password reset and anything that costs money.
  • Secrets come from a manager at runtime, are rotatable without a code change, and never appear in an image layer, a log line or an error response.
  • The application's database user cannot drop collections or read other databases.
  • Logs are redacted at the emitter for tokens, cookies, passwords and PII — verified by a test.
  • Any user-supplied URL that the server fetches is allowlisted and IP-checked against private ranges, with redirects disabled.
  • Dependency and image scanning run in CI, and there is an owner for triage rather than a wall of ignored alerts.
  • Error responses carry a correlation id and never a stack trace or a driver message.

Data

  • Backups exist, and a restore has been performed into a scratch environment with the RTO measured. An untested backup is not a backup.
  • Every migration follows expand/contract; the only irreversible step is isolated in its own later release.
  • Backfills are throttled, resumable and idempotent, and they watch replication lag.
  • Index builds happen as a monitored operation before the deploy, never inside it.
  • Every ephemeral collection has a TTL — sessions, idempotency keys, outbox rows, processed-event markers, soft deletes.
  • There is a retention and deletion policy per collection, and an inventory of where PII lives.
  • The upgrade path from every currently shipped version has been tested with realistic data.

Operability

  • Every log line is structured and carries a request id, tenant id and service version.
  • Metrics are labelled by route pattern, never raw URL. Cardinality is deliberate.
  • RED metrics exist per route; database operation duration and pool checkout time are separately visible.
  • Traces are wired, with correlation across services and into queue handlers.
  • There is at least one SLO with a burn-rate alert, and the error budget policy is agreed in advance.
  • Every alert is actionable, urgent and links to a runbook naming the first three commands.
  • Queue depth and oldest-job age are monitored; the latter is the one that pages.
  • You can answer “is it slow for everyone or one tenant?” from a dashboard, without writing a query.

Delivery

  • CI runs format, lint with --fatal-infos, tests, and a contract diff that fails on an unversioned breaking API change.
  • Code generation runs in CI and the build fails if the working tree changes.
  • Integration tests run against a real database and cache in containers, not against mocks.
  • The artefact is built once and promoted; environments never rebuild.
  • Deploys are rolling with maxUnavailable: 0, a preStop drain, and automated smoke tests afterwards.
  • Feature flags exist and are the mechanism for disabling a bad feature without a release.
  • Configuration is validated at boot and the process refuses to start when it is invalid.

Cost

  • Cost per request and per tenant are measurable, and a per-tenant outlier triggers investigation.
  • Log and metric volume are bounded by sampling and retention tiers, and reviewed like any other resource.
  • Unused indexes are audited; collections without a retention policy are treated as a bug.
  • Non-production environments scale to zero out of hours.
  • There is a daily spend-anomaly alert that reaches an engineer, not only finance.

Using this in a design review

Do not read it aloud. Pick the five items most relevant to what is being proposed and ask for evidence — “show me where the tenant filter is applied”, “what happens when the payment provider takes ten seconds”, “what does rollback look like after this migration”. The value is in the specific answer, and the absence of one is the finding.

Using it in an interview

The closing thought

Almost every question in a senior backend interview is one of these items dressed as a scenario. “How do you prevent double charging?” is idempotency. “What happens if the database is slow?” is timeouts, pools and bulkheads. “How would you scale this?” is indexes, caching and the shard key. “How do you know it works?” is SLOs and the four states of every screen. If you can explain why each item is on the list — the specific failure it prevents and what it costs you — you are not reciting a checklist, you are describing judgement. That is the thing being assessed.

Request Pipeline Handbook — Node, Express and MongoDB from the event loop to a sharded cluster.

Written against Node 20/22, Express 4 and 5, and MongoDB 7. Where behaviour differs between versions the current behaviour is stated and the older one noted, because production runs on both.

The three chapters to reread: the event loop, indexes and query plans, and diagnosing a slow API.
